The Bones of Empire

The Bones of Empire

Riarhin VII

Part I — A Place at the Table

Chapter 1 — Below the Chain

Mab Tulley wanted her blanket back.

Riarhin had been asleep with it over her head. She came awake to a hand on her shoulder and the smell of hides, and for a moment the hand and the smell belonged together in a place she had no intention of remembering. Her good arm moved. Mab caught the wrist before it reached her.

“Blanket,” she said. “You can keep your shirt.”

The river lay grey beyond her shoulder. A thin rain had begun, too fine to make separate drops on the boards. Saikou sat against the hides with his coat drawn over his knees. There was nobody at the other end of the barge where the city had been.

Riarhin released Mab’s sleeve.

“Sorry.”

“People have said worse before breakfast.”

Mab shook the blanket, folded it twice and put it over Lyra, who lay with her back to the salted skins. Then she stepped across Riarhin to reach the pole. She had taken her pipe out for the rain and tucked it into the front of her coat, where it made a square lump.

“Get under that bit,” she said, pointing with her chin. “There’s a cover. I’ll want to use the stern.”

Riarhin put her right hand on the gunwale and tried to stand.

The wound in her side pulled her short. She stopped with one knee under her and waited, looking down at a knot in the board, until she could draw a whole breath. Mab watched without offering help. When Riarhin moved again, the barge-woman put the butt of the pole within reach.

“Mind the hook.”

She used it. Getting upright was a short, ugly business. The few feet to the shelter took longer. She had remembered the cover as canvas. It was a low wooden lid over a place where Mab kept a cooking pot, dry fuel and things too small to be sold by the hide. Berent had already cleared a space. He sat beside it with his stick between his knees and a loaf in his lap.

“How far?” she asked.

“To breakfast?”

“To somewhere with a roof.”

“This has a roof.”

She looked at him.

He held out the bread. “You’ve to bend.”

There was no answer she could make without hurting herself. She took the bread and let him steady her while she lowered herself onto the folded sail he had found. The little space smelt of damp wood and old onions. Rain clicked on the lid above her.

Berent had cut the loaf with a short knife borrowed from Mab. He cut another piece now and began working a heel of hard cheese into strips thin enough to bite. His knee stood out at an awkward angle beneath the cloth. He had wrapped the joint, though the wrapping was wet.

“Change that,” she said.

“When we stop.”

“It’s wet.”

“So is the rest of me.” He handed her the cheese. “You first.”

She ate. The bread scraped the roof of her mouth. She had to think about swallowing, and then about taking another bite, and by the third she wanted it. Berent kept cutting without looking at her.

Beyond the opening, Cadan lifted Lyra. She made a small sound when he shifted his hold, and he stopped immediately.

“The left,” she said.

He changed his hands. He brought her across the barge with her grammar laid on her stomach, one of her own hands resting on it. There had been things Riarhin had meant to do when they were clear of the city. Find somewhere warm. Get the wounds looked at. Buy clothes that had belonged to nobody who was being sought. She had arranged these things in an order during the night. Watching Lyra’s bare ankle come out from the blanket, she could remember only that she had still not done any of them.

Cadan crouched at the shelter.

“Where would you like her?”

“Here,” Riarhin said, beginning to move.

“Stay,” said Lyra.

The word came out with so little breath that all three of them obeyed it.

“Beside you.”

They made a place. Berent crawled out into the rain, carrying the bread, and Cadan lowered her onto the sail. Her hair brushed Riarhin’s hand. It had been cut in places where a comb could no longer go through it. One uneven length lay against her cheek, dark with moisture.

“Your book,” Riarhin said.

“I know where it is.”

“Do you want—”

“I want you to stop moving.”

She stopped.

Lyra turned carefully onto her side. Her knees touched Riarhin’s thigh. She shut her eyes, opened them again and looked past her at the low lid, measuring the space. Riarhin knew the look. She moved her own leg out of the way of the opening.

“You can see out,” she said.

“Yes.”

They lay without speaking while Berent argued with Mab about the bread. He said he could eat standing. Mab said she had never disputed it; she wanted him out of the place where the pole came down.

Lyra’s hand came under the edge of Riarhin’s coat and held a fold of the shirt.

They stopped at a landing below a mill, where the bank had been cut into steps and strengthened with hurdles. The miller came out to tell Mab the landing was private. Mab asked whether his wife was in. He looked at the barge, then at her, and went back inside.

By the time Riarhin had got herself out from under the cover, a woman was coming down the steps with a basket on one arm and a lantern in the other. She wore a man’s leather apron over her skirts. One corner of the apron was white with flour. She and Mab kissed with the quick awkwardness of women holding things.

“You’re late.”

“There was a chain.”

“There’s generally a chain.”

“They’ve taken to thinking about it.”

The miller’s wife looked at the passengers. She counted them without moving her lips. Her eyes stopped on Lyra.

“The room over the wheel’s empty.”

“Too many steps,” Mab said.

“The drying room, then. He’ll complain.”

“He’s begun.”

The woman put the basket in Mab’s hands and came aboard. She asked Lyra whether she could walk. Lyra said she could, and the woman asked how far. After a moment Lyra looked at the bank.

“Perhaps not that.”

They carried her between them on the folded sail. The miller’s wife walked backwards up the first steps, strong in the shoulders and annoyed when Cadan tried to take her end. Riarhin followed with the grammar tucked under her right arm. At the top the miller stood holding a sack whose bottom had been mended in red thread.

“I need that room.”

“You needed it yesterday,” his wife said. “You didn’t put anything in it.”

“I was going to.”

“Then you’ve had your disappointment early.”

He moved aside.

The drying room was long and low, with poles laid across supports under the ceiling. Empty cloth bags hung from some of them. Heat came through the wall beside the mill hearth; the plaster there had cracked and been rubbed smooth by hands. There were two stools, a bench and a wooden bed frame carrying a thin mattress. Riarhin looked at the window, the door, the little square hatch beside the hearth, and then at Lyra being set down on the bed.

The woman noticed the last part.

“That’s for the ash. It won’t take a person.”

Riarhin put the grammar beside the pillow.

“I wasn’t asking.”

“No. Your man with the sleeves was.”

Cadan had opened the hatch. He closed it gently.

“Thank you,” he said.

The woman regarded him for a moment, then told him where the water was.

Her name was Bette. She gave it when Saikou asked how the lodging should be paid for, and seemed surprised that this was a question requiring discussion. The price she named was more than Riarhin had expected and less than Saikou had prepared himself to offer. He paid it. Bette counted the coins into her apron pocket and went to fetch clean linen.

“Separate water,” she said on her way out. “For the sores. Don’t wash a face with it afterwards.”

When she had gone, Riarhin sat on the bench and tried to undo her coat.

The second fastening defeated her. Her fingers had stiffened in the cold, and the leather loop had swollen. She worked at it until the pain in her arm began to throb in time with her heart. Then she heard herself make a sound she would have disliked in someone else.

Saikou turned from the window.

“May I?”

She held the fastening out. He undid it, and the one below, and helped her draw the coat away from the bandage. It stuck. He stopped.

“We’ll wet that.”

“I know.”

“Yes.” He laid the coat across her lap and went to the hearth.

She watched him find a cup, reject it, find a basin and put it down where there was no water. His hand went to the inside pocket of his coat. He took out a little book and opened it. After looking at the page he closed it again.

“Cadan’s fetching it,” she said.

“I heard.”

She waited. He stood beside the empty basin with the book in his hand.

“Saikou.”

He looked up.

“Sit down until it comes.”

He did, on the other end of the bench. For a while they watched Lyra sleep.

Bette had once worked in a household with horses, and cleaned Riarhin’s wounds as if she expected to be kicked. She announced each touch before making it and ignored the answer unless it was useful. There was a younger woman with her, narrow-faced, carrying a wooden box. She looked at the cut in Riarhin’s arm, asked how old it was, and then asked again when Riarhin gave her the answer.

“Four days,” Saikou said.

“Five mornings,” Riarhin corrected.

The younger woman looked at the two of them.

“Was it washed?”

“Yes.”

“With what?”

“Water.”

“Clean?”

“As we could get.”

She accepted that and went to work. Her hands were warmer than Riarhin had expected. She kept her tools on a folded cloth and made Bette move the lamp twice. When she was done she told Riarhin what would require attention, what could wait, and what she must stop doing for the next few days.

“Lifting,” Riarhin said.

“Among other things.”

“Walking?”

“Walking where?”

“To a boat.”

“Is the boat leaving?”

“Not yet.”

“Then not yet.”

She packed her box. Bette gave her a bowl from the hearth, and she ate standing by the door before going home.

Later, when the room had grown dark except for the lamp, Berent moved the second stool into the corner by the entrance. He set his stick against the wall and began to lower himself onto it. Riarhin watched the effort he made to keep his weight off the bad leg.

“We can take turns,” she said.

“You can sleep.”

“You need sleep too.”

“I’ve had some.”

“When?”

“On the barge.”

“You were at the tiller.”

“Not all the time.” He settled, breathing through his nose, and reached for the blanket Bette had given him. “I can sit in a chair, Riarhin.”

The way he said her name made her look at him properly.

“I know.”

“Then let me.”

Cadan stood by the hearth, warming his hands. He turned them slowly, showing one side and then the other to the heat.

“I can take the second part,” he said. “If you tell me when the first part ends.”

Berent glanced at him. “I’ll wake you.”

“Thank you.”

There was a scrape at the door. The miller entered carrying an extra mattress. He put it down hard enough to raise dust and told them it had belonged to his daughter before she married.

“I’ll want it clean.”

“We’ll keep it covered,” Saikou said.

“And there’s to be nobody smoking.”

“None of us smokes.”

The miller looked disappointed. He inspected the room, found nothing else that could readily be forbidden and left.

Berent waited until his steps had gone.

“Can you all manage that?”

Cadan looked at him gravely. “I believe so.”

Riarhin laughed. It hurt enough to make her press her hand against her side, which made Berent laugh too, and then Lyra opened her eyes and asked what they had done.

“Nothing,” Riarhin said.

“That’s generally when I worry.”

She stayed awake long enough to drink half a cup of broth. Riarhin held it while she drank. When Lyra’s hands began to shake, neither of them mentioned it.

The letter had dried badly. A drop of river water had gone through the first line, spreading the pencil into a grey thumbprint. Riarhin laid it on the grammar, smoothed the fold with her right hand and read what she had managed on the barge.

A.,

We are out. I am coming to Silkstrand.

She had written I am well and crossed it out. Below the crossing-out she had written I have been hurt, but I can travel when there is a boat.

She looked at the sleeping room. Lyra was on the bed. Saikou had taken the mattress by the hearth, with his satchel under his head. Cadan lay on his back beside him, hands folded over his middle. Berent sat at the door with his eyes open.

She added the names of the living.

Then she sat for some time with the pencil resting against the paper. She could hear the wheel through the wall, a catch and a fall, a catch and a fall. Ailsa would hear how she had chosen the words. She would read the crossed-out line before the one beneath it. She might hold the page flat with the heel of her hand, the way she held cloth that insisted on curling, and be angry while she looked for something she could do.

Riarhin wrote, I want to see you.

Below it, after a space, she wrote, Please tell me where to come.

She folded the letter before she could add anything else.

Chapter 2 — The Door Ailsa Opens

The house had a blue door and a woman outside it beating a rug with a piece of cane.

Riarhin had passed it once already. The number on the lintel had been painted over when somebody repaired the front, and the blue was almost black beneath the street’s shade. She had expected a sign. Ailsa’s answer had said blue door, third turning above the covered well, and nothing about the rug, the crates beside it, or the little boy sitting on the step with one stocking down.

The boy watched her stop.

“Are you wanting someone?”

“Ailsa.”

“She’s in.”

“Thank you.”

He remained on the step.

The woman struck the rug again. Dust drifted towards Riarhin and settled on the shoulder of her coat.

“You’ll have to go round him,” she said. “He’s resting.”

The boy drew his knees closer together, which made almost no difference. Riarhin went round him. The stairs from the quay had left her short of breath, and the last few streets had made her aware of the edge of the bandage beneath her clothes. She had sent the others ahead with Saikou to the rooms arranged by his factor. She had wanted to come here by herself.

Now she stood before the blue door with no hand free. In one she held Remorse, wrapped. The other carried a parcel of oranges bought from a man whose price she had failed to question.

She put the oranges on the step and knocked.

“Mind those,” she said to the boy.

He inspected the parcel. “I am.”

Inside, something scraped the floor. A voice called from farther back in the house, asking whether anybody was going to answer. Then the bolt lifted.

Ailsa opened the door with a length of red cloth over one shoulder and a pin between her lips.

For a moment neither of them moved.

Riarhin had carried the shape of her through so many places that the difference was startling. Ailsa’s hair was shorter. The new fastening drew it back from her temples, showing a little more grey above the ears. Her cheek was marked where she had slept against a crease. She looked tired and solid and was looking at Riarhin as if something expected had arrived in an unexpected size.

She took the pin from her mouth.

“Oh,” she said.

Riarhin had prepared a greeting. She could remember preparing it, even the place on the ship where she had stood while deciding how little to say. The words had gone.

Ailsa put her hand against the side of Riarhin’s face.

It was warm. Riarhin shut her eyes.

“You’re here.”

“Yes.”

“Come in, then.”

She opened her eyes. “There are oranges.”

“I can see the oranges.”

“On the step.”

“I’ll get them.”

“The boy—”

“He’s got a name. He’ll give it to you if you wait long enough.” Ailsa took the wrapped sword, leaned it inside the door and drew Riarhin across the threshold. “Come here.”

The embrace caught the wound in her side. She made a noise before she could stop herself.

Ailsa released her at once.

“Where?”

“It’s all right.”

“Where?”

“Left side. Arm.”

Ailsa looked down, then up at her. The red cloth had slipped to the floor between them.

“Can I touch you anywhere?”

Riarhin nodded.

“Show me.”

She took Ailsa’s hand and put it at the back of her neck. Ailsa came close carefully, and Riarhin bent her head until their foreheads touched. The street was making an intolerable amount of noise. The rug beat against the wall, a cart wheel rattled over the paving, and behind them a woman began to say something and stopped.

Ailsa kissed her.

Riarhin had thought about this too. Thinking had been of little use.

When they drew apart, Ailsa was smiling and wet-eyed and angry. All three showed plainly. Riarhin put her right hand against her cheek, and Ailsa turned to kiss the palm before taking it down.

“Sit before you fall.”

“I’m not going to fall.”

“You’re leaning on the door.”

She was. She let herself be led to the chair beside the front window.

The house belonged to a woman who let rooms and disapproved of meals taken in them. Consequently everybody ate in the room at the back, except when they wished to disagree with her. Riarhin learned this from Iona, who brought a cup of tea and explained the household before asking whether she might embrace her.

“Yes.”

“Where?”

Ailsa laughed from the hearth. “I’ve asked.”

Iona bent and put her arms lightly around Riarhin’s shoulders. She smelt of soap and the hot metal of an iron. When she straightened she looked at Riarhin’s face for a moment, then took the cup out of her hands and put it on the table.

“You’ll spill it if you keep that elbow up.”

“It’s better up.”

“Then we’ll put something under it.”

She folded a cushion and fetched a second chair. Ailsa watched without intervening. Riarhin remembered passing Ailsa a folded length across the cutting table at Glenruan, their hands meeting under it. She shifted her injured arm on the cushion. Iona moved a bundle away from the hearth with her foot. Ailsa caught it before it touched the floor and put it on the dresser. Neither looked at the other.

Mairi came in with her son behind her. He was the boy from the step and had an orange in each hand.

“I kept them,” he told Riarhin.

“So I see.”

“He’s been very helpful,” Mairi said, in a voice that suggested they had discussed the meaning of the word recently. “Have you eaten?”

“On the ship.”

“When?”

Riarhin considered.

Ailsa turned from the hearth. “Don’t bother. There’s soup.”

It was fish soup with pieces of potato and something sharp she could not immediately place. She had eaten well enough at sea after the first few days, when swallowing ceased to be work and she could sit through a whole meal. This was different. The bowl was hot against her hand. Ailsa stood beside her cutting bread, and the open window admitted voices from a street she had known before any of these people came into it. She took a mouthful and had to put the spoon down.

Nobody asked why.

Mairi began telling Iona about a woman who wanted three hems finished before the end of the week and seemed to believe that wanting all three ought to make each cheaper. Iona asked whether she had offered to come and do one herself. Ailsa passed Riarhin the bread.

She ate the rest of the soup.

Afterwards Iona and Mairi went out with the boy, taking two oranges and a bundle wrapped in brown cloth. The woman who owned the house looked in, found them sitting at the table where meals were meant to be eaten and could only ask whether they had finished. Ailsa said they had. When the woman had gone she moved her chair closer.

“How bad?”

“Better.”

“That wasn’t it.”

Riarhin rested her right hand on the edge of the table. A little fish bone lay beside her bowl. She had set it there with the spoon and forgotten it.

“I was cut here.” She indicated her side. “And through the arm. The arm is closing. The side is slower.”

“Who looks at them?”

“There was a woman below Valora. Another in Fairport. On the ship, the mate.”

“The mate.”

“He was good.”

“I’m sure.”

“He’d had practice.”

Ailsa rubbed a hand over her mouth. “I know what that means.”

Riarhin looked at her then.

“I can walk. I can eat. I slept last night.”

“All night?”

“Most of it.”

“Did you wake because it hurt?”

“Yes.”

“Then say that.”

Outside, the rug was being beaten again. Ailsa looked towards the window as though she might go out and take the cane away.

“I had your letter,” she said. “I knew you were coming. I kept it beside the bed, and every time I read the thing I could see you crossing out being well. You left enough of it for me to read.”

“I didn’t have another sheet.”

“You had a whole other side.”

Riarhin looked at the table. After a moment Ailsa reached across and took her hand.

“I’m glad you left it,” she said. “I’d have been furious with you if you’d sent the clean one.”

“I didn’t know what to tell you.”

“You could have told me you were frightened.”

Riarhin felt her fingers tighten.

Ailsa waited. Her thumb moved once along the side of Riarhin’s hand and stopped.

“I thought I wouldn’t get out,” Riarhin said.

Ailsa’s face changed. She came round the table, pulled the other chair close and sat with their knees touching.

“I’m here,” Riarhin said.

“Aye.”

“I came.”

“I can see you.”

For a while they sat that way. Riarhin could hear Ailsa breathing through her nose. She wanted to say something that would stop the hurt in her face, and found nothing she trusted enough to use.

Ailsa leaned forward and laid her head against Riarhin’s right shoulder.

The room upstairs was smaller than the room in Kilrenny. It had a bed, a chest with a broken handle, a washstand and a window that opened over the lane. Somebody in the next building kept pigeons. They walked on the roof and made sounds like cautious men disputing a price.

Ailsa shut the door with her heel.

“You can laugh,” she said. “I did.”

“At what?”

“The room.”

“I wasn’t.”

“No. You were looking for a second way out.”

Riarhin turned from the window.

“Roof,” Ailsa said. “Into the pigeon place, I suppose. Though the man’s vicious about them. He’ll come after you with something.”

“It’s a short drop to the lower roof.”

“Good. We’ll remember.” She put the red cloth on the chest. “Now let me see the bandage.”

Riarhin stood while she undid the shirt. It took longer than it should because Ailsa’s fingers were shaking. When the linen showed, Ailsa made herself finish unfastening the cuff before she looked properly. The arm had been freshly dressed that morning. The side had a stain she had hoped to turn away from the light.

“Has that been there long?”

“Since the stairs.”

“Were there stairs at the other place?”

“Saikou found rooms below.”

“Of course he did.”

“I wanted to come.”

“I know you did.”

Ailsa drew the shirt off the good shoulder and let it hang while she looked at the cut. She did not touch it. Then she kissed Riarhin above the bandage, on the unmarked skin, and rested her forehead there for a moment.

Riarhin put her hand in her hair.

The pigeons went on disputing. A boy shouted from the lane; someone shouted back. Ailsa lifted her face, and the next kiss was less careful.

They managed the bed badly and laughed at the managing. Riarhin could not lie on her left side. Ailsa’s elbow found a hollow in the mattress, and the pillow fell, and for a little while the important thing was whether they could reach it without either of them getting up. When Ailsa finally leaned across her to pull it back, Riarhin held her there.

“Stay.”

“I live here.”

“Just there.”

Ailsa stopped moving. Her hair brushed Riarhin’s mouth. Riarhin put her good hand against the warmth of her back and felt the breath go out of her.

There was no hurry after that.

She woke to voices below the window and the smell of onions cooking in fat.

The room was darkening. Ailsa lay on her back beside her, awake, looking at the ceiling. Their hands were linked above the blanket. Riarhin had slept long enough for the stiffness to settle in her shoulder, but the pain in her side was quiet. She listened to the noises of the house. A door closed. Someone was laughing in the stairwell.

“What time?”

“Nearly supper.”

“I slept.”

“You did.”

“Did you?”

“For a bit.”

Ailsa turned her head. “Are you staying tonight?”

“If I can.”

“You can. I meant, have you promised anyone to be elsewhere?”

“No.”

“Then I’ll tell them below.”

She began to get up. Riarhin let her hand go reluctantly, and Ailsa smiled when she noticed. She put on a loose gown, opened the door and spoke to somebody on the stairs. Riarhin heard her say another bowl, then no, she’s awake now. The answer made her laugh.

When she came back she carried the parcel of oranges, much reduced.

“The little one thinks they were for him.”

“They can be.”

“Don’t tell him that. Mairi’s trying to teach him to ask.”

Ailsa sat on the bed and began peeling one. She used her thumb, working it under the rind in four clean strips, and handed Riarhin a segment.

“The children are coming,” she said.

Riarhin swallowed.

“Here?”

“Here first.”

“Both?”

“Both. Their father’s bringing them.”

“When did you hear?”

“Before your letter. Not much before.” Ailsa laid a strip of peel on the paper. “I asked him. When we reached here. I wasn’t having everything said to them by somebody who’d heard it from somebody else.”

“Are they safe?”

“As safe as anybody travelling out of Ubral now. He’s taking care.”

Riarhin looked at her hand on the blanket. There was a narrow crease in the skin where Ailsa had held it while she slept.

“I could arrange—”

“It’s arranged.”

“Yes.”

“You can be here when they come.”

She looked up.

“If you want,” Ailsa said. “I’d like that.”

Riarhin took another piece of orange.

“I want to.”

“Good.”

Ailsa ate the last segment and folded the peel into the paper. Downstairs someone struck a pot with a spoon, calling the house to supper. She got off the bed and held out the gown Riarhin had left over the chair.

“We’ll be slow on the stairs,” she said. “They can wait.”

Chapter 3 — A House Offered

Saikou arrived while Ailsa was arguing over a length of cloth.

The buyer had laid it across both forearms and was holding it up to the light, looking for faults. He had found one, which Ailsa had shown him before he began, and seemed unable to forgive her for anticipating him. Riarhin sat by the window with a cup of tea and an orange pip under her thumb. Ailsa had told her to stay out of it.

“You’ll lose that much at the edge,” the buyer said.

“You’re buying what’s inside the edge.”

“But I must cut it.”

“You must cut all of it, unless you’ve somebody shaped like a table.”

He looked at Riarhin for support. She drank her tea.

Saikou waited in the doorway with his hat in his hands. When Ailsa finally brought the buyer to a price he could deplore while paying it, Saikou moved aside to let him pass. The man told him that northern women were difficult. Saikou said he had found considerable variation.

Ailsa laughed after the door shut.

“Would you like tea?”

“If there is some.”

“There will be.”

He sat at the table. The chair had one short leg, and he adjusted it with a folded scrap taken from his pocket. He had shaved that morning. There was a little missed patch beneath his jaw which Riarhin could have told him about and chose to leave alone.

“My mother has written,” he said.

“Has she?” Ailsa put the pot on the hearth. “To you or to me?”

“To both of us. Yours is rather longer.”

He took out two packets. One was narrow and folded in the way Riarhin knew; the other had been enclosed in heavier paper, with Ailsa’s name written in a hand large enough to be read without bringing it close. The seal had not been broken.

Ailsa wiped her hands on her apron before taking it.

“Will you stay while I read?”

“If you wish.”

“You’ve come all this way.”

“Three streets.”

“Then you’ll be very tired.”

She fetched the tea and sat down.

The paper made a good sound as she opened it. She read quickly at first, slowed, and went back to the beginning of the second sheet. Riarhin watched the movement of her eyes. There were four sheets altogether. Ailsa put each finished one face down beside the cup, neatly aligned, until at the last she stopped with her finger resting halfway down the page.

“She’s thought of the children.”

“Yes,” Saikou said.

Ailsa looked at him. “That wasn’t praise. I’m telling you where I am.”

“I beg your pardon.”

She finished the letter. Then she collected the sheets, put them in order again and held them out to Riarhin.

“Read it.”

“Is it private?”

“I’m giving it to you.”

Riarhin took it.

Nakato Ira wrote warmly. She hoped the journey to Silkstrand had proved less arduous than the circumstances of departure. She regretted that distance prevented her receiving Ailsa herself. Her son had spoken of the household with affection, and she was glad that arrangements made in haste could now give way to a more comfortable prospect.

Riarhin looked up at Saikou. He was watching the hearth.

There was a house on the Bronze Coast of Dhahara, outside the press of the harbor but within easy reach of it. The house stood on land held by a branch of Nakato’s commercial interests. It had a garden, a dependable well and rooms sufficient for guests. The people who maintained it would remain employed at the accustomed rates. Provision could be made for tuition suitable to the children, including languages and whatever further studies they showed an inclination to pursue. If Ailsa wished to continue her trade, Nakato’s factor could introduce buyers without claiming an exclusive right to the work.

The next sheet described passage. The third described the house. On the fourth Nakato mentioned, almost as an afterthought, that Ailsa would of course be under no obligation to remain if the place did not suit her. An allowance for a further journey would be placed at her disposal, independently of Saikou’s accounts.

Riarhin read that part twice.

“Well?” Ailsa asked.

“It’s generous.”

“Very.”

“What does she want?”

Saikou turned from the hearth. “Several things.”

“From her.”

“I cannot give you an answer she has not given me.”

“Do you believe that?”

“I believe she would like Ailsa to be happy in a house she has provided. I believe she would enjoy hearing that the children were doing well. I also believe that, when next I wished to refuse her something, she would find an occasion to tell me how well they were doing.”

Ailsa took her cup between both hands.

“Would she turn us out?”

“No.”

“Take the money back?”

“Not under these terms.”

“You sound sure.”

“I know my mother.”

Riarhin looked at the letter again. The handwriting had no hesitation in it. The lines ran straight to the same margin, ending with enough space that no word had to be crowded. She could imagine the room in which it had been written, though she had never seen it: good light, sufficient paper, someone outside the door ready to take what was finished.

“Could she keep you there?” she asked Ailsa.

Ailsa leaned back.

“She can’t keep me here, apparently.”

“That’s not what I meant.”

“I know what you meant. I’m asking whether you’ve read this part.”

“I have.”

“And whether it’s true.”

Saikou said, “It can be made true in arrangements you can use without approaching a member of my family. I would insist on that.”

“Then insist.”

He looked at her with something like relief.

“I will.”

The factor’s room overlooked an inner courtyard. Water fell from the mouth of a stone animal into a basin, loud enough to make the conversation at the other end of the room indistinct. The animal had lost one ear and a portion of its nose. Somebody had planted a small flowering thing in the broken place.

Ailsa arrived carrying her own list of questions. Riarhin had offered to write them neatly. Ailsa said she could read her own hand, thank you, and folded the page into her sleeve.

The factor was a woman in her middle years with a soft voice and a habit of moving a ring up and down the first joint of her finger while she listened. She greeted Saikou by name and asked after his mother. He said that they were both dependent on correspondence for the answer. She smiled, accepted the correction and offered chairs.

Ailsa asked about the well first.

“The water is sweet,” the factor said.

“Who draws it?”

“There are household servants.”

“How many households use it?”

The ring stopped.

“I should have to inquire.”

“Please do. Is it sweet all year?”

“I am told so.”

“By somebody living there?”

“By the agent responsible for the property.”

Ailsa wrote a mark beside the first question. Riarhin watched the factor’s face. The woman had expected gratitude, perhaps, or doubt about the money. She had prepared for both. Now she brought a fresh sheet closer and began writing questions of her own.

They discussed the cost of fuel, the nearest place where cloth could be washed without someone claiming a fee, and whether the tutors were employed by the household or by a school. The factor knew the last answer. The older children could attend a school maintained through subscriptions from merchant families. Languages and accounts were taught there, and drawing, and the legal forms used in commerce. Younger children could receive lessons at home until they were ready.

“Who chooses when they’re ready?”

“The tutor, in consultation with you.”

“And their father.”

The factor added something to the page.

“Certainly.”

Riarhin looked at Ailsa. Ailsa was examining the next question.

“Is the house mine?”

“It would be for your use.”

“Then no.”

“For as long as you wished to reside there.”

“And if I wanted somewhere else?”

“You would be free to leave.”

“What about the things I brought?”

“Your possessions remain yours.”

“And things I made?”

“Of course.”

Ailsa nodded. “Put that down.”

The factor’s smile had altered. She was enjoying herself now, though perhaps less than she had intended. She asked whether Ailsa had conducted business in Silkstrand before.

“Only since I came.”

“You are taking to it.”

“I was asking what a house cost before I came here.”

Saikou covered his mouth with his hand.

They left with copies of the offer, a list of answers still to come and an appointment to examine the passage arrangements. Outside, the courtyard opened onto a lane where two boys were trying to persuade a dog to leave the cool shade under a cart. The dog had judged the matter correctly and refused.

Riarhin waited until they were beyond the gate.

“You’re considering it.”

“I’ve been considering it since I read it.”

“It’s a long way.”

“So was here.”

“You know what his mother is.”

Ailsa stopped walking.

Saikou went on a few paces, then stopped too, looking with great attention at a seller of copper pans.

“What is she?” Ailsa asked.

“Powerful.”

“Aye.”

“She wants things.”

“So do I.”

Riarhin felt heat come into her face. She had not expected to be embarrassed in the lane.

“I don’t want you hurt.”

“I know that. But you keep talking as though I’ve a house at home with no one taking an interest in it.” Ailsa moved the folded papers to her other hand. “I could go back. There are people I want to see. There’s work I understand. There’s also the reason I had to leave. You know more about that than I do.”

“Yes.”

“I can stay here and make enough to pay for a room. Perhaps more than enough, once I know the buyers. Or I can see this place and see what the children think of it.”

“The children might like it.”

Ailsa stared at her, then laughed once in disbelief.

“Oh, that would be dreadful.”

Riarhin looked away.

A hand caught hers.

“I’m not going to stop loving you because somebody gives me a garden.”

“That isn’t—”

“Perhaps it isn’t. Come and look at the pans.”

She took Riarhin with her. Saikou had been driven into a purchase and was holding a little copper pot with an expression of patient defeat.

“What’s it for?” Ailsa asked.

“I have no idea.”

“Then we’ll find something.”

Lyra read the offer with a cushion behind her back and her feet on the lower rung of a chair. The rooms Saikou had taken opened onto a garden in which nothing except rosemary appeared to have survived the winter care. A servant had put a bowl of warm water beside Lyra and forgotten the towel. Riarhin found it on a bench outside.

“The passage money must be available there,” Lyra said. “Not merely here. If she has to come back to Silkstrand to collect the price of leaving Dhahara, it’s a less useful generosity.”

“Saikou’s attending to it.”

“Good. And these introductions to buyers should be introductions. That word must survive into the local instructions.”

She turned a sheet. Her fingers were steadier now. She still tired suddenly, without warning, as if somebody had put down a shutter behind her eyes.

“Do you think she should go?” Riarhin asked.

“I think she should see the answers.”

“And then?”

“And then she’ll know more than I do.”

Riarhin sat on the edge of the bed. Lyra looked at her above the paper.

“You dislike that.”

“Yes.”

“Poor creature.”

It was said with sufficient dryness that Riarhin laughed.

Lyra smiled and put the papers aside.

“I’ve found somewhere to go,” she said.

Riarhin stopped laughing.

“Where?”

“Here. In the city. There’s a woman who copies correspondence for three houses and would like to take on a fourth without doing the work herself. I can write. I can also tell her when a sentence says more than she meant to send.”

“You need to rest.”

“I can rest between sentences.”

“Lyra.”

“She knows I’ve been ill. She’s offering a small beginning. I haven’t agreed.”

Riarhin looked at the bowl. Steam no longer rose from it.

“What do you want?”

“A room whose door I can shut without listening to find out who has the key.”

“You have that here.”

“Saikou has it here. I’m his guest.”

“Let me see the place.”

Lyra watched her for a moment.

“You may come with me.”

“All right.”

“And meet the woman.”

“Yes.”

“You’re not to frighten her.”

“I don’t frighten everybody.”

“No. It takes you longer with some.”

She reached for the towel. Riarhin put it in her hand and stayed while she washed.

Chapter 4 — Those Who Arrive

Ailsa’s eldest came down the gangplank carrying a box his father had told him to leave.

Riarhin could tell this from the expression on the man behind him. The boy had chosen an object too large to be managed with dignity and was determined to demonstrate that it could be managed anyway. Halfway down he caught a corner against the rail, turned, and nearly presented the whole thing to the water.

His father put a hand under it.

“I have it.”

“I can carry it.”

“I can see that.”

“Then let go.”

“When you’re on something wider than your foot.”

Ailsa was already moving. She caught her son at the end of the plank, box and all, and held him so abruptly that he made an offended sound into her shoulder. Then he dropped the box. His father caught it with a noise that made a nearby sailor turn and grin.

Riarhin stood back beneath the awning.

The younger child came more slowly, holding the rail and looking towards the land before taking each step. She was taller than Riarhin remembered. The ship’s movement had given her a cautious sway which continued after she reached the quay. Ailsa took her face in both hands, kissed her and held on. The girl began to cry. Her mother drew her close and held her.

Around them the unloading went on. A sack split on the stone, scattering pale grain into the gaps. Two men shouted over whose turn it was to move a cart. Somebody was trying to sell hot food to the people arriving and taking a professional interest in where the queue would form.

The children’s father put down the box at last and straightened carefully. He was a broad man, more weathered across the forehead than Riarhin recalled, with a torn place in one sleeve neatly bound round with thread. He watched Ailsa and the girl, then saw Riarhin.

He nodded.

She returned it.

“The rest is coming,” he said.

“How much?”

“Less than they began with. More than I asked for.”

The eldest heard him and looked round indignantly. His father pointed to the box.

“You can complain when you’ve got that to the house.”

Ailsa released the girl enough to look at her.

“Did you sleep?”

“Sometimes.”

“Did you eat?”

“She ate,” her father said. “The difficulty was persuading her to stop when the sea began.”

The girl wiped her face against her mother’s shoulder and said that everybody had eaten then. Her brother supplied names. Their father accepted the correction, which deprived them of much of its pleasure.

Ailsa looked across at Riarhin.

“Will you take the small bag?”

She had been preparing to offer. It felt strangely good to be asked first.

She took it in her right hand. The girl came away from Ailsa and stood in front of her, examining the face she knew and the arm she had not seen carried so carefully.

“Are you still hurt?”

“A little.”

“Mother said you were.”

“She was right.”

“Can you walk to the house?”

“Yes.”

“We could stop.”

“We could.”

The girl took her free hand, remembered the arm, and let go quickly.

“That side’s best left,” Riarhin said.

“I forgot.”

“So do I.”

They walked together, with the bag between them. Behind, the eldest had found a young porter willing to carry the box for a sum he considered excessive. His father was explaining that discovering the value of another man’s labour could be part of a journey’s instruction.

There were too many people for the back room, so the woman who owned the house declared that they might use the front as well, provided nobody treated this as an arrangement. She herself took a plate into it and stayed through most of the meal.

The children told the journey in pieces. Each had witnessed a different ship. In one, the master was a man of great knowledge who had predicted a change of wind; in the other he was a bully who had confiscated a knife because he disliked useful occupation. Their father supplied the missing detail that the knife had been used to cut something belonging to the ship.

“It was loose.”

“It was supposed to be loose.”

“Then it was badly tied.”

Ailsa put her head in her hands. Riarhin watched the boy see her laughing and begin, despite himself, to smile.

Later, when the children had been sent to choose sleeping places, their father sat at the table with a fresh cup. He asked about the offer.

Ailsa gave him the papers and let him read.

He was slower than she had been. He went back over the description of the school and asked what one word meant. Lyra, who had come for the meal, supplied it without taking the paper away. He nodded and read on.

“What work is there?” he asked at last.

“For you?”

“For anybody. I’d like to see a place before I put them in it.”

“Harbor work,” Ailsa said. “Repairs. Warehouses. There’ll be more than that.”

“Work I can do.”

“You can ask.”

“I intend to.”

Riarhin sat with her hands in her lap. There was a question she wanted to ask, but it was not hers to ask and neither parent looked towards her to invite it.

Ailsa’s former husband folded the papers along their existing creases.

“I’ll come as far as the coast.”

“You needn’t decide tonight.”

“I’ve had the sea to think about it.”

“And?”

“And I’m already a long way from my bench. A little more won’t make the work there finish itself.” He rubbed his thumb over the cut edge of the paper. “I’m not promising to stay.”

“I haven’t asked you to.”

“But I want to know the people teaching them.”

“So do I.”

He looked up then, and some argument Riarhin had not heard ended between them. Ailsa poured him more tea.

Lyra shifted in her chair. Riarhin saw the tiredness come and stood before she remembered that standing quickly still hurt. Ailsa noticed. She noticed everything tonight.

“I’ll walk her back,” Riarhin said.

“You’ll both take the chair,” Ailsa said. “The man’s outside. I asked him to wait.”

Aurine was waiting in the garden when they reached Saikou’s lodging.

She stood beneath the bare vine beside the door, carrying a narrow case and wearing a travelling coat whose hem had dried in stiff folds. She had cut her hair shorter. Her face looked thin and ordinary in the lamplight, and Riarhin felt a rush of anger before she could decide what it was for.

“You might have written.”

“I did.”

“To whom?”

“To the address I was given.”

Saikou came out behind her.

“I gave her the address,” he said.

“When?”

“Before Valora. In case we were separated and survived.”

“You knew she’d come.”

“I knew she might.”

Lyra leaned against the arm of the hired chair. The carriers waited in the lane, politely uninterested in a quarrel that was keeping them from their next fare.

“Inside,” she said.

They went inside.

Aurine put down the case and knelt beside Lyra before asking permission. She stopped with her hand above the blanket.

“May I look at your feet?”

Lyra considered her.

“Yes.”

Riarhin stood by the door while Aurine worked. Aurine asked for water, looked at what had been done during the sea passage and made one adjustment to the wrapping. Riarhin remembered those useful hands in Theona. She stepped closer to the bed, then passed Aurine the water.

When Lyra was in bed, they sat at the table outside her room.

Aurine described her escape in a flat, tired voice. She had used the confusion after the feast, stayed out of the principal roads, and found a carrier who cared more about payment than about passengers’ histories. She named the towns where she had stopped and the people who had helped her, except for one woman whose name she said she had promised to withhold. She had come through Fairport. By then she knew the queen was dead.

“Did you know we were alive?” Riarhin asked.

“Not until yesterday.”

“Where were you staying?”

Aurine gave an address.

Saikou wrote it down. She watched him do it without protest.

“I’m asking for work,” she said.

“You had work.”

“I know.”

“You had more than one employer.”

“I know that too.”

Riarhin pushed her chair back an inch. The movement hurt her side. Aurine’s eyes went to the bandage beneath the shirt, then returned to her face.

“Don’t,” Riarhin said.

“I wasn’t going to.”

There was a pause. From Lyra’s room came the sound of a cup being set down.

“What do you want?” Saikou asked.

“Passage. Pay. Work I can do without being asked afterwards why I knew how to do it.”

“Are you still sending reports?” Riarhin asked.

“I haven’t sent one since before Valora.”

“That isn’t the question.”

Aurine looked at Saikou.

“Ask her,” he said.

“I don’t know what I’ll be asked to send.”

Riarhin almost laughed. The answer was so much worse than the easy lie she had been ready for that it held her in place.

“Then here’s what you can be asked,” she said. “You’ll tell us a message has come before you answer it. You’ll show us the part that concerns the company. If you can’t do that, you leave the company before answering.”

“And if leaving isn’t possible?”

“Then you tell us that.”

“You may not like what I tell you.”

“I haven’t liked much of it yet.”

Saikou put down the pen.

“You are to be paid for the medical work,” he said. “That must be separate from any claim my mother believes she has on you. I can arrange the payment. I cannot settle the claim without knowing it.”

Aurine stopped moving.

“I wasn’t asking you to.”

“No. I’m telling you what I can do.”

She looked at Riarhin again. “Will you permit it?”

Riarhin thought of Lyra’s feet, the careful adjustment, the way Aurine had stopped her hand until she was invited. She also thought of a letter travelling to a woman in Kassamira while she slept in a room whose door she believed secure.

“For the voyage,” she said. “We’ll decide again after it.”

Aurine nodded.

“Where am I to sleep?”

That made Riarhin angrier than the request for work. She could feel it, unreasonable and hot. The woman had survived. So had they. She would need a bed, food, something in which to wash the road from her hair. These were ordinary needs and had arrived in the middle of a judgment Riarhin had not finished making.

Saikou rang for the servant.

When she returned to Ailsa’s house, the children were asleep and their father had gone to the room arranged for him near the quay. Ailsa sat by the hearth with a stocking over one hand, searching for the place where a thread had gone.

“Long talk?”

“Yes.”

“Did you eat?”

“Yes.”

“Truthfully?”

“A piece of bread.”

Ailsa put the stocking down and went to the covered pot.

Riarhin stood inside the door. There was a space beside Ailsa’s chair where she had left her wrapped sword that morning. It was still empty.

“Someone came back,” she said.

“A friend?”

She had no quick answer.

“A woman who worked with us.”

Ailsa brought the bowl to the table and sat opposite while she ate. Riarhin told her what she could. It was a poor account, full of things left out because another person had a right to them. Ailsa listened until she stopped.

“Do you want her with you?”

“I want what she can do.”

“Then you’ll have her.”

“I know.”

“Does she want to be there?”

“She wants passage.”

“That’s a beginning.”

Riarhin looked at the soup. Ailsa waited with her hand beside the bowl. After a while Riarhin covered it with her own.

Chapter 5 — Passage Money

The ship’s master refused the chest before she looked inside it.

“No.”

Saikou had brought two men to carry it. They set it down on the quay and looked at one another with the relief of people whose difficult job might have ended sooner than expected.

“It contains books,” he said.

“I’ve carried books.”

“Then you know they require—”

“Room. So does wool. Wool pays better.”

The master stood on the plank with one hand on the rail. She was a broad-shouldered woman with a close cap under her hat and a pale scar dividing the outer half of one eyebrow. Her name was Zerra. Riarhin had heard three people call her Mistress Zerra and one call her something else when she was beyond hearing. When he returned, she asked to see his cargo before allowing it aboard.

Saikou offered to pay for the space.

“You’ve paid for people. I’ve agreed to people.”

“And their possessions.”

“Within reason.”

“What would you regard as reasonable?”

“One chest I can get past when somebody’s being sick.”

Saikou looked at the chest.

“One smaller chest,” she said.

Riarhin turned away before he saw her smile.

The ship lay dark and high beside the quay, with men working over her as if she were a house whose occupants had all decided to alter something at once. One was checking a line, another laying narrow boards beside an open hatch, and a third sitting cross-legged with a piece of canvas across his lap. The canvas man had a daughter on the quay watching him sew. She called up a question, and he answered without looking down. Riarhin could not hear either clearly over the knock of mallets from the next berth.

Ailsa stood farther along with the children’s father, discussing the space assigned to the household. The eldest had found something to admire on the ship. The younger was examining the distance between the plank and the water.

Berent came up beside Riarhin.

“She’s found it.”

“What?”

“The way to frighten him.”

Saikou was asking whether the larger chest might be stowed among cargo not required during the voyage. Mistress Zerra was explaining that everything became required when one had to get at the thing beneath it.

“He’ll manage,” Riarhin said.

“He may have to leave a book.”

“That would be serious.”

Berent nodded solemnly. “I’ve never seen him do it.”

They watched until Saikou accepted that a decision had been made. Then Berent went to help him choose what could go in a smaller box.

A woman in a faded blue coat came down the quay with a porter behind her. She carried her own narrow case. The porter carried everything else, which included a covered basin and a bundle of clean linen larger than Saikou’s rejected chest.

Mistress Zerra greeted her by name.

“Dema. Tell me you have fewer bottles this time.”

“Better packed.”

“That wasn’t what I asked.”

Dema handed her a letter. The master read it, looked at the porter’s load and called for someone to clear a place below.

Saikou had stopped beside his books.

“My mother?”

“Her factor. Passage out, a room ashore for the last examinations, and passage back. She has also paid for materials.”

Dema looked at Riarhin’s arm, then at Berent’s stance.

“I shall want to see both of you before we sail.”

“Will you?” Riarhin said.

“If you want the work done. I can enjoy a sea voyage without patients.”

Riarhin found that she liked her a little.

Dema asked Zerra when the ship would be quiet at the first sheltered anchorage. The master began to answer, then frowned.

“How quiet?”

“No loading over my head. Nobody opening the door to ask whether I have finished.”

Zerra looked at the paid letter again.

“I’ll speak to the mate.”

The rooms were quiet in the afternoon. Most of the household had gone out to buy things for the passage, which had become a useful excuse for buying things they had wanted before there was a passage. Riarhin found Saikou sitting amid papers on the floor.

“May I come in?”

“You may have to come over.”

She chose a way between the piles and sat in the chair by the table. Her coat hung open. The little wrapped shape was in its inside pocket, against her right hip, where she could reach it without using the injured arm. She had changed the wrapping only to secure an outer fold that had worked loose. The linen itself remained between her and the object.

Saikou looked up from a page.

“I should tell you what I have.”

“Yes.”

“I would prefer you to read it as well, but not all at once. Some of these copies concern a site which may have been given more than one name.”

“Tell me first.”

He gathered three sheets and laid them on the table, moving a cup to make room.

“There was a place in the Himadri mountains built to contain something its makers could not destroy. I believe it was the amulet.”

“Believe.”

“Yes. Two descriptions agree on the object. A third describes the place without describing what was kept there. The date in that copy has been altered. I cannot establish when.”

“Who made the copies?”

“Different people. One for a collector. One for a temple household. The third is an extract from an account of a journey.”

“Your mother’s people?”

“Obtained two of them.”

“And the other?”

“I bought it.”

“With whose money?”

“My own.” He paused. “Earned through business my mother made possible, if you wish to follow it that far.”

She deserved that. She left it alone.

“What became of the place?”

“It was raided. The object was taken out. People continued to speak of the defenses afterwards, though I cannot tell you how many of them saw what they described.”

“Who took it?”

“A ruler whose name does not survive reliably in these accounts.”

“The prince.”

Saikou put one hand on the edge of the table.

“Yes.”

She remembered him interested in a lost kingdom when the little green thing had still been a prize she could carry in her hand. His questions had seemed to be about somewhere else. They had been, and they had not.

“Did you know then?”

“I suspected a connection. I did not know you would give it to Veralyn.”

“You could have asked.”

“Yes.”

He let the answer stand. She had expected him to qualify it, and was left with nothing to push against except what he had admitted.

Outside, a hawker was selling a remedy for toothache. He named three people who had been cured, repeating their names with increasing confidence as he moved along the lane.

Riarhin looked at the nearest page. A drawing occupied one corner. It was a rough rendering of a stair between walls, with marks at intervals along the sides. The marks might have been lamps or openings. Beside it, in a different hand from the main text, someone had written a warning too cramped for her to read from the chair.

“Would putting it back work?”

“I don’t know.”

“Then why go?”

“Because someone there once knew how to keep it. Because that knowledge may not be entirely gone. Because I have spent years finding people who would like to possess it, and none who can show me that possession is safe.”

“Including your mother.”

“Including my mother.”

He sat back on his heels. It was an uncomfortable posture, but he seemed to have forgotten it.

“She helped us,” Riarhin said.

“She did.”

“Ailsa’s free.”

“Yes.”

“If we don’t do this?”

“Ailsa is free.”

“And you?”

“I made an undertaking. It was not hers.”

Riarhin laid her right hand flat on the table. She could feel the grain beneath the fingertips. At the bottom of the stair drawing a small figure had been added to show its size. A person, reduced to a head and two strokes. Whoever drew it had thought a reader ought to know how small they would be there.

“I’ll go,” she said.

Saikou closed his eyes briefly.

“Read these first.”

“You’ve told me enough to decide.”

“I may have told you enough to decide wrongly.”

She looked at him.

He pushed the first page towards her.

“Please.”

They read through the afternoon. Saikou supplied words she could not make out and refused to supply certainty where the copying had failed. One passage said that the return stair must be kept clear. Another could as easily have referred to a procession as a return. He had marked the difference in the margin. Riarhin asked why he had left a word untranslated.

“Because I have found it used for two things that are not the same.”

“What things?”

“A room in which someone waits to be received. And a place where something is held apart.”

She looked again at the drawing.

“That could matter.”

“It could matter considerably.”

By the time Ailsa returned, Riarhin had a headache and Saikou had removed six books from the pile meant for the ship. He had put them back twice, and finally wrapped them for storage in Silkstrand.

Ailsa set down a basket and looked at the floor.

“Has there been a fight?”

“The ship has won,” Saikou said.

“Usually does.”

She showed Riarhin the cap she had bought for the younger child. Riarhin admired it inadequately, tried again, and was forgiven. Then Ailsa saw the pages on the table.

“Is that where you’re going?”

“Eventually.”

“Looks cold.”

“Yes.”

Ailsa touched the edge of the drawing with one finger.

“Will you tell me before you leave the coast?”

“I will.”

“Tell me what you know. I don’t need a promise about the rest.”

Riarhin put her hand over Ailsa’s for a moment, careful not to crease the page.

Lyra’s new room stood above a courtyard where a woman kept two tubs of herbs and a cage with no bird in it. The bird had belonged to her husband, she said. She had liked the cage better and saw no reason to throw away both.

The woman who employed her lived below. She came up carrying a packet tied with ordinary cord and asked Lyra to read one letter before supper. She had brought three in case the first was too easy. Lyra accepted the packet and said she would do one. The woman looked as though she might bargain, reconsidered and went downstairs.

Riarhin stood by the window. It had a sound fastening and opened over a low roof. She had inspected both before Lyra arrived and had been careful to say nothing about it.

“Stop looking pleased with yourself,” Lyra said.

“I’ve done nothing.”

“I’m sure.”

The room had a bed, a table wide enough to work at, and a chest for which Lyra held the key. She put it down, picked it up again and tucked it into the little purse at her waist. Riarhin noticed and looked at the herbs outside instead.

“Will you write?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“Through Saikou’s people?”

“At first. Then we’ll see what other arrangements there are.”

“If you need—”

“I’ll ask.”

She wanted to believe that. Lyra saw the doubt and came to stand beside her.

“I may not ask you.”

Riarhin swallowed.

“All right.”

“Is it?”

“I’m trying.”

Lyra took her hand. There was strength in the fingers again, though not much. Riarhin turned to her and they held one another, carefully at first and then less carefully, until the pain in her side made her breathe in sharply.

Lyra drew back.

“You must stop collecting those.”

“I know.”

“You say that very readily.”

“I have practice.”

Lyra smiled. She touched Riarhin’s cheek, then lowered her hand.

“Go and eat with Ailsa. She’s expecting you.”

Riarhin stayed a moment longer. On the table, beside the packet of correspondence, the grammar lay open. A word had been tried three times on a scrap of paper. The third attempt had no correction through it.

She left Lyra at the table with the lamp moved closer.

Cadan had packed by the time everyone else began.

His bundle lay beside his bed, small and square. Riarhin found him repairing the seam of a sleeve with a needle borrowed from Ailsa. He sewed slowly and well, looking at each finished stitch before making the next.

“You needn’t come,” she said.

He looked up.

“I know.”

“There’ll be work here.”

“Yes.”

“Different work.”

He waited, the needle held between his fingers.

“Why do you want to?”

“You give good instruction.”

She had heard the answer before. In this little room, with the patched sleeve in his lap, she found it harder to decide whether he meant more than he said.

“I don’t always know what I’m doing.”

“No.”

He returned the needle to the cloth. She stood until the next stitch was finished, then went to the door.

“Keep a warm coat where you can reach it,” she said. “The sea won’t be warm because we’re going somewhere that is.”

“I will.”

Downstairs, Berent was disputing the weight of a bag with the boy who had carried the box from the ship. Neither appeared to be winning. Riarhin joined them long enough to establish that the bag contained stones.

“They’re good stones,” the boy said.

“For what?” Berent asked.

The boy considered this an unnecessarily narrow question.

Riarhin took one out. It was smooth, dark and crossed by a single pale band. He had found it somewhere on his first sea journey and wanted to keep it. She put it back.

“One,” she said.

He chose three and waited.

“One.”

Berent began to laugh.

Chapter 6 — The Things He Carries

For the first two days, the younger child hated everybody who had said she would enjoy the sea.

She lay on a folded sail with her eyes shut and accepted water as a personal concession. Her brother, who had been ill for only half a morning, developed views on endurance. Their father sent him to help the cook. Ailsa sat beside the sail, sewing, and occasionally held out the cup without asking whether it was wanted.

Riarhin had thought she could be useful. She had carried a woman out of Valora; she could surely hold a child through a bout of sickness. The girl wanted her mother. When Riarhin offered a hand she turned her face into Ailsa’s skirt, and Riarhin took the empty cup away to fill it.

Above, the ship made noises which became familiar without becoming reassuring. Something heavy moved whenever the hull lifted. A rope rubbed against wood just over the place where she slept. Men crossed the deck at night, their steps sounding through the planks as though they were walking across the ceiling of her room. Sometimes they ran. The first time, she had got up so quickly that she reopened the edge of the wound and spent the remainder of the watch furious with herself while Aurine changed the dressing. Dema examined it at dawn and moved her treatment ahead of Berent’s.

“Something was wrong,” she said.

“Something required doing.”

“They were running.”

“Yes.”

Aurine held the cloth until Riarhin stopped trying to move it away.

“Hold this.”

She held it.

In the morning she asked Mistress Zerra what the running had meant. The master was studying the sky over the stern and appeared to have forgotten Riarhin was a passenger whose question might require courtesy.

“Wind.”

“Was there danger?”

“There’d have been some if they hadn’t got up.”

She moved past Riarhin to speak to the mate.

Riarhin found somewhere out of the way and sat down.

The third morning was bright enough to warm the deck where the wind could not reach it. Somebody had hung bedding along a line, and it moved in pale shapes against the water. The younger child came up, stood a little while holding her father’s hand and demanded food.

The cook, a narrow man with a shining bald place at the back of his head, said it was a pity because they had thrown all hers away. She looked at him. He looked back solemnly. After a moment she saw the bowl behind him and accused him of lying.

“I’ve been told.”

He gave her the bowl. Her brother hovered until the cook asked whether he intended to watch her chew every mouthful.

Riarhin sat on a coil of rope with Ailsa’s work spread across her knees. She had agreed to keep the pieces separate. There were six of them, all the same colour and cut so similarly that she could see no practical reason for separating them. Ailsa could. Each time a corner moved she corrected it without stopping the needle.

“What will they be?”

“Sleeves.”

“Six?”

“People keep having two arms.”

“For whom?”

“Iona has a buyer. She can send the finished things from the first port.”

Riarhin looked across to where Iona and Mairi were arguing over a fold. They had arranged their own passage to the coast after hearing the terms and had been careful to make clear that the decision was theirs. Mairi wanted to see whether she could obtain work through the estate’s contacts. Iona had said she was going to look. That was all she had promised, and she repeated it whenever anyone began assigning her a place in the proposed household.

Donnel’s girl had stayed in Silkstrand. She had found a household from nearer home and a reason to remain that she explained to Ailsa and no one else. Riarhin had given her the address for letters. The girl had thanked her, folded it into a purse, and gone out to meet someone waiting at the corner. Riarhin could still see the way she had quickened her step when she saw him.

The ship rolled. One of the pieces of cloth slid towards the gap between Riarhin’s knees.

Ailsa caught it.

“You were employed for that.”

“I was thinking.”

“Expensive habit.”

Riarhin put the pieces back in order. Ailsa showed her which two she had exchanged. They worked in silence for a while, the cloth warming under their hands.

At the first sheltered anchorage, Dema claimed the little cabin and sent everyone except Aurine out of it.

Riarhin lay on a folded blanket with her shirt open. The hull moved gently beneath her. Above, a crate dragged across the deck and stopped. Dema waited until the footsteps had gone before she uncovered her case.

There were instruments in it, and stoppered jars, and a thin book with several pages marked by scraps of cloth. She washed, examined the wound and asked Riarhin to move the arm through the range she could manage. Then she made her stop pretending the last part was possible.

“That pulls underneath,” she said.

“Yes.”

“I shall work there first. The skin can wait.”

Aurine sat beside the berth. Dema showed her where to support the elbow and asked whether Riarhin had been feverish. The answer led to more questions about the wound, its cleaning and the journey since it was made. Riarhin listened to the two women discuss her as a body which might be helped. Neither asked who had held the sword.

Dema laid two fingers against the unbroken skin above the cut. The ache eased. Riarhin let out a breath she had not meant to hold.

“If it’s that easy—”

“That has eased your exhaustion. You still have a torn muscle. Keep the arm there.”

She moved the book closer and began the longer work.

Riarhin could follow the first few words. They named the parts Dema had touched, then joined them in a relation she could feel more readily than understand. Warmth spread deep beneath the wound. It became a pull, insistent enough that she tried to move away from it. Aurine steadied her elbow.

“Tell her,” Aurine said.

“It’s pulling.”

Dema changed the position of her fingers. She did not stop speaking. The pull settled into something slower, an ache with a direction, and Riarhin stared at a knot in the timber while the ship shifted around them.

A sailor laughed outside. The mate silenced him before he reached the door.

By the time Dema finished, Riarhin’s hair was wet at the temples. The healer sat back, drank from the cup Aurine offered and waited before testing the arm. Riarhin lifted it. She reached the place where movement usually caught, passed it and stopped because she expected pain.

“Farther,” Dema said.

She went farther.

The scar remained. A narrow part of it had turned pale, almost silver, against the surrounding skin. Dema examined the colour in the window’s light, made a note and covered the arm lightly.

“The side needs its own work. Tomorrow, if this stays clean. After that I want sleep and food doing some of the labour.”

Riarhin sat up. Hunger struck so suddenly that she felt faint.

Dema saw her face.

“Aurine, will you ask the cook for something substantial? She may be hungry again before she finishes it.”

“Does that happen often?” Riarhin asked.

“Often enough that I warned him.”

Ailsa met her outside with the food. Riarhin ate standing until Ailsa took her by the sleeve and led her to a place where she could sit. When she lifted the bowl, she used her left hand without thinking.

Ailsa touched Riarhin’s wrist, then sat close enough for their shoulders to meet.

Saikou had chosen a place beneath the lee of the deckhouse. He sat with a cloth spread in front of him, his smaller chest open beside one knee. On the cloth lay the silver mirror, a little stoppered bottle, two folded packets and an object Riarhin had never seen before.

It was iron, blackened with age, about the length of her thumb. At first she took it for part of a lock. Then he turned it and she saw the shape of a closed door worked into one face, shallow lines almost worn away beneath the polish of use. A narrow loop at the top held a cord. The cord was new.

She stopped far enough away not to cast a shadow over the work.

“May I sit?”

“Please.”

He drew a corner of the cloth away from the place she chose. The mirror lay face down. He had cleaned its rim, though a dull patch remained near the handle, where his thumb habitually rested.

“Another mirror?” she asked, indicating the iron.

“No. A gate.”

“I can see that.”

“The Sealed Gate, if we are to be formal.”

He picked it up by the cord. It turned once in the light, then steadied.

“How long have you had it?”

“Long enough to have replaced this twice.” He touched the cord. “The second time because I was careless. The first time for a reason I prefer.”

“Which?”

“It caught on a nail while I was trying to shut a door.”

“That sounds careless too.”

“There was something coming through.”

She looked at him.

He smiled slightly. “I got the door shut.”

A sailor passed carrying a bucket and slowed to look. Saikou waited until he had gone before laying the iron down.

“Does everyone need to stay away?” Riarhin asked.

“For cleaning, no. For the working, I prefer them to know what they are crossing.”

He took a strip of cloth, dampened one corner and began rubbing the shallow lines. He worked with the patient attention he gave damaged pages. Riarhin watched his hands. There was a scar across the base of one finger she had noticed before but never asked about. The cleaning revealed a narrow line within the door’s frame, so fine that from farther away it would have looked like a crack.

“What does it keep out?”

“What the particular rite is prepared to exclude, if the working holds.”

“People?”

“Sometimes.”

“Everything?”

He looked up. “No.”

“Would it keep the amulet safe?”

“I would not use that word.”

He returned to the cleaning, slower now.

“I can mark a boundary. I can make crossing it difficult for something the working recognises. That is useful. It does not tell me what I have enclosed, what it can do while enclosed, or whether keeping it there is wise.”

Riarhin looked at the little iron door. It seemed a poor object to ask such things of, worn almost smooth and lying on a cloth that had been mended at two corners.

“And if there isn’t time?”

“There are ways to spend more than time.”

She waited.

He turned the Symbol so she could see a thin piece set across the lower edge, partly hidden by the frame. It had been sealed there with something dark.

“I can break this. Then the call is immediate. The consequences are not.”

“Can you mend it?”

“I have done.”

“How long does it take?”

“That has depended on what I did before breaking it, what I did with it, and how ready I was to admit those things afterwards.”

He saw her expression and put the Symbol down.

“I cannot give you a number of days. I can tell you that carrying it broken would not give me a second use because I wanted one.”

She nodded. That, at least, was clear.

Saikou brought out a length of cord marked with three little knots and laid it in a half-circle on the cloth. He set the Symbol beyond it, facing him. Then he rested both hands on his knees and sat quietly enough that Riarhin could hear the bedding striking the line overhead.

She thought he was waiting for something. After a while she understood that he was doing something and that she had mistaken stillness for its absence.

The iron made a small sound.

It could have been the cord tightening against the loop. Saikou leaned forward, examined it without touching, and breathed out. Then he gathered the marked cord, covered the Symbol and began wrapping it separately from the mirror.

“Is it sound?”

“As far as that tells me.”

“Does it tell you much?”

“Enough to be glad I looked.”

By afternoon Berent had persuaded a sailor to show him the knot used to secure one of the movable rails. He made it correctly on the second attempt and explained that it was a poor knot for a horse line. The sailor, whose name was Hesk, replied that their horses had adapted well to being made of wood.

“Too much slipping,” Berent said, pulling the line.

“It’s meant to come away.”

“Not when something pulls.”

“Depends what’s pulling.”

Riarhin sat nearby with her back to the deckhouse. She had meant to close her eyes for a few minutes. Instead she watched the two men each demonstrate the other’s ignorance with considerable enjoyment.

Hesk was older than Berent by perhaps ten years, heavy through the middle, with pale hair escaping a leather tie. He could squat on his heels without moving his knees outward. Berent watched him do it and shifted his own bad leg into a more comfortable position.

“Again,” Hesk said.

“I have it.”

“Then do it again.”

Berent did. Hesk jerked the wrong end and left him holding loose rope.

“That would have been your horse.”

“My horse would have the sense to stand still.”

“Then we should have hired it.”

Cadan came up carrying a bowl. He stood for a moment, observing, then asked Hesk where the dirty water should go. Hesk pointed, watched him begin to move, and called him back to specify which side when the wind stood there. Cadan thanked him.

Berent looked after him.

“He’ll remember.”

“Good. We’ve enough remembering for people who won’t.” Hesk handed him the rope again. “Your turn.”

Riarhin shut her eyes at last. The sun warmed the sound side of her face. She heard the line move through Berent’s hands, Hesk’s objection, Berent’s reply. Ailsa laughed somewhere beyond them. The ship lifted, and she let it.

Chapter 7 — A Second Wake

The first man to see it was too embarrassed to say what he had seen.

He stayed at the stern after the others had gone back to work. When the mate called, he came promptly and did the job badly. The mate sent him back to do it again. This time he took Hesk with him.

Riarhin was awake because the dressing at her side had begun to itch. She had come up with her coat over her shoulders, meaning to stand in the air until she could bear lying down again. The night was clear. The ship’s wake showed pale behind her, widening into water whose movement she could hear better than see.

Hesk leaned over the rail. The younger sailor pointed. He kept his hand low, as if he did not wish the thing to notice.

Riarhin moved towards them.

“What is it?”

Hesk glanced at her. “Water.”

“I thought so.”

“Then you’re ahead of him.”

The younger sailor swallowed whatever he had been about to say. Riarhin stopped at the rail and looked down.

At first she saw only the wake. Then, farther out on the left, another line of disturbance gathered itself from the dark. It held its distance while the ship rose and fell. There was no hull above it. She could have mistaken it for the passage of a large animal if it had not kept such an even breadth.

Hesk’s hand tightened on the rail.

The mate came up behind them. He looked once, said something to Hesk which Riarhin did not catch, and sent the younger sailor to fetch the master.

“Below,” he told Riarhin.

She considered objecting. The deck was narrow there, and the men had already begun to move around her as if she were a barrel left in the wrong place. She stepped back.

“Saikou,” she said. “The man with the books. You may want him.”

“Fetch him, then.”

Saikou came to the rail, watched the second wake through two long lifts of the stern, and turned to the younger sailor.

“If it calls again, fetch someone. Don’t answer. Don’t ask what it wants.”

The sailor looked at Hesk.

“Have you answered already?”

“No.”

“Good. Stay with him.”

Mistress Zerra knew of three things which might follow a ship in that manner. She told Saikou two and declined to name the third at sea. He listened, keeping one hand on the rail.

“I would look aboard first,” he said. “Watch it when we lift. It’s keeping the ship’s motion, even out there. Has anything been brought out of the water since your last stop?”

“There may have been.”

“Then leave it where it is until we have light. Nothing overboard.” He looked back at the sailor. “When did you first notice it?”

The younger sailor said he had noticed it at the end of his watch.

“What made you look?” Saikou asked.

“I heard somebody.”

“On the deck?”

“In the water.”

“What did they say?”

The sailor looked at the master.

“Answer,” she said.

“My name.”

“Which name?” Saikou asked.

“The one I’ve got.”

“The name these people use?”

After a moment the sailor shook his head.

Saikou waited. Riarhin watched the pale line beyond the stern. It had not come closer. She began measuring how long a boat would need to cross the distance, then looked back along the wake. The ship had been making better speed than a boat could.

“My mother’s name for me,” the sailor said at last.

Hesk looked down at his hands.

“Has anyone aboard heard it?”

“Him.” The sailor nodded at Hesk. “I told him when I first came.”

“Anybody else?”

“I don’t know.”

“Was it calling you towards it?” Saikou asked. “You needn’t say the name.”

The sailor nodded.

“Or asking you to come back?”

After a moment the sailor said, “Back.”

Saikou turned to Mistress Zerra.

“Something brought aboard at the last landing, then. A piece taken from a fixed place. Wood, perhaps.”

“Half the things aboard have been in water.”

“Ask who brought something home.”

She looked at Hesk.

He took his hand off the rail.

“There was a fitting,” he said.

They waited for daylight to examine it properly. During the remaining dark hours the master changed the watches and ordered that nobody go aft alone. The younger sailor was given work near the mast, where other men could see him. He objected until Hesk told him to be grateful for a job where he could complain within hearing.

The fitting lay on a piece of sacking beside the hatch. It was curved wood, no longer than a man’s forearm, carved on one face and scarred on the other where iron had been pulled from it. Salt had dried in the deeper cuts. One end showed a fresh break.

Riarhin had expected something beautiful, or at least valuable enough to account for the trouble. The carving was so worn she could make out only a suggestion of eyes beneath a curved line. Hesk crouched beside it and ran one thumb over the smooth back.

“Good wood,” he said.

Mistress Zerra stood over him.
