“It was caught under a landing. I thought it might serve for a handle.”

“Did you ask?”

“Who?”

“The people whose landing it was.”

“They’d have charged me for it.”

The master closed her eyes briefly, then opened them and looked at Saikou.

“What do you need?”

“Where he found it. Who was with him. Whether anyone has cut or burned another part.”

“I cut this,” Hesk said, touching the break. “The end was split. It went in the cook’s fire.”

The cook, who had been listening from beyond the hatch, came closer.

“You told me that was rubbish.”

“It was a split bit of wood.”

“And this is a whole bit of wood,” the cook said. “Look how well we’re getting on with that.”

Saikou raised a hand. They stopped.

“Was any ash kept?”

“For scouring.”

“Let me see it, please.”

The cook went away muttering. Hesk remained where he was, his hand resting on the object. Riarhin watched the hand. He had brought it aboard openly. Other people had seen it and thought no more of it than he had. She had probably stepped past it while inspecting a doorway.

“Take your hand away,” Saikou said gently.

Hesk did.

The working began beside the deckhouse, where Saikou had cleaned his Symbols. Mistress Zerra cleared the space and put Berent at one approach and a sailor at the other. Berent asked what he should do if someone insisted on crossing.

“Tell me,” the master said.

“And if there isn’t time?”

“Put your stick out. They’ve been stepping over it for three days. Let them discover a reason.”

Saikou laid the fitting on the sacking. Beside it he put a little of the ash, dampened so the wind could not carry it. The silver mirror rested in his left hand, its face uncovered. He asked Hesk to describe the landing again.

“Low steps. A post missing. There was a boat turned over on the beach.”

“What colour?”

“Boat colour.”

Saikou looked at him.

“Blue under the old paint. Or green. I didn’t attend to it.”

“What did you attend to?”

“The wood.”

“Then tell me about the wood.”

Hesk described how it had been caught, how far he had put his arm under the step, the resistance he had felt before it came free. As he spoke, Saikou passed the mirror over the fresh break without letting it touch. Its surface held the pale sky and a moving edge of sail.

Riarhin saw a dark line enter the reflection.

She looked up. Nothing crossed the sky.

The line thickened. For a moment the glass seemed to show the underside of steps, wet timber and a place below them where daylight ended. Then Saikou’s hand shook, and the image broke into the ordinary reflection of his coat.

He lowered the mirror to his knee. Sweat stood at his temples, though the morning was cool.

“Was there a cord?” he asked.

Hesk blinked.

“Round it. Or near it.”

“A bit of something.”

“Did you cut it?”

“It was rotten.”

“Did you cut it?”

“Yes.”

Saikou covered the mirror.

Mistress Zerra stood with her arms folded. “What have we taken?”

“A thing attached to a place,” he said. “Intentionally, I think. I can establish the connection. I cannot tell you from that glimpse what the people who put it there intended.”

“Can it be put back?”

“The piece can. The burned part cannot.”

The master looked towards the wake. It lay beyond the stern in the full daylight, a smooth trough in the water with ripples breaking along both edges.

“Can we put it over?”

“I would rather not find out whether that is the same as returning it.”

She swore, briefly and with conviction.

“How long to keep it quiet?”

“I may be able to separate the fitting from the occupied part of the deck. That could give us time.”

“Could.”

“Yes.”

She looked at the object and then at Hesk.

“Tell the mate where you found the landing. Properly this time.”

The Gate Symbol hung openly from Saikou’s neck while he prepared the boundary.

He chose the little compartment beneath the stern steps, empty except for a brush and an old bucket. The fitting went inside on its sacking, with the ash wrapped beside it. Saikou used cord along the threshold, fixed where feet would not casually disturb it, and a dark powder pressed into the join of the boards. He showed Mistress Zerra each part before beginning.

“This holds the passage,” he said. “It does not make the thing harmless. Nobody is to fetch a bucket through it because they have always kept a bucket there.”

She turned and looked directly at the cook.

“I’ve heard,” the cook said.

Riarhin stood a few paces away, where she could see the approach. Her arm ached from the morning’s cold. She had wanted to be given something to do and had received a place to stand. Beyond her, Ailsa had taken the children forward. The girl was asking a question and her father was answering without looking back. The ordinary sound of it steadied Riarhin.

Saikou knelt.

He began quietly. She could distinguish the words at first, though they belonged to a language she had heard spoken only by other people who preferred listeners not to interrupt. Then the sound altered. It seemed closer than he was, as though someone had leaned beside her and was speaking into a space no wider than a doorframe.

The ship rose. A coil slid on the deck. Berent caught it with the end of his stick before it reached the cord.

Saikou’s voice stopped. He lifted one hand towards Berent, palm out, until the loose coil was drawn clear. Then he resumed at the phrase Riarhin had heard before the ship lifted. He had kept his place.

The Symbol lay against the open part of his shirt, dark iron on skin. Beneath the stern steps something tapped once against a board. The younger sailor made a sound and put his hand over his mouth.

The tapping came again, twice.

Saikou laid his right palm flat outside the threshold. Riarhin saw his fingers press down. A thin line appeared along the cord, so pale it could have been light caught in a wet thread, except that the sun lay on the other side of the ship.

The tapping stopped.

For several breaths nothing moved except the ship. Then Saikou lifted his hand, sat back and said, in his own voice, “Keep clear of that.”

Mistress Zerra went to the stern.

The second wake had fallen farther behind. It remained visible, narrowing as the ship moved on. Riarhin watched until she understood that it was not following their change of course at once. The master had already begun giving orders.

Saikou stood with difficulty. Riarhin put a hand under his elbow, and he accepted it before noticing who it was.

“Is that enough?” she asked.

“For the captain to work with.”

They reached the landing late in the day. The return had cost wind, time and a place in the order of ships expected at the next harbor. Mistress Zerra named these losses to Hesk as though she were arranging objects in front of him which he would be required to carry.

He listened without answering.

The people at the landing came down to meet the boat. They saw the fitting before they heard the explanation. An old woman sat on the lowest dry step while a younger man did most of the talking. Whenever he paused she added something short, and he began again with less confidence.

Riarhin stayed in the boat. Saikou had asked her to watch the men aboard while he and Hesk went up. She could understand parts of the exchange, enough to recognise that the younger man’s first account had omitted an important detail and that the woman intended to put it back.

A name was spoken. Then another.

The fitting had belonged to a boat. That much she learned when Saikou translated for Hesk. The boat had failed to return. The wood had washed ashore later and been tied beneath the landing by a woman who came there to speak to someone whose body had not come home. Whether the voice in the water was his, or something that had learned where to find a voice wanted badly enough, the old woman declined to say.

Hesk looked at the fresh break in the wood.

He began explaining the usefulness of the piece and stopped halfway through.

“What do you want done?” he asked.

Saikou translated.

The old woman answered at length. The younger man objected to one part and was dismissed with a gesture. Saikou listened, asked a question and turned back to Hesk.

“You are to put it where she tells you. You are to tell her exactly what you burned. Then you will help repair the step you broke getting it out.”

“It was broken before.”

Saikou looked at him.

Hesk shut his mouth.

They were there until the light began to go. The boat rose and fell against the steps while Riarhin held the line. Hesk worked with a borrowed tool, badly at first, then more competently after the younger man lost patience and showed him the angle. The old woman stayed to watch. She sent a child for food and ate while the men worked.

When the repaired step held their weight, she tested it herself. Then she sat again, facing the water. Saikou stood at a little distance with the mirror covered in his hand. He did not uncover it.

On the way back to the ship, Hesk looked behind them several times.

The water showed the passage of their own boat and nothing beside it.

Chapter 8 — The Long Watch

Hesk spent the next morning sharpening a knife for the cook.

He had not been asked. The cook watched for a while, then brought him two more and a thing used for scraping which Riarhin could not identify. Hesk accepted them without comment. By the time he finished, the story had gone round the ship three times, losing the old woman’s food and gaining a voice which threatened to eat everybody aboard.

“That part wasn’t said,” Riarhin told the youngest sailor.

He looked disappointed.

“What was?”

“She wanted the step mended.”

“That was all?”

“It took some time.”

He considered this and went away. Later she heard him explain to someone else that the step had been impossible to mend until Saikou spoke over it. Saikou, within hearing, shut his eyes.

“Will you correct him?” she asked.

“In a moment.”

“You’ve heard it?”

“I have heard a remarkable account of my own morning.”

He sat beneath the deckhouse with the Gate Symbol on a cloth. The line across its lower edge remained intact. He cleaned powder from his fingernails with a sliver of wood and placed each little scraping in a fold of the cloth instead of letting it fall through the deck.

“Does that matter?” Riarhin asked.

“Leaving a piece of a boundary where somebody else may find it?”

“Yes.”

“I prefer not to make them discover whether it does.”

She sat beside him. The ship had found a better wind. The bedding on the line stretched and fell, stretched and fell, showing patches made in cloth from different households. Ailsa’s eldest was helping his father repair a wooden fastening. He was being allowed to use a knife under conditions which he considered humiliating and Riarhin considered wise.

“Were you frightened?” she asked.

Saikou kept cleaning the Symbol.

“At which point?”

“When it answered.”

“Yes.”

“You didn’t look it.”

“I have had practice looking at something else.”

She watched his hands. There was a tremor when he turned the little iron piece, less than yesterday but present.

“What do you do afterwards?”

“This. Eat. Sleep, if I can.”

“And what you owe?”

He laid the wood down.

“I attend to what I did. In this case, I held a boundary for people who needed time to return something. I removed the working when it was finished. I did not leave an exclusion in a ship’s passage and invite the next occupant to discover it. There are services I can perform. There are things I should refrain from claiming about what happened.”

“Such as mending the step.”

“Yes. That would be a good beginning.”

The youngest sailor crossed the deck carrying a basket. Saikou called him over.

Riarhin got up before the correction began. She had a feeling it would be kind, exact and difficult to escape.

Days acquired places on the ship.

The warm place beside the deckhouse belonged to sewing after the morning work. The strip of shade beneath the hanging canvas belonged to sleepers until the mate needed the space. The stern belonged to the master unless someone had been invited there, and the place Riarhin had chosen to sit on the first day belonged to a coil which was apparently more important than she was.

She learned these things by being moved.

Dema treated the wound in her side during another quiet stop. This time Riarhin knew to eat first. The work took longer; afterwards she slept through the unloading and woke to find her bandages folded on the chest. There was a scar beneath them, closed along its whole length.

The strength came back with the following days. Dema had her lift, reach and turn while the ship lay still, then sent her to walk the deck. When Riarhin asked whether she could practise with the sword, the healer watched her complete the movements without it before agreeing.

“Slowly first.”

Riarhin obeyed for longer than she expected to.

Berent’s knee took several treatments. He swore during the first examination, apologised, and was told to save his breath. Dema worked on the damaged tissue with his leg supported, leaving time between the workings to see what it would bear. Aurine learned the exercises with him. Hesk supplied a rail at the proper height and complained when Berent began using it better than he did.

Near the end of the crossing, Riarhin found Berent crouched beside the cook’s locker. He stood with a sack in both hands. She waited for the familiar hitch in the movement. It did not come.

“What?” he asked.

“Nothing.”

“You were staring.”

“I’m entitled to admire a man carrying onions.”

He gave her the sack.

Dema made them both rest after exertion, inspected the scars and adjusted the work as they recovered. Aurine continued the ordinary care between her visits. When she asked to examine Riarhin’s arm one afternoon, Riarhin held it out.

“You can look.”

“I’m asking whether I can touch.”

Riarhin remembered how the arm had felt under Dema’s hands, held through a movement she could not have stopped.

“Yes.”

Afterwards they sat together in the shade. Aurine had bought a packet of leaves at the last port and was separating the damaged ones from the rest. Riarhin asked what they were for.

“To see whether they are what the woman told me.”

“Don’t you know?”

“I know what the dried leaf is called where I learned it. I know this woman’s name for it. I don’t yet know whether they do the same thing.”

“How will you find out?”

“Carefully.”

She put one aside, looked at the next and handed it to Riarhin.

“Smell that.”

It was sharp, bitter at the back of the nose. Riarhin remembered a room in Taharka and the sound of water being poured from a height.

“I’ve had it.”

“For what?”

“Pain.”

“Did it help?”

“Yes.”

“Did it make you sick?”

“A little.”

Aurine nodded and retrieved the leaf. “That may be useful.”

She did not ask whose room it had been. Riarhin stayed until the packet was sorted.

They put in at a harbor whose white walls held the afternoon heat long after the streets beneath them had cooled. Mistress Zerra allowed passengers ashore on the understanding that she was not employing anyone to find them afterwards. Berent repeated the instruction to Ailsa’s eldest, who replied that he was standing within arm’s reach of his father and could hardly be considered missing.

“Give it time,” his father said.

Riarhin walked with Ailsa through a street of stalls shaded by woven awnings. They had nothing particular to buy. This was Ailsa’s condition for bringing her: they would look at things, sit when they wanted, and return with whatever had pleased them, if anything had. Riarhin had agreed and then spent the first quarter-hour calculating what the journey might need.

Ailsa took her by the sleeve and turned her towards a stall of coloured glass.

“That one,” she said.

“Which?”

“The ugly yellow one.”

It was a bird, or had begun as a bird. The glassmaker had drawn the neck too far and given it feet that appeared capable of holding down a roof in a gale. Riarhin picked it up.

“It stands.”

“It would.”

“Why do you want it?”

“Look at it.”

She did. The bird had one blue eye larger than the other. Its head inclined with the suspicion of a person who had overheard part of a conversation and intended to hear the rest. Riarhin began to laugh.

Ailsa paid for it before she could stop.

They took the bird to a place where food was sold in bowls that had to be returned. Riarhin put it between them on the table. Every time Ailsa looked down at it she smiled. Sweat trickled down her temple. She pushed her hair away, tasted the drink the woman brought and asked what was in it.

The woman told her. Ailsa asked how much. They discussed the quantity as though this might be something she would make at home.

Riarhin watched her over the rim of her own cup.

“What?” Ailsa asked.

“Nothing.”

“That’s an attentive nothing.”

“I like being here.”

Ailsa’s face softened.

“So do I.”

They stayed until the woman wanted the table. On the way back Riarhin carried the bird wrapped in a scrap of paper, supporting it carefully beneath its ridiculous feet.

Beyond that port, the water changed colour in ways she had no names for. It could be brown beside the ship and blue farther away, or green through the top of a wave lifted by the sun. Once something larger than the boat they had used at the landing rose beside them, breathed and sank. The children ran to the rail. So did several men who had been to sea for years.

Hesk called it by one name. Mistress Zerra called it by another and told them all to get their weight off that side. The creature surfaced again at a distance. Riarhin could see the long wet back and a fin cut by an old scar.

Ailsa stood beside her with one hand on the rail.

“Did you see?”

“Yes.”

“How big?”

“I don’t know.”

They waited, hoping it would return. It did not. The children were still talking about it at supper.

That evening the wind slackened. Work grew quieter without ceasing. Saikou came to sit with Riarhin where she was watching the last light on the water. He carried a cup and the little book with Amari’s writing in it.

He set the book down between them.

“I’ve found his reckoning for the lodging in Theona,” he said.

“Was it wrong?”

“No. He charged Tavin twice for the blanket.”

Riarhin smiled.

“He sold it.”

“He sold the first one. Then he borrowed the second, claiming he had mislaid his own.”

“Amari knew.”

“There is a note.”

He opened the book and showed her. Beside a sum, in a broader hand than his own, were the words He can be cold or honest.

Riarhin laughed. Saikou’s mouth moved, then he laughed too, suddenly, a sound she had heard too rarely from him. It went on long enough that he had to put the cup down.

“I never asked which he chose,” he said.

“Neither.”

“No. I suppose not.”

He left the book open. The wind turned a corner of the page and he put his hand over it.

For a while they watched the water. Somewhere forward, Berent was teaching the eldest a game whose rules appeared to alter whenever the boy began winning. His sister had noticed and was protesting on his behalf. Cadan was listening to the argument with serious interest.

“Amari knew you a long time,” Riarhin said.

“Yes.”

“Before the beach.”

“Yes.”

Saikou looked down at the page.

“There was a house once,” he said. “A school, really. People sent children to it because a woman there could teach them to write three languages. Something came into the house. The people who asked me to attend were careful to say it had come from outside.”

“Had it?”

“I could not establish that.”

He moved his hand so the page lay flat.

“There were seventeen children. I remember their sleeping places. A long room, with bedding along two walls and a stove which smoked. The youngest had been put nearest the door because she called for someone in the night. It would have been kinder to move her farther from it, but the people looking after them wanted not to wake the rest.”

Riarhin waited.

“I can remember sixteen faces.”

“Were they all there?”

“Yes.”

“Could one have been somewhere else when you looked?”

“I spoke to them individually.”

The water made a soft, steady sound against the hull.

“What happened?”

“The working succeeded. The children slept. In the morning a boy who had not spoken for several days asked for breakfast, and another complained that he was getting more than his share.”

He smiled briefly at the memory. Then it went.

“That particular thing does not take the memory of its expulsion. There are reasons it is difficult to confront; that is not one of them.”

“Then something else happened.”

“Perhaps.”

“Have you gone back?”

“Twice.”

He shut the book, keeping a finger between the pages.

Riarhin knew better than to ask whether it hurt. She had seen him put his hand towards an empty place beside a table often enough during the last weeks. She sat with her shoulders against the timber and let the question she wanted to ask remain unspoken.

After a while he said, “There was also a Trow. I have written about him.”

“What did you offer him?”

“A memory. I thought it a very clever exchange. I set three mirrors near a mummer’s encampment and waited for moonlight. I remember being absurdly pleased to have arranged the weather.”

“You arranged it?”

“I waited for it. At the time I considered the distinction unimportant.”

“What did you want?”

“That is the difficulty.” He took his cup, found it empty and set it down again. “I know what I offered. I know that I believed the exchange favourable. I cannot remember what made it so.”

Riarhin looked at his profile against the light. She could remember voices from rooms whose walls were long gone, a horse shaking flies from its mane beside a particular stone, her mother lifting a cup in one hand while fastening her hair with the other. She tried to imagine an empty place of the sort he described and found herself supplying an image for it, which defeated the attempt.

“I don’t know how to help you,” she said.

“I wasn’t asking you to.”

“No.”

He looked at her, and the tiredness in his face eased a little.

“Thank you for not proposing a cure.”

She nodded.

The cook came aft with the pot and asked whether they intended to eat. Saikou rose to help him, then sat down when the cook told him that carrying a cup did not constitute a shortage of hands. Riarhin moved the book away from the place where the bowls would go.

Ailsa sewed a little pocket into the inside of her travelling bag for the glass bird.

Riarhin discovered it while looking for a clean cloth. The bird sat upright within its padded nest, glaring at the folded linen. She took it out and set it on the bed while she searched.

Ailsa came in behind her.

“He’s not to be left there. Somebody will sit on him.”

“It would take courage.”

“Put him back.”

Riarhin found the cloth and did as she was told. The cabin moved around them. They had learned where to brace a foot and which hook would hold a coat. Ailsa’s daughter slept on the other side of the partition, breathing through her mouth. Her brother and father had taken places nearer the hatch, where the air moved more freely.

“Saikou talked,” Riarhin said quietly.

“About Amari?”

“A little. Other things.”

Ailsa nodded. She sat on the edge of the bed and began unfastening her shoes.

“He seemed easier when he came down.”

“I didn’t do anything.”

“You sat.”

Riarhin leaned against the partition. It was warm from the bodies on the other side. Her arm was tired, and she had been looking forward to lying down for the last hour without admitting it to herself.

Ailsa finished with the shoes and reached for her hand.

“Come on. There’s a place for you.”

Riarhin lay down carefully. Ailsa put an arm around her sound side and drew the blanket over them both. For a while they listened to the ship.

In the morning, Mistress Zerra said, they would be near enough to see the coast if the air stayed clear. There would be a pilot, and boats coming out, and people wanting things before the passengers had finished wondering at the view. Riarhin thought of the little iron door on Saikou’s cloth, then of the blue door in Silkstrand, and let both go.

Ailsa’s foot found the warmth behind her knee.

She slept.

Part II — Three Shores

Chapter 9 — The Warm Port

The pilot came aboard wearing a clean white coat and carrying his shoes.

He put them down before he greeted Mistress Zerra, wiped the soles of his feet on a piece of sacking and asked for water. She gave him the cup she had been drinking from. He rinsed his mouth, spat over the side and returned it. Then they began discussing the harbor as if both had been interrupted in the middle of an argument.

Riarhin stood beside the deckhouse with Ailsa. Beyond the ship, boats crossed one another’s courses in water bright enough to hurt the eyes. The shore rose behind them in bands of roofs and trees. A line of dark poles carried ropes above a part of the quay, and something moved along them: a load swinging against the sky, drawn from a high platform towards a warehouse whose doors stood open. Farther out, a tower showed green glass beneath its roof. Another, broader and closer to the harbor mouth, held a blue light visible even in the morning.

“There,” Ailsa said.

She was pointing at a little boat so full of fruit that the man in it appeared to sit in the middle of a garden. He called up to the ship. The cook answered, and a negotiation began which continued while the pilot gave directions over both voices.

Ailsa’s daughter came to the rail. She had dressed carefully for arrival and was regretting the weight of her gown. Her father loosened the fastening at the throat. She allowed it, too interested in the shore to object.

“What’s the green one?” she asked.

“Lighthouse,” he said.

“The blue?”

“I expect that is too.”

Saikou stood a little apart with his hat shading his eyes. Riarhin saw him look from one tower to the other and remember something. She waited for him to offer it. He took his hat off, wiped his forehead and put it back on.

The pilot called. Mistress Zerra repeated the call to a man forward. A rope moved through a fitting with a long protesting sound, and the ship began to turn.

By the time they reached the quay, everyone aboard had found a reason to be in someone else’s way.

Nalia was waiting under a strip of shade between two warehouses.

Riarhin saw her before Saikou did: a young woman holding a folded packet, listening to an older man whose hands moved as if he were showing her the size of a loss. She wore a light coat over loose trousers and had fastened her hair close to her head. One ear showed beneath it, its edge subtly unlike the ear of the man she was listening to. The difference caught Riarhin’s attention before the face did.

The woman answered the man, pointed towards a doorway and waited until he went through it. Then she turned to Saikou.

“Ira?”

“Yes.”

“Which of these is yours?”

She indicated the passengers and baggage gathering on the quay. Saikou began to name people.

“I meant the chests.”

“The smaller one. And that case. The people are travelling with me.”

“I hoped so. Otherwise we’ve sent far too few carts.”

She offered her hand.

“Nalia. Your mother’s instructions reached us before the ship.”

“By how much?”

“Enough for the house. Not enough for the answers your guests wanted. Two are still missing.”

Ailsa came forward.

“The well?”

“That one I have. The water’s used by the household and three houses beside the lower road. You’re not acquiring the right to shut them out.”

“Good.”

Nalia looked at her properly for the first time.

“It’s sound through the dry part of the year. People differ on whether they like the taste after heavy rain. I went to drink it. It tastes of water.”

Ailsa smiled. “And the washing?”

“There’s a place. I’ll show you. First we must get your possessions released.”

“They’ve only just come off the ship.”

“Yes. That’s when it begins.”

She took the packet under one arm and led them towards a shaded table where a clerk was making lines on a sheet with the edge of a ruler. He kept his forefinger pressed at the ruler’s centre as he drew. Nalia waited until the line was finished before laying down her papers.

The clerk read the first page, looked at the pile of baggage and asked a question.

Nalia answered. He asked another. She replied more briefly.

Riarhin understood enough to recognise that the second question had repeated the first in a form that would produce a different fee. Nalia took a little folded paper from inside her packet and put it beside the clerk’s hand. He looked at it, then at her, and changed the question.

Ailsa’s son shifted his weight. His father put a hand on his shoulder before he could speak.

The clerk pointed towards another desk.

“That’s the maritime examination,” Nalia told Saikou. “I’ll get the household things through here. Take the books yourself.”

“Why?”

“Because you can answer what they are without coming back to ask me.”

Saikou looked at the second table. A man in a dark blue coat stood beside it with two attendants. His face was shaded by the warehouse roof, but his hands lay in the light, square and clean against the wood.

Nalia lowered her voice.

“He asked for you by name.”

The examiner knew Saikou had come from Silkstrand. He knew the number of travelling chests and that one had been reduced at the master’s insistence. He knew which passengers had boarded injured, and that House Ira had paid a healer to accompany them. Riarhin could account for all these things having travelled ahead of them, but hearing them in a stranger’s voice made her move her coat away from the sunlit edge of the table.

The examiner noticed the movement. He gave no sign of noticing more.

“Your purpose?” he asked Saikou.

“Travel. Some inquiries.”

“Commercial?”

“Possibly.”

“Your mother conducts commercial inquiries.”

“She does.”

“On her behalf?”

“No.”

The examiner turned a page in the packet before him.

“You have an interest in objects of religious or historical value.”

“I have several interests.”

“Have you brought such objects?”

Saikou put his hand inside his coat, slowly enough for the attendants to see, and took out the wrapped mirror.

“A Symbol used in my work. Another among my possessions. Copies of old writings. Nothing for sale.”

The examiner looked at the mirror without asking him to uncover it.

“What sort of work?”

“Invocation. Investigation. Occasionally the repair of a difficulty somebody else has made.”

“Does your work require authorisation here?”

“I had hoped to learn that before undertaking any.”

The man looked up. For the first time Riarhin saw something like amusement in his face.

“A prudent hope.”

He asked to see the books. Saikou opened the smaller chest. The examiner lifted two volumes, inspected the binding of a third and spent more time than Riarhin liked looking at the wrapped papers beneath it. He did not open them. Instead he asked where they intended to stay.

Saikou gave the estate’s address.

The examiner wrote it down.

“Any items acquired here for removal by sea must be declared separately. A private sale may not establish the seller’s right to dispose of them.”

“We are arriving,” Saikou said.

“Yes.” The examiner returned the page to its place. “It is useful to know before acquiring.”

He dismissed them with a movement of two fingers.

Riarhin followed Saikou away from the table. The little shape in her coat seemed to have acquired a weight unrelated to its size. She kept her hand away from it.

“Will he search us?” she asked.

“He could have done so.”

“Why didn’t he?”

“I don’t know.”

She looked back. The examiner had turned to another passenger. The man was opening a box of tools with the aggrieved care of someone who had packed them well and expected them to remain packed.

Their baggage cleared the quay in three loads. The last did not go by the route Nalia had arranged because men working the ropeway had stopped work at a platform farther up. A load hung motionless between the poles. Below it a crowd had gathered around a man holding a piece of paper.

Nalia listened, spoke to a woman at the edge of the group and returned looking annoyed.

“We’ll use the lower road.”

“What’s happened?” Ailsa asked.

“A permit has arrived for a cargo they were told would wait. The crews disagree with the permit.”

“Who gave it?”

“The office which does not have to lower the thing.”

She said it without humour and went to renegotiate with the carter.

Riarhin watched the suspended load. No one was bringing it down. One of the men on the platform sat with his legs dangling over the edge. A woman below passed up food in a basket. The man holding the paper kept pointing at it as if someone above had failed to see it yet.

When the carts moved, they took a road past yards where nets hung beside lengths of rope and children ran in and out of doorways whose shade was full of seated adults. Ailsa’s daughter wanted to know why everyone was looking at them. Her brother said it was because she kept looking first. Their father told them both that people had eyes for that purpose.

The heat gathered beneath Riarhin’s coat. She had loosened her shirt, but kept the coat on for the sake of the wrapped object in its pocket. Aurine looked across from the other cart and lifted a water flask. Riarhin nodded. The woman passed it over at the next stop without asking a question.

Above the road the houses grew farther apart. Trees shaded the walls, and a smell like crushed green peel came from somewhere she could not see. At a turning, Nalia pointed towards a gateway.

“There. We’ve reached the part your hostess described.”

Ailsa leaned forward. The gate stood open.

Chapter 10 — Rooms Facing the Sea

The first thing Ailsa did was ask where the people who worked in the house lived.

The woman receiving them had been prepared to show bedrooms. She held a ring with four keys on it and had arranged herself beside the entrance so the arriving guests could see the long room beyond, its shutters open towards the sea. At Ailsa’s question she looked at Nalia.

“She wants to know,” Nalia said.

“I understood.”

The woman returned her attention to Ailsa.

“Some here. Some below. My sister has a house near the well.”

“Will we be putting anybody out of a room?”

“No.”

“Good. Show us what’s ours, then.”

The woman’s name was Savi. She spoke the trade language precisely and with an expression suggesting that she had heard it used less precisely by people who considered themselves entitled to correct her. Riarhin liked her at once, then reminded herself that liking someone at once was not an accomplishment.

Savi led them through the long room. The floor was cool beneath the soles of their shoes. Light came through the shutters in narrow bands, crossing woven mats and the legs of chairs. A table stood against one wall with a bowl of water on it and a branch of small white flowers laid beside the bowl rather than in it. At the far end, two doors opened onto a covered walk.

Ailsa’s daughter went towards the sea view, stopped and looked back to see whether she was allowed. Savi nodded.

She ran.

“Slowly on the end steps,” Savi called after her. “They are worn.”

The girl slowed before reaching them.

Riarhin went out onto the covered walk. The garden fell away through trees towards roofs on the lower road. Beyond the roofs lay the sea, broken by pale strips near the shore and darker farther out. A woman was spreading cloth over a hedge. Two boys carried a basket between them, arguing in a language Riarhin understood only in fragments.

Ailsa came beside her.

“Well?”

“It’s a house.”

“Aye. She didn’t lie about that.”

“It’s a very good house.”

“I can see why someone would want to live here.”

Ailsa rested both hands on the railing and let her shoulders drop.

“Go and choose a room,” Riarhin said.

“Will you come?”

“Yes.”

Choosing rooms took most of the afternoon, partly because people kept changing their minds and partly because Savi insisted that a pleasant room at that hour might become an oven later. She opened shutters, shut others and brought them back after an interval to feel the difference. The children’s father asked where a table could be put for repairs. She showed him a shaded space off the lower yard.

“For the things you bring,” she said. “The house work goes through me.”

He nodded. “Fair.”

“And no shavings in the drain.”

He looked at the drain, then at the wood stacked above it.

“I’d have said that myself.”

“Then we should agree.”

The children chose adjacent rooms and immediately began discussing whether they could instead share one and use the other for purposes their parents had not authorised. Iona wanted a room with a door to the outside if one was available. Mairi wanted to know where her son could go without entering the road. Savi answered each question, sometimes with a question of her own.

Saikou took the smallest of the visitors’ rooms. Nalia looked inside it and told him the wall would be noisy in the morning because the household began work on the other side before guests generally wished to be awake.

“I rise early.”

“You can discuss that with them at the time.”

Cadan asked where she preferred him to wash his clothes. Savi showed him, and he thanked her with the same care he had used when asking where a dead man’s papers should be put. She watched him a little longer than the question required.

Berent chose a room near the kitchen. He put his bag on the bed and asked Savi when the household ate. She told him, adding that the cook disliked visitors who arrived before the food was ready. He thanked her for the warning.

Riarhin went with Ailsa to a room at the quiet end of the covered walk. There was space to move around the bed. A window opened towards the garden; another looked into a small court where a tree grew close enough to brush the wall. Riarhin could hear leaves rubbing against plaster.

Ailsa put her bag down and drew out the glass bird.

She set it on the sill, moved it away from direct sunlight and stepped back.

“He looks pleased.”

“He looks prepared to complain.”

“Then he’ll settle.”

She turned and began undoing Riarhin’s coat.

“You’re wet through.”

“Under the coat.”

“Under everything, by the feel.”

Riarhin put a hand over hers at the inner fastening.

“I’ll do that part.”

Ailsa looked at the pocket, then at her face. She withdrew her hand.

“Where do you want it?”

“With me.”

“I meant the coat.”

“Oh.”

For a moment the mistake stood between them. Ailsa took the coat by its shoulders when Riarhin had drawn it off and laid it on the chair within reach. Then she brought the basin closer.

“Wash first. You can look at it again afterwards.”

Riarhin did not look at the pocket while she washed. She kept having to turn her head away. The water smelled faintly of the clay jar in which it had stood, and felt cool along her neck. Ailsa wrung out a cloth and wiped the part of her back she could not comfortably reach.

Outside, the children discovered something in the tree and called for their father.

The tutor came the following morning carrying a book wrapped in cloth and accompanied by a boy older than Ailsa’s son. The boy carried nothing and had the air of someone who had been brought to demonstrate that a promise could be fulfilled.

The tutor asked the children what they could read. They answered differently, and their mother began to qualify the answers. He listened, then asked each child to read a little from a page he laid on the table.

Riarhin stood beyond the doorway with a cup in her hand. Ailsa sat at the table beside their father. Nalia had made the introductions and remained only long enough to explain how the tutor would be paid if they wanted him to return.

The older boy waited under a shutter, watching Ailsa’s son with frank curiosity. When the tutor moved to the girl, the two boys began talking. Riarhin heard the word for knife, then several words she could not distinguish because they had begun speaking over one another. Within a minute they were disagreeing about something each considered obvious.

The tutor let them continue until the girl had finished.

“Good,” he said. “You have both found a reason to learn the other’s language.”

Ailsa’s son looked offended at the suggestion that his argument might have educational value.

Later the tutor showed the parents examples of the work done at the school. There were clean pages of letters, calculations and drawings, and a copy of a shipping form so carefully written that Riarhin recognised the hand of someone practising elegance as well as use. One exercise concerned an official who had brought order to a harbor after years of local disputes.

“Which harbor?” Ailsa’s former husband asked.

The tutor named it.

“Was it disorderly?”

“In some respects.”

“Before this man?”

“And after.”

The tutor turned the page. There was a second exercise beneath the first, written in a different hand. The first sentence had been copied; below it, a student had challenged the account of the taxes.

“We require them to identify what supports the challenge,” he said. “Some are disappointed to discover that disliking an official does not suffice.”

“And the first account?” Riarhin asked from the doorway.

He looked up.

“We ask the same of it.”

She could not tell how often that happened. The tutor had brought a promising pupil and good examples; he was seeking employment. He left the second page beside the first for Ailsa to examine.

Ailsa asked him to come again.

Savi took them to the well after the worst heat had passed. It stood in a walled yard below the garden, shaded on one side by trees whose roots had lifted two of the paving stones. A woman from the lower road was drawing water when they arrived. She finished, set the pot against her hip and spoke to Savi, looking directly at Ailsa as she did so.

Savi answered. The woman nodded to Ailsa, who nodded back.

“What did she ask?”

“Whether the gate would continue to be open.”

“Yes,” Ailsa said.

Savi translated the answer after the woman had already begun to smile.

On the way back they passed a little open shelter where two people were sorting flowers into shallow baskets. One of them wore a working shirt marked with dust. The other had a length of bright cloth folded over one shoulder and was correcting the first person’s arrangement with great impatience.

Ailsa asked what the flowers were for.

“An observance at the old basin,” Savi said. “Before the water is lowered for cleaning.”

“May we see?”

“If you keep out of the workers’ way.”

They went down the next morning with the children. People had gathered around a stone basin older than the walls beside it. There were places where the carving had worn almost flat, and others where repair work stood out pale against the darker stone. A man stood in the shallow water receiving the flowers. He arranged them along a ledge beneath the surface, where they should have floated free.

They stayed where he put them.

Riarhin watched his hand move away. The flowers held against the gentle movement of the water, bright beneath it. The child beside her whispered a question. Her father shook his head slightly, meaning wait.

A woman at the edge of the basin began singing. Others joined when they knew the words. The man in the water lowered his head. For a short time the sound of the nearby road seemed far away.

Then a worker said something practical, the singing ended, and people began shifting baskets and setting tools in order. A boy went in up to his knees and was sent out to remove his good trousers first.

Savi asked whether they wished to stay. Ailsa’s daughter did. Her brother wanted breakfast. The disagreement was resolved by their father taking him back while the others remained.

Riarhin sat in the shade beside Ailsa and watched the flowers remain under the water.

That evening Nalia came to discuss the onward journey. She had changed into a plain coat, and the packet under her arm looked thicker than at the quay. Saikou offered her a seat. She asked whether she was being asked to advise as House Ira’s agent or engaged independently for the inland work.

“Does it change the advice?” Riarhin asked.

“It changes who pays me for the afternoon.”

“And the advice?”

“It may change what I am permitted to undertake after giving it.”

Saikou set his cup down.

“Independently, if we can agree terms.”

“Good.”

She opened the packet.

The route north depended on a load expected at a warehouse beyond the salt pans. It should already have arrived. The carriers meant to take it inland would not reserve places indefinitely for passengers attached to an absent load. Admiral Voss’s office could tell them what had happened, though Nalia said its account would begin with the measures taken to protect the cargo. Voss governed from the white fort above the harbor; his officers had held the carriers’ answers for two days.

“Have you asked?” Riarhin said.

“Twice. The versions differ.”

“Then we’ll ask him together.”

Nalia looked at her arm.

“Does that work?”

“Enough.”

“That is a word he uses too.”

Riarhin smiled despite herself. Nalia put another page on the table and began explaining what her fee would cover.

Dema came to the estate on the second morning with her narrow case. She watched Riarhin lift a water jar, tested Berent’s knee under load and asked Aurine what she had noticed since they came ashore. There was little to add. Berent had walked farther than he intended in the town and slept well afterwards.

The healer left them a small parcel of supplies and the name of a practitioner near the harbor. Her return passage had been arranged before they left Silkstrand. When Ailsa offered her a room, she thanked her and declined; she had a household of her own waiting across the sea.

“Have you sent your account?” Saikou asked.

“To the factor who engaged me. The work is paid.”

Dema closed the case. Riarhin helped carry it to the cart. On the way back she picked up the water jar and took it into the house with her.

Chapter 11 — At the Salt Pans

The fort’s oldest doorway was too low for the officer who showed them through it.

He bent his head without noticing. Above the lintel, a new coat of whitewash ended at a stone on which old cuts remained visible. Riarhin saw the edge of a figure beneath the paint, a hand perhaps, turned palm outward. The officer led them into a room where the shutters stood open towards the harbor. A map occupied most of one wall. Someone had pinned scraps to it, covering the coastline in places where a visitor might have hoped to see the shore.

Admiral Voss stood at the window.

He turned when Saikou entered. One eye moved towards them before the other. The second had a glass shine which caught the light and held it after his face had turned away.

“Master Ira.”

“Admiral.”

“You have been kept waiting.”

“Yes.”

Voss’s mouth twitched. He indicated chairs. Nalia took the one nearest the papers laid on the table. Riarhin remained standing until the admiral looked at her, then sat where she could see the doorway without turning.

“We have a difficulty at the southern landing,” Voss said. “Your inquiry about passage has reached me.”

“So I understand.”

“A lighter carrying goods for the inland road was attacked outside the channel. The men brought her into an old basin near the pans. She remains there. The cargo has not been brought out.”

“What prevents it?” Saikou asked.

“The people employed to do so give several accounts.”

“Have you heard them?”

“My officer has.”

Voss took a folded sheet from the table and held it out. Saikou read it standing, though a chair was beside him. Riarhin watched his eyes move down the page. They stopped near the bottom. He returned to an earlier line, then handed it to her.

The report named the attack, the cargo and the lighter’s damage. Below, in a less certain hand, someone had added that the basin had begun holding water above the tide, that ropes drawn across the inner sill grew hot, and that a worker sent beneath the old quay had failed to return.

“When?” Saikou asked.

“Yesterday.”

“At which tide?”

Voss looked towards the officer.

“The morning ebb.”

Saikou folded the paper once along its existing crease.

“He may still be alive.”

“That is what the people there say.”

“What do you say?”

The officer shifted his weight. Voss answered for him.

“We intend to recover him.”

Saikou waited.

“Your experience has been recommended,” Voss said.

“By whom?”

“By more than one person. The reasons differed.”

Riarhin looked at the glass eye. A drop of moisture lay beneath it. Voss took a cloth from his sleeve and touched the place without looking away from Saikou.

“We can give you transport, men and this.”

He indicated a long wrapped object beside the table. The officer drew back the cloth. Inside lay an iron hook with a heavy wooden grip, the metal dark except where hands or rope had polished it. Marks had been cut round its socket. They were too close together to read without turning it.

“From a shipbreaker,” Voss said. “It has been used with good results where other equipment failed.”

Saikou looked at the hook and then at the officer.

“Has it been put into the basin?”

“No.”

“Keep it here.”

The officer frowned.

“It was brought for—”

“I can see what it was brought for. I would prefer the men searching for your worker not to be commanded by something you brought to move a ship.”

Voss regarded him. Saikou had not raised his voice. Riarhin found that she was listening as she listened when a good commander ceased explaining and gave an order.

“Take what assistance you require,” Voss said at last. “Leave the hook.”

Nalia laid one hand on the edge of the report.

“And the recovered cargo?”

“The cargo is under the Admiralty’s protection.”

“It belongs to the road households and the lighthouse store. The attack did not change the owners.”

“Whoever owns it will be glad to receive it intact.”

“I should like that included.”

Voss looked at her hand on the paper.

“We are discussing a missing man.”

“Yes,” Nalia said. “His employers sent the goods. They are waiting for both.”

For a moment the room was quiet enough to hear a man coughing outside the door. Voss took another sheet, wrote three lines and gave it to the officer.

“There. Recovery under supervision, with ownership to be established on delivery.”

Nalia read it. She did not look pleased.

Saikou asked how soon they could leave.

The road to the pans ran between low walls and patches of cultivation whose names Riarhin had not learned. Here and there channels crossed beneath flat stones. People worked beside them in hats wide enough to shade their shoulders. The cart driver greeted some and passed others without a word. He had an opinion about every patch of damaged road and expressed most of them to his mule.

Nalia sat beside him, asking questions whenever he paused for breath. Berent and Aurine had come in the second cart with supplies. Cadan stayed at the estate; Riarhin had asked him to help Savi move a broken piece of furniture, and he had accepted this as readily as he had accepted other instructions.

The missing man’s sister met them where the road divided.

She had been standing beneath a little tree with a water jar beside her. When the carts stopped she asked whether they were the people from the fort. Nalia answered before the accompanying officer could.

“We’ve come from there. He knows about workings.” She indicated Saikou. “She treats wounds. These two can help with the recovery.”

The woman looked at each in turn. Her eyes stopped at the supplies beside Aurine, then went to Saikou’s satchel.

“We’ve strong people,” she said.

“Good,” Riarhin answered. “We’ll need them.”

That seemed to satisfy her more than an account of what they could do. She picked up the jar and climbed onto the back of the first cart without asking.

Her name was Renu. Her brother had gone beneath the quay to inspect the lower passage because he had worked there before. His name was written incorrectly in the officer’s report. She corrected it, watched Saikou make the correction on his own paper, and began speaking faster.

The basin had been used by boats long before Voss’s men came. People knew which steps could be walked at low water and which wall should never be used to tie a load. They had told the lighter’s men. The lighter’s men had been frightened, one of them wounded, and had wanted the boat close to solid ground. A new iron ring had been fixed where an old marking had been cut away.

“Who fixed it?” the officer asked.

“Ask the men who put it there.”

“They’re not here.”

“My brother is.”

He looked away.

Saikou asked how the ropes had lain. Renu showed him with her hands: one to the new ring, another across the inner sill, the boat drawn sideways between them. He asked which had grown hot first. She knew. He asked whether the water rose against the outer wall or the inner. She knew that too.

He put his papers away.

“When we get there, nobody cuts the rope across the sill.”

“The men wanted to.”

“Tell them to wait. Where is the diver?”

“Already there.”

“Good.”

Riarhin watched him settle back against the cart. He had ceased looking at the report. The officer tried to supply another detail, and Saikou lifted a hand without impatience.

“In a moment. Let her finish.”

The salt pans spread flat and bright beneath the afternoon. Low banks separated one shallow enclosure from another. Farther out, where the ground gave way to mud and broken stone, the old basin lay between walls darkened by water. The lighter sat unnaturally high within it, tilted towards the inner quay. Men stood on the bank looking down. One had a rope across his shoulder and kept changing the place where it touched him.

Riarhin could hear the water before she reached it. There was a pressure in the sound, a continuous low note beneath the lap and splash. The surface within the basin stood above the open channel. Along the outer sill a clear lip of water curved upward and held, trembling in the wind.

Aurine stopped beside her.

“Oh,” she said softly.

Saikou kept walking.

A woman with close-cropped hair came up from the steps. She carried a stone weight on a cord and had a fresh scrape along her shoulder. Renu spoke to her, then introduced her as the diver. She gave Saikou her name, Leela, and immediately told him which passage she had tried.

He knelt to look at the wet marks on the stones.

“How far?”

“To the second pier. There’s air beyond it.”

“Moving?”

“Yes.”

“Towards you?”

She nodded.

Saikou looked up at the lighter.

“Something is still drawing through. He may have an air pocket.”

“I thought that.”

“Can you reach it without crossing under the boat?”

“If they’ll stop pulling her.”

The officer began to speak. Saikou stood.

“Stop pulling the boat.”

The men looked towards the officer. He hesitated, then repeated the order. They lowered the rope with visible relief.

Riarhin went to the edge. The stone beneath her feet showed several kinds of repair: fitted blocks, rougher patches and a long pale strip where a groove had been filled. A little plant grew from the join. Someone had placed flowers beside an opening in the wall, away from the workers’ feet.

Renu caught her looking.

“For my brother.”

“Who placed them?”

“I did.”

Riarhin nodded and moved so her boot would not brush them.

Saikou called Leela back to the steps. Riarhin went to join them.

Chapter 12 — The Work Under Water

Saikou put his hand on the stone and withdrew it at once.

“That one?” Leela asked.

“Yes.”

“I told them.”

The officer stood behind her, hearing enough to know he was being included. He looked at the lighter. Its cargo lay under a wet cloth, one corner loose and trailing in water.

“What is happening?” he asked.

“The basin is holding against a load it was not built to hold there,” Saikou said. “The new mooring crosses an older working. Pulling the boat tighter has made it worse.”

“Can you stop it?”

“I can help you release it without dropping the water on the passage your man is in. First we find exactly where he is.”

He turned to Renu.

“Something of his?”

She pulled a folded scrap from her sleeve. It was a strip of cloth with a small knot at one end, damp from being held.

“He tore it wrapping his hand. I kept it.”

Saikou took it carefully. He asked which hand and what had cut it. Renu answered. Then he went to a place where the light fell clear of the moving water and uncovered the mirror.

Riarhin stayed close enough to keep people from crowding him. The officer tried to look over his shoulder. She put out her left hand, palm against the front of his coat.

“Give him room.”

“I’m responsible here.”

“Then do that.”

He stepped back.

Saikou’s lips moved. The mirror tilted until Riarhin could see only a white rectangle of sky. Something dark crossed it, too briefly for her to make out. Saikou turned a little towards the wall and held still.

A sound came from beneath the quay.

Renu moved first. Leela caught her arm before she reached the edge. The sound came again: a weak blow against wood, then a scrape.

Saikou lowered the glass.

“Beyond the second pier,” he said. “Higher than the water in the passage. Leela, is there a ledge?”

“There was one.”

“Then we work on the assumption it still holds him. No one shouts into the opening. Let her listen.”

He returned the strip of cloth to Renu. She folded it once and pressed it between both hands.

Riarhin had recovered people from places defended by men. She had recovered things from places defended by habit, pride and the belief that a door with enough locks must be safe. She had never tried to recover a man from a wall holding up water.

Leela had.

She explained the passage with a piece of broken tile, drawing on dry stone above the steps. Her finger supplied what the drawing could not: the turn around a pier, the narrowing under the quay, the place where a swimmer must roll onto one side. Riarhin crouched beside the drawing. Berent knelt opposite her and moved a loose chip of tile to mark the hauling place.

“How do you bring him through if he can’t swim?” he asked.

“Slowly.”

“With a line?”

“Past the first turn. Not round the second. It catches.”

“What if we keep it high?”

She looked at him, then redrew the second turn.

“If someone holds it there.”

Riarhin looked at the space beside the outer opening. There was room for a person on the dry side of the pier, reached by a narrow shelf of stone. The shelf would be slippery, but the distance was short. She could get there and manage the line.

“I can hold it.”

Leela shook her head.

“The stones lift when the water changes. Have you worked that shelf?”

“No.”

“Then I want someone who has.”

There were six people within hearing who had worked the basin for years. Riarhin felt the absurdity of her own offer arrive a moment later than the resentment. She pointed to the shelf instead.

“Who can get there?”

A young man with a scar at his chin stepped forward. Leela gave him a look of appraisal and nodded.

“You listen to me,” she told him. “Not the men pulling.”

He said he would.

Berent began arranging the line with two of the workers. He wanted a different angle and showed them why by drawing the rope tight while one man held it against his palm. The man felt the change, nodded and moved the hauling place. The worker called another man over and showed him the new position. Berent tested the line again.

Saikou went to the inner sill.

Riarhin followed. He had the Gate Symbol uncovered and a pouch of powder in his hand. The stone was too wet in places to take a clean line. He examined the joins, moved to an older seam and asked Leela whether water ever crossed it from the inland side.

“During the great rains.”

“Since the last repair?”

“Yes.”

He nodded and moved farther back.

“The older boundary runs here,” he told Riarhin, marking the place with his toe. “I can brace it while they release the tension. It will keep the spill away from the steps for a little while. The outer basin will still drain. The men must bring the boat towards the old channel, not towards us.”

“How long?”

“Long enough if they begin when Leela says. I will not be able to look down the passage and tell them for her.”

“I’ll stand where I can hear.”

“Good.”

He set the Symbol on a dry fold of cloth and began.

Leela entered the water without ceremony.

One moment she was standing on the lowest step with the weight in her hand; then her head was gone and the surface closed over her. Renu gripped Riarhin’s wrist. Riarhin let her. Beneath them, a few bubbles moved towards the opening and stopped.

The line lay over the young man’s hand at the pier. He watched it so intently that his mouth had fallen open. Beyond him, Berent had placed himself where he could see both the boat and the steps. He held a length of rope across his knees, demonstrating to the workers how much slack he wanted preserved.

Saikou’s voice came from behind the wall.

Riarhin could not understand the words. She could hear where breath went into them, where it was held and where released. She watched his hands as she might have watched an unfamiliar swordsman, trying to catch the decision before the movement.

The cut along the old seam brightened. Water rose against it in a thin, trembling sheet no higher than Riarhin’s waist, clear enough that she could see the far stones through it and distorted enough that they appeared to move. Sunlight broke along its upper edge. Renu’s grip tightened until it hurt.

The young man at the pier called out.

“Keep him looking at the line,” Saikou said from beyond the wall.

Riarhin repeated it. The man dragged his eyes from the water and bent over his work.

A hand appeared beneath the quay. It struck the stone, missed, and disappeared. Then Leela’s head came up at the opening. She had one arm under someone whose face Riarhin could not see.

“Hold,” she called.

Berent held his hand up. The hauling men stopped.

Leela took a breath and went under again.

Renu made a broken sound against her teeth. Riarhin looked at the line, at the current beginning to move along the lighter’s side, and at the young man on the shelf. He shifted his grip. The rope caught against the corner.

“Higher,” she said. “Your left hand. Lift it.”

He did. The rope came free.

Leela surfaced a second time, dragging the missing man across the lower opening. He was conscious. His mouth was working, but no sound reached them. His injured hand hung at an angle that made Riarhin’s own fingers curl.

“Now,” Leela said.

Berent lowered his hand.

The hauling men moved the lighter towards the old channel. The rope across the sill slackened. Water struck the clear sheet and rose against it, making its surface bulge towards the steps. Riarhin heard Saikou’s voice deepen and continue.

Leela pushed the man into waiting hands.

They brought him up the steps with his feet dragging. Aurine was there before the workers had laid him down. Renu let go of Riarhin and dropped beside her brother. He turned his head towards her voice and began to cough.

Below, the old basin emptied into the channel with a sound like a long breath taken through stone.

Afterwards there was more work than there had seemed to be before the rescue.

The lighter could not be moved until part of the cargo came off. The men unloading it wanted to begin at the easiest side; Leela objected because that would tip the boat farther. Berent stayed to arrange a line for the heaviest bundles. Saikou sat in the shade while Riarhin brought water and held the cup until his fingers were steady enough to take it.

“Good?” she asked.

He nodded, drank and shook his head at the second cup.

“In a moment.”

His face had lost colour. Sweat darkened his collar. The Symbol lay wrapped beside his knee, intact. He put a hand over it when a worker came close with a wet coil.

“Further away, please.”

The man moved the rope and thanked him. Saikou looked up as if the thanks had arrived through a noise he had been trying to hear past.

“You should thank Leela.”

“I have.”

“Then you are ahead of me.”

The worker smiled and went back to the boat.

Riarhin sat beside Saikou. She could see Renu kneeling with Aurine, holding her brother’s good hand while the woman cleaned the injured one. Every now and then Renu leaned close to hear something he said. He had been caught on the ledge when the water rose, exactly where Leela had believed he might be. He had heard the people above him and had been unable to make them hear him.

At the lighter, the officer was writing down what came off.

A woman challenged him over the first group of sacks. They were for the lighthouse store, she said. He asked for a mark. She showed it. He wrote something and told the men to carry them to the waiting cart.

The cart had the Admiralty’s mark on its side.

Nalia crossed the quay quickly.

“That goes to the store,” she said.

“It goes for examination.”

“It has been examined.”

“Here. Not at the warehouse.”

She held up the paper from Voss.

“Read it,” she said.

The officer read it.

“Ownership to be established on delivery,” he said. “Precisely.”

Riarhin started to get up. Saikou caught her sleeve.

“Listen first.”

Nalia had begun speaking again, more slowly. The woman who owned the sacks stood beside her. Workers stopped unloading one by one until only the water moved.

They stayed until the last cargo had been put under cover at a store above the pans. The officer placed a seal on the doors. Nalia insisted that one of the owners place a second beside it and that both should be present when the doors opened. He refused twice and agreed when he saw that the workers had gone to eat rather than wait for him.

Renu brought Saikou a bowl of food. Leela was already eating from hers. He thanked her and ate with the bowl balanced on his knee.

Riarhin walked down to the old wall while the light was low. The water had fallen enough to expose the line of an earlier quay. Beneath a newer patch of masonry, carved steps continued into mud. Someone had repaired the edge with stone taken from a building whose decoration faced inward. She could see part of a face in the join, an eye and the curve of a mouth, fitted into the wall where only a person kneeling at low tide would see it.

Leela came down carrying the stone weight.

“They’ll cut the new ring out tomorrow,” she said. “If they do what they’ve agreed.”

“Who put it there?”

“Men working for the harbor office. They said we needed a place for larger boats.”

“Did you?”

“Yes.”

Riarhin looked at her.

“There’s a place farther along,” Leela said. “It needs clearing. More work. They wanted this before the next convoy.”

She set the weight down to adjust the cord. Her hands were scraped and swollen. Riarhin watched her tie a knot one-handed and understood that the other shoulder had begun to hurt.

“Can I do that?”

Leela gave her the loose end.

“Hold it there. Tighter.”

Riarhin held the cord while Leela made the knot. When it held, Leela tested it twice, picked up the weight and led the way back towards supper.

Chapter 13 — The Verdict Arrives

The order came while Berent was helping Renu’s brother into a cart.

The man had insisted on going to the store himself. His arm was bound against his chest, and each step down from the house had required an argument with his sister. She had won most of them and lost the one that mattered. Now she stood beside the wheel with a parcel of food and told him that if he fainted she would leave him where he fell.

“You won’t,” he said.

“That’s why you’re so tiresome.”

Berent steadied him by the good elbow. The cart was high. He could step onto the hub, but from there he had to turn without using the injured hand. Berent showed him where to put his foot and waited while he did it. Riarhin held the horse. It was a patient little animal with one ear turned towards the basket of food.

Nalia came through the yard carrying a sheet of paper.

“Don’t go yet.”

Renu turned. “What now?”

“The cargo has been impounded.”

“It was impounded yesterday.”

“For recovery. This is a judgment.”

“Against whom?”

Nalia looked at the man in the cart.

“Against the masters and owners of goods carried contrary to the harbor’s protection.”

He swore. Renu took the paper from her.

She read part of it, came to a word she did not know and handed it back.

“Say it plainly.”

“They claim the lighter entered a prohibited basin while carrying goods from an unexamined vessel. They claim the recovery revealed property whose ownership is doubtful. They intend to remove everything to the Admiralty warehouse until examination is complete.”

“The boat went in because men were shooting at it.”

“I know.”

“Does he?”

“The officer who gave the account knows.”

Renu looked towards her brother. He had stopped trying to climb into the cart and stood with one foot on the hub, holding the side with his good hand. His face had gone pale beneath the colour of the sun.

“Get him down,” Aurine said from the doorway.

“I’m going.”

“You can go sitting beside your sister when you’ve had water. Get down.”

He began objecting, then stopped and allowed Berent to help him. The horse stretched its neck towards the parcel. Riarhin shortened the rein.

“Who can hear a challenge?” she asked Nalia.

“The office that issued it. Or a court that will question whether that office can make this judgment.”

“And can one?”

“Yes.”

“Before they take the goods?”

Nalia folded the order.

“Only if the people at the store know they have reason to wait.”

By the time they arrived, men in harbor coats had begun carrying sacks down the steps.

The local seal lay broken beside the door. The Admiralty seal had been removed cleanly and put in a box. Riarhin noticed the difference before she saw the woman from the day before standing between a handcart and the road. Revati, from the lighthouse store, had brought two young men. Both stood stiffly behind her, watching the road.

One of the harbor men was telling her to move.

“You can stand over there. Nobody is preventing you from witnessing.”

“You have opened our door without the keeper.”

“We have an order.”

“I’ve seen it.”

“Then move.”

She stayed where she was.

Nalia passed Riarhin and went directly to the officer. She had brought the recovery instructions, their copy of the sealed inventory and the new judgment. She placed all three on a low wall, weighted the corners with stones and began asking questions.

Riarhin moved to Revati’s side.

“Is this your cargo?”

“The grain is for the store. The tools belong to the road carriers. Two chests belong to households up-country. Everybody can tell them what belongs to whom.”

“Why don’t they accept it?”

“Then they would have to give it back.”

A man came out carrying a box which Riarhin recognised from the lighter. Behind him another followed with a coil of line. They stepped round Revati without meeting her eyes. She turned so that they had to come closer to the wall to pass.

“Don’t make them hurt her,” one of the young men said quietly.

Riarhin looked at him. He was watching her hand, which had gone near the knife at her belt without her deciding to put it there.

“I haven’t drawn.”

“They’ll remember that you could. She lives here.”

She moved her hand away.

The young man breathed out. He was frightened enough that the breath shook.

Nalia called Riarhin over. The officer had found a new basis for his examination: the company’s assistance in recovering objects of unknown origin from an old site. He wished to establish whether any such object had already been removed in their personal baggage.

Riarhin looked at him until Nalia touched her elbow.

“Answer the question.”

“We took no object from the site.”

“The hook?”

“Stayed at the fort.”

“Any carved stone? Metal fitting? Religious article?”

“No.”

“Your companion performed a working there.”

“With what he brought.”

“Which must be examined.”

Saikou came up beside her.

“My Symbols were declared on arrival.”

“Were they recorded?”

“The examiner saw them.”

“Then the record will establish that.”

“Fetch it,” Saikou said.

The officer hesitated.

“You may accompany us.”

“I am here. You are the one uncertain what your office knows.”

People close enough to hear had stopped moving. The officer looked beyond Saikou, taking in the gathered workers, the owners and the cart with an injured man sitting in it.

“You should consider the consequences of obstructing an examination.”

“I have. Consider the consequences of holding a working Symbol after its owner has told you not to uncover it.”

Saikou said this without menace. The officer looked at the satchel, then decided to speak to Nalia again.

They gained an hour by sending for the first examiner’s record and another by insisting that no cargo move until the quantities in the recovery inventory had been compared with what remained in the store. Nalia sent one of Revati’s young men to the lighthouse court. Revati stayed by the handcart. Renu took her brother home before he could faint and told him to be angry on a mattress.

Riarhin went behind the store to look at the other door.

It opened onto a narrow yard with a low wall beyond it. A cart could be brought along the lane. The door had a cheap lock. Even with one arm she could open it, and there were enough people willing to carry goods once somebody told them the risk had been accepted. By night they could have most of the cargo elsewhere.

She heard footsteps and turned.

Revati stood in the doorway.

“Yes,” the woman said. “It opens.”

“I can see.”

“And the lane goes to the lower road. There’s a place to turn a cart.”

Riarhin waited.

“My nephew looked yesterday. He offered to bring friends.”

“What did you tell him?”

“That I wanted to know where we should keep the store after they closed this one. And which of his friends would carry the grain to the old women who collect it here when the harbor men stopped us at the lane.”

She came into the yard. One of the broken seals had been swept to the edge of the wall. She picked it up and rubbed dust from the impression with her thumb.

“We can move this load. Then the next boat comes. They know its master. They know where we live. I want them to agree they cannot do this every time they need something.”

“They may not agree.”

“No. But we’ve asked for a hearing before. We’re not beginning because you came.”

Riarhin looked at the door again. It was still a cheap lock, and opening it would solve very little.

“What do you need from me?”

“Say where you saw the cargo. Say who told you to recover it. Stay until they’ve heard you.”

“I meant to go north.”

“I know.”

Revati tucked the broken seal into her sleeve. She had made the request without softening it, and now she waited for an answer.

“I’ll stay,” Riarhin said.

At the estate, Ailsa had spent the afternoon arguing with her son about lessons. She began telling Riarhin as they sat down to supper, stopped at the look on her face and asked whether she wanted to eat first.

“Yes.”

Ailsa passed the bowl. Around them the household ate with the new familiarity that had begun to form around repeated places. Mairi’s boy sat beside Savi’s nephew and was acquiring words which Mairi had not yet learned to recognise. Iona was talking to a visitor about where certain cloth could be bought without paying the price asked at the harbor. The children’s father had found work repairing a loading frame and was pleased enough by the prospect to pretend it was no more than something to do.

Riarhin ate slowly. The food was hot with something that caught her at the back of the throat. She reached for water. Ailsa put a different cup beside her and said that would help more.

After supper they walked to the end of the covered way.

“You’re staying longer,” Ailsa said.

“Perhaps.”

“Good.”

“It’s trouble.”

“I supposed it was. I’m still glad.”

Riarhin leaned her good shoulder against the post. Down on the lower road a man called to somebody, and a woman answered from farther away than he had expected. The sound made Ailsa smile.

“I keep thinking I should go down and see if she needs something,” she said. “I don’t understand a word.”

“You’ll learn.”

“Some of it. The children are beginning already.”

Riarhin turned to her. “Are you happy?”

Ailsa took time to answer.

“Today I was. For a good part of it.”

Riarhin put her hand on the railing beside Ailsa’s. After a moment their fingers touched.

Chapter 14 — The Green Lantern

The tavern’s owner asked Nalia which business she had brought through the door.

“Mine.”

“That’s not an answer.”

“It is tonight.”

The owner looked at the three people behind her. Saikou inclined his head. Riarhin remained where she could move aside for a man carrying a pot. Berent had come because Nalia said the people they were meeting preferred witnesses who had actually done the work.

“Sit there,” the owner said, indicating a table beyond the main room. “You can be private until somebody wants the seats.”

A green lantern hung above the entrance. It was unlit. Riarhin had looked at it on the way in and looked away before the owner noticed her doing so. Saikou had seen it too. He had adjusted the way his satchel hung and then ordered food.

The table stood near an open court. Rainwater had been collected in jars beneath the roof, though there had been no rain since their arrival. A little boy was making a boat from a leaf and testing it in the nearest jar. Each failure produced an alteration. He had reached a design which floated crookedly but stayed afloat.

Two women joined them after the food came. One had the hands of someone used to rope and carried a folded scarf over her arm. The other wore a plain coat with a narrow band of decoration at the cuffs. She greeted Nalia warmly, Saikou courteously and Riarhin with a long assessing look.

“You stayed,” she said.

“I said I would.”

“People do.”

Nalia introduced her as the advocate who would bring Revati’s challenge. The woman with the scarf worked among the boat crews represented by the Sea King’s people. She gave a name Riarhin had difficulty hearing over a burst of laughter from the front room and did not repeat it.

“There is another way to take your party inland,” the boat-woman said. “If the road carriers remain delayed.”

“At what cost?” Saikou asked.

She named it.

Nalia laughed outright.

“That much for a promise?”

“For boats, men and a promise.”

“Whose men?”

“Men who know the landings.”

“Everybody knows the landings. Which men?”

The woman smiled and supplied a more useful answer. Riarhin watched Nalia’s face as the two talked. The young woman knew what question to ask and what silence to leave afterwards. She had been less sure with Renu and Leela. Here she was at home.

The advocate let the negotiation proceed until Nalia had established a possible price without accepting it. Then she put a folded paper beside Saikou’s plate.

“This is the hearing I can obtain. It may preserve the cargo while the Admiralty questions our standing. It cannot guarantee your departure.”

“If we use her boats?” Riarhin asked.

“Your testimony can be taken before you go. But the officer may prefer to examine you again once the owners have spoken.”

“He could keep asking.”

“Yes.”

The boat-woman broke a piece of bread.

“My offer remains.”

“For how long?” Nalia asked.

“Long enough for you to dislike it twice.”

They ate while the particulars were discussed. The boy’s leaf boat capsized. He lifted it out, considered it carefully and began again.

Revati’s keeper lived in rooms beside the lighthouse court, where the lower windows were screened against insects and the upper ones opened towards the ropeways. The keeper was older than Revati, broad in the face, with a white line across one wrist where a bracelet might once have been.
