She had a pot on the fire and no intention of leaving it to burn while visitors explained their urgency.

“Speak from there,” she told the advocate. “I can hear.”

Her niece stood at the table cleaning greens. A slim folded booklet lay beside the pile. She covered it with a cloth when the visitors entered, then uncovered it again as if annoyed at herself for doing so.

Riarhin smelled fish and something fermented and sharp. Her stomach, which had already received a meal at the tavern, responded with enthusiasm. The keeper looked at her and fetched another bowl.

“You’ve eaten?”

“Yes.”

“You can have a little.”

There was no useful answer to that. Riarhin sat.

The keeper brought out a bundle wrapped in dark cloth. Inside were copies of earlier agreements, some on paper, some written on material Riarhin did not recognise. A little bronze fitting lay among them, green in the hollows and bright where it had been handled.

“This comes from the old basin,” the keeper said. “My mother’s mother kept it when the harbor office rebuilt the outer stairs. They said the marking had no purpose because the new work would bear the load.”

Saikou leaned forward without touching it.

“May I?”

“You may look. You may tell me what you propose before you do anything else.”

He nodded.

The niece made a small impatient sound. The keeper turned to her.

“What?”

“The harbor office that did the stairs is not the harbor office now.”

“The stones remain.”

“Yes. So does this room. It doesn’t follow that everything said in it was wise.”

The keeper held her gaze for a moment, then went back to the pot.

Riarhin saw the booklet on the table. It carried the Admiralty’s mark.

Saikou examined the bronze with a light brought close. He turned his own head instead of the object, looking at the cuts from different angles. At one point he held a hand over part of it, making shade. Riarhin knew he had found something before he spoke.

“This edge was exposed to a working of the same kind as the sill,” he said. “I can test whether the connection is to that particular site. The result will establish a relation. It will not tell a court whose grandmother owned it.”

“I can tell them that,” the keeper said.

“Yes.”

“Who hears what you find?”

“You. Whoever you wish to be present. I should keep a note of the test and its limit.”

“Where?”

“With my papers.”

“Does House Ira read your papers?”

Saikou looked up.

“Not at my invitation.”

“That is a different answer.”

“It is the one I can give.”

The keeper took the bronze and wrapped it again.

“Tomorrow, with the advocate here. No removal. I keep a copy of what you write.”

“Agreed.”

The niece set down her knife.

“What if he finds it isn’t from there?”

“Then I will have learned something,” the keeper said.

The girl looked as though she wanted to believe that the answer would survive the result. She picked up the knife again.

They stayed to eat. The keeper insisted, then complained that visitors always arrived on a day when she had nothing fit to offer them. Her niece rolled her eyes where she could see it.

The booklet concerned an examination for navigators. The niece had saved the fee and wanted to attend instruction given at the harbor. Her aunt believed the office would take her money and refuse the position afterwards. The girl believed this was possible and resented its being treated as certain by someone who had never applied.

Saikou listened while he ate. Nalia asked which part of the examination she intended to take. The answer led to a discussion of a teacher Nalia knew and an error in one of the sample problems. The keeper intervened to say that the wrong answer had passed three officials before reaching the booklet, so perhaps errors were part of what they wished to teach.

“Then I shall be well prepared,” the girl said.

Berent choked on his food. Riarhin put a hand against his back until he could breathe properly, which annoyed him enough to help.

The keeper looked at him.

“Too much pepper?”

“No.”

“I can bring something plain.”

“It’s good.”

“Then eat more slowly.”

Her niece laughed. The argument resumed, less sharply now, and wandered into the subject of a cousin who had once announced an intention to join a ship and had come home before it sailed because he disliked the master’s boots. The keeper told this story against her niece, who had not been born when it happened and objected to being held responsible.

Riarhin leaned back with the bowl warm in her hand. Through the upper window she could see a load moving along the ropeway, dark against the last light. Somebody on the platform called. A voice below answered. The load continued on its way.

When they left, the keeper packed food for them despite their protests. Her niece came to the door with Nalia and asked, quietly, for the teacher’s name.

Nalia gave it.

Saikou paused beneath the unlit lantern on their way past the tavern. The owner sat outside on a stool with a cup between her hands. She looked at him and then at the satchel.

“You found your answer?”

“Part of one.”

“Enough?”

“For tomorrow.”

She nodded.

Riarhin waited while he asked whether the older keepers still used a particular observance before lowering the basin. The owner said some did and some claimed they did. Saikou smiled as though he had been expecting precisely that distinction. He thanked her and they went on.

“You knew the mark,” Riarhin said.

“I knew what sort of boundary it belonged to.”

“But you asked to test it.”

“It could have been carried from another basin. Or copied. A good copy might answer much as the old one does.”

“Will you know?”

“I expect to establish enough to say which claim my evidence supports.”

He walked with his hands behind his back, looking up at the ropeways.

“The keeper’s mother’s mother knew it mattered,” he said. “That is often why there is still something to examine.”

Nalia, a few paces ahead, was arguing with Berent over whether the boat-woman’s price had been insulting. Berent said an insult at least should be free. Nalia told him he had lived a sheltered life.

He laughed loudly enough to turn a head at the next doorway.

Chapter 15 — What the Light Kept

The keeper had cleared half the table. The other half held the morning meal, a mended sandal and three bowls waiting to be carried downstairs. Her niece was trying to find the sandal’s fellow without admitting that she had moved it.

Saikou stood in the doorway until the keeper invited him across. Riarhin watched him glance at the bowl beneath the window, at the bronze inside it, and at the damp place where water had run over the rim.

“Did you wash it?”

“Last night.”

“What with?”

“Water.”

He waited.

“And lime.”

“Leave it in the bowl, please.”

The keeper’s hand stopped. He set his satchel down well away from the wet patch and turned towards the niece.

“Is there a shutter on that window?”

She closed it. The sharp bar of sunlight disappeared from the table. Water still shone beneath the bronze, but the faint gleam along its curved edge had gone.

Riarhin had taken the gleam for reflected light.

“Was it going to do something?” the keeper asked.

“It was beginning to answer the light. The lime has cleared part of the cut. I would rather you did not lift it while it was answering.”

Her hand withdrew to her lap.

Saikou asked for clean sand. When it came he poured it around the fitting, keeping his sleeve out of the bowl, and let the grains settle. Then he asked the keeper to open the shutter a finger’s breadth. A strip of morning crossed the wall. He moved the bowl into it with both hands against the dry underside.

Nothing shone.

“Now we can handle it.”

“You have seen one before,” the advocate said.

“Something built on the same principle. I have also seen a man pick one up by its bright edge.”

He left the bowl where it was while he and the keeper agreed on the words he would use. The niece found her sandal beneath a stool. She sat down to put it on and remained there, fastening and unfastening the strap.

The advocate wanted to know whether the fitting came from the basin. The keeper wanted to know whether it belonged to the work her grandmother had helped maintain. Saikou asked them to separate the questions.

“The work was altered. There may be pieces there from several repairs. I can begin with its connection to the sill we used yesterday.”

“And the age?”

“Look at the metal. Look at your papers. Those may serve us better.”

He unwrapped the mirror. Riarhin moved a bowl out of his way before he asked. His fingers touched the table beside hers in thanks, and then his attention was elsewhere.

The niece’s strap creaked; a seller called in the street; below, someone scraped the bottom of a pot. Riarhin kept hearing them while her eyes remained on the little bronze piece. Its green hollows held the light unevenly. There was an edge within the edge, a line she had not noticed when it lay among the papers. She followed it until her eyes hurt.

Saikou covered the mirror.

“Yes,” he said. “That sill.”

The niece let out her breath.

He asked for paper and wrote the question first. The advocate leaned over his shoulder. When he had finished the answer she made him read the whole thing aloud, including the words which said what he had not tested.

“Can you be mistaken?” she asked.

“Yes. Put your other evidence beside it.”

The keeper brought out the repair account. Her grandmother’s mark stood beside an entry for two lengths of bronze, one of which had been returned. The niece had made a copy in a larger hand. She put it next to the original and held the curled edge down with her sandal.

“The ink on this one will still be wet,” she said. “Don’t put them together.”

Saikou moved his own sheet aside. There was a pinprick of blood at one nostril. He wiped it before the keeper saw and folded the cloth into his palm.

Riarhin saw. When he reached for the second piece of paper she pulled it towards herself.

“I can copy that.”

“You can.”

He sat back. The keeper poured him something from the small pot near the hearth, and he drank it without asking its name.

The court met above the landing, where voices from the stairs came up through the floor. Two shutters were hooked open towards the water. Every few moments the ropeway’s shadow passed over one of them, and people at the back of the room shifted as though a bird had flown close.

Revati had brought the owners. Renu stood with her brother, whose bandaged hand rested in a sling. He had shaved badly that morning. A patch beneath his jaw had escaped him; Renu kept looking at it as if she would prefer to be at home putting him right.

The Admiralty officer sat beside a chest of papers. Riarhin recognised the clerk who had inventoried the lighter. A different man had come with them, thin and neatly dressed, carrying no weapon. Nalia saw him and stopped speaking in the middle of a sentence.

“Trouble?” Riarhin asked.

“He remembers things.”

“So do you.”

“Yes.”

The three people hearing the claim took their places. The eldest wore a cord around her wrist, frayed where it crossed a scar. She asked the owners to identify their goods before she would hear why anyone else should possess them.

A man who had lost two chests of household things could list every cooking pot and could not remember how many sheets his wife had packed. She supplied the number from the back. He corrected himself, reddening. Revati identified her grain by the seal on the bags. The road contractor gave the weight of his tools and the name of the smith who had made the larger picks. The smith’s son was present. He wanted it recorded that two of the picks had been made by him.

The elder allowed the correction.

When the officer spoke, his claim sounded better than it had at the store. The basin was closed to trade. The lighter had entered without permission. Its master had fled, and the goods had been recovered through assistance requested and paid for by the Admiralty. There were duties outstanding. The court, he suggested, was being asked to release property before any of those matters had been settled.

Revati began to answer. The elder lifted one finger. Revati closed her mouth with an effort Riarhin could see in her throat.

The thin man rose.

“The master did not choose that basin merely because he was pursued.”

He unfolded a paper. Nalia looked down at her hands.

“There is a separate landing charge at the outer quay. Boats carrying inland repair stores have sometimes been entered as household traffic to avoid it. The lighter’s declaration did not identify these tools.”

The road contractor shifted on his bench.

Riarhin looked at Nalia. Nalia was watching the paper now.

“Who entered it?” the elder asked.

“An agent employed by the carrier. It is a familiar arrangement.” He turned towards Nalia. “Some of those present have used it.”

Heat rose into Riarhin’s face. For a moment she thought of the door and the narrow stair beyond it, of how little room there was for an officer to draw. Nalia put two fingers on her sleeve.

Then she stood.

“A client of House Ira used the household rate for replacement fittings sent to a private landing. I prepared that declaration.”

The thin man inclined his head. His pleasure was small and unmistakable.

“Replacement fittings,” Nalia continued. “For a store which also served a house. The office accepted the entry. The same office later assessed road stone at the household rate because the receiving house owned the road. If you intend to collect the difference, bring both accounts.”

“Then you acknowledge the practice.”

“I have just described it.”

“Which enabled this cargo to enter a closed basin.”

“No. Those are two different entries. Give the court the second one.”

He hesitated. It was the first hesitation Riarhin had seen from him.

Nalia held out her hand. The elder told him to surrender the paper.

The permit for the basin was attached beneath the disputed declaration. It bore the harbor office’s mark. The officer said that permission covered unloading during repairs, not the resumption of ordinary traffic. Nalia asked which repairs. The road contractor started to speak and was silenced by a man at the end of his bench.

That man rose. He had sat so quietly that Riarhin had mistaken him for another owner. His coat was good and dusty. He introduced himself as the road-holder’s steward and produced a letter whose cord the elder examined before opening it.

“The tools are needed where the upper road has slipped,” he said. “Our carts are waiting above the break. Yours will be waiting below it in three days.”

The officer told him that urgency did not establish ownership.

“Ask the smith’s son.”

“I refer to the recovery claim.”

“The men you engaged lifted them out of the water. Pay the men. Send us the tools.”

Riarhin almost smiled. The steward did not look at her. He was watching the elder, and when she asked about the repair grant he answered in a language Riarhin could follow only in pieces. Nalia bent close and translated the parts that mattered. The road-holder would pay an assessed difference in duty under protest. He would not acknowledge Admiralty ownership of the landing or the road. He wanted the protest entered before the money changed hands.

The thin man began making notes.

The keeper’s evidence came after the permit.

She spoke without ceremony. Her grandmother had worked on the basin. Her mother had kept the account. The household had continued to supply people and materials after the outer stairs were rebuilt. The inner sill had remained in use; its maintenance had been paid for by the landing households, whose last contribution was written in the book Revati carried.

The officer asked whether the keeper herself had performed any of the work.

“No. I was helping my mother wash salt sores when she was too old to bend.”

He glanced towards the people hearing the claim.

“Then your knowledge is inherited.”

“So is the permit you brought.”

A laugh broke at the back of the room. The elder struck the table once. The keeper waited until the room was quiet and then identified the most recent repairer, who was sitting beside the door with plaster dust on his sleeve.

Saikou’s sheet was read. He answered three questions. On the fourth, the thin man asked whether the finding established that the keeper’s grandmother had owned the fitting.

“It does not.”

“Or the basin.”

“No.”

“Then how does it assist us?”

“The fitting belongs to the working at that sill. You proposed that it might have come from another site. It did not.”

“Yet you cannot tell us who made it.”

“The keeper’s evidence concerns who maintained it.”

The elder put his sheet beside the account. Saikou returned to his place.

Riarhin had been ready to speak about the water, the held wall and Leela disappearing beneath it. When she was called, they wanted to know where she had been standing when the cargo was unloaded, who had named the owners and whether she had heard anyone relinquish a claim. She answered. The elder thanked her and called Berent.

He got through his account without mentioning the rescue until she asked about the injured man. Then he described where Leela had brought him out and who had taken his weight.

“Could the goods have been recovered before him?”

“Some.”

“Why were they not?”

Berent looked towards Renu and her brother.

“He was breathing.”

The elder made a note.

They waited while the three conferred. Renu sat down at last. Her brother said something which made her slap his good shoulder; a moment later she rested her hand there and left it.

The ruling took longer to read than Riarhin expected. The goods were to be released to the named owners. A recovery payment was allowed, but the amount had to be settled against the actual labor supplied. The Admiralty’s duty claim could proceed separately. The permit remained evidence in that claim, as did Nalia’s acknowledgment of the earlier declaration. No right over the inland road was conceded.

The elder looked at the officer when she reached the sentence requiring release before the next tide.

He said he would need a copy.

“You will have one.”

“A witnessed copy.”

“Yes.”

“And authority from the Governor’s office to—”

The road-holder’s steward pushed his chair back.

“I can send my carts to the other landing. I would rather not. Which answer shall I carry?”

The officer looked at him. Somewhere below, a load struck the boards and a man shouted for help turning it.

“The store will be opened.”

Revati closed her eyes. Only for a moment. Then she began telling the owners who must go with her and who must fetch a cart.

Outside, Nalia stood against the wall while the others went down towards the landing. Her hands shook when she tried to refold her papers. She swore, softly and at length, and began again.

Riarhin waited.

“You knew him,” she said at last.

“I hoped he’d found better work.”

“Has he?”

“He seems well fed.”

Nalia pushed the papers into their case. There was a pale mark on her upper lip where she had been pressing it against her teeth.

“What happens to your client?”

“The account may be reopened. House Ira will want to know why.”

“And you?”

“I shall send them the hearing.” She looked towards the landing. “All of it. Let someone earn the trouble of reading it.”

Below them, the first grain bags were coming out. Revati inspected each seal. The smith’s son had found one of his picks and was making the contractor admire its head. Renu and her brother had gone home.

The keeper waited at the turn of the stair with her niece. Saikou returned the wrapped bronze. She checked the knot, tucked it beneath her arm and invited him to eat before he went. Her niece interrupted to ask whether he knew anything about the navigation examination.

“Very little,” he said. “I have known several excellent navigators. None recommended my interference.”

The girl grinned. She took the advocate’s case so the older woman could use the rail.

Nalia watched them descend, then let out a long breath.

“Come on. If we leave Revati to count alone, she’ll stay until dark.”

At the store the officer had set up a table. He checked each owner’s name against the witnessed copy and entered the goods as they passed him. He made Revati wait while he sharpened his pen. She stood with one hand on the next sack and looked out towards the water.

When he finished, she gave the bag to Berent.

“Mind the bottom.”

Berent turned it, found the frayed seam and put his arm beneath it. Riarhin took the bundle he had left by the door and tucked it beneath her arm.

Chapter 16 — The Road Inland

Ailsa came home with a length of cloth draped over her shoulder and two women following her into the courtyard. One carried a basket. The other had a piece of thread between her teeth and was speaking around it with considerable force.

Riarhin moved her feet off the stool.

“No,” Ailsa said. “Stay there. I want your sleeve.”

She laid the cloth across Riarhin’s arm. It was finer than it looked, rough for an instant against the skin and then cool. The woman with the thread pinched it near the elbow. Ailsa turned Riarhin’s wrist, her thumb passing over the pale scar.

“Here. If it’s cut as she says, it pulls when you reach.”

The other woman took the thread from her mouth.

“It pulls on her because she sits like a bent nail.”

“I have paid to be insulted before,” Riarhin said. “Never this early.”

Ailsa laughed. The woman examined Riarhin’s shoulder and conceded an alteration. They argued over how much cloth it would take. Riarhin sat with her arm extended, listening to figures she had no reason to remember and enjoying the sensation.

The women stayed for a drink. Ailsa had samples in the house, and she fetched them while they discussed a buyer who ordered everything urgently and paid as though the year were his own invention. Savi came out to ask how many would be eating. One of the visitors began to excuse herself. Savi looked at the basket she had brought and told her to stop being foolish.

When they had gone, Ailsa put the cloth away herself.

“An order?” Riarhin asked.

“If the samples suit. Work coats for a storehouse. They tear at the shoulder. The woman who buys them is tired of replacing whole coats for want of a finger’s breadth.”

“Can you do it?”

“I can make six. Then I’ll know whether I want sixty.”

She held a paper out of reach of the boy passing behind her with wet hands. He made a face and went round the other side of the table.

“Your father wants you,” she said.

“He always wants me when I’ve nearly finished something.”

“You have nearly finished washing your hands. Be generous.”

He grinned despite himself. Riarhin watched him go through the courtyard towards the covered work space. His father had taken the shutters off one of the unused rooms and was showing a local carpenter where the lower wood had swelled. They had been at it since morning. Neither man looked inclined to finish.

“They’ve asked him to stay on,” Ailsa said. “There are rooms above a workshop nearer the quay.”

“Will he?”

“He’s looking at them tomorrow.”

The boy bent over the shutter. His father moved a chisel out of his way without breaking off his conversation.

“I thought he might want to take them home,” Riarhin said.

“So did I.”

Ailsa gathered the cups. Riarhin took two and followed her inside.

Aurine had spread her belongings across the floor in the smallest room. They made less of a pile than Riarhin expected: spare clothes, a roll of instruments, papers tied between thin boards, several little packets whose contents she could smell from the door.

“Come in,” Aurine said. “Nothing open will kill you.”

“That sounds particular.”

“It is.”

Riarhin stepped over the clothes. Aurine remained on the floor, sorting leaves by touch as much as sight. She looked tired. There was a raw place on the side of one finger where a thread had cut it.

“Renu’s brother?”

“Sleeping. Complaining when awake. He’ll keep the hand if he leaves the dressing alone.”

“Will he?”

“His sister has opinions.”

Riarhin leaned against the wall. The room faced the yard where water was heated, and a smell of smoke came through the shutter. Aurine selected one packet, frowned at it and set it aside.

“We said we’d speak when we landed,” Riarhin said.

“We did.”

“I’ve kept finding other things to do.”

“I noticed.”

Aurine put the leaves down. Riarhin wished she had continued. She had been able to watch the hands and approach the conversation sideways; now there was only the woman sitting on the floor, waiting to be told whether she was wanted.

“Do you have work here?”

“An offer. Two mornings at a household near the lower quay. Their old physician cannot manage the stairs. He would like someone to climb them and then come down to be told what she overlooked.”

“Sounds pleasant.”

“He is good. I could learn a great deal.”

Riarhin looked at the packed instruments.

“Would you rather?”

Aurine drew one knee up and rested her wrist on it.

“I want to come.”

“Why?”

“I haven’t seen the mountains.”

Riarhin waited for more. Aurine’s mouth tightened, and she looked suddenly younger than she generally allowed herself to look.

“I know that will not recommend me to you as a necessary expense.”

“You’re paid separately from the guides. Nalia’s arranging the account.”

Aurine’s gaze came back to her.

“The same terms,” Riarhin said. “If Nakato writes about us, I see it before you answer. If you choose her work, you tell me before you do it where you can. You don’t get to decide that I’ll thank you afterwards.”

“I remember.”

“So do I.”

The words came out harder than she had intended. Aurine lowered her eyes to the leaf packets.

Riarhin pushed away from the wall. She could have left then. The terms were clear, and she had said nothing she would have to qualify later. Instead she crouched, slowly enough that Aurine had time to move a bundle from beneath her foot.

“What’s wrong with that one?”

“It was dried too hot.”

“Can you use it?”

“For something less demanding.”

Aurine held it out. Riarhin rubbed a leaf between her right thumb and finger. There was hardly any scent.

“The good one is there,” Aurine said.

She tried it. The bitterness reached the back of her nose at once.

“You can tell before you crush it?”

“Usually.”

“Show me.”

Aurine took a leaf from each packet and laid them on Riarhin’s palm.

They went down to the shore in the late afternoon. Savi had told them which stretch was safe at that tide and where the sand hid a drainage cut. Ailsa repeated the warning twice. Her daughter repeated it to Berent, who solemnly thanked her and asked whether there were any other rules he should know.

She supplied several.

The eldest carried the food and complained that this prevented him from carrying anything interesting. His father offered to exchange the basket for the jar of water. After testing its weight the boy retained the basket.

There were other households along the shore. Children had built a wall of wet sand around a crab and were negotiating the conditions under which it might leave. Two men sat mending a net, close enough to the water that each arriving wave darkened the ground beneath their heels. Farther out, a boat held almost still while a woman standing in it took soundings with a knotted line.

Riarhin removed her boots. The water was warm at the edge and cooler a few steps out. She had forgotten how much she liked standing in the sea when nobody was trying to get her into a boat.

Ailsa came beside her, gathering her hem.

“You’ll ruin that,” Riarhin said.

“I know how to wash it.”

The water reached their calves. Ailsa’s hand found hers. Behind them Berent was denying that the girl had beaten him in a race, on the grounds that she had begun before agreeing to race at all. Cadan was asked to judge. He said the result seemed clear. Berent told him that was an ugly betrayal after all they had been through.

Saikou sat on a folded cloth above the wet line. The boy brought him a shell. He examined it, held it to his ear and returned it with an expression of grave dissatisfaction.

“It is still the sea,” he said.

“What did you expect?”

“A change.”

The boy went to find him another.

Riarhin leaned against Ailsa’s shoulder. They stood long enough for the water to dig the sand from under their heels.

“I shall miss this,” she said.

“We’ve been here half an hour.”

“I’m trying to be affecting.”

“You are.”

Ailsa squeezed her hand. Neither of them moved when the next wave came.

They ate above the tide line. Iona had brought more food than anyone could have needed and explained that this was because she had seen Berent at breakfast. He said a man had to recover from disappointment somehow. The girl asked what had disappointed him. He looked at the portion she was putting on his plate and said things were improving.

The children’s father opened the water jar. He passed Riarhin a cup and asked about the road. She told him what Nalia had told her: boat passage first, then the inland routes, with the season deciding more than their preference. He listened without pretending he knew the country. When the children called him he gave the jar to Ailsa and went to see what they had found.

Riarhin watched him lift the girl over a runnel. She had crossed it herself twice already. This time she held up her arms.

Ailsa’s shoulder touched hers.

“Here,” she said, offering another piece of bread.

Nalia arrived after supper with the final account and a bruise on her forearm where a packing case had fallen against her. She would not sit until Riarhin had agreed that the road money belonged in a separate purse.

“If I have to ask you for every ferry payment, we’ll reach the mountains old.”

“You’ll reach them old. I may be fortunate.”

“You’ll reach them with no luggage.”

They settled the amount. Nalia’s fee covered her own work. Translators, guides, animals and local assistance would be paid in addition, and she would name a cost before committing them whenever time allowed. The papers gave her no authority over the object they carried. Riarhin insisted on that sentence. Nalia read it twice and changed nothing.

“House Ira?” Riarhin asked.

“Will receive the coastal account. Nothing in this engagement makes your inland business theirs.”

“Will that suit them?”

“They may employ somebody else if it does not.”

Saikou, reading at the other end of the table, looked up. Nalia met his gaze. After a moment he returned to the page.

She had chosen to come. Riarhin felt a brief and unreasonable resentment at how much easier that made the journey.

The road-holder’s steward had sent an introduction with the released tools. Their first boat would carry part of that cargo upriver. The same document named the company as witnesses to the coastal recovery. Nalia tapped that passage.

“The duty claim will follow. Keep the hearing with it.”

“How far?”

“As far as somebody finds it useful.”

She gathered the papers and left them the departure hour.

Saikou stayed at the table. There were two letters beside him, both addressed. Riarhin recognised Nakato’s name on one. He saw her looking and moved it towards her.

“She will know we are going inland.”

“Will she know where?”

“The route we have bought. I have not sent her a temple plan.”

Riarhin read it. He had described the estate, the work at the basin and the pending dispute. The account of Ailsa was short. The account of the house’s water arrangements was longer. At the end he asked for copies of two records whose titles she did not recognise.

“Will you tell me if those come?”

“Yes.”

She returned the letter. He folded it along its existing creases.

“Are you afraid of this place?” she asked.

“The mountains?”

“What we’re going to find.”

He rested his hands on the table. Beyond the door somebody was shaking out a blanket. Dust showed for a moment in the lamp’s reach.

“I have entered places more dangerous than they appeared,” he said. “I have also been frightened by things which proved quite ordinary. The first sight of them has usually told me which precautions to take. It has seldom told me everything.”

“And this one?”

“I expect a working built to refuse us. We shall prepare for one.”

There was comfort in the steadiness of his voice. She did not resent taking it.

Ailsa had put the yellow glass bird on the shelf beside the bed. Its great feet were partly hidden by a folded cloth. Riarhin lifted it and held it towards the lamp. Light went through the body and left a golden stain across her wrist.

“It’s still ugly.”

“You bought passage for it.”

“I was overruled.”

“Put it down before you begin liking it.”

She did. Ailsa closed the door.

They undressed without talking about the morning. Ailsa caught the hem of Riarhin’s shirt, and Riarhin lifted both arms to let her take it off. Ailsa’s hand lingered over the scar at her side. Riarhin put her left hand at the back of Ailsa’s neck and drew her close.

Later she lay with her cheek against Ailsa’s shoulder. Somewhere outside, the boy laughed in his sleep. A door moved. The house settled around the ordinary noises of people who expected to wake there.

“Where do I write?” Riarhin asked.

“Here.”

“If you leave?”

“Savi will know where to send it. If she leaves, she will make the next person know. We’ve discussed it.”

“Of course you have.”

Ailsa stroked her hair. Her hand slowed and rested.

“Come back to me,” she said.

Riarhin opened her mouth. She had promised things before. Some had been small enough to keep, and some had been made because the person before her needed to hear them. Ailsa’s fingers moved once against her scalp.

“I want to.”

“I know. Come back.”

Riarhin held her until the lamp went out.

At the landing, the boatman was more interested in the tools than the six passengers. He made Berent shift a bundle, inspected the boards beneath it and called for another mat. Cadan brought one. The boatman took it without thanks and immediately asked him to fetch a second.

Ailsa stood with the children above the steps. Her daughter had given Riarhin a shell, then explained that it must be returned because it belonged to a collection. Riarhin had agreed to the loan. The shell lay in the pocket opposite the linen bundle, hard against the seam.

Saikou went aboard. Aurine followed him, stepping over the load to the place Nalia indicated. Berent offered Riarhin a hand. She took it this time and sat beside him while the boatman checked the line.

Ailsa came down one step. Riarhin stood again, to the boatman’s annoyance, and reached up. Their fingers met. Ailsa pressed her hand hard enough to hurt.

Then the boat moved, and they let go.

The girl waved with both arms. Her brother lifted one hand, suddenly solemn. Ailsa remained on the step until the bend took the landing from sight.

Riarhin sat down. Berent gave her room without making an occasion of it. Above the cargo, Nalia was explaining to the boatman why the papers must stay dry. He told her that if she knew a river without water in it he would gladly use that one.

Saikou laughed.

Riarhin kept her face turned towards the bend a little longer. Then she took off her coat and folded it beneath her arm.

Part III — The Season of Rain

Chapter 17 — The River Road

On the fourth morning the boatman took away the cup Riarhin had been using and gave her another.

“That one goes home here.”

She looked at the cup. There was a chip in its rim, a brown glaze inside it and nothing else that suggested an attachment to this particular landing. He had already turned away. A girl on the steps caught the line he threw and made it fast above the wet mark.

“The woman who lent it wants it back,” Berent said.

“I understood him.”

“I thought you were waiting for it to fly.”

Riarhin drank from the replacement. It tasted of warm clay. On the landing a woman came out from beneath an awning, counted the cups the boatman was passing up and pointed at the one still in Saikou’s hand. He surrendered it. She brought them a pot in exchange, wrapped in cloth and hot enough to require both hands beneath it.

They ate while the tools were unloaded. The porridge was thinner than at the coast, with something sour stirred through it which woke the mouth. Cadan scraped his bowl and asked whether there was more. The woman held out the pot. He served Berent before himself, carefully equalising the portions until Berent told him that generosity ceased to be pleasant when it involved so much inspection.

The road contractor’s men were waiting with a cart. They had brought replacement food for the onward passage and a rolled mat for the boatman’s daughter. She unrolled it immediately, though her father had told her to put it under the awning. A pattern of yellow fish appeared across the boards. She knelt on it and ran a finger along the largest.

The river beyond her was wider here. Low banks showed between fields, and the houses stood back among trees whose roots held the earth in ridges. Riarhin had seen cattle on those banks the evening before. Now a man was moving them farther inland. The smallest animal kept breaking away to crop the fresh growth beside the path; each time he returned for it, the rest stopped too.

Nalia finished her food standing. She and the contractor’s men checked the papers against the tools. They spoke more quickly than Riarhin could follow. She caught the words for iron, broken ground and a place upstream, then a form of address which made one of the men glance towards the house.

Nalia paused.

“Say that again.”

He did, with emphasis.

She looked at the boatman. He wiped his bowl with a piece of bread and answered at length. The man from the road shook his head before he had finished.

“What is it?” Riarhin asked.

“I think he is saying our letter provides a place in the renewal. He may mean it provides a right to ask for a place. I would prefer not to arrive announcing the wrong one.”

“You don’t know?”

“I know what those words would mean at home.”

The woman who owned the cups came over. She listened, made the man repeat the passage a third time, and took the paper out of his hand. Her forefinger followed the writing without touching it. When she had finished she told Nalia something in the coast’s trade speech.

Nalia thanked her and asked her name.

“Anju.”

“Do you know who can introduce us there?”

Anju looked at the loaded boat and then at the cart.

“I am going. You can pay for my bundle.”

The boatman objected. The river had a shallower reach ahead; the bundle, he said, would contain the whole house. Anju told her daughter to fetch it. It proved to be small enough to fit beneath Riarhin’s knees.

Her daughter rolled the fish mat again, reluctantly. Anju touched the back of her head as she passed and gave her a succession of instructions. The girl listened with the air of someone who had already received them more than once. At the last, she looked up sharply and asked a question.

Anju answered too softly for Riarhin to hear.

The girl carried the empty pot inside.

They left the deep river after midday.

The next boat was broader, with a patched shade stretched over hoops and a flat space at the front where the ferryman could work his pole. His nephew tried to help Berent down the steps while he was carrying a bundle. Berent caught the young man’s wrist before they could pull each other off balance.

“Wait.”

The nephew let go.

“Take this end. There. Together.”

They brought the bundle down between them. Berent stepped aboard and lowered it without difficulty. The nephew offered him a place beneath the shade, away from the loading.

“I’ll sit where I can see the load.”

“It’s tied.”

“The middle one is.”

The young man looked. A bundle of picks lay against the side, held by a line which passed beneath another package. He pulled it. It came loose.

Berent took the end and showed him where to lead it. When they pushed off, the nephew was sitting opposite him, asking whether a loop would be better than a turn. The lesson became an argument. Berent let the boat drift while the nephew demonstrated.

Anju took the place nearest the front. Her bundle had a wet patch where water from the steps had reached it. She opened the wrapping, inspected the little covered vessels inside and moved one into the sun.

“Seed?” Riarhin asked.

“Some.”

“For sale?”

“For showing.”

She lifted a lid. Pale grains lay in the hollow of the vessel, each selected so carefully that Riarhin felt clumsy merely looking at them. Anju picked out one with a dark mark and put it in her mouth.

“That one shouldn’t have been there.”

“What are you showing?”

“The lower fields still bear. People who have not walked them say otherwise.”

She replaced the lid and covered the vessels. Riarhin waited, but Anju settled back beneath the shade and closed her eyes.

The boat moved between banks so close that leaves brushed the cloth above them. The air smelled of mud warmed through. Insects gathered in the folds of the shade. Aurine took a piece of cloth from her bag and tied it over the back of her neck; within a short time everyone except the ferryman had copied her.

At a fork, the nephew began moving the pole to the other side. His uncle spoke one word. He stopped.

The open branch ahead was broad and nearly still. The route the ferryman chose bent away beneath low trees, with a fallen trunk visible in the water farther up. Nalia asked about the open channel. They had been told it would bring them to the overnight landing before dark.

“It would,” the ferryman said.

“Can this boat pass?”

“It can.”

He pushed them into the narrower branch. Nalia watched the fallen tree grow closer. Then she asked what the difficulty was.

“The boat comes here with a load. It goes back through there empty. That is how we cross.”

“We could pay the difference.”

He looked at her with real annoyance.

“You have paid for the journey.”

Saikou, who had been watching the open water, leaned forward.

“Keep your hand inside,” he told the nephew.

The young man snatched it from the side. A trailing cord had fallen into the water. It drew straight beneath the surface, though the boat was almost still, and began to tighten around the peg where it was fastened.

The ferryman set his pole. Saikou placed two fingers on the nephew’s forearm before he could seize the cord.

“Leave it until he has turned us.”

The boat’s bow moved. For a moment Riarhin saw the open channel past Saikou’s shoulder: a strip of white sky in the water, perfectly clear, with none of the leaves floating on the rest of the river. The ferryman thrust again. They slid beneath the trees. The cord loosened and floated beside them.

The nephew hauled it in. He examined the wet end, then tucked it under his foot.

“Does that happen on the empty crossing?” Saikou asked.

“No.”

“Even at this water?”

“Even then.”

“How far does it follow if you take the wrong branch?”

“To the old steps. My brother thought he could unload there and make the return count.”

The ferryman’s attention remained on the trunk ahead. He called to his nephew. Between them they brought the boat close enough to the bank for the younger man to step out with a rope.

“What happened?” Nalia asked.

“He lost the load. Kept the boat. That was generous, my mother said.”

Nalia looked down at the account in her lap. She closed it.

Riarhin glanced at Saikou. He was watching the marks along the bank now, following something she could not distinguish among the roots.

“You knew,” she said quietly.

“I knew the line was being taken against the current.”

“By what?”

“I would begin with what the ferryman’s mother told him. After we have put his cargo ashore.”

He stood to help ease the shade beneath the fallen trunk. Riarhin bent with the others. Leaves dragged across her hair; water dripped down the back of her neck. Berent swore as a branch caught his sleeve. The nephew, on the bank, laughed and pulled harder.

They came out into sunlight.

The landing where they slept belonged to Anju’s cousin. He heard her call before he saw the boat and came down with a lamp, still chewing his supper. Anju spoke to him while the baggage came ashore. He looked at the company, asked Nalia a question, and shouted for someone to bring another mat.

There was room beneath the roof beside the house. A screen divided it from the yard; behind the screen, somebody was washing a child who objected to having each part of his body cleaned as though the objection were new. Riarhin sat on the outer step and pulled a thorn from the hem of her coat.

The meal came in bowls too shallow for her to hold comfortably. She watched Anju shape a mouthful with her fingers and tried it. Half of it fell back into the bowl.

“You’re putting your hand where your mouth should be,” Anju said.

“That sounds like a larger failing.”

Anju demonstrated again. Riarhin tried with less food. This time it worked.

Across the mat, Berent had solved the problem by finding bread. Saikou ate easily with his right hand. His left rested beside his knee, the fingers occasionally moving as if they expected something to be placed in them.

The washed child emerged in a clean garment and immediately sat in the dirt. His mother made a sound of despair so familiar that Riarhin laughed before she could help it. The woman looked at her and laughed too.

After the food, Anju opened her vessels for her cousin. He listened to the account of the lower fields without interrupting until she came to the last one. Then he asked whether she had been given a place.

“I have the household’s mark.”

“That is not what I asked.”

She wrapped the vessels.

“The holder was alive when we sent the contribution. They accepted it.”

He rubbed his mouth. Nalia asked something carefully, using the phrase they had discussed at the earlier landing. Anju’s cousin answered, stopped, and began again in slower trade speech.

The grant at Darava was being renewed. The late holder’s kin disagreed over who should receive it. Until the renewal, certain households were barred from speaking in the sanctuary’s assembly. Anju’s had helped open a channel without the holder’s order.

“The water was already in our houses,” she said.

Her cousin lifted his hands. He did not dispute it. Riarhin saw that this made Anju angrier.

“What will they do if you go?” she asked.

“Tell me I should have sent someone.”

“Who?”

Anju looked at the vessels between them.

“My husband is dead. My girl is at home. I have come.”

The child wandered over to touch one of the lids. Anju caught his hand and set him on her lap. He leaned back against her as though the argument had been intended to arrange a comfortable place for him.

Later, as they spread their bedding, Riarhin went to Nalia.

“Can our introduction get her heard?”

“I will ask.”

“Will you?”

Nalia stopped unfolding her blanket.

“Yes. I will also ask what claim I would be making by bringing her beside us. We’ve been travelling with her since breakfast.”

Riarhin looked towards the house. Anju was helping her cousin cover the remaining food.

“She came to help us.”

“She was going to Darava. We paid for her bundle.”

She lay down with the coat beneath her head and listened to the household put itself to bed. Beyond the roof, the river moved in the dark. Once she heard the ferryman speaking to his nephew, the low patient voice of a man explaining a mistake they had both survived.

They reached Darava by road. The boat had gone as far as it could with the water so low, and the cart they hired at the last landing had one wheel repaired in a different wood. Berent inspected it, accepted the repair and insisted on having the axle greased before they loaded. The driver argued until he saw the coin in Nalia’s hand.

Anju walked beside the cart for the last stretch. People greeted her. One woman came out of a doorway, embraced her and began talking before she released her. Two men sitting beneath a tree watched the exchange and looked away when Anju turned towards them.

The sanctuary roof showed above the houses. Scaffolding covered one corner. Men were passing fresh plaster from hand to hand while an older woman below shouted advice which none of them appeared to welcome. From somewhere beyond the wall came the uneven beat of a drum being tested.

Nalia took their introduction to the outer court. Riarhin waited beneath an awning with Saikou, watching a clerk turn the steward’s letter over.

His gaze stopped at the mark. He brought the cloth closer.

Then he reached for another paper.

The coastal copy had travelled with a merchant bringing accounts for the renewal. It had arrived that morning, while they were still greasing the axle. The clerk laid the two papers side by side and sent a boy through the inner door.

Nalia asked whether there was a difficulty.

“The chamberlain will receive you tomorrow.”

“And our onward passage?”

“He will receive you tomorrow.”

Riarhin looked out through the gate. Anju stood beside the cart, her bundle in both hands. The two men from the tree had followed them. One spoke to the gatekeeper, who shook his head before the question was finished.

Anju lifted the bundle a little, as though its contents could be seen through the cloth.

The gatekeeper looked away.

Chapter 18 — Two Seats Prepared

The chamberlain had prepared two seats at the same height. One stood nearer the inner door. A servant moved it a hand’s breadth away, considered the result, then moved the other towards the window.

Riarhin watched from the visitors’ bench. Nalia was beside her, wearing the coat she had kept wrapped throughout the journey. Saikou sat at the end where he could see the courtyard. He had declined a place closer to the chamberlain, which Riarhin thought wise. The nearer benches already had people standing over them, explaining whose aunt had sat there at the last renewal.

The chamberlain came in carrying a roll of paper. He was a broad man with a close grey beard, and he had shaved a narrow place beneath his chin so badly that a little red patch showed when he lifted his head. He looked at the seats.

“Put them where they were.”

The servant obeyed.

“Before that.”

The servant moved them again. This time the chamberlain let him leave.

A woman entered first, walking quickly enough that the man behind her had difficulty keeping the shade over her head. She stopped beneath the roof and told him to go and drink. He lowered the shade with visible relief.

“Devaki,” Nalia murmured.

She wore a plain outer cloth over good work beneath it. There was wet dust on the hem. When she sat, she drew the fabric clear of the floor with a practiced turn of her wrist. Her gaze crossed the room, stopped briefly on Riarhin’s ears, and moved on.

Arun arrived with his mother. He was younger than Riarhin expected, though no boy: a man with the beginnings of heaviness at the jaw and an expression sharpened by insufficient sleep. His mother touched his sleeve before he sat. He looked down, saw that one fastening had come loose and secured it.

Devaki smiled at her.

“You should have sent him to bed.”

“I did. Perhaps you would like the task tonight.”

Arun sat very carefully. Several people laughed. The chamberlain waited until they had finished, then began.

The opening named the authority under which they met. Ahimsa’s precedence appeared early and was repeated in a form Nalia translated differently the second time. A man behind Arun objected to the change. The chamberlain answered without consulting the paper. The man objected again, more quietly, and asked that his reservation be entered.

Riarhin shifted on the bench. Which of these disputes had begun in another room, a generation earlier?

Arun’s mother supplied the descent. Her voice was clear, almost conversational. She named the late holder as her brother, then their mother and their mother’s mother, and identified the household in which each renewal had been witnessed. A clerk read a matching passage from a book. No one challenged it.

Riarhin leaned towards Nalia.

“Then it’s his.”

“It makes him the first claimant.”

“What else does he need?”

Nalia inclined her head towards the courtyard.

“The people who will have to open the water for him.”

Devaki spoke next. She did not dispute the names. She described a season in which Arun’s uncle had been away, a broken retaining wall and the money her household had borrowed to repair it. The chamberlain tried to move her to the most recent account. She continued through the intervening years, looking directly at Arun while she did so.

At last he stood.

“I know who paid for the wall.”

“Then you know why I will not give the debt to my daughters and the grant to you.”

“I offered to assume the debt.”

“Through a collector who proposed to recover it from the lower fields.”

“They use the water.”

A woman on the far bench struck her palm against her knee. Arun turned towards her, already angry, and his mother caught the loose edge of his sleeve.

He stopped. Riarhin saw the effort it cost him.

“They use it,” he repeated, more evenly. “The upper villages paid when the south gate failed. The next repair cannot always belong to the people who happen to be standing nearest it.”

Devaki looked away. For the first time she seemed to be listening to him rather than waiting for him to finish.

The sanctuary custodian rose from beside the wall. She was small and old, with a fresh white scar at the base of her thumb. She held nothing in her hands.

“The renewal binds a holder to the work,” she said. “We are still waiting to hear which work each of you undertakes.”

“The grant states it,” the chamberlain said.

“The copy states part of it.”

He pressed the roll flat beneath his hand.

“We have discussed the condition of the original.”

“And we shall discuss it again.”

They were given food during the adjournment. Riarhin took a plate into the courtyard, where a tree grew through an opening in the paving and the air moved more freely. Arun stood beyond it with his mother. She was telling him something he plainly did not wish to hear. He took a mouthful at the wrong moment, and she waited until he had swallowed before continuing.

Devaki came out alone. She accepted a cup from a servant, tasted it and called him back.

“Who made this?”

He looked alarmed.

“It’s good. Bring another for the woman by the door. She has been watching everyone drink for an hour.”

The servant went. Devaki joined the custodian in the shade.

Riarhin looked through the outer gate. Anju was there.

She had brought her vessels again. One stood open beside her on the step, its pale contents shielded by the shadow of her hand. A young clerk listened while she spoke. He seemed neither hostile nor persuaded. When she finished he returned the little household mark she had shown him.

Riarhin set down her plate.

Nalia caught up with her before the gate.

“I have asked.”

“And?”

“They will accept her written account through an admitted household.”

“She can speak perfectly well.”

“Yes.”

“Then why—”

“Because her household is under exclusion. I have asked about that too.”

Riarhin went down the steps. Anju covered the vessel when she saw her.

“Are you being fed?” she asked.

“Come and eat.”

“I have food.”

“It’s cooler inside.”

Anju glanced towards the gatekeeper. He had heard them. He moved to one side of the opening, leaving enough room for Riarhin to pass and no invitation for anyone else.

“They’ll take an account,” Riarhin said.

“Through another household. I know.”

“Devaki?”

Anju’s face changed.

“Her men shut the gate.”

“Arun, then.”

“He wants to count our fields as land newly watered by his grant. My husband’s father planted there.”

Riarhin looked at the vessel between them. She understood, suddenly, why Anju had brought the grains. Not proof that land existed, which no one doubted. Proof of what had lived there before the proposed generosity.

“Who will speak for you?”

“My cousin has asked a household upriver. They have not answered.”

“I can ask in there.”

Anju shook her head so hard that a strand of hair escaped its fastening.

“You ask for yourself. If they give you something, do not offer us in payment.”

Riarhin wanted Anju to know she had meant to help. She looked down at her empty hands.

“I won’t.”

Anju studied her. Then she took a little parcel of food from her bundle and held it out.

“For the road,” she said.

“We may not be leaving.”

“Then for waiting.”

The chamberlain received them after the others had gone. He had removed the outer layer of his clothing, and the damp line beneath his collar made him look tired rather than imposing. The coast’s allegation lay open on the table.

Nalia put the complete hearing beside it.

He read slowly. Riarhin could hear children in the courtyard beyond the wall, arguing over something that rolled. A hard object struck the paving. Someone shouted an accusation. The chamberlain paused with his finger on the paper, listened until the argument subsided, and continued.

When he finished, he tapped the road-holder’s introduction.

“He may recommend you. He cannot pledge the passage stores of this grant.”

“We have money,” Nalia said.

“You have money for hire. The upper carriers want a household answerable for the animals and the return. Until the renewal, neither claimant can undertake that liability for the whole district.”

“Could we engage them separately?”

“You may ask. You will be asking families who disagree over the same renewal.”

Riarhin leaned forward.

“Does the Admiralty’s claim matter here?”

“It matters if you leave obligations behind you and use our assurance to incur more.”

“The goods were returned.”

“The duty claim remains.”

“We didn’t own the goods.”

“Then it should be possible to establish what you did owe.”

She began to answer, but Nalia placed the separate account of their engagement before him. His eyebrows lifted. He read it, and the irritation in Riarhin’s chest subsided a little. He was willing to look. She had been preparing to fight a refusal which had not yet been made.

Saikou waited until the chamberlain set the account down.

“The older grant is in the tomb?”

A muscle moved beside the man’s jaw.

“A record of its original terms. There are later additions here.”

“Why has it not been copied in full?”

“It was. Water entered the archive during my predecessor’s service. The surviving copy lacks the lower part. The inscription is in a chamber which has remained closed since a death there.”

“Whose death?”

“A repairer’s.”

“During an attempt to enter?”

“During an attempt to open what should have been an ordinary service door.”

Saikou’s expression sharpened. He asked where the man had stood, what had happened to the people behind him and whether anything had been taken out afterwards. The chamberlain could answer the second question and part of the third. He sent for a clerk who had been present.

Riarhin watched Saikou listen. He did not reach for the mirror. When the clerk finished, he asked for a drawing of the approach and told him to include the place where the first workman had fallen, even if it lay outside the door’s proper name.

“I would need to examine it,” he said. “With whoever keeps the place.”

The chamberlain rested his forearms on the table.

“That could be arranged.”

“And the families entitled to refuse us,” Saikou added.

The chamberlain sat back.

Outside, the hard object struck the paving again. This time it was followed by breaking pottery and a silence so complete that Riarhin almost laughed.

“If you can obtain an accurate copy,” the chamberlain said, “we could decide the renewal. Your introductions could proceed with it.”

“What do you expect the copy to say?” Riarhin asked.

He looked at her.

“I expect to have something I can read to both of them.”

They found Anju at the house where she had taken a room. The owner knew her cousin and had given her a place beneath the stairs. Her vessels stood on the lowest step. The household’s children had been warned away from them so thoroughly that they sat across the yard watching them with fascination.

Riarhin told her about the tomb.

Anju listened, then asked whether the lower fields appeared in the surviving copy. Nalia said that she had not seen enough of it to know.

“They will be called the reed holding,” Anju said. “Before my husband’s grandfather’s time, perhaps they were. If you hear that name, ask where it lies.”

“I will.”

“Write it.”

Nalia took out her paper. Anju watched until the words were down.

The house’s owner appeared with a child under one arm and a broken pot in the other. It had been the outer courtyard next to the chamberlain’s rooms; the boy had sent his rolling wheel through a gap in the wall. The owner wanted to know who had promised him a replacement before she decided how angry to be.

Anju drew the child onto her lap. He was too old to sit there comfortably and too frightened to object.

“Did you apologise?”

“Yes.”

“Did they hear?”

He considered this.

“Go again.”

He slid down and went. Anju watched him reach the door, then leaned back against the stair.

Away from the gatekeeper, Anju let her shoulders sag. She rubbed her eyes with the heel of one hand. Riarhin sat on the step above the vessels. She unwrapped the food Anju had given her and offered half back.

For a while they ate without discussing what either could do.

Chapter 19 — Mira’s Rehearsal

Mira stopped the drum with one raised hand.

The last beat remained in the room longer than it should have. Riarhin felt it in the boards beneath her feet, a dull pressure which rose through her soles and settled behind her ribs. One of the younger dancers broke her stance too soon. Mira looked at her. She put her foot back and waited.

“Who moved the meal?”

The organiser standing beside the door smiled as though the question might yet become a pleasantry.

“The guests are being received in the lower court.”

“I know where the guests are. Where is our meal?”

“The same kitchen serves—”

“Then it had better serve.”

He lowered his voice. “We cannot keep the delegation waiting.”

“You can tell them I am keeping them waiting.”

The young dancer’s mouth twitched. Mira saw that too, and this time her own mouth moved a little in answer.

Riarhin stood out of the way while the organiser went through a small door at the back. The performance room opened onto a yard with a covered passage on two sides. Cloths hung between the posts to soften the light. A painter crouched beneath one, renewing a border in a colour so bright that it seemed wet even where it had dried. Beyond him, a boy was polishing the small golden elephant which would be carried at the rain observance. He kept turning it to admire his progress and finding another fingerprint.

The dancers relaxed. Someone sat down too quickly and groaned. The drummer rubbed a thumb along the edge of his drum and watched the door through which the organiser had gone.

Mira came over. She was older than the dancers around her, with strong feet, a broad mouth and a narrow white line across one eyebrow. She wore no ornament that could catch during the movement. Riarhin had seen her step backwards through a complicated turn without once looking at the floor.

“You are the people asking about the tomb.”

“We are,” Nalia said.

“Then you will be disappointed here. Everyone is alive.”

“For now,” said the drummer.

“Eat when it comes and you may remain so.”

He bowed from where he sat. The others laughed. Mira took the introduction Nalia offered, read it and handed it back.

“The custodian is at the south gate. She’ll return before evening. If you have come to watch, sit where you can keep your feet out of the dancers’ way. If you have come for a blessing, ask someone who has eaten.”

Saikou was looking towards a shallow basin at the far end of the room. Three small lamps stood beside it. Their flames leaned towards the water, though the cloths at the open doorway moved the other way.

Mira followed his gaze.

“You have seen that before?”

“Not in this form. May I stand nearer?”

“Stand beside the post. Don’t put a shadow across the bowl.”

He moved immediately to the correct side. She watched him do it, then returned to the dancers.

Riarhin sat on the edge of the low platform. A woman near her was winding a strip of cloth around an ankle. The first fold had slipped, and the rest would not lie flat. Riarhin pointed.

“Shall I hold it?”

The woman gave her the end. Her skin was warm beneath Riarhin’s fingers. She rewound the strip, tested the ankle against the floor and shook her head.

“Too much?”

“Too little. It will move.”

They tried again. This time it held. The woman thanked her and stood, taking her weight slowly before attempting a turn.

At the back, the organiser returned empty-handed.

Mira folded her arms.

“There is a difficulty with the serving vessels,” he said.

The drummer laid his head on his drum.

The vessels were in a locked room. The man with the key had been sent to escort guests. The food existed; Riarhin could smell it. Between the food and the people waiting for it stood a cook who refused to put a hot pot on a painted ceremonial tray, and a servant who had been told to produce the tray and was determined to succeed at the task he actually possessed.

Nalia listened to both of them. She asked the cook what else would serve.

“Bowls.”

“From where?”

“Any household in Darava.”

The servant looked offended. The cook turned away and struck a lid into place hard enough to make him jump.

Riarhin went back to the rehearsal room. Berent was helping the painter shift a bench away from the wet border. Cadan held the other end, waiting for an instruction more exact than the one he had been given.

“Put it by the post,” the painter said.

“Which side?”

“The side where I won’t fall over it.”

Cadan considered the room, chose a place and lowered the bench. The painter looked relieved.

“I need bowls,” Riarhin told Berent.

He pointed at a stack beside the courtyard well.

“For washing feet.”

“Preferably not those.”

He wiped his hands on his coat. “How particular these people are.”

They borrowed from the house across the passage. The woman there wanted to know who was borrowing and who would wash. Riarhin supplied her name. The woman repeated it incorrectly, heard the correction without trying again, and gave her six bowls. Berent took six more. Cadan came for the pot after the cook established that he could carry it without setting it on the painted tray.

When they brought the meal in, Mira made the youngest dancer serve herself first. The girl looked towards the organiser. Mira took the ladle from her, filled the bowl and gave it back.

“Sit.”

The company ate at the edge of the room with the performers. Riarhin had intended to leave the food to them until the woman whose ankle she had held pushed a bowl into her hand and refused to retrieve it. It was hotter than she expected. She took a mouthful too quickly and spent the next few moments pretending she had not.

Mira watched with amusement.

“You can put it down.”

“I know.”

“Your face suggests you are enduring a trial.”

Berent laughed into his bowl. Riarhin waited until he looked up, then put the stack of empty vessels beside him.

“We promised to wash.”

His laughter ended.

After the meal, the rehearsal began again.

The first movement looked simple. The dancers crossed the floor at walking pace, each turning the head a little before the next step. Riarhin followed one woman and lost her among the others without understanding how. The drum altered. A second rhythm entered beneath it, beaten on a smaller instrument whose player watched Mira’s shoulders rather than her feet.

The cloth at the open door lifted and fell. The lamps remained bent towards the basin.

Mira stopped them twice. Once she moved a man’s elbow the width of two fingers. Once she made the whole line repeat a passage while she stood among them, saying the words without dancing. Riarhin understood several of the words. She could not have explained why the second attempt felt less hurried though it took no longer.

A story emerged from the movement. A woman received something and concealed it; another found the hiding place and refused to look. A figure crossed between them with empty hands. The drummer made a sharp, mocking sound, and people around Riarhin smiled.

Nalia leaned close.

“He’s the envoy who has brought no gift.”

“I understood that part.”

“Then you’re ahead of me. I thought he was a husband.”

On the floor the empty-handed figure found a flower, offered it with extravagant confidence and was ignored. Berent made a small approving noise. The dancer heard it and extended the offer towards him instead. Berent put a hand to his chest, delighted, until Mira called the man back into position.

Riarhin laughed. It came easily and remained after the joke had passed. She rested against the post, her side warm from the floor, and let herself watch without trying to find out which part would become useful.

Near the end, the dancer with the wrapped ankle missed a landing.

It was a small failure. Her heel touched early, her weight went across it, and she caught herself before falling. Mira had stopped the drum before the others turned.

“Sit down.”

“I can finish.”

“You can sit down.”

Aurine was already moving. She knelt beside the woman and asked permission to touch the ankle. Mira sent a boy through the side door.

The practitioner who came carried a narrow wooden box. He had a swollen joint in one of his own hands, which made Riarhin watch how he opened it. He listened to Aurine’s account, asked the dancer where the pain had begun and loosened the cloth.

Aurine held the foot while he worked. He pressed above the tender place, waited for the woman’s breathing to settle and spoke a few words too low for Riarhin to catch. A thread laid across his fingers tightened although neither hand moved. The dancer flinched.

“Now bend it towards me.”

She did. Tears stood in her eyes, but she bent it farther than before.

Aurine looked at the thread. The practitioner wound it around a piece of wood and closed the box.

“Can she dance?” Mira asked.

“Tomorrow, if she walks without favouring it. Today she watches.”

The dancer began to protest. He held up his swollen hand.

“You may learn from mine or buy your own.”

She looked away. Mira took a folded cloth, put it beneath her leg and told the others where to begin without her.

Aurine stayed beside the practitioner. Her first question concerned the pressure he had used, not the thread. He answered by moving her thumb to the place on his own wrist where she could feel the difference. She tried it, frowned, and asked again.

Saikou spoke to Mira after the last passage.

The lamps were being extinguished in a particular order. He waited until the last flame had gone and the surface of the basin lay still.

“There is a receiving boundary in the floor,” he said. “A form of invitation. Does the movement complete it?”

“Among other things.”

“I have seen a similar arrangement used to give a presence a place to stand.”

Mira sat on the platform and began wiping her feet with a damp cloth.

“If you write that, write who stands there.”

“I have not established that.”

“Then leave room until you have spoken to the people whose grandmother’s grandmother taught the movement.”

He inclined his head. “I was asking about the mechanism.”

“I heard you. A man came last year and described us as people who call an air spirit into a prepared vessel. The chamberlain asked whether a cheaper vessel would do.”

Riarhin looked towards the basin. It was plain fired clay with a repaired rim.

“Did you tell him?”

“I asked whether he would receive Ahimsa’s envoy in a storage jar.”

Saikou smiled, but Mira did not. He sat on the bench beside the platform, leaving her room.

“May I watch the preparation?”

“You watched part of it today.”

“The part before anyone dances.”

“Yes. Come before the first meal. There is work you can help with.”

He agreed. She wrung out the cloth.

“Your people need somewhere to stay?”

Nalia, approaching with the introduction folded in her hand, said that they did.

“The rooms beyond the rehearsal yard are empty. Two have roofs that can be trusted. The third needs the bed moved when it rains. We have to clear them before the visitors arrive for the observance.”

“How long?” Nalia asked.

“Until they arrive.”

“And payment?”

Mira looked at the washed bowls drying beside the wall.

“Help us clear them. Pay the woman who keeps them. She has been waiting for the chamberlain’s office to remember that empty rooms still require work.”

Nalia went to find her.

The rooms smelled of stored cloth and old limewash. Berent pressed a hand against the bed frame, listened to the creak and chose the other one. Cadan opened a shutter, found a nest behind it and closed it carefully again.

“Occupied,” he said.

Riarhin set down her bag. Through the wall she could hear the smaller drum. Someone was practising the passage that had defeated him earlier. It went wrong in the same place, stopped, began again. At the fourth attempt the rhythm held.

She took out the girl’s shell and put it on the shelf above the bed. The glass bird would be catching the evening light at home. Perhaps the girl had already asked for her shell back. Perhaps Ailsa had laughed and told her that Riarhin was slow about returning things.

Riarhin sat on the edge of the bed and took out paper.

You would have liked the dancing, she wrote.

She considered that, then added: You would have liked watching Berent fall in love with a man who offered him a flower more.

From the next room Berent asked whether anyone had seen his spare shirt. She kept writing.

Chapter 20 — Where the Water Went

Devaki arrived before the room keeper had finished scolding them about the bowls.

They had returned all twelve. Unfortunately, two belonged to a different household. Berent held one in each hand while the woman described the glaze, the rim and a defect in the firing which apparently made the distinction obvious. He was studying them with the concentration he had once given a map.

Devaki listened from the doorway.

“Those are my aunt’s.”

The room keeper pointed at him triumphantly.

“My aunt will deny it,” Devaki added. “She owes three to the sanctuary kitchen.”

Berent lowered the bowls.

“May I put them somewhere?”

“Preferably near someone else,” Riarhin said.

Devaki had come with a cart. She wanted Saikou to see the water works before he examined the tomb. The custodian, who followed her into the yard, said that she had wanted this too, though for reasons Devaki had not yet mentioned.

“We shall take both sets of reasons,” Saikou said.

Nalia went to ask whether Anju could come. Devaki’s expression closed.

“I invited you.”

“She knows the lower fields,” Riarhin said.

“So do I.”

The custodian stepped around her into the yard.

“Then we shall have people to correct one another. Bring water. It will be hot before we return.”

The road followed a channel which still carried water, though the river had drawn back from the landing below the town. Narrow gates led from it into smaller channels among the fields. Some stood open a hand’s breadth. Others were stopped with boards and mud. At one gate, a man sat beneath a sunshade with a stick laid across his knees. He lifted it when Devaki’s cart passed, then put it down again.

Anju watched him until he was out of sight.

The main work stood where the ground rose, a long bank faced in stone with trees growing along its crown. At first Riarhin thought the trees had been planted to shade it. Then she saw how their roots passed through openings in the masonry and disappeared into a second line of stone beneath. Small strips of cloth hung from some of the branches. One had been replaced so recently that its colour looked raw beside the faded others.

Devaki got down and spoke to the people waiting at the gatehouse. A woman gave her an account of the night’s flow. A boy brought a stool which she ignored until the custodian took it.

“This wall was rebuilt in my mother’s time,” Devaki told them. “The lower face had begun to separate. We had one season before the rains brought it down.”

She led them along the base. Riarhin could see the change in the stone, older blocks above a neat band of smaller work. Marks crossed some of the joins. They had been recut after the stones were fitted; in places the line went over mortar, broke, and resumed on the other side.

Saikou stopped at one of them. He held a hand near the surface without touching it.

“Who attends this?”

The gatehouse woman came forward.

“My household. The upper lengths are another’s.”

“Is the warmth usual at this hour?”

“Since the channel was lowered.”

He looked along the marks to the roots above.

“You have taken the load off the water and put more of it here.”

“Yes.”

“How long before you raise it?”

“When the rain gives us enough.”

He nodded, then asked to see the place where the two households’ work met. She showed him a joint with a narrow gap left deliberately open. A strip of wood lay loose within it. Saikou crouched, examined the edge and rose without disturbing anything.

“Good,” he said.

The woman’s shoulders eased. Riarhin had not noticed that she had been holding them tight.

Devaki continued towards the gatehouse. Inside, the floor lay cool beneath their feet. A broad opening looked onto the water, and the wall beside it carried a painted account in several hands. The earliest figures had been retouched without quite matching the colour. Newer writing fitted awkwardly beneath them.

A great dark stain ran through part of the account.

“That was the year the upper river failed,” Devaki said. “The old holding was enlarged. They drew water through the dry channels and kept it here. Three districts planted because of it.”

“Two,” said a man at the doorway.

She turned.

“Three received water.”

“The third planted and lost the crop.”

He wore a cloth twisted around his head, and the skin above one ankle was marked by old sores. He had come with a woman carrying a bundle of short wooden measures. She put the bundle down and leaned against the wall, already tired of an argument which Riarhin had only just entered.

Devaki introduced him as a cultivator from the eastern reach. She gave his name too quickly for Riarhin to catch, and he repeated it himself: Venu. He had come to check the marked depths before the rite.

“My mother ate because of this work,” he said, touching the painted wall. “Her brother lost his house to it. You leave him outside the story.”

“The lower bank failed,” Devaki said.

“It was cut.”

“To save the holding.”

“And the people below it were to be warned.”

The room went quiet. Outside, a pole scraped over stone.

Riarhin looked from one to the other. She had expected Devaki to deny the cutting. Instead the older woman rested her hand against the sill.

“The warning was late.”

“Too late for the people in the houses. Early enough for the tax account.”

His companion touched his arm. He pulled away, then took her hand and held it for a moment before releasing it.

Saikou asked where the breach had been. Venu pointed through the window, beyond the present wall.

“Can we walk there?”

“If you want to see a field.”

“I do.”

The field was planted. Water lay between the rows in narrow strips which reflected the sky. A woman stood at the far end with her garment gathered above her knees, clearing a small obstruction with her foot. She looked up when they came, then returned to it.

A line of broken stone crossed the near edge. One piece had a shallow basin worn into it by use. Another showed a socket where a doorpost had stood.

Venu put his hand on the larger stone.

“My mother’s brother’s house.”

“How long ago?” Riarhin asked.

“Before I was born.”

“Was he compensated?”
