Venu looked at her. There was no hostility in it, only a long consideration of how to answer someone who could ask the question so readily.

“His son was given land. Farther from the water. The old holding remained on the account until my father went to the court himself.”

“Who changed it?”

“Arun’s mother’s father.”

Devaki stood a little apart. Dust had whitened the lower part of her skirt. She looked tired now, and older than she had at the hearing.

“We have maintained the bank since,” she said.

“Yes,” Venu answered. “You have.”

Riarhin wanted someone to contradict the other. The facts were settling into place with nothing she could prise apart. The water had saved crops. The same work had put pressure on the lower bank. Someone had cut it to keep the larger holding from breaking. Warning had gone too late. Devaki’s household had kept later floods out, and Venu preferred the claimant whose family had listened when his own demanded correction.

Anju stepped over the broken line into the field.

“This is where your new measure begins?” she asked him.

“The next gate.”

“Then why does the collector begin here?”

Venu looked at his companion. She unfolded one of the measures from the bundle and came forward.

Devaki followed them. Within a few moments the three were disputing the placement of a marker. They began naming banks and channels Riarhin had not seen. She went to Saikou, who was making a drawing of the old breach.

“Will the inscription decide that?”

“Possibly the boundary. We must ask for the words which describe it, not merely the name.”

“And who gets the grant?”

“That will be for the renewal.”

She watched him draw the door socket. His page held the surviving stones, the water and a short note identifying whose account he had heard. He left space beneath it.

“You think there’s another?”

“There are fields on the other side.”

They ate beneath the gatehouse roof. The woman responsible for the marks sent her boy for sour fruit and made him wash it before he brought it in. Venu sat opposite Devaki. Their argument had narrowed to the marker, and both were now blaming a man who was absent.

Anju held her bowl between her palms. Riarhin sat beside her.

“You knew about the houses.”

“Everyone knows.”

“I didn’t.”

“No.”

Anju took a mouthful. Her gaze followed the boy as he went out to fetch more water.

“When our gate was closed, we used the old cut to let the flood out. They said we had no right. I asked whose permission the water had sought to come in.”

“Did that help?”

“It pleased my daughter.”

Riarhin laughed. Anju smiled into her bowl, then looked down at the food as though the thought of the girl had made her hungry.

The custodian called them together before the cart left. She had brought a board and chalk. Nalia wrote the questions as they were agreed.

They needed the order of succession attached to the grant. They needed the holding’s boundaries before the enlargement, the terms governing emergency release and the duties which passed with the right to collect. Devaki insisted on the entry for maintenance. Venu wanted the provision for households whose land had been surrendered. Anju asked that the reed holding be identified by both its banks.

The chamberlain’s clerk, who had accompanied them without saying much, objected to the length of the request.

“We were sent to recover the grant.”

The custodian looked at the board.

“We are asking what it grants.”

Saikou asked who would read the older hand. The custodian named two people. One was away. The other would attend, provided his household agreed to his entering the tomb.

“He has a new child,” she said. “His wife may prefer the court to wait.”

The clerk began to object again, then stopped. Devaki said she would speak with the household and was told firmly that this would not help.

Riarhin could have offered to make the copy herself. She had copied difficult things in worse places. She looked at the list and at the words which had already required three explanations before everyone accepted them.

“I’ll carry the boards,” she said.

The custodian handed her one.

On the road home, Devaki asked the driver to stop beside a stand of flowering trees. The boy from the gatehouse had run after them with a little packet she had forgotten. He arrived breathless and offended by the distance.

“I called.”

“I am old,” she said. “You should shout more rudely.”

He gave her the packet. She opened it, found a piece of food inside and broke it in two. He took the larger piece without pretending he had not noticed.

As he ran back, Devaki held the rest on her knee.

“I paid for the wall,” she said to Riarhin. “I sold things I wanted to keep. He was still a child.”

“Arun?”

“He has never disputed that.”

The cart moved. Riarhin waited.

“Sometimes I wish he would.”

Devaki ate the small piece slowly. After a while she asked the driver to take them by the northern gate, where a woman she knew had been ill. The driver turned without surprise.

Chapter 21 — The Rain Arrives

Before dawn, the courtyard smelled of oil and cut leaves.

Riarhin had expected to be early. Half the work was already done. Women sat beneath the covered walk threading flowers while a man on a ladder fastened the finished lengths above the doors. Beside the well, the youngest dancers were washing the floor. They worked towards one another with such vigour that Mira had to send one back to wipe the water from the place the drummers would sit.

Saikou stood near the basin with his sleeves rolled above his wrists. A woman Riarhin had not seen before was showing him how far to lower a length of cloth into the water. He watched once, repeated the movement and adjusted his hands before she could correct him. She nodded and gave him the other end.

Riarhin found Nalia carrying a basket of lamps.

“Where?” she asked.

“The far wall. Leave them in the basket until someone tells you which goes where.”

She took it. The handles had been repaired with cord which pressed into her palms. Beyond the court, people were beginning to arrive with their own flowers, bowls of grain and small bundles wrapped in cloth. A child carried a carved elephant on a tray. It leaned badly to one side. His grandmother kept a hand beneath the tray without taking it from him.

Anju stood outside the gate.

Riarhin saw her and stopped. The basket pulled at her fingers. Anju wore a fresh garment and had combed her hair carefully, drawing it back so tightly that her face looked narrower. Her daughter had come from the river house. The girl stood beside her with an empty bowl tucked beneath one arm.

The gatekeeper was speaking to them.

Riarhin put the basket down. Nalia caught her eye and shook her head once, urgently, before turning to the custodian. The older woman listened. Then she crossed the court to the gate.

The conversation was short. Anju answered a question, held up the empty bowl and said something which made the gatekeeper look towards the inner passage. The custodian moved him aside.

“Everyone may receive the rain,” she said, loudly enough for the people waiting to hear. “We are not convening the renewal.”

Anju came through. Her daughter’s eyes were bright with triumph. Anju touched her shoulder and murmured a warning. The girl lowered her chin, though she could not quite stop smiling.

Riarhin picked up the lamps again.

The golden elephants were brought out after the first light reached the upper wall.

Some were small enough for one person to carry. Others stood on frames lifted by four. Riarhin watched the bearers take their weight together. The gilding caught at different moments, ears bright, then forehead, then the curve of a trunk, while the shadowed portions retained the soft dull colour of metal which had passed through many hands.

The last offering stood covered beside the inner door. The cloth lay over it without revealing much of its shape. Two bearers inspected the poles and said nothing to each other. When a servant set a jar down too close, one moved it away with his foot.

Riarhin was given a place near a column. She could see the dancers and the open sky beyond the court. The room keeper had brought a cushion and placed it beneath an elderly man before he could refuse. Berent sat farther back with the girl from the river house. She was explaining the order of the offerings. He appeared to be listening with complete seriousness, which made her sit very straight.

Cadan stood beside the door. He had been asked to keep the passage clear. A richly dressed man tried to come through carrying a folded stool. Cadan politely directed him around the other side. The man glanced at the stool, then at Cadan’s empty hands, and went the longer way.

The opening voices came from behind the dancers.

Riarhin understood the invocation unevenly. The people around her answered phrases she was still trying to untangle. She followed the sound instead. It moved between low voices and high ones, with a pause after each answer in which the courtyard seemed larger than it had a moment before.

Mira entered during one of those pauses.

The woman Riarhin had watched arguing over bowls was still visible in her: the strong feet, the level head, the readiness of the hands. Her clothing changed the reach of each movement. A turn carried colour farther than the body itself. Small ornaments sounded at her ankles, and for a while Riarhin could distinguish each footfall from the answering drum.

Then the other dancers joined her.

The first passage was the one they had rehearsed. Riarhin recognised it with a pleasure almost like greeting someone in a strange city. The envoy came empty-handed; a ripple of amusement passed through the people watching. The flower was offered. This time the refusal had a weight she had not heard before. The drum beneath it grew slower, leaving the gesture suspended until Riarhin felt an unreasonable wish that the receiver would take what was offered and let the scene move on.

The dancer withdrew the flower.

At the basin, the lamps inclined.

A breeze crossed the courtyard. It reached Riarhin from the open sky, lifting the damp hair at the back of her neck. Several people looked up. Above the roof, the morning remained pale and clear.

Saikou knelt beside the cloth he had helped lower. His task was finished. He sat back on his heels and watched, the mirror still wrapped at his side.

The voices changed. Mira turned towards the open court with both hands held low. Behind her, the line of dancers widened until there was room between each body. Riarhin could see through the intervals to the far wall, where the flowers began to move.

They moved inwards.

Each hanging length bent towards the centre of the room. There was no flutter in them now. The finest petals held still, drawn along the same curve as the heavier stems. A child near the column whispered a question. His mother put her hand over his and waited.

The first drop struck the basin.

Riarhin heard it clearly. A single round sound. The surface opened and closed.

No drop had fallen from above.

She looked up at the dry beams, then back. A second drop formed in the air no higher than Mira’s shoulder. It brightened, fell and struck the water. Another appeared farther away. Another. They hung through the room at different heights, small pieces of light which grew heavy and descended.

One touched the back of Riarhin’s hand. It was cold.

She looked towards Saikou. His face had opened into an expression she had never seen during an examination. He watched the falling drops with his head slightly tilted, as though someone were speaking a language he had once loved and not heard for a long time.

Mira passed between the lamps. The drops fell around her without breaking the movement. Her hand swept low above the basin, then rose. The whole room inhaled.

Somewhere beyond the wall, thunder answered.

The rain did not begin all at once.

First the sky darkened over one end of the courtyard. The roofs beyond it took on a dull shine. People under the far awning turned to look out, and Riarhin saw them smile before she heard the rain reach that part of the town.

The dancers continued. Their clothes had darkened where the drops touched them. A thread of water ran from Mira’s wrist when she lifted her hand. Behind her, the young woman with the injured ankle landed cleanly and held her place. The practitioner sat against the wall watching her feet.

The thunder came again, nearer. A little boy tried to stand on his father’s knees. His father lifted him instead, forgetting for a moment the rule against blocking the view. No one told him to put the child down.

Rain crossed the open court in a moving line.

It struck the hot paving and brought up a smell so rich that Riarhin closed her eyes. Wet stone, dust, the green bitterness of crushed leaves. She had known rain on cold hills, on city roofs, on the river below Valora. Here it met earth which seemed to answer through every open door.

When she looked again, Anju’s daughter had put the bowl out.

Her mother held her sleeve to keep her from leaning too far. The girl watched water collect with a stillness unlike anything Riarhin had seen from her. When there was enough to cover the bottom, she brought the bowl back and held it for Anju to see.

Anju put two fingers into it. She touched them to the girl’s forehead, then to her own.

The last offering was carried through the rain. Water flattened its covering against a hollow shape. The bearers kept their pace. Nobody shook it, uncovered it or tried to see beneath the cloth. Mira turned as it passed and completed a movement Riarhin had not seen at rehearsal.

For an instant she thought there was someone standing beyond the bearers.

The figure occupied the falling rain without interrupting it. It was taller than a person, or farther away; she could not place it against the wall. Something about the head suggested a crown until she moved, and then it resembled branches, and then there was only water striking the paving.

She looked at the woman beside her. The woman was watching Mira. Tears ran down her face without any effort to wipe them away.

The drum stopped.

The dancers held their final places while the rain filled the silence. Mira’s chest rose and fell. She lowered her hands slowly. Then she stepped back, and the people began to speak.

Not loudly at first. The room seemed to require care. A child asked whether he could go outside. His mother said no, then laughed at herself and said yes.

He ran.

Others followed. The courtyard filled with people lifting their faces, holding out bowls, turning their palms upwards. Riarhin remained beside the column until a drop worked its way down her collar. She started, looked up at the leak and laughed.

Berent was already in the open. He had helped the elderly man down from the platform, then forgotten to come back under the roof. Anju’s daughter threw water at him from her bowl. He looked appalled, which delighted her so much that she did it again.

Riarhin went out.

The first full strike of rain took her breath. It ran through her hair, across her eyelids and down her arms. Her clothes clung at once. She tipped her head back. There was no cold waiting beneath it, no need to reach a door before her hands stopped working. She stood until someone collided with her shoulder and apologised without slowing.

Nalia came through the court holding her papers beneath her coat.

“Move,” she said. “Some of us possess things that dissolve.”

Riarhin took her free hand and turned her once under the rain.

Nalia made an indignant sound, lost her footing, recovered and began laughing so hard she could not finish the threat she had started. Riarhin let go before the papers suffered. Nalia ran beneath the awning and shook water from her hair at everyone close enough to object.

Saikou stood in the doorway with the covered mirror tucked safely away. Mira came beside him. They watched the children for a moment.

He said something Riarhin could not hear. Mira replied. He bowed his head, and she touched his arm as she passed.

Food appeared along the covered walk. The people serving it had already been at work while the rite continued; their own garments were damp with kitchen heat rather than rain. Berent offered to carry a pot and was accepted before he could discover its weight. Cadan took the other handle. Together they brought it through the crowd while Anju’s daughter cleared a path with the authority of someone who had recently explained the whole occasion to a foreign knight.

The dancers ate near the inner wall. Riarhin brought Mira a cup and found her laughing with the drummer, who was demonstrating a gesture she insisted he had remembered wrongly. He repeated it more extravagantly. She threw a wet cloth at him.

“Was that in the story?” Riarhin asked.

“It is now,” he said.

Mira took the cup. Her hands were trembling with exertion.

“Thank you.”

Riarhin sat beside her. For a while neither spoke. Rain drummed on the roof above them; the sound was so full that conversation elsewhere came and went in pieces. The young dancer leaned back against the wall and shut her eyes. Someone put a plate into her lap. She opened one eye, found the food and began eating.

“I saw something,” Riarhin said at last.

Mira turned the cup in her hands.

“Did you?”

“Beyond the bearers.”

“Then remember what you saw before somebody tells you what it must have been.”

Riarhin watched the courtyard. The place where the figure had stood, if it had stood anywhere, was now occupied by two children pushing a leaf along the water with their toes.

“Do you know?”

Mira drank.

“I know where my feet go. I know whom we receive.”

She looked across to a woman who had begun gathering the extinguished lamps.

“And I know she will be furious if I leave all the clearing to her again.”

Riarhin stood and offered a hand. Mira took it, rose with a small grunt and went to help.

By evening the courtyard channels were running steadily.

The gatehouse woman arrived with mud to her knees. She spoke to the custodian while wiping her hands on a cloth which was already wet through. Devaki joined them. Arun came out of the visitors’ room, listened and asked for his outer coat.

Riarhin was fastening her own when Anju touched her arm.

“Leave the sword. You’ll want both hands.”

They walked to the lower sluice carrying lamps beneath covers. Rain shone in every patch of light. The road which had been dusty that morning had become soft enough to take a deep print; at the edges it ran away underfoot. Berent caught Nalia’s elbow when she slipped and released it as soon as she was steady. She thanked him without a joke.

The gate was working. Water ran through the opening in a clean, powerful stream. The difficulty lay in the little channel beside it, where cut leaves and a piece of cloth had gathered against a wooden brace. Two people were already clearing it. Anju took a forked stick from a boy and showed Riarhin where to stand.

“Lift towards me. Don’t push it through.”

Riarhin obeyed. The sodden mass came up heavier than she expected. She took its weight with both hands and dragged it onto the bank. Beneath it, water began to move again.

Arun held the lamp while the gatehouse woman checked the marks. Devaki kept the rain off the measuring board with her own cloth. They had been arguing over the grant since before Riarhin arrived; now one had to hold still while the other leaned across him, and both did so without comment.

The woman called a depth. Nalia wrote it beneath her coat.

“Again after the next watch,” Devaki said.

“I have people coming,” Arun answered.

“People who know this gate?”

“Two from here. One of mine with them.”

Devaki looked towards the woman, who nodded. The matter was settled.

Anju wiped her face. Her daughter waited beneath the bank’s small shelter with the empty bowls from the earlier meal. She had been told to bring them home and had followed instead.

“Go on,” Anju called. “Before they think we’ve stolen them.”

The girl held up one bowl. Rain had collected in it again.

Anju laughed, a sudden full sound which carried over the channel. The girl laughed back and ran towards the town, holding the bowl level as long as she could.

Riarhin stood with water streaming from her sleeves. Her arms were tired from lifting; they would be sore in the morning. She flexed her left hand and felt the easy answering movement all the way to her shoulder.

“Another?” she asked.

Anju gave her the stick.

Chapter 22 — Guests for the Season

The room keeper moved their bed during the second week of rain.

She did it while they were out, with the assistance of two boys and a considerable injury to the plaster beside the door. When Riarhin returned, the shell had been placed on a different shelf, her folded clothes were on a stool, and the place where her pillow had lain was occupied by a basin.

Water fell into it at regular intervals.

“You said this roof could be trusted,” she said.

“It can. The water comes from the other room.”

The keeper pointed at the wall. Riarhin followed the dark line down from the ceiling and decided that an argument would get her nowhere. She moved her bag to the dry end of the bed.

From the adjoining room came a scraping sound, followed by Berent asking whether the boys had considered lifting their end before turning it. They assured him that they had. The scrape resumed.

Saikou stood in the doorway holding a broken buckle.

“Has anyone a needle?”

“Ailsa would,” Riarhin said.

She could see the little roll spread on Ailsa’s knee, each thread laid apart, and Ailsa looking up as somebody reached across them with wet hands.

“Aurine,” she added. “Ask Aurine.”

He looked at the buckle, then at the strap from which it had torn.

“I have one. Somewhere.”

He went back to his room.

Riarhin sat on the moved bed. Through the window she could see the courtyard tree, its leaves shivering under water which continued to fall after the sky had cleared. Her letter to Ailsa had left with a carrier three mornings before. She had entrusted it to a woman who knew the estate’s district and disliked being told twice where a thing must go. She had given the woman the address once and heard it repeated correctly.

She took out the shell. Sand remained in one of its folds. She brushed it into her palm and considered putting it into the letter she had not yet begun.

In the next room Saikou dropped something.

Then he dropped it again, harder.

He had three stitches in the strap when she came in. Two were too close to the torn edge. The third had pulled through.

“Shall I hold it?”

“No, thank you.”

He drew the thread out, knotted it and began again. The leather had softened with damp. Each time the needle went through, he pulled before it was quite clear, widening the hole he meant to secure.

Riarhin sat on the floor opposite him.

“You need another piece beneath it.”

“I know how it is done.”

She looked at him. He kept his eyes on the strap.

Amari would have taken it away. Perhaps he would have made a joke, perhaps simply held out his hand until Saikou surrendered it. Riarhin had no right to the familiarity and no patience for pretending the leather would mend itself out of respect for its owner.

“Then stop pulling.”

Saikou set the needle down. His hand remained beside it, fingers stiff.

“I said I would attend to it.”

“Yes.”

Outside, someone emptied the basin. Water struck the courtyard in a single heavy rush.

“I am trying,” he said.

She looked away first.

They sat until the sound of the rain on the roof became distinct again. Then Saikou held out the strap.

“A piece underneath?”

“I think so.”

He found a worn scrap among his belongings. She trimmed it, made the holes with her knife and gave it back. He sewed. She held the leather where he asked her to, and when the thread caught, neither spoke until it came free.

The finished work was ugly. The buckle held.

Saikou turned it over in his hands.

“I was rude.”

“You were.”

“I am sorry.”

She nodded. He fastened the strap around his rolled bedding and pulled it hard enough to test it. It stayed.

“Will you have some food?” he asked.

“If there is any.”

“There is. I went to ask before I began this.”

“Then it may be cold.”

“It will certainly be cold.”

They went to eat it.

Anju invited them to a meal on the first evening she had her daughter to herself again. Lali had been helping in the sanctuary kitchens, returning with news of everyone’s mistakes and enough confidence to correct her mother’s account of them. Anju said the invitation was intended to make her speak to somebody else.

The rain had brought a sour smell into the passage below their room. Inside, Anju had washed the floor, moved the vessels out of the way and borrowed a lamp with a steadier flame. Lali received the company with exaggerated courtesy until Berent bowed as low as she did. She broke into laughter and was sent to fetch the food.

Venu and his wife were there. Riarhin had not expected them. Anju saw her glance and said that they had disagreed about a gate, not sworn to eat separately for the remainder of their lives.

The food was hot and thick with something which left warmth in Riarhin’s mouth long after she swallowed. Venu’s wife watched the first mouthful with amusement.

“More?”

“In a moment.”

Berent accepted more immediately. His eyes began to water. Lali leaned forward to see whether he would admit it.

He told her the lamp smoked.

“The lamp is behind you.”

“A treacherous lamp.”

Cadan ate steadily. When Anju asked whether it was too hot, he considered the question and said that he liked it. She gave him another serving.

The argument began over a payment which Venu had made for the upper gate. Anju said the lower households had supplied labour of equal value. Venu said the account did not show it. His wife put down her bowl.

“The account shows what they were allowed to enter.”

“I know that.”

“Then stop quoting it as if the page had walked here by itself.”

He looked at her, annoyed. She returned the look until he lowered his eyes to the food.

Nalia asked who had witnessed the labour. Anju named three households. One supported Devaki, one Arun, and the third had refused both invitations to the renewal because its oldest member lay dying. The chamberlain would not accept the first two without the third, lest either side accuse him of choosing its witnesses.

“Can someone visit them?” Riarhin asked.

“People have visited them all week,” Anju said. “They would like to sit with their mother.”

Riarhin set down the question she had been preparing.

Cadan spoke from beside Berent.

“Must the account wait until she dies?”

Anju turned towards him. He had asked gently, with the same attention he gave any uncertain instruction.

“Yes,” she said after a moment. “If that is how long they need.”

He nodded and resumed eating.

Venu’s wife reached for the serving bowl. Her hand shook enough to strike its rim. She laughed at herself, angrily, and told her husband to take it before she poured food into his lap.

For a little while they discussed the dying woman. She had once pursued Venu through a field because he had stolen fruit from a tree she insisted was too young to bear being climbed. Venu said he had not climbed it. His wife asked how he had obtained the fruit. He looked towards Lali and announced that there were children present.

Lali demanded the story. Anju made him tell it.

Nalia’s carrier came back three days later without the deposit.

He stood beneath the covered walk explaining why the boats could not be held at the price agreed. The rains had changed the journey. The animals needed for the road beyond were committed elsewhere. His cousin had misunderstood which household would guarantee the hire.

Nalia let him finish. Then she made him say how much money remained.

“Most.”

“How much?”

He named it. She shut her eyes.

Riarhin stood at the doorway with her hands full of wet clothes. She could hear every word. Nalia could see that she could hear.

“Put it here,” she said, indicating the bench.

The carrier counted out the returned money. Nalia waited until he had gone before speaking again.

“I believed him.”

“So did his cousin, apparently.”

“He knew exactly which part I had misunderstood.”

Riarhin put the clothes down.

“Will you go after him?”

“And employ someone else to explain my mistake to a court? For that amount?” She sat. “No.”

They counted what had come back. It would pay for another attempt, though not at the price Nalia had promised. Riarhin expected her to begin defending the first decision. Instead she took out her account and crossed through the sum.

“I shall pay the difference in the deposit. The road will cost what it costs.”

“Find the road first.”

Nalia’s pen stopped. Then she nodded.

That afternoon she went with Anju to deliver food to the dying woman’s household. She carried the baskets back, stayed to help wash them and returned after dark with a smear of flour on her sleeve. She mentioned no arrangements.

The following evening, a woman came to see her. She had a small child asleep against her shoulder and spoke so softly that Riarhin, sitting on the other side of the yard, heard almost nothing. Nalia moved the papers out of the way so the woman could lower the child onto the bench.

They spoke until the lamp needed oil.

When the woman had gone, Nalia stayed beside the papers. Riarhin crossed the yard.

“Another carrier?”

“A cousin in the upland trade. She will introduce us when the renewal is settled. No money until we meet him.”

“Good.”

“She told me which question to ask first.”

“Will you remember?”

Nalia gave her a tired, hostile look. Riarhin smiled and went to find more oil.

By the time the custodian came with permission for the examination, Cadan had learned to mend the courtyard screens.

The room keeper taught him by putting a sound one beside the damaged frame. He studied the joining, undid his first attempt and began again. She left him with it. Two hours later she returned to find the work finished and the scraps sorted by length.

“You can throw those away.”

“Some may serve for repairs.”

She looked at the scraps, then at the screen.

“Keep the longer ones.”

He did. Riarhin sat with him while he worked on the second frame. The strip he passed through the upright was smooth from soaking, and his fingers moved with an ease she had usually seen employed at other tasks.

“If there were no instructions,” she asked, “what would you do?”

He looked up.

“Here?”

“Anywhere.”

“I would have to choose.”

“Would you like that?”

He returned his attention to the frame. For a while she thought he would not answer.

“I have liked some of my choices.”

Across the yard, the custodian appeared beneath the arch. Saikou came out to meet her. The record-reader’s household had agreed. The approach had drained enough to be examined, and the people entitled to attend had selected their representative.

They would go in the morning.

Riarhin stood. Cadan passed another strip through the frame and drew it into place. He had almost finished. She left him the light.

Chapter 23 — The Outer Lamp

The path to the tomb climbed through wet trees. At the last bend, the town disappeared behind leaves and the sound of the rain channels faded. Riarhin could hear their own footsteps, the knocking of a tool against the mason’s leg and, farther up, a steady tapping.

The custodian stopped beneath a stone arch whose carving had been softened by water. She waited for the others to gather before speaking to the man at work beyond it.

He sat beside a drain with his robe gathered clear of the mud. A little wooden scraper lay across his knee. He had been lifting roots from between the stones, and he continued until the custodian repeated his name.

“Saran.”

He laid the scraper down and stood.

“I heard you.”

“Then why did you not answer?”

“The root would have gone back into the joint.”

He showed her a thin length of it, intact. She took it, examined the end and handed it back. Riarhin thought she was trying not to smile.

Saran was a lean man with a broad forehead and hair clipped so close that rain shone on the skin beneath it. His clothes had been repaired often and washed recently. A small lamp in a covered carrier stood beside his tools. It was not lit.

The chamberlain’s clerk introduced Saikou as the practitioner engaged to examine the sealed chamber. Saran’s gaze moved to the satchel and stayed there for a moment. Then he bowed.

“Of the House of the Outer Lamp,” he said, giving the words as part of his name.

The clerk had already turned towards the building. The custodian waited until Saran finished before returning the greeting.

The tomb stood low against the slope. Its roof had been covered with earth so long that a tree grew near the highest point; its roots had been guided into stone troughs to keep them from the chambers beneath. Along the front ran a sheltered walk. Offerings stood in shallow recesses, some fresh, some reduced to dry stems. A family had left a child’s clay animal beside a bowl. One leg was missing.

Riarhin moved her foot around it.

The door they had come to inspect stood at the end of the walk, narrower than the main entrance. Water had left a pale fan across its lower part. Beside it, the mason set down his tools and took out a wedge.

Saran caught his wrist.

The movement was so quick that Riarhin’s hand went to her own belt before she could consider it. Saran released the man at once.

“Not that joint.”

“I have opened stone doors before.”

“Look beneath the moss.”

The mason bent. Saran handed him the scraper. Together they cleared a narrow place beside the sill. Under the green lay a cut which turned into the joint the wedge would have entered.

Saikou was beside them before the clerk finished asking what had happened.

“Leave the tool out,” he said. “All of you, back from the door.”

They moved. He asked Saran which part had been exposed when the repairer died. Saran pointed higher, where a broken line disappeared beneath a patch of mortar.

“The first stone came out there.”

“And the man behind him?”

“Across the turn. He heard him fall.”

Saikou looked from the patched mark to the sill. Then he set his satchel down, took out the mirror and asked the custodian for the dead man’s name.

She supplied it. She had known him.

“What do you intend?”

“To test whether the broken mark is connected to his death. Nothing will be moved.”

She considered that, then agreed.

Saran stepped back beside Cadan. Riarhin watched the two men stand in nearly the same posture, hands clear of their clothes, their attention fixed on Saikou’s work. Riarhin shifted until she could see both their faces.

The test took little time. Saikou covered the mirror and asked for chalk.

“The mark was active. It still is. This opening crosses its line of refusal.” He indicated the patched joint. “Pulling the next stone may extend that line through the place where we are standing.”

The mason put his wedge away.

“What is the door for?” Riarhin asked.

“Service,” the clerk said.

“Service to what?”

He looked at the custodian.

Saran answered. “The lamps and the outer ward. It gives access to their housing. It does not promise entry to the chamber.”

He sounded impatient, as though someone had already explained this and been ignored. The custodian looked towards the covered lamp at his feet.

“Can you show us?”

“Yes.”

“Without opening it.”

“Yes.”

They went around the building by a path barely wide enough for two feet together. Berent carried the boards. The mason had left the heavy tools under the sheltered walk and brought a brush and a little hammer instead.

Saran stopped where the side wall met an older length of stone. He brushed leaves from a shallow recess and showed them a second marking. It was larger and more worn than the one at the door.

“The approach,” he said. “Here is the turn. Here, the lamp housing. The chamber is beyond.”

Riarhin followed his finger. At first she saw only decoration. Then the repetition resolved into a series of spaces joined by narrow passages. The diagram had been placed low enough for someone kneeling to read it. A later drain cut across one corner.

“Why put it outside?”

“So a person repairing the way in can learn where to stand.”

The mason made a sound in his throat. Saran looked at him, then lowered his hand.

“I should have stopped you sooner.”

“You stopped me.”

Saikou knelt beside the recess. Water dripped from the tree behind him and struck the shoulder of his coat. He ignored it.

“This branch ends before the chamber.”

“Yes.”

“But these marks continue.”

“The work within. The approach serves it.”

“Is it intact?”

“I have not entered.”

Saikou looked up.

“Have you seen one operating?”

“On a smaller scale.”

“Where?”

Saran glanced at the custodian. “At the house where I was taught.”

She was watching him closely. He added that it had guarded a store of dangerous remains. Riarhin waited for him to say more. He did not.

They copied the diagram. The mason took its measurements and marked the places where later work had covered the lines. When they returned to the front, the clerk wanted to know whether the door could now be opened.

“Tomorrow,” the custodian said. “Through the permitted entrance. With the reader and the household witness. We will not make a second hole because the first asks more of us.”

He looked towards Saikou for support. Saikou was cleaning damp from the mirror’s cover.

“She is right.”

Saran accompanied them down the hill. He carried the covered lamp and his little scraper, walking beside Cadan without apparent discomfort. At the bend above the town he spoke to Saikou. Riarhin could not hear the question, but she saw Saikou stop.

The others continued a few paces before noticing.

“What did you ask?” Riarhin said.

Saran turned.

“Whether the thing he seeks to return has a green face within a frame of brass.”

She had the knife half clear before she knew she had moved. The custodian stepped back. Cadan watched Riarhin’s hand.

Saikou’s voice remained level.

“Leave it where it is.”

For a moment she thought he meant the amulet. Then she saw his gaze on the knife and let it settle.

“Where did you hear that?”

Saran looked at her rather than Saikou now.

“He asked the keeper of records for accounts of the Himadri holding. The request came through a house which knows mine. I was shown the terms.”

“What terms?”

Saikou answered. “Those I sent from the coast. They named the emptied chamber and the raider’s destination. They did not describe the object.”

“The descriptions survive elsewhere,” Saran said.

Riarhin could hear water moving in the ditch beside the path. A cart passed below them, its driver calling to someone who had stepped into the road. She wanted the quiet of a room with one door and no strangers behind her.

“We’ll speak at the house.”

Saran bowed. “With your companions, if you permit.”

“With my companions.”

He waited until everyone had sat. Aurine stood beside the window. Nalia put paper before her and told him that she would be recording what he said. He agreed without looking at the sheet.

His branch had once served a mountain holding. He had never seen it. The oldest surviving instruction in his house described the duties of those who kept the approach: lamps, thresholds, receiving places, the separation of a visitor from what the visitor carried. Over time the house had taken work elsewhere. The names of some duties had survived after the work itself was gone.

“The object was taken,” he said. “Its absence did not end the obligation.”

“To fetch it?” Riarhin asked.

“To keep it. To restore what was entrusted.”

“Those are not the same thing.”

“No.”

Saikou asked about the central chamber. Saran described a place of negation without using the word Riarhin expected. He spoke of a work maintained around something which could not safely be broken. When he drew its arrangement, Saikou leaned forward.

“That is an approach.”

“Yes.”

“Where are the controls?”

“The instruction concerns the receiving.”

Riarhin watched his face. He had answered smoothly until then. Now he took the charcoal from the paper and wiped his fingers.

“You want to come.”

“Yes.”

“And to put it back.”

“If it is what I believe.”

“What else do you want?”

He looked at the covered lamp beside his foot.

“My house should be remembered for what it kept.”

No one spoke. In the courtyard, Lali began calling for the room keeper. Her voice moved past the door and away.

Saran asked to see the amulet.

“No,” Riarhin said.

He accepted the refusal without protest. She disliked that too. She would have preferred a demand she could answer, an impatience which gave her something firm to push against.

Saikou asked him to leave the drawing. He did. Nalia read her account back, and he corrected the name of one office before signing beneath it. When he rose, Cadan stood to give him room.

“We examine the tomb first,” Riarhin said. “Nothing beyond that is agreed.”

“I understand.”

After he had gone, she remained by the door.

“Did you expect the request to find him?”

Saikou shook his head.

“I expected it to find people who knew more than I did.”

“He knew enough to recognise us.”

“Yes.”

She went back to the table. The drawing was simple. A person entering from below would follow the lamps to the place marked for receiving. Along one edge Saran had drawn a small closed form, then stopped.

Riarhin put her finger beside it.

“Tomorrow,” she said, “we find out what he knows about keeping people alive.”

Chapter 24 — The Kings’ Tomb

The reader’s wife came to the gate with him.

She carried their child against her shoulder, wrapped lightly despite the damp morning. The child slept with one hand outside the cloth. Riarhin watched the fingers close when the man touched them.

“You have everything?” his wife asked.

He lifted the case of writing tools.

“I meant the food.”

“Yes.”

She looked at Saikou.

“He will come out when you say?”

“I will tell him when we need to leave.”

“I asked whether he will do it.”

The reader made a small protesting sound. She ignored him. Saikou turned towards the man and waited.

“I will,” he said.

His wife nodded. She adjusted the cloth beneath the sleeping head, kissed him and went down the path. He watched until the trees hid her. Then he looked at Riarhin and said his name was Keshan, as though he had only just remembered that they had not been introduced.

The custodian laid the permissions on a low stone beside the entrance. A woman from the families represented within the tomb examined each mark before touching her own to the page. She had brought a little bowl of flowers. No one stepped past her until she had placed it in the recess and finished speaking.

Riarhin followed the words as far as she could. They named the people entering, their purpose and what they might take out. Copies of the inscriptions. Measures of the approach. No vessel, bone, ornament or piece of stone. Saran’s drawing materials were included after the custodian added a line.

The family witness looked at him.

“You may record what you find. You may not decide that it was yours before we arrived.”

He bowed.

Berent checked the lamps. Aurine remained outside with the mason and the supplies; she had chosen the sheltered walk as the place where anyone brought out would be treated. Nalia stood beside Keshan, comparing her list of questions with his. Cadan waited with a coil of line over one arm.

Saikou opened his satchel last.

The main door moved more easily than the service door had suggested. Two people drew it back on its track, using handles worn smooth by generations of palms. Cool air came out. It smelled of stone and old oil, with none of the sweetness Riarhin had expected from a place of burial.

The first chamber was narrow. Light from the entrance reached a row of carved feet, then faded before the bodies above them could be seen. The family witness lit a lamp in a recess. A seated figure emerged, hands resting on knees, its face damaged where water had run down from a crack in the roof.

The custodian named it. The witness used a second name, a household form which made Keshan smile. Riarhin heard the affection in it and kept her eyes on the path beneath her feet.

Saikou stopped before the inner turn.

A dry thread stretched across the opening at ankle height. It was so fine that Riarhin saw it only after he pointed. Beyond it, an unlit wick lay in a shallow cup. There was no oil in the cup.

“No one cross yet.”

Saran knelt on the near side, looked at the thread and asked the custodian when the lamp had last been supplied.

“Before the repairer’s death.”

“Was the thread there then?”

“I did not attend that part of the work.”

He leaned forward without crossing. Saikou touched his shoulder.

“The shadow.”

Saran withdrew at once.

The lamp beside them cast his shadow along the wall. Where it reached the dry cup, its head turned towards the chamber beyond.

Saran was facing the other way.

Riarhin felt the back of her neck tighten. Her left hand had closed around the wrapped hilt at her side. She loosened it deliberately.

Saikou moved their lamp behind the stone pier. The shadow vanished.

“Keep the lights on this side of the turn until we have marked a way through.”

He asked the family witness whether an old attendance was still maintained within. She looked at the custodian. Neither answered immediately.

“There is a place prepared,” the witness said. “Sometimes people hear someone there.”

“What do they hear?”

“A request to be received.”

“Has anyone answered?”

“My grandfather said his uncle did.”

“And returned?”

“Yes. He would not speak of it afterwards.”

Saikou nodded once. He took out the iron Symbol and a narrow packet of filings. Saran remained kneeling, his face still.

“Do you know where the old boundary ends?” Saikou asked him.

“Yes.”

“Show me from here.”

Saran pointed to a worn cut above the cup. Saikou followed it with his eyes, then indicated a different join farther back.

“That one has been carried through the newer stone.”

Saran looked again. He nodded.

Together they traced a way which kept the company out of the old attendance’s place. Saikou set the Symbol on cloth and laid his own boundary across the junction. His voice was quiet. Riarhin could hear the words become firmer as he repeated them, taking a shape the air seemed to resist.

The dry wick bent.

Berent shifted the lamp farther behind the pier. The wick straightened.

“Now,” Saikou said. “One at a time. Keep to the wall. Do not step across the filings, and do not answer anything you cannot see.”

Cadan went first with the line. The family witness followed, then the custodian. Riarhin waited until Keshan had adjusted his case so it would not swing into the old cup. He breathed through his mouth, eyes fixed on Cadan’s back.

“Take your time,” she told him.

He nodded without looking at her and went through.

The record chamber had been enlarged at least twice. Riarhin could see where an earlier wall had been cut away and the marks above a doorway interrupted by a later ceiling. A row of stone vessels stood along the far side. Each had a little shelf before it. Some shelves held remains of offerings; one was empty except for a bowl turned upside down.

The family witness crossed to a vessel and touched the shelf. She did not open it. The custodian waited beside her until she had finished.

Keshan set his case on a dry stone. Nalia held the light while he examined the inscription.

He read the first line softly, changed one word and began again.

“How old?” Riarhin asked.

“The lettering? Older than this wall.”

He showed her a seam beneath the text. The whole inscribed face had been moved from somewhere else and fitted into the chamber. A later hand had added words along the lower edge.

“This is the grant. That is its renewal after the enlargement.”

“Can you read both?”

“Yes. The lower part will take longer.”

Nalia laid out the questions. He looked at them before beginning his copy. When he came to the succession clause, he stopped and asked the custodian whether the district still followed the sister’s line in the absence of an admitted daughter. She answered with two examples from recent generations. He wrote them separately from the inscription.

Riarhin held a board beneath his page. Her task required stillness. She had expected danger to demand something more from her, and found the effort of remaining motionless while he compared a single cut with its neighbour strangely difficult.

From the passage came a soft sound.

Berent lifted his head. Riarhin looked towards Saikou, visible beyond the turn. He raised one hand without interrupting his words.

Wait.

The sound came again. It might have been a voice from the outer chamber, distorted by the stone. Keshan’s pen stopped. The family witness put her palm flat on the shelf before the vessel.

“Continue,” Saikou said.

Riarhin kept the board steady. Keshan bent over the inscription again. His first stroke blotted. He turned the page, found a clean place and copied the line from the beginning.

The voice, if it was a voice, did not grow louder. It remained just beyond comprehension, near enough to tempt the listener into moving towards it.

Riarhin watched the place where the passage bent. A shadow reached across the floor and ended before the filings. Its edge was sharp though their lamps had been screened.

Cadan stood on the safe side of the line. He watched it with the calm interest she had seen him give Saikou’s damaged strap, a thing whose purpose might become clear if he waited.

“Stay where you are,” she said.

“I am.”

She turned back to Keshan. He had reached the lower addition.

They rested in the first chamber after the initial copy. Saikou withdrew his working only when everyone had crossed and the lamps were back behind the pier. The shadow had gone. The dry cup remained empty.

Keshan drank, ate the food his wife had packed and examined the ink on his fingers. They trembled when he held them away from his body. Riarhin sat beside him while Nalia and the custodian compared the copied text with their questions.

“We have to go back,” he said.

“Do we?”

“The lower field is described by a channel which no longer bears that name. There is another line behind the deposit. I must see whether it qualifies the grant or the release.”

“You promised to come out when he said.”

“I have come out.”

He looked towards Saikou, who was wiping the Symbol with a dry cloth.

“May we go in again?”

Saikou examined the seal, ran a finger along the edge and asked Saran whether they could move the light to reach the obscured line without crossing the old attendance’s place.

Saran drew the turn on a board. He set two small stones where the lamps would stand. Then he moved one back.

“There. The first position puts the reader’s shadow across it.”

Saikou studied the arrangement.

“We can. After I have rested.”

Keshan leaned against the wall and shut his eyes. Relief moved through his face so openly that Riarhin found herself looking away.

The family witness took out a little packet of flowers. She sorted them into two portions, rejecting a damaged one which she placed in her own lap. Riarhin asked about the empty shelf.

“The unreceived king,” the woman said. “Some call him that.”

“Was he buried here?”

“No one agrees. My mother said the place was left for a guest who never came.”

“And you?”

“I turn the bowl at the appointed time.”

She held out a flower. Riarhin took it, uncertain what was expected. The woman indicated the nearest shelf.

“For the people we do know.”

Riarhin placed it there.

On the second entry, Saran found the diagram.

It lay on the underside of the stone which carried the lower addition, exposed where a support had fallen away. He was helping place the lamp and saw the cut as its light passed beneath the edge.

He stopped so suddenly that the custodian asked whether anything had moved.

“No. A mark.”

She came to look. Riarhin remained beside Keshan until he finished the disputed line. Then she lowered the board and joined them.

The drawing showed two passages. One entered a broad central space. The other ended in a narrower space beside it. Between them ran several parallel strokes which met a square form beneath the centre.

Saran touched the stone just outside the cuts.

“This was brought from a keeper’s house.”

“How do you know?”

“The lower mark. My house preserves it.”

“Your house preserves several things,” the custodian said. “Some were learned from others.”

He withdrew his hand.

“May it be taken out?”

“No.”

“The stone has already been reused.”

“It now carries part of the grant you came to copy. You may make a record. The families will decide whether anything more may be done.”

Saran’s face tightened. For a moment Riarhin thought he would argue. Then he asked for a board.

“The families kept an account of where the stone came from,” the custodian said. “I will attach that to your copy.”

Saran looked at her. She waited until he nodded.

Riarhin held the lamp while he copied. He worked quickly but checked each line twice. Saikou could see them from the passage. At Riarhin’s request Saran turned the board towards him before continuing.

“The chamber is here?” Saikou asked.

“The receiving place.”

“And beside it?”

“Attendance.”

Saikou looked at the parallel strokes.

“Copy all of those. Do not regularise the spacing.”

Saran began to answer, then looked again at the stone. He erased one part of his drawing and measured it against a strip of paper.

The soft voice returned beyond the turn. This time Keshan heard a word in it. Riarhin saw his lips shape an answer.

She put her hand over his.

He stopped.

Saikou’s voice sharpened. The air at the junction shone briefly, a narrow trembling sheet which made the far wall appear farther away. The shadow recoiled to the cup.

“We are leaving.”

Keshan closed his case at once. Saran rolled the drawing, losing a piece of charcoal in his haste. Cadan reached to retrieve it, saw where it had fallen and left it.

Riarhin went last before Saikou. At the turn, something spoke close enough to her ear that she felt the breath of it.

She kept walking.

Outside, the light struck her eyes. Aurine took the board from her hands and asked who needed attention. Riarhin looked back at the doorway. Saikou came out carrying the covered Symbol. He sat on the low wall without choosing a dry place.

“Water,” he said.

Aurine brought it. He drank with both hands around the cup.

Keshan stood beneath the arch, looking at the path down to the town. Then he laughed once, with no amusement at all, and wiped his face on his sleeve.

“Have we got it?” Berent asked.

Nalia held the case against her chest.

“We have it.”

The family witness went back to close the door. The mason helped her. Saran remained beside the covered lamp until the stone had settled into its track.

As they began the descent, Riarhin asked Saikou what had spoken.

“Something accustomed to being answered.”

“A king?”

“I would not grant it that on its own testimony.”

He stopped to recover his breath. She waited with him while the others went ahead. Below, Keshan had begun to hurry. His wife was coming up the path with the child in her arms.

Riarhin watched him reach them.

Chapter 25 — The Record Read Twice

The chamberlain asked to see the copy before anyone else.

Nalia gave him the inventory of what had been copied. He read it, turned the sheet over and looked up.

“The text.”

“At the reading,” she said.

“I engaged the work.”

“The families permitted it. The custodian witnessed it. The questions were agreed at the gatehouse.”

Riarhin stood beside the window. Rain had begun again, a light steady fall which barely disturbed the leaves. The chamberlain looked towards her.

“Your passage is still waiting.”

“So are the people who need to hear this.”

He put the inventory down. The care with which he aligned it to the edge of the table made her wish she had chosen a less ready answer.

“I have forty-seven households asking what they owe,” he said. “Two claimants asking what they may collect. Work crews waiting to know who pays them. If the reading makes an error, I shall spend the season answering for it.”

“Then sit with Keshan,” Saikou said. “Check the words with him. The copy stays with the custodian until they are read.”

The chamberlain kept him standing while he finished the sheet before him. Then he sent for another stool.

They read it first in the little room behind the assembly hall.

Keshan supplied the older words. The custodian gave their later usage where it had changed. The chamberlain compared the surviving archive copy, marking every place where the versions agreed. When they came to the clause concerning emergency release, he asked for it again.

Keshan read it.

“The permission belongs to the holder,” the chamberlain said.

“The duty belongs to the holder,” the custodian answered. “Read what follows.”

Keshan did. The next line named the water households charged to act when delay would endanger dwellings. Their action had to be witnessed and reported. It did not extinguish a claim for damage caused by unnecessary cutting.

Riarhin thought of Anju’s bundle of grains. She kept her mouth shut.

The chamberlain rubbed the shaved place beneath his chin until it reddened.

“That will be difficult.”

“Yes,” the custodian said.

The succession clause gave Arun the stronger standing. Devaki’s branch could not claim the grant solely by maintaining the work during another holder’s absence. But the costs of necessary repairs passed with the revenues, subject to an account before the water households and the sanctuary. Neither claimant could turn a repaired channel into a new grant of every field it reached.

The chamberlain asked them to distinguish that last point carefully.

“Some of the fields are new.”

“Then establish which,” Nalia said.

He glanced at her. “Will you remain to do that?”

“No.”

“Then allow for the work when you tell me how quickly it may be done.”

She lowered her eyes to the page. After a moment she amended the sentence she had been preparing.

They continued until the room keeper brought food and threatened to remove it again if anyone put a wet pen beside the bowls.

At the public reading, Anju sat behind a household entitled to introduce her. The woman at its head had come directly from the sickroom. Her hair was loosely fastened, and she looked as though she had slept in her clothes.

“My mother says she remembers the men going to the channel,” she told the assembly. “She sent me because she is tired of hearing that everyone is waiting for her to die.”

A few people laughed, then stopped when they saw her face.

“She is also tired of waiting for her broth. I will answer what I can and go home.”

The chamberlain made room for her evidence before the formal reading. Anju’s account followed. She lost the order of events once, snapped at a correction, then checked her own account and accepted it. She had to ask her daughter for the name of the man who had brought the first warning.

Lali supplied it. Her voice carried clearly through the room.

Keshan began the text.

Riarhin watched Arun while the succession was read. Relief came into him so visibly that he seemed to sit differently afterwards, his shoulders dropping, his hands releasing the edge of his seat. His mother touched his wrist beneath the fold of his sleeve.

Devaki looked at the reader. She did not look away even when the clause concerning her branch was repeated.

Then they reached the repairs.

Arun’s expression changed. He asked whether the debt could be assessed separately from the right to collect. The custodian said it could be assessed separately, but neither could be accepted without the other. Venu stood to ask how a disputed repair would be valued. Another cultivator wanted to know who would decide whether a field had already been planted before the enlargement.

The room grew loud.

Riarhin had expected the words to produce a silence. They produced people standing, calling for witnesses, objecting to the choice of a witness and demanding to see the part which affected them. The chamberlain let the first burst run, then struck the table and began taking the questions in order.

Anju waited with both hands on her bundle.

When her household’s exclusion was reached, the custodian asked whether the emergency action was now accepted as having been witnessed. The chamberlain said that it was. Devaki began to raise a further objection, stopped, and asked instead whether the later widening of the cut was included.

“No,” the custodian said. “That requires its own account.”

Anju’s shoulders sank. Then she heard the next sentence.

The household could speak in the renewal. The claim about the widening would be heard without keeping it outside the gate.

Lali turned towards her mother. Anju pressed a hand over the girl’s and held it still.

They did not finish that day.

The next morning the benches had been moved closer together. The people who came carried food, cups and papers of their own. Arun sat with two of the gatehouse workers while Devaki’s daughter checked the account of the wall. Riarhin had not met the daughter before. She resembled her mother most when someone suggested that a sum might be approximate.

Riarhin was asked about the old breach. She described the stones she had seen and refused to give a measurement she had not taken. Venu nodded, satisfied. The clerk recorded a visit to be made when the rain eased.

By afternoon they had terms narrow enough for the people present to accept. Arun would receive the grant with its maintenance duties. Devaki’s proven expenditure would be repaid from specified revenues, with the water households witnessing the account. The old holdings would remain distinct from new reclamation until measured. Emergency release would be reported to the gatehouse and reviewed afterwards; permission could not be withheld until the houses were already under water.

Several people disliked the terms. They signed or marked them anyway. One household reserved a question about its boundary and received a date on which to return.

The custodian brought out the seal.

Riarhin stood at the back beside Berent. He watched the people move forward, each making a place for the next.

“Will it hold?” she asked him.

“For a while.”

She looked at him.

“That isn’t a poor result,” he said.

Arun received the completed record. Devaki’s daughter stood close enough to see that the repayment clause had not been omitted. Anju was called with the other water households. She rose so quickly that one of her vessels rolled against the bench. Lali caught it.

Devaki found Riarhin in the courtyard afterwards.

“You brought back what he needed.”

“We brought back what was there.”

“Yes. I know.”

She sat on the low edge of the well. Her face was grey with fatigue. Riarhin remained standing until Devaki moved a fold of cloth aside to make room.

For a while they watched people leave. Arun’s mother paused by the gate to speak to someone, saw Devaki and lifted her hand. Devaki returned the gesture.

“Will you stay?” Riarhin asked.

“Where would I go? My house is here.”

“I meant with the work.”

“So did I.”

Riarhin let the answer stand.

Devaki rubbed the place where her sleeve had pressed against her wrist. “My daughter will make him pay. She has begun enjoying the account.”

“She seems good at it.”

“She is unbearable.”

The affection in the word made Riarhin smile. Devaki smiled too, unwillingly, and then the expression went.

“I wanted the seat.”

Riarhin looked towards the empty hall.

“I know.”

Devaki stayed beside her until the courtyard was nearly clear. When she rose, she asked whether Riarhin had been given the onward introduction.

“Not yet.”

“Go and ask. He will forget something useful merely to prove the day has tired him.”

The chamberlain had not forgotten. The documents waited on his table, bearing the renewed authority and a feather enclosed beneath a transparent cover. He explained where the introduction would be recognised, which house must be approached for the next stage, and which costs remained theirs.

Nalia listened and wrote them down.

At the door, he called Riarhin back.

“If someone asks what we decided, take the copy. Do not tell them we gave every man permission to cut a bank.”

“I won’t.”

He seemed about to add something. Instead he nodded and reached for the next petition.

Chapter 26 — A Letter Read Aloud

Ailsa had written on both sides and then along one edge.

Riarhin carried the letter to the room before opening it. The seal was sound. Nalia had collected it from the carrier herself, paid what was due and put it into Riarhin’s hand, saying that the woman would return towards the coast in four days.

There was time to answer.

Riarhin sat beneath the window, where the light reached the whole page.

The six coats suited. I have agreed to make twenty-four, with a woman here who knows more about the cloth than I do and will not allow me to forget it. I like her. You would too, though the two of you should not be left to discuss my faults together.

Riarhin smiled before she had reached the next line.

The boy had begun lessons in navigation and had acquired an opinion about every vessel visible from the shore. His father had made him explain one of those opinions to a man who had spent thirty years at sea. The result had apparently been good for both of them. The girl had found a friend who spoke rapidly enough to leave the tutor behind, and now refused to admit that any of the words she brought home could possibly be rude.

Iona had changed her mind about a buyer. Mairi had taken paid work outside the estate and was delighted by the irritation this caused a man who had assumed she would continue doing his washing. Savi remained the authority on where anything actually belonged.

The bird is still ugly. I keep it where I can see it from the bed.

Riarhin stopped there. She read the sentence again, then put the letter down long enough to look at the square of sunlight on the opposite wall.

Beneath the window someone was drawing water. The rope squeaked, stopped, squeaked again. She could feel Ailsa’s hand at the back of her neck with such clarity that she turned her head before remembering where she was.

She took up the page.

There were practical requests. Ailsa wanted to know whether the inland cloth with the narrow dark border could be obtained without buying a whole merchant’s stock. Where should she send the next letter? Had the healer’s work held when Riarhin began carrying things she ought to have asked someone else to carry?

At the edge, in smaller writing, came the message from the girl.

She says you may keep the shell until you come home. She does not say you may lose it.

Riarhin took it from the shelf and put it on the letter. Then she laughed, wiped her face with the heel of her hand and began her answer.

The departure meal was held in the courtyard because the room keeper would not have so many people bringing food through the rooms she had just cleaned.

They had planned to leave before the next heavy rain. The upland carrier had come himself, inspected the company’s baggage and named a price which caused Nalia to ask him several sharp questions. He answered all of them without altering the price. She paid the deposit after he showed her where the animals were kept.

Now his assistant sat with Berent, explaining the loads. They used pieces of bread to represent the animals until Lali ate one and made the arrangement collapse. Berent accused her of attacking the supply train. She told him he should have guarded it.

Anju brought a covered dish and news that the first measurement of the lower boundary had been agreed. Venu arrived shortly afterwards with a different account of how it had been agreed. They were seated far enough apart to eat before beginning the discussion.

Mira came late, still wearing the plain working clothes in which she rehearsed. The drummer followed carrying something for the room keeper, who accepted it after asking whether it had been borrowed. Keshan and his wife brought the child. He was awake this time, staring at Cadan with the solemn attention of someone trying to decide whether a face would do anything interesting.

Cadan offered a finger. The child gripped it.

“Now you must stay,” Keshan’s wife said.

Cadan looked at the small hand and waited until it let go.

Riarhin moved among them with cups. She had bought the cloth Ailsa wanted, after asking Nalia to make certain the border was woven rather than painted. It lay wrapped with the return letter. She kept checking that no one had set a wet vessel beside it.

Mira noticed.

“A gift?”

“An order.”

“For whom?”

“Someone I want to see.”

Mira took her cup without making the answer larger.

Across the court, Arun had arrived with his mother. He spoke first to the room keeper, then to Anju. Whatever he said made her frown. He listened to the reply, glanced at Venu and sat down between them. Devaki did not come, but her daughter sent a basket of fruit and a note reminding Nalia of a payment which had been made twice.

Nalia swore when she read it.

“Against you?” Berent asked.

“In our favour.”

“Then say something grateful.”

“I am grateful. I dislike being corrected by someone who is right so often.”

She put the note safely away.

They ate until the light began to fail. The talk crossed and divided: a baby’s sleep, a broken cart, an old performance in which the drummer had entered at the wrong moment and been compelled to remain kneeling for an entire passage. He denied the last account until Mira named the person who had helped him stand afterwards.

Saikou laughed with the others. Riarhin saw him reach for the fruit beside him, pause, and offer it to Keshan instead.

The evening went on.

Saran came after the guests had left. He had been invited to the meal but declined, saying that he had work to complete before departing if the company would take him. Now he brought a small bundle and the covered lamp.

Riarhin had kept everyone back. The table held the copied diagram, Saran’s earlier account and a letter which Saikou had received by the same carrier as hers.

He opened that first.

“My mother asks for an account of what we have found.”

“What does she know?” Riarhin asked.

“That I requested the mountain records. That people have supplied copies. That we have been delayed here.”

“Does she know what I carry?”

“She has enough to suspect it. I have not confirmed that I have seen it in your keeping.”

Nalia drew the lamp closer to the page.

“What is she asking you to send?”

“The descriptions of the object. Measurements. A copy of any receiving instructions. The names of people claiming prior custody.”

Aurine looked towards Saran. He remained still.

Saikou turned the letter so Riarhin could read the relevant passage. Beneath the request, Nakato offered to arrange secure custody while the more dangerous parts of the journey were undertaken. The phrasing made it sound like a burden she was prepared to accept for his sake.

Riarhin read it twice.

“What will you answer?”

“That I will not send what would help someone remove it again.”

“She supplied the records.”

“Yes.”

“Will she stop?”

“Possibly.”

He took the letter back and folded it. Riarhin thought of the questions he had sent from the coast, the people they had reached, and Saran standing on the hill asking about green stone. She looked at Saikou. He was still smoothing the fold.

“Let us hear him,” she said.

Saran had brought a copy of an instruction from his own house. The paper was recent. The text, he said, had been copied many times. He identified the places where the older words were no longer understood with certainty before Saikou could ask.

The holding had been built around an object brought from Dhahara’s lower country. It had not been destroyed there. Something beneath the central chamber had kept its reach apart from the world beyond. The keepers served that separation and the approaches which protected it.

“A prince came,” Saran said. “From beyond Dhahara. The account says he left three hundred men before he reached the receiving place.”

“Three hundred dead?” Berent asked.

“That is how my house preserves it.”

“And took it out?”

“Yes. To Ashaan.”

Saikou’s hand moved to a second page. Riarhin recognised the copy he had shown her in Silkstrand.

“The country that became the Red Wastes,” he said.

Saran nodded.

Riarhin rested both hands flat on the table. Beneath her coat, the little wrapped shape remained as undemanding as it had been all evening. She wanted, abruptly, to put it into the middle of the papers and let everyone see how small it was. She kept her hands where they were.

“How did he reach it?”

“The defenses were breached.”

“By losing three hundred men?”

“That was part of the cost.”

Saikou tapped the instruction. “This passage names a reception.”

Saran looked down.

“An entry. The word can serve both.”

“What did your house do?” Riarhin asked.

“Kept the outer lamps. Preserved the approach.”

Nalia had been reading the custodian’s note attached to the diagram. She turned it towards him.

“She says the stone came from a house abandoned after its keepers were accused of showing a raider the way in.”

“The accusation survived. So did people who denied it.”

“Your people?”

“Some.”

Riarhin looked at him until he raised his eyes.

“I was not there,” he said. His voice had roughened. “Neither were the people who taught me. They kept instructions through years in which there was no place left to perform them. I know what I can do. I know what has been said of us.”

“Do you know whether it is true?”

“No.”

Saikou moved the tomb drawing beside the house’s instruction. The two approaches resembled one another. They did not agree in every part.

“Then we compare them,” he said. “You do not supply a missing action merely because your house expects it to be there.”

Saran’s jaw tightened. After a moment he nodded.

They admitted him with terms.

He would bring the whole surviving instruction, including the passages he could not explain. He would identify uncertainty before asking anyone to act. The amulet remained with Riarhin. No rite involving it would begin without the company being told what was intended and how it could be stopped.

Saran asked that the House of the Outer Lamp be named in the record of the return.

“If we get there,” Riarhin said.

“If we get there.”

“And the record says what happened.”

“Yes.”

Nalia wrote the agreement. He signed it with the same careful hand he had used on the drawing. Cadan watched him put the pen down.

“Will you carry your own lamp?” he asked.

“I will.”

“Good.”

Riarhin could not tell what judgment had been made. She was too tired to ask for it.

When the others left, Saikou remained to pack the records. He separated the copies they would carry from those entrusted to the custodian, then wrapped both against damp. Riarhin brought him another strip of cloth.

“Do you believe him?”

“I believe he wants to return it.”

“So do I.”

Saikou tied the wrapping. His repaired buckle lay beside the packet. He fastened it slowly and tested the stitch before lifting the bundle.

“We will have to keep asking the other questions,” he said.

They left through the market while shutters were still being opened.

Anju and Lali came as far as the road outside the town. The girl carried nothing this time except a piece of fruit she had been told not to eat until after breakfast. She kept turning it in her hand.

At the last shade tree, Anju stopped.

“Venu says the marker must move,” she told Riarhin.

“Must it?”

“We shall see when somebody measures properly.”

Lali bit into the fruit. Anju glanced at her and decided to ignore it.

Riarhin embraced her. Anju held her for a moment, then stepped back and straightened the fold of her coat as if she had been wearing it badly throughout their acquaintance.

“Write to the sanctuary. They know where to find us.”

“I will.”

“Not only if something terrible happens.”

Riarhin smiled. “I will try.”

Beyond the tree, the carrier was arranging the line of animals. Berent moved a bundle, checked the balance and spoke to the man leading the first one. Saran waited with his lamp. Nalia had the introduction beneath her coat, Aurine the medical supplies secured where she could reach them, and Cadan a repaired screen for the room keeper’s cousin at their first stopping place.

It looked awkward to carry. He had declined every offer to leave it behind.

Riarhin lifted her own pack. The weight settled across both shoulders. She turned once to wave, then went to join them.

Part IV — Above the Roads

Chapter 27 — The Last Good Road

The elephant took the bundle from Berent and gave it back.

He had been holding it where the mahout told him, both arms extended, waiting for the animal to lift it to the platform. The trunk closed around the cloth, raised it, paused and deposited it squarely against his chest. Berent staggered. The mahout began to laugh.

“What have I done?”

“Made it interesting.”

Cadan came to take the other end. The elephant investigated his sleeve. He remained still until it lost interest, then helped Berent lower the load.

Riarhin stood beside the corral with her own pack over one shoulder. The road from Darava had brought them through cultivated hills, past houses with drying racks which could be rolled under shelter at the first rain. Here the roofs were heavier, the yards enclosed against animals rather than passers-by. The air still held the warmth of the lower country, but a breeze came down from the heights with a clean edge she felt through her damp shirt.

Their onward guide had taken one look at the carrier’s arrangement and begun unloading it.

Her name was Dawa. She wore a short, patched outer coat and carried a little wooden measure tucked into her belt. She had introduced herself to each of them, asked who had hired the animals and then spoken to the carrier for so long that Nalia fetched a stool.

The lowland plan put the heavier loads together. Dawa separated them, divided two bundles and required a third to be repacked entirely. The carrier objected that the balance was sound.

“On your road,” she said.

“On any road.”

She pointed to the slope beyond the corral.

“Show me.”

He looked at it. Then he called for another length of cloth.

Berent listened while she explained the narrow turn they would reach before evening. He took the wooden measure, tested the width of the repacked load and moved the fastening himself. Dawa watched him do it without offering praise. When he finished, she gave him the next one.

Nalia sat with the accounts on her knee. The road-holder’s introduction had secured their passage through the last collection post without difficulty. At this yard it obtained a civil hearing and nothing more. Dawa’s animals would not go beyond the settlement above the first ridge. A second household kept the higher route. Its people would decide whether the ground was fit when they arrived.

“How will we know before we pay?” Nalia asked.

“You pay for the ground I take you over. You ask them about theirs.”

Nalia wrote that down.

The mahout came across the yard. He and Dawa examined a section of the route drawn in dust. He shook his head before she finished pointing.

“The outer track,” he said.

“It adds half a day.”

“The inner cut is soft underneath.”

“It carried the last load.”

He looked towards the elephant. She was rubbing one side against a post, with obvious satisfaction and considerable damage to the post.
