“She weighs more than the last load.”

Dawa stood. “The outer track, then.”

The carrier muttered something. The mahout answered without looking at him. Riarhin understood the phrase about whose bones would remain under the slope, and the carrier found something else to attend to.

They left the elephant at the upper yard. The mahout led her to water while the loads were transferred, speaking to her continuously in a voice Riarhin could scarcely hear. The animal placed each foot with a care which made the men hurrying around her seem foolish.

Riarhin waited until the mahout had finished.

“You know her well.”

He smiled at that, one hand resting against the trunk.

“Better than she knows me. She still expects me to be sensible.”

Behind him the path narrowed between stone walls. Dawa had gone ahead to inspect a drain, and the company waited in the shade. The air was cooler here. Riarhin took off her coat, folded it with the wrapped object safely inside and laid it over her pack.

For the first time in many days she could see where she wanted to put her feet and had no need to wait for someone to translate the decision. The ground rose towards a shoulder of rock. Beyond it, the track would meet their road again. From above she could inspect the next bend while the others finished loading.

She told Berent where she was going and climbed.

The rock was sound. Her boots found holds in the dry ribs between the wet places, and her left hand answered without hesitation when she reached above her head. At the shoulder she paused, looking back. The yard had become small. Saikou stood with Aurine beside the bundles; Cadan was helping the carrier roll the repaired screen into a shape which would pass between the walls.

Riarhin smiled and turned uphill.

“Stay.”

Dawa’s voice came from below the shoulder.

Riarhin stopped with one foot raised.

“Put it back. Where it was.”

She did. Dawa came up the lower path, slowly enough to make clear that speed was not required. At the shoulder she crouched and pointed beyond Riarhin’s boot.

A flat stone lay half hidden beneath thin soil. Beyond it the ground looked firmer, dry and pale under the short grass.

“The path?” Riarhin asked.

“The cover over the old water cut.”

She looked again. A line ran across the slope, visible now that she knew to follow it. The stones had been fitted to let people cross. The one she had nearly stepped onto had shifted, exposing a darkness too narrow to judge.

“How far down?”

“Far enough for us to spend the afternoon finding where it comes out.”

Riarhin lowered herself beside her. Together they looked into the gap. A little water sounded beneath it, moving somewhere beyond sight.

“I should have asked.”

“Yes.”

Dawa picked up a loose branch and pushed it into the soft ground before the crossing, leaning it at an angle travellers would notice. Then she took Riarhin along the proper path.

At the bend, Riarhin saw why the upper road had been built where it was. It followed a ledge between two old channels. One carried water. The other held a line of stones cut into shapes which had once belonged to a larger work: a hand, part of an animal’s flank, a face turned into the slope.

“Where did those come from?”

“A house higher up. Before my grandmother’s time.”

“A temple?”

“Perhaps. They keep the path.”

Dawa tested the next stretch with her staff. Riarhin followed her footprints.

At the night house, the hearth stood in the middle of the room. Smoke found its way through the roof slowly enough that Riarhin chose a place near the door. Tea came in thick bowls, too hot to hold for long. The food was harder than what they had eaten in Darava and tasted better after the climb.

Berent sat opposite a man whose coat had been repaired with three different kinds of thread. They compared the loads while eating. The man laughed at something Berent said, then gave him a piece of dried meat from his own portion. Berent accepted it as gravely as a favour at court.

Dawa spread a rough map beside the hearth. She used sticks for the paths and a handful of stones for the stopping places. Saran watched without offering to improve it.

“The upper crew sent down before the last rain,” she said. “Their store at the second shelter is sound. Beyond that, we need a report.”

“How old a report?” Saikou asked.

“From someone who has walked it since the thaw opened the western face.”

“And if none comes?”

“We wait. Or take the longer road while there is still time to come back.”

Nalia asked for the last point at which the choice could be made without losing the provisions paid for. Dawa moved one stone. Berent asked what could be left there for a return. She moved a second beside it.

Riarhin watched the small arrangement grow. The distance to the mountains had seemed vague until now, a direction into which Saikou’s papers extended. Here it had places where somebody would cook, a roof beneath which they might wait, and a point beyond which turning back would require food already left behind.

Dawa put the last stone away instead of setting it down.

“We will speak about that part when we know who is taking you.”

Later, Riarhin went outside. The clouds had opened above the ridge. Stars showed between them in such numbers that she found herself looking for familiar gaps rather than familiar shapes.

Berent came out carrying two bowls. He gave her one.

“Still hot.”

“I can see.”

“People keep telling me after I’ve burnt my mouth. I thought I’d try beforehand.”

She drank carefully. They stood beside the wall until the clouds closed again.

Chapter 28 — The Lease Stone

The crack ran through the word for return.

Nalia pointed it out because she could read that part of the inscription. The stone stood beneath a timber roof at the settlement’s entrance, its lower face dark with wet. A pale line descended from the top, crossed several names and widened at the word she had found. Someone had tied a strip of cloth across the opening. It had broken with the next movement of the stone.

A woman carrying a basket stopped to look at them.

“Did it glow?” Riarhin asked.

“That depends who is telling it.”

“Were you here?”

“I heard it break. My brother saw the light. He was drunk, but he has seen things sober that I would trust less.”

She shifted the basket and went on.

Beyond the stone, the road divided. One branch climbed towards a bridge whose near towers showed above the roofs. The other passed through the settlement to a yard filled with unloaded frames. No crew worked there. Men and women sat beneath the eaves repairing clothing, sorting tools and doing all the things which made the stillness of the empty frames more conspicuous.

Dawa spoke to a woman at the first house. Her expression changed. She asked another question, then came back.

“We stay here.”

“For how long?” Nalia asked.

“Until there is a route somebody will maintain.”

The Aeler official received them beside a brazier which gave out more heat than the room required. He had broad hands and close-cropped grey hair. His chair had been built low enough for his feet to rest firmly on the floor; the chair offered to Riarhin left her knees uncomfortably high.

He examined the Peacock Feather introduction with proper care. He asked where they intended to go, heard the answer and put the document down.

“The leased crossing is temporarily closed.”

“Dawa says the crews have withdrawn.”

“They have declined their obligations.”

“Which obligations?” Nalia asked.

He brought out an account. The crossing served the ore route and the higher settlements. Its maintenance had been supplied by local households under the lease. The administration provided iron, cordage and payment. The most recent allocation had been diverted after a failure at the mine approach. The crews had refused to continue until the missing supplies were replaced.

Nalia followed the figures with her finger.

“The money is entered as paid.”

“To the account.”

“Was it received?”

“The account received it.”

She looked up. Riarhin recognised the change in her face.

“Then the work has been purchased twice. Once with the allocation, once with the promise of the same allocation when it becomes available.”

The official leaned back.

“You are here for passage.”

“Yes. Over the work.”

He closed the account.

Outside, Nalia was angry enough to walk past Dawa before noticing her.

“They want to hold the money until the crews accept the old terms. It’s an advance dressed as arrears.”

Dawa listened, then led her into the yard.

A woman there had laid a torn carrying harness across her lap. One strap had parted near the shoulder. She turned it over when Dawa spoke to her, showing a stain darker than the leather.

“My son came down in that,” she said.

Nalia stopped.

“He is alive. Before you ask. He cannot close this hand.” She folded her own fingers to demonstrate, leaving two extended. “They paid us after that too.”

Dawa said the woman’s name was Peli. She had led the maintenance crew before the withdrawal.

“The money matters,” Peli told Nalia. “Bring us all of it. Then ask who will sign that another load may cross.”

Nalia crouched beside the harness. This time she asked no question until Peli had finished describing where the fastening broke.

Peli’s son came to fetch the harness before she had put it away.

He was taller than she was, with the soft unfinished beard of a young man who had been shaving in the hope that this would improve it. He took the harness in his left hand and began coiling the loose strap around it. The fingers of the other hand remained partly bent.

His mother reached to help. He turned away.

“I have it.”

“I can see.”

“Then let me have it.”

She withdrew her hand. He wound the last length himself, awkwardly but securely, and looked at the people watching him.

“What are you here for?”

“Passage,” Riarhin said.

“Of course.”

He went into the house. After a moment Peli asked Aurine whether she would examine the hand. She asked quietly, though her son could have heard from where he stood inside.

“If he wants me to.”

Peli nodded. She did not call after him.

Nalia went with Dawa to inspect the account kept by the crew households. Riarhin remained under the eaves. She could see the young man through the open door, trying to hang the harness on a peg above his shoulder. He set it down, brought a stool and completed the task before returning the stool to its place.

Then he came out again.

“You treat hands?” he asked Aurine.

“I treat what I know how to treat. I can look and tell you whether I know enough.”

He considered her, then nodded towards the house.

Inside, a little girl was sitting at the table with a length of cord looped over her toes. She was making some sort of pattern, crossing one end over the other and holding the work taut with her foot. She looked up when they entered and asked her brother whether he wanted the seat by the light.

He said he did. She moved without fuss.

His name was Toran. The injury had been cleaned and closed; the damage beneath it had remained. He could feel Aurine touch the fingers, though the sensation changed near the knuckle. She asked him to move them one at a time, watching his face when the smallest failed to answer.

“They said it would improve.”

“Has it?”

“A little.”

“Show me what you could do at first.”

He did. Aurine compared the movements and asked what treatment he had received. There had been ordinary care and one small working for the pain. The practitioner capable of attempting more lived beyond the toll route. Toran had an introduction, a sum put aside and no way across which his mother’s crew considered fit to use.

Peli stood at the door. He spoke without looking at her.

“I can cross the old bridge.”

“We discussed this.”

“We discussed you crossing it. I apparently cease to have feet when it is my turn.”

Riarhin looked down at her hands. Aurine kept her attention on his.

“I would want a healer who can work on the deeper injury,” she said. “I can help you keep what movement you have while you wait. I cannot promise more than that.”

Toran’s mouth tightened. Then he asked her to show him.

The exercises were slow and undignified. He pressed against her fingers, relaxed, tried again. His sister watched from the other side of the table. When he became angry at the smallest finger, she looked away and busied herself with the cord.

Riarhin remembered Dema telling her to stop pretending the last part of a movement was possible. Nakato’s money had brought that woman to a ship before it sailed. Toran’s introduction lay above the hearth in a wrapping which had begun to yellow from smoke.

She wanted to say that they would find a way. She watched Aurine give him work he could actually do and said nothing.

When the examination ended, Toran asked whether she would come again before they left. Aurine agreed.

His sister held up the cord pattern. He inspected it carefully, pointed to one crossing and told her it was wrong.

“It is supposed to be a bird.”

“Then it has landed badly.”

She struck him with the loose end. He laughed, and for the first time his mother smiled.

Outside, Peli took the harness down from its peg. She carried it back into the yard folded so the torn place remained visible.

Rani Jaya arrived while they were still in the yard.

She had come on a small, strong horse which disliked the crowded gate. The groom reached for its bridle. She told him to wait, turned the animal herself and dismounted on the side where she had room. The empty sleeve of her coat was folded and fastened close. A shaped leather cuff at her other side held a metal attachment which Riarhin first took for a tool carried at the belt.

Peli stood. Jaya told her to sit and asked to see the harness.

She examined the break with her one hand, holding the strap against her knee. Her face tightened.

“This is the one they said had been cut.”

“Look at the fibres.”

“I am looking.”

The official from the warm room came into the yard with a clerk. He greeted Jaya formally. She handed him the strap.

He did not take it.

“We have made an offer.”

“You have made several. Which one shall I answer?”

He named the repair allocation. Jaya asked when the cordage would arrive. He said it had been requisitioned. She asked from where. The clerk consulted a paper, then looked at him.

Riarhin watched Peli fold the harness. Her movements were patient and exact. She had heard this before.

Dawa moved closer to Jaya and spoke about the upper settlement. Two households had people at the mine. Their ordinary relief had missed the last crossing. Food remained below, but the medicines requested by the mine-captain had not gone through.

“Korr sent again?” Jaya asked.

“His runner came at dawn.”

“What changed?”

“The engineer is worse.”

The official said the man had been relieved of duty. Dawa looked at him.

“He is still down there.”

“Under supervision.”

Jaya turned towards the company. “Which of you treats illness?”

Aurine stepped forward.

“What illness?”

“Come and hear what they have brought.”

The runner sat in a house near the upper road with both boots off. A young woman was drying them by the hearth while he drank. He had a blister torn open on one heel and a fresh scrape beneath his chin.

Aurine sat opposite him. Saikou remained beside the door until he asked whether he might listen.

The engineer had stopped sleeping. He heard people beyond the lower door. Sometimes he answered them in a voice the runner did not recognise. At first the crew thought he was feverish. They brought him up to the rest chamber. He became calm there, then tried to return below as soon as they stopped watching him.

“Does he know where he is?” Aurine asked.

“Most of the time.”

“Does he know you?”

“Yes.”

“Has he struck anyone?”

“Not yet.”

Saikou came nearer.

“What happens to his hands when the other voice speaks?”

The runner looked up sharply.

“He closes them. Like this.”

He folded his thumbs inside his fingers.

“Does he open them when asked?”

“No.”

“And afterwards?”

“He says they hurt.”

Saikou asked about food, water, the lamps and the places where the voice could be heard. He separated what the runner had seen from what Korr had told him without making the man feel corrected. Riarhin watched the runner begin to answer more confidently.

At last Saikou asked whether the lower seal had been touched.

“No one has entered. The keeper is still there.”

“Has anyone promised the speaker anything?”

The runner looked at the floor.

“What was promised?”

“Only that we would listen.”

Saikou sat down.

“Tell me the words.”

Outside, Jaya waited beneath the eaves. The light had begun to leave the yard.

“Can you help him?” she asked.

“I believe so. I need to examine him.”

“The main approach is closed. The old bridge reaches the service way.”

Dawa made a sound of disagreement.

Jaya looked at her. “I know.”

“What is wrong with the old bridge?” Riarhin asked.

“Several things,” Peli said from the doorway. She had come while they were inside. “The ropes are not the worst.”

Saikou asked its name.

“The Bridge of Nine Echoes.”

He looked towards the high road, then back at the runner’s house.

“We inspect it first.”

Peli nodded. She lifted the lamp from its hook and handed it to the man by the door.

Chapter 29 — Nine Echoes

Peli took them before the sun reached the crossing.

The path followed a cut in the slope where the ground had frozen in shadow. Lower down, they had walked through running water. Here it hung in small white shelves from the rock. Riarhin tested each pale patch before putting her weight on it.

The bridge appeared between two shoulders of stone. Its far end stood in sunlight. The span between lay in shade above a chasm whose bottom she could not see. Ice held along one side of the near tower, thick around an old carved face which seemed to be looking into the gap.

Peli stopped well before the anchor.

“We speak here. At the bridge, use your hands.”

Berent set down the light coil he had brought. Cadan lowered a bundle of stakes. Saikou looked at the open space beneath the span, then towards the rock behind them.

“Does it answer from both sides?”

“Sometimes from the side you are going to.”

“Before you speak?”

Peli’s eyes moved to him.

“Sometimes.”

He nodded. “No names. No counting aloud. If you hear an instruction from the bridge, look at the person you think gave it before moving.”

Peli showed them the signals she used. Stop. Come back. Lower. Hold. She made each of them repeat the movements until she was satisfied.

Riarhin found herself enjoying the precision. It was work she understood. Berent checked the anchors with Peli while she and Cadan prepared the safety line. Saikou walked the edge of the approach without crossing onto the boards, studying the places where sound returned.

A small stone fell from the wall behind them.

Its knock came from the far tower first.

Riarhin held still. Then the stone struck near her boot and rolled towards the drop. The sound repeated, softening as it went, though she could not have said where it was going.

Saikou raised his hand.

They waited.

He took the mirror from its wrapping and held it so the approach, rather than the bridge, lay within its face. Peli watched him, her mouth set. After a while he lowered it and pointed to the place where the stone had come free.

Riarhin moved to it. The cut was fresh, the fragment ordinary rock. She showed him the piece which remained in her hand.

He nodded, and they resumed.

The first anchor was sound. The second had moved enough to open the ground behind it. Peli brushed loose soil from the gap and drew Berent’s attention to an older mark on the post. He measured the change with a strip of cord and showed Riarhin the length between his fingers.

A person could cross. A load would pull differently.

Peli indicated the secondary fixing higher on the rock. Cadan climbed to it, tested the ring without committing his weight and signalled back. Berent came to help. They worked a line through the ring and brought it down to the approach.

Riarhin stood where she could see both them and the span. On the far side, a pale shape moved beside the tower.

She looked directly at it.

Nothing stood there.

Saikou touched her sleeve and pointed to her feet. She had moved closer to the first board without noticing.

She stepped back.

The sound came again. This time it had the rhythm of speech. Riarhin could hear a beginning, a pause and the rising end of a question. There were no words she could hold onto.

Saikou went still.

His face changed so slightly that she might have missed it had she not been looking at him. His eyes moved towards the far tower. His lips parted.

Riarhin caught his wrist.

He closed his mouth. After a moment he put his other hand over hers, acknowledging the grip without asking her to release it.

The sound continued. She felt his pulse beneath her fingers.

Then he shook his head.

Once. Deliberately.

He turned away from the bridge and led her back to the place where they had agreed speech was safe. He did not speak until Peli had joined them.

“It offers information,” he said.

“What information?” Riarhin asked.

“Something I want.”

“Do you believe it has it?”

“I believe it knows how to make the offer.”

He wrapped the mirror with more care than it needed. Riarhin waited. He gave her no further answer, and she did not ask for one.

Peli sat on a stone. “My brother heard his own voice telling him the other end was safe.”

“Was it?”

“For him. The next load broke two boards.”

Berent and Cadan returned from the higher fixing. They had a sound second line, a measured movement at the first anchor and a span whose damaged boards could be bypassed by a person who knew where to tread. The far approach remained to be examined.

“I can cross,” Cadan said.

Peli shook her head.

“I am light enough.”

“You have not seen the landing beneath the far tower. If the last board shifts, you need to know where the old step lies.”

“Can you show me?”

“From there.”

He considered that and nodded. “Then I will hold the line.”

She accepted the offer. Berent arranged the belay and had them test it before she stepped onto the first board.

Peli crossed slowly. She stopped at each damaged place, lowered herself when the side rope dipped and moved her weight towards the boards which still held their colour. Halfway over, she paused without looking down.

Saikou raised his hand before Riarhin heard anything.

They remained still. The line lay between Cadan’s palms. Berent watched Peli’s shoulders. No one moved to pull her back.

She touched her forehead, then pointed to the far side. Their agreed signal: she had heard it and was continuing by her own decision.

Riarhin let out a breath.

At the tower Peli disappeared below the final board. The line slackened. Cadan took it in carefully, leaving the amount Berent had shown him. A little later she reappeared on the landing and raised both arms.

Clear.

She inspected the far anchor, the service path and the low opening where it entered the slope. When she returned she brought a broken iron fastening from beside that opening. She laid it on the stone between them.

“The path holds. The outer door has been forced.”

“Recently?” Saikou asked.

“The break is bright. No rust in it.”

He examined the fastening without touching its inner face.

“The relief party will need a place to stop short of that door.”

“There is one.”

“Room to turn an injured man?”

“Yes.”

“Room for you to withdraw without passing him?”

Peli thought, then changed her answer.

“We make room before we bring anyone out.”

Saikou nodded.

The journey back took longer. Berent carried the measurements; Peli carried the broken fastening. Riarhin walked beside Saikou. At a narrow part she went ahead, turned and waited for him to catch up.

He rubbed his eyes before fastening the satchel. Riarhin had seen him do little more than listen.

“Was it Amari?” she asked when the others were out of hearing.

He shook his head.

She waited, but he kept walking. At the next bend he stopped to adjust the strap of the satchel.

“There are things I cannot remember,” he said. “It offered to make one of them whole.”

“What did it want?”

“For me to answer.”

She looked back. The bridge was hidden by the rock now. She could hear only wind moving in the scrub above them.

Saikou fastened the strap.

“Come. We have enough to do with what we know.”

Chapter 30 — The Relief Load

Aurine took the heavy jar off the carrying frame.

The clerk put it back.

She looked at him, lifted it again and set it beside the wall.

“It was requested.”

“By whom?”

“The engineer.”

“Before he became ill?”

The clerk consulted the list. Aurine waited with her hand on the jar.

“It is entered here.”

“That tells us where the request is. When was it made?”

They stood in the settlement yard while the relief party assembled around them. Peli had chosen the carriers. Two were from households with people below; another maintained the old bridge’s far approach. Dawa would take them to the crossing and remain responsible for the return stores. Berent was cutting padding for the frames. Every time the clerk set something down, somebody else wanted the space.

The jar held a strong preparation used to keep a man working through a long shift. Aurine had smelled it, tasted a drop on the back of her hand and asked whether the engineer had already been taking it. The runner said he had. She told the clerk to bring the record of what he had been given, not another jar.

“What do you want instead?”

“Clean water vessels. Food he can swallow. Cloth. Something to keep him warm when we stop.”

“Those are ordinary supplies.”

“Yes. Find them.”

Riarhin helped her repack the frame. They left room for a man’s own belongings if he could be brought out. Aurine insisted on that too.

“He may want something which matters more to him than our spare pot.”

Riarhin put the pot aside.

Jaya came with the payment and enough people to make clear that it was hers.

Peli counted the money in front of her crew. Then she returned one sealed portion.

“For the repair,” she said. “We have not agreed to repair the crossing.”

Jaya took it back.

“The relief is separate.”

“The relief and our return. No ore. No inspection made in our names while we are below. If the route changes, I turn us round.”

“You will tell the mine-captain why.”

“If there is time.”

Jaya’s gaze moved over the crew. One young man looked down. Peli waited.

“Agreed.”

The Aeler official wanted the clerk to record that the relief had been supplied under the lease. Nalia asked which supply he had provided. He named the cordage being brought from the store. Peli told two of her people to inspect it before it was included in the load.

Riarhin stood beside the wall, listening. When the discussion turned to the order of crossing, she began to point out where a guard should stand. Peli let her finish.

“Who are you guarding against?”

“Anything coming from the far approach.”

“Then stand where Saikou puts you after I have brought the first load over. Until then, keep the near landing clear.”

Riarhin lowered her hand.

“All right.”

Peli looked at her a moment longer, then went back to the crew.

Berent carried the first padded frame to the low platform used for loading animals. He asked a man to lie on it. The man objected, laughing, until Aurine asked whether he preferred to discover its shortcomings while unconscious.

They lifted him together. At the first turn, the rear bearer lost sight of the front man’s signal. Berent stopped them, changed the order and tried again. By the third attempt they could turn through a space no wider than the service passage without tilting the man off the frame.

The volunteer asked whether he would be fed for this.

“Only if you lie still,” Aurine said.

He folded his hands on his stomach and shut his eyes.

Jaya remained after the others had gone to gather the supplies.

She stood beside the practice platform watching Berent take the frame apart for the journey. The leather cuff at her side had worked loose. She braced it against the timber and tightened the fastening with her hand.

Riarhin offered to hold it. Jaya declined, then changed her mind when the strap slipped.

“Here.”

The metal attachment was heavier than it looked. It fitted a tool to the shortened arm, held at an angle which would let her brace or hook without pretending she had fingers. Riarhin supported its weight while Jaya drew the strap through.

“You work with that?”

“When people let me. They have become tender about the sight of a ruler doing something badly.”

“Do you do it badly?”

“Some of it.”

She tested the fit and took the weight back.

The yard had quieted. A man passed carrying folded blankets. Jaya watched him go before speaking again.

“The lease brought work. Tools we could not have made here. Buyers who paid before the ore left the valley. My father wanted it.”

“And you?”

“I wanted the school they promised at the second settlement. It opened. My cousin’s daughter teaches there now.”

Riarhin looked towards the official’s room.

“He will say that is why people must accept the rest.”

“He already has.”

Jaya rubbed the skin above the cuff. The mark there was old, polished by contact.

“If I close the route, the households lose wages before the administration loses iron. If I let them carry on, Peli sends another child home in a torn harness. I have told both sides I can obtain terms. They are beginning to ask when.”

“You’ve supplied the relief.”

“Relief is a day’s work. I need an agreement which remains after you leave.”

Riarhin said nothing. Jaya waited.

Berent returned for the last piece of the frame. He waited until they moved aside, then carried it through the gate. Jaya followed him with her eyes.

“Do not let the account say my people waited while strangers saved them.”

“I’ll tell Nalia.”

“Tell whoever asks.”

Saikou prepared his cord in a room above the yard.

The window was open, though the air had grown cold. On a cloth before him lay the Gate Symbol, a little seal, two lengths of cord and the runner’s written account. Saran sat across the table, winding the unused cord into a loose coil. He stopped when Riarhin entered.

“May I?”

Saikou nodded towards the bench.

She sat. The cord in his hands had a knot unlike any she used. It made a closed shape without drawing tight at the centre. He passed the end through, examined the result and undid it.

“What was wrong?”

“The tension.”

He began again. This time he kept it.

“Is what’s in him the thing below the seal?”

“No.”

She looked up at the certainty.

“The keeper’s reports describe a bound fragment beneath the workings. The engineer’s affliction moves with him and answers through his body. It may have been drawn by what is below. It may have found an opportunity there. Removing it from him will not mend the lower seal.”

“Can it go into someone else?”

“Yes.”

“What do we do?”

He laid the finished cord beside the Symbol.

“You follow the boundary I give you. You do not promise to receive it, bear it or hear it in exchange for letting him go. If he asks for water, Aurine decides. If he asks you to open the lower door, you refuse.”

“How will I know which one asks?”

“You may not. That is why we agree beforehand.”

Saran looked towards the window. From the yard came the sound of the carriers lowering their practice load.

Saikou asked whether he knew the service passage’s older marks. Saran said he knew the forms represented in the runner’s sketch, not what had been altered in place.

“Tell me if they differ.”

“I will.”

Riarhin watched Saikou seal the knot. His fingers were steady. The room had become quiet around the work, though the yard below remained full of people.

When he finished, he turned the Symbol towards the light and inspected the fine line within the door’s frame.

“Will that hold?” she asked.

“It is sound.”

“And if you have to hurry?”

He wrapped the cord separately.

“Then I can spend something I would prefer to keep.”

They ate before leaving. Peli insisted on it, and for once nobody found a reason to hurry the meal. The carriers sat with their frames beside them. Dawa checked the clouds above the ridge and came back under the roof without comment.

Jaya stopped by Saikou’s place.

“There is a house beyond the toll station which keeps the Wanderer’s threshold. If you need assistance afterwards, ask for the keeper there. Tell them who crossed.”

He thanked her.

She moved on to Peli, and the two women spoke quietly. Riarhin caught none of it. When Jaya left, Peli stood, tightened the belt of her coat and called the crew together.

At the edge of the yard, Nalia handed Riarhin a copy of the agreement.

“I will bring the other through with the relief stores. If someone tries to make this into a different service, I would like us both able to read.”

Riarhin tucked it inside her coat, away from the wrapped amulet.

Then they followed Peli up the road.

Chapter 31 — The Door Below

The bridge took them most of the morning.

Riarhin kept the near landing clear as instructed. Loads went ahead of people where Peli required it; at the damaged places they were passed by line rather than carried. The new cordage held. Twice someone stopped in the middle of the span. Peli kept the people behind them back until they moved again.

Saikou crossed without looking towards the far tower. Riarhin watched his feet find each sound board. At the landing he turned and held up his hand for the next person.

Nothing spoke her name. She had been waiting for it so intently that the absence felt like another trick.

Beyond the bridge, the service way entered the slope beneath a low stone roof. Peli had brought tools to clear the turning place. They moved fallen rubble, widened the space beside the entrance and marked the footing before taking the first load below.

The broken door had been propped open. Its iron fastening lay where Peli had found it. Warm air moved through the gap, carrying the smell of wet cloth and men who had spent too long without daylight.

Korr met them beyond it.

The mine-captain wore a little bell tied close to his throat. He touched it once before speaking, settling it against the fabric so it would not move. His beard was cropped short, his eyes reddened by poor sleep.

“You took your time.”

Peli put a hand on the carrying frame.

“We brought it here.”

He looked at the load. Then he stepped aside.

The engineer lay in the rest chamber beneath two blankets. His boots stood under the bench, cleaned and set together by someone else. A cup sat within reach of his right hand. His left was closed against his chest.

His name was Hadrik. Aurine used it when she knelt beside him. He opened his eyes. Aurine repeated his name before he asked whether the relief had come.

“It has.”

“Good.”

He tried to sit. She helped him, supporting his back while he coughed. He was a broad man made narrow by the way he held himself, shoulders drawn forward, chin lowered as though he expected a blow.

Saikou stood near the entrance. He asked Korr to move everyone who was not needed beyond the next turn and to keep the lower door shut. Korr looked towards the passage descending from the chamber.

“The keeper is beyond it.”

“Will the keeper answer a signal from this side?”

“Yes.”

“Then give it. Do not open for an unfamiliar answer.”

Korr went himself.

Riarhin took the place Saikou indicated, where she could see both Hadrik and the passage. Cadan stood opposite her. Berent and Peli checked the way back to the turning place while the supplies were carried into the outer store. Nalia remained there with the crew, keeping the empty frames ready.

Aurine asked Hadrik what he had eaten. He answered, then corrected himself. He could not remember whether the last bowl had come before or after he slept.

“Do you know what is happening to you?”

His eyes moved towards Saikou.

“It says I asked.”

“Did you?”

“I asked who was there.”

Saikou came forward. He crouched far enough away that Hadrik would not have to draw back.

“I am going to ask you something. Answer only if the answer is yours.”

Hadrik watched him.

“Do you want it to leave?”

“Yes.”

The closed hand jerked against his chest.

Saikou waited. Hadrik forced his fingers apart with the other hand, breathing hard.

“Yes,” he said again.

They prepared the chamber with him sitting upright.

Saikou would not bind him while he could keep his own place. He laid the boundary wide enough to include Aurine, the bench and the little area in which Hadrik’s feet rested. Saran checked the old mark at the passage and reported where the later masonry interrupted it. Saikou moved his own line farther back.

The Gate Symbol stood uncovered on a dry stone. Beside it lay the sealed knot.

Riarhin had seen men prepare to fight with less care and more ceremony. Saikou tested the light, the footing, the width of the passage behind him. He made sure Aurine could withdraw without stepping over Hadrik’s legs. Then he asked the engineer to place both hands where they could be seen.

Hadrik laid them on his knees.

The left began to close.

“Look at her,” Saikou said, indicating Aurine. “She will tell you when to drink. You need not listen to anyone else.”

Hadrik turned towards her. She held the cup without offering it yet.

Saikou began.

At first Riarhin heard only the change in Hadrik’s breathing. It shortened, then slowed until the intervals between breaths became unbearable. His chest remained still. Aurine watched his face, one hand close to his wrist.

The voice came from the wrong place.

It sounded behind Riarhin, low and companionable, as though someone had leaned close to make a private remark. She did not turn. Hadrik’s lips moved in front of her. The sounds did not meet them.

Saikou continued. The knot lay across his fingers. With each phrase he tightened a different part of it, drawing the closed shape smaller without pulling the central opening shut.

Hadrik bent forward.

Aurine moved with him. She did not put herself between him and Saikou. His hand caught her sleeve, gripped and let go. She said his name once.

The voice rose.

Riarhin could hear words now, but they changed as she tried to hold them. An accusation became a request. A promise arrived in the tone of someone reminding her of an agreement already made. Beneath her coat, the linen pressed against her hip. She kept both hands in sight.

Saikou lifted the knot.

Hadrik’s shadow stood up behind him.

It rose along the wall until its head reached the roof. The body on the bench remained folded beneath it, shaking. Cadan shifted his weight. Riarhin raised one finger without looking at him. He became still.

Saikou spoke the final phrase.

The knot closed.

For a moment the chamber seemed to lean around the shadow. The lamps narrowed to blue points. Something struck the old mark at the passage with a sound like a hand slapped flat against a door.

Then the light returned.

Hadrik drew a breath so harsh that Aurine caught him by the shoulders. He coughed, spat into the cloth she held and began to sob without lifting his head.

Saikou lowered his hand. The cord had darkened along the knot.

“Keep the door shut,” he said.

Korr’s answer came from beyond the turn. “It is shut.”

They waited before moving Hadrik.

He drank a little. He could open both hands. When Aurine asked whether he could hear the voice, he shook his head, then asked whether it might come back. She looked towards Saikou.

“We will keep you away from the place where it reached you,” he said. “You will have people with you.”

Hadrik wanted his boots. Berent brought them and knelt to help. The engineer apologised for needing it. Berent told him the boots were badly designed and had no right to make a difficult day worse.

They lifted him onto the padded frame. Peli arranged the bearers in the order practised in the yard. Hadrik’s tools were wrapped beside him; Aurine had asked which he wanted, and he had chosen two rather than the whole case.

Saikou remained to close his work. Saran helped gather what could be removed safely. Riarhin waited at the passage while the first bearers turned towards the outer store.

The tremor began beneath her feet.

It was small enough at first to resemble a loaded cart passing above. Dust shook from a seam in the roof. Korr looked up and shouted a word which sent the miners towards the nearest supports.

Peli stopped the frame before it entered the narrowest turn.

A stone dropped from the roof behind them. It struck the floor, split and sent chips against Riarhin’s boots. Cadan pulled one of the relief workers back by the collar. The man fell against him. Both stayed upright.

The second shock moved the wall.

Riarhin saw the joint open. Air came through it, hot enough to touch her cheek. The old mark beside the passage broke across its centre. Beyond the lower door came a sound which had none of Hadrik’s borrowed breath in it.

Saikou was already moving.

“Everyone past this turn.”

Peli ordered the front bearers forward. The frame caught where the fallen stone had narrowed the passage. Berent and a miner lifted one end, cleared the obstruction and set it down again. Hadrik cried out. Aurine kept a hand on him, speaking close to his face.

Something dark ran along the broken joint.

It spread too quickly to be smoke. Where it touched the wall, the stone lost its surface, becoming a depth into which the lamplight disappeared. Saran stepped towards the old mark with the loose cord in his hand.

“No,” Saikou said. “That boundary is gone.”

He pushed Saran past him and set his palm against the new turn.

Riarhin saw the iron Symbol between his fingers. He turned it once, hard.

The little line within the door’s frame opened.

Light struck across the passage.

It had no source she could look at. It stood between the two walls, clear in the centre and edged with lines which folded into one another faster than she could follow. The darkness met it and spread sideways. Every loose grain of dust in the passage shone. Riarhin could see the bones of her fingers through the hand she had raised against the glare.

Saikou’s voice filled the space.

The miners worked beneath it. Korr and another man drove a support into place where the roof had shifted. The timber bent, settled and held. Peli’s crew got the frame through the turn. Nalia took the loose medical bundle from Aurine and dragged it clear so it would not catch beneath a bearer’s foot.

“Riarhin.”

Saikou had not turned. She went to him.

“Keep the way open.”

She looked at the people still on his side of the barrier. Cadan, Korr, the miner at the support. She took the miner’s free arm and pointed to the exit. He shook his head, tried the wedge once more and then came. Korr followed him, carrying the hammer. Cadan remained until Riarhin pushed him towards the turn.

The shining boundary bowed inwards.

Saikou staggered. Riarhin caught his belt before he fell against it. He had both hands closed around the Symbol now. His mouth continued to shape words, though she could no longer separate them from the sound in the passage.

“Everyone through,” she said.

He did not seem to hear.

“Saikou. They are through.”

His eyes found hers. He nodded.

They withdrew together. Behind them, the light narrowed to the shape of a closed door and remained there for one more breath. The darkness pressed against it.

Then they turned the corner.

Peli would not let them hurry onto the bridge.

She held the first bearers at the landing, made them secure the frame and sent one man ahead to confirm the far line. Hadrik lay beneath his blanket with his eyes open. Aurine sat beside him until the crossing was ready.

Riarhin had Saikou under one arm. He could walk, but only when reminded to place the next foot. The Symbol lay in his other hand. It looked like a small piece of old iron. Nothing in it answered the light.

“Can you keep it?” she asked.

He closed his fingers.

They crossed with the others holding the safety line. Halfway over, the bridge made a sound beneath them which might have become a voice. Saikou did not look up. Riarhin kept her eyes on the next board.

At the near landing Dawa took his other side. They lowered him onto a folded coat, and he bent forward until his forehead touched his knees.

No one asked him to explain.

Peli waited for the last person. Korr came over carrying the hammer and the little bell held silent against his throat. When he stepped off the boards, she checked the faces around her, then gave the signal to leave.

Chapter 32 — After the Working

Saikou slept while people argued outside his door.

Riarhin sat beside the bed with his satchel on her lap. The Gate Symbol lay bare on the table. Its narrow inner line remained open. The iron had cooled.

Aurine came in with the local practitioner. They disagreed quietly about a drink prepared for him. The practitioner wanted to restore warmth; Aurine wanted him awake enough to swallow before anyone put another thing in his mouth. At last they set the cup beside the bed.

The practitioner examined his hands. Riarhin watched the fingers turn beneath hers, the nails dark with stone dust which had resisted washing.

“Will he recover?”

“He is exhausted. Let him be exhausted.”

She covered the hands and went out. Aurine stayed to move the lamp farther from his eyes.

In the yard, someone was describing the light. Each account seemed to require it to have reached a little farther. Riarhin heard the official ask whether the working could be repeated at the main crossing.

She went to the door.

“No.”

He looked past her towards the bed.

“When he has rested.”

“No.”

Nalia appeared beside him carrying the agreement. “You have a relief account to settle. We can do that while he sleeps.”

The official began to answer. She opened the paper and read the clause concerning return before he could finish.

Riarhin closed the door.

Hadrik would not sleep without someone in the room.

He admitted it to Aurine after trying to make the request sound like a concern about his medicine. She asked whether he wanted the door open or shut. He said open, then changed his mind when he saw the dark passage beyond it.

Riarhin took a stool inside.

“I can sit.”

He nodded. She settled where he could see her without turning his head.

For a while he talked about his tools. The smaller one had been given to him by a woman who taught him to read the movement in a support before touching it. He wanted to know whether the case had come out. Riarhin said they had brought the two he chose. His face tightened, then eased.

“The others can wait.”

“Yes.”

“They should not have opened that service door with a bar.”

“Tell Korr when you’ve slept.”

Hadrik looked at her.

“Do you need me to say who ordered it?”

“Not now.”

He turned his face towards the wall. Riarhin thought he had fallen asleep until he spoke again.

“I wanted it to stop talking. I thought if I went nearer, it would finish what it was saying.”

She kept her hands on her knees.

“Did you understand it?”

“Sometimes. I wish I hadn’t.”

He drew the blanket higher. She sat until his breathing changed, then remained a little longer to be certain.

Outside, Berent brought her tea. He had washed, but a pale line of dust remained behind one ear. She told him. He rubbed the wrong side and she laughed, tired enough that it hurt her throat.

He sat beside her on the step.

“Peli’s people are paid.”

“All of it?”

“Nalia made them count it where the families could see.”

“And the repair?”

“Still being argued.”

Riarhin drank. The tea had cooled enough not to burn her mouth.

Saikou woke before evening.

His first question was whether Hadrik had crossed. His second concerned the lower keeper. Korr had sent a signal before withdrawing; the keeper had answered from behind the inner seal. A relief crew now waited above the old approach, with orders to maintain contact and open nothing merely because a voice asked them to.

Saikou listened, then asked for the Symbol.

Riarhin placed it in his hand.

He turned it towards the light, examined both edges and set it on the cloth beside him.

“It will need restoring.”

“Can you do it?”

“With time. And the right place to work.”

“Jaya mentioned a house.”

“I remember.”

She brought him food. He managed half before putting the bowl down. He kept his eyes on the bowl as she took it. She left it within reach.

“You were very good,” she said.

He looked at her.

“At frightening me.”

His mouth moved. It took her a moment to recognise the smile.

“I frightened myself.”

She sat beside the bed. Outside, the argument in the yard had become less urgent. Someone had brought more benches; the legs scraped on the stone as people made room.

After a while he asked whether the crew had got the account they were promised.

“Yes.”

“Good.”

He closed his eyes. She let him sleep.

On the following afternoon, Peli brought food from the crew households.

She had intended to leave it and return to the yard. Toran, who carried the larger pot, told her there was no space in the kitchen for another vessel and set it on the table before she could argue. The room keeper brought bowls. Berent found spoons which belonged to the house this time and took care to say so.

Hadrik came as far as the doorway. He held the frame of it with one hand, unwilling to enter a room already full of people. Peli moved a chair without looking directly at him. After a while he sat.

Saikou was awake. Aurine allowed him to join them on condition that someone else carried his bowl. He objected to the phrasing and submitted to the arrangement. Riarhin sat beside him with her chair turned so he could lean back without being hemmed in.

The crew discussed the crossing. Their account dwelt on a sack which had split, a bearer who had tried to scratch his nose at the wrong moment and Korr’s refusal to abandon the hammer. Korr, present and eating steadily, said a hammer was easier to retain than requisition.

“That one belongs to my uncle,” Peli said.

“He can collect it.”

“He has been dead eleven years.”

“Then he will be patient.”

The laughter startled Hadrik. He looked up, saw the faces around him and began to smile. Toran passed him the bread. For a little while he ate without watching the door.

Peli told Saikou that her son had an introduction to a healer beyond the crossing. She asked whether the old route could be used again after the new lines had been checked.

“The bridge must be inspected by your people,” he said. “I cannot promise that what speaks there has gone.”

“We know how to cross without answering.”

“Then take someone who will remember that if your son hears what he most wants to hear.”

Toran looked at him sharply. Saikou held his gaze without kindness or severity, and after a moment the young man nodded.

“I will go with him,” Peli said.

“When the route is ready,” Toran added. She looked at him, surprised, and he concentrated on tearing the bread.

Riarhin had put aside money from her own purse. She waited until the meal ended before speaking to Peli, prepared for the offer to be refused. Peli heard her out, asked whether it was a loan and said she would discuss it with her son.

“It is not a loan.”

“Then I will tell him that.”

She took no money that evening. Riarhin kept it apart from the company’s accounts.

Later, when the bowls had been cleared, Saikou remained at the table. The colour had begun to return to his face. He watched the last of the crew leave, each turning to say something to Peli or the room keeper before going through the door.

“I had forgotten how loud people become afterwards,” he said.

“Do you mind?”

“No.”

He rested his hand beside the empty bowl and listened until the yard was quiet.

The waiver came with a list of crossings at which it would be honoured.

Jaya read the list before handing it to Nalia. She made the official add the service route by which they had entered, since his first version named only the closed main approach. He said the omission was an error. She watched while it was corrected.

Peli’s crew were named in the account. Korr supplied the description of the support they had secured, the injured man brought out and the stores delivered. Saikou’s working was recorded separately. No one present agreed that the lower danger had been removed.

When the formal business ended, Jaya gave Riarhin a second letter.

“For the threshold house. They will ask what he has undertaken.”

“What should I tell them?”

“What he did. Let him tell them what he owes.”

Riarhin put the letter away.

At the yard gate, Peli was talking to her son. His damaged hand rested against his coat. He lifted the other when he saw Riarhin, a brief greeting without ceremony. She returned it.

Beyond them the cracked lease stone stood beneath its roof. Someone had taken away the broken strip of cloth. The opening remained visible from the road.

Dawa was waiting beside the packs. They would not leave until Saikou could walk the first stretch without being carried. She had arranged another night’s lodging and moved the provisions under cover before asking whether anyone objected.

Riarhin thanked her.

Dawa looked towards the darkening ridge. “Eat while it is hot.”

Riarhin drew the bowl towards her.

Chapter 33 — A Useful Offer

The factor had bought the warm room.

He had also bought supper for anyone willing to hear him, though he expressed the arrangement as hospitality and seemed genuinely pleased when the wayhouse cook brought more than he had ordered. He rose when Nalia came in, recognised her before she recognised him and held out both hands.

She stopped beside the door.

“I thought you were on the coast.”

“I was.”

“That is generally where I leave you.”

He laughed. His name was Vesran. He had worked for a trading house which Nalia had represented in a dispute over damaged cargo. She introduced him as a client of that house, then corrected herself: a former client. Vesran laid a hand on his chest and bowed to Nalia.

The room was warm enough to hurt Riarhin’s cold fingers. A pot steamed beside the hearth. Seven places had been laid, and Vesran asked the cook for another when he saw Dawa at the back.

“Your guide must join us.”

Dawa looked at Nalia, who gave a small shrug. She sat near the door.

Saikou had walked the day’s road without help. He still tired early, and Riarhin saw him notice the chair nearest the fire. Vesran noticed too. He drew it out, talking all the while about the road and the pleasure of unexpected company.

“How unexpected?” Riarhin asked.

“Your business at Darava has been discussed. The merchant carrying the renewal accounts told us which road you intended.”

“Who is us?”

He smiled at her across the table.

“People who would like to help.”

Nalia took off her coat. “Give them names. They may then decide.”

The offer came after the first bowl.

Storage under joint seals at a house below the toll station. Transport paid for through the next two changes of animals. Copies supplied from collections to which ordinary visitors seldom gained access. A fee for Saikou’s examination of an object whose history, Vesran said, interested several people of means.

Riarhin kept eating. The stew was good. She disliked that this made his company harder to resent.

“Which object?”

“A small ornament brought from Viterra. Its form is described in a coastal inquiry.”

Nalia put down her spoon.

“The inquiry which exceeded its authority and failed to obtain the cargo?”

“The inquiry which remained open after the cargo was released.”

He said it pleasantly. She looked at the paper he laid beside her bowl.

The thin man from the coastal hearing had signed one of the endorsements. A commercial mark stood below his name. Farther down, in a different hand, House Ira was described as an interested party whose participation would be welcomed.

Saikou reached for the sheet.

“Welcomed by whom?”

“The principals.”

“Has my mother agreed?”

“Her interest is well understood.”

“That was not my question.”

Vesran’s smile became still. He had encountered this tone before, Riarhin thought, though perhaps not from Saikou.

“I have authority to offer terms. I cannot speak to every exchange between the principals.”

“Then we shall establish what she has authorised.”

He folded the sheet without accepting it and returned it to Nalia.

Vesran looked towards Riarhin. “You would retain a seal. Nothing could be removed without your presence.”

“Could I take it away?”

“Once the examination was completed.”

“Who decides when that is?”

“The appointed examiners.”

She took another mouthful. Across from her, Cadan had finished his food and was watching the factor with an attention which had begun to disturb him.

“No,” Riarhin said.

“We have not settled the duration.”

“No.”

Nalia asked Vesran to walk with her after the meal. Riarhin followed at a distance, making no attempt to conceal it.

The yard smelled of animal dung and rain-wet timber. A groom had hung a lamp beside the stable door, and its light moved across the packed loads as people passed. Vesran stopped beneath the eaves. Nalia stood where he could see her face.

“Which of my records did you use?”

He looked towards Riarhin.

“She may hear,” Nalia said.

“You gave the coastal house the names of carriers you trusted. It was part of the service.”

“For their cargo.”

“Yes.”

“And you asked those carriers about mine.”

“I asked where an old acquaintance might be met.”

Nalia turned away for a moment. Riarhin watched her swallow the first answer.

“You also sent the allegation inland.”

“I sent a file which concerned a possible client. You know how often papers become separated from their useful context.”

Vesran took out a smaller folded sheet. He offered it to her rather than laying it down.

“Your own practice could have regular work. The difficulty would be exclusivity in certain classes of claim, not in everything you undertake.”

Nalia did not take it.

“You have used my introductions to follow the people employing me.”

“Your introductions reached households already trading with us.”

“Yes. I understand that now.”

She stepped back beneath the rain. It was a deliberate step, though she had room to remain dry.

“Send your future work elsewhere.”

He looked disappointed. Riarhin believed that he was. He had thought he was offering something reasonable, and he still expected Nalia to discover it after her temper passed.

She went back into the house.

Riarhin remained. Vesran put the smaller paper away.

“You will need supplies higher up.”

“We know.”

“Some of the cheaper terms depend on introductions.”

“Then give us the price without them.”

He inclined his head. “I will ask.”

Saikou waited until the room had quieted before uncovering the mirror.

Riarhin sat opposite him. She had seen him use it to follow a trace, and once before had seen evidence that his mother could reach him through the Witness. This preparation took longer. He placed a folded letter beside the glass, checked the name beneath its seal and asked her to keep the door closed.

“Will she answer?”

“If the message reaches her and she chooses.”

“Will she know where we are?”

“She may learn it. I will not put words into her mouth merely to avoid asking.”

Riarhin nodded. He began.

The room remained a room: bedding, a chair with a worn arm, the sound of someone coughing on the other side of the wall. Saikou held the mirror at an angle which reflected neither his face nor hers. For a long time nothing changed.

Then he listened.

Riarhin heard no voice. She could see the effort in his stillness, the way he waited past the point at which a man merely hoping for an answer might have begun inventing one. At last he spoke Nakato’s name and asked a narrow question about Vesran’s authority.

The answer occupied less time than the preparation.

Saikou covered the mirror and stayed seated until Riarhin brought him water.

“She has authorised no transfer to that house.”

“What did she authorise?”

“Her own offer. It remains available.”

“Did she say that?”

“Yes.”

Riarhin sat back.

“And what did it cost to ask?”

“I have opened a conversation she wished to have.”

He took the letter from beside the glass and folded it carefully. She watched him put it away without pretending the answer relieved him.

The morning price was higher.

Nalia checked each item. One increase reflected the actual loss of a combined shipment; another was explained badly enough that she refused it and bought elsewhere. They spent much of the morning carrying their own parcels between storehouses while Vesran’s servants loaded his animals in the yard.

Riarhin found Nalia beside the last sack.

“I made them useful introductions,” she said. “I was proud of that.”

“They were useful.”

“To more than the people I intended.”

Riarhin took one end of the sack. Nalia took the other. They carried it to Dawa’s waiting animal, set it down and caught their breath.

“Do you still want me with you?” Nalia asked.

“Yes.”

The answer made her look up.

“Tell me sooner when you recognise someone,” Riarhin added.

“I will.”

At the gate, Vesran wished them a safe journey. Saikou thanked him. Vesran began to speak again, then folded the offer smaller. They left him beneath the wayhouse sign.

Chapter 34 — The House of Repair

The threshold house had a broken gate.

Riarhin found this reassuring for reasons she did not examine. One leaf stood open, supported on a block while a woman worked at its lower fastening. The other remained shut, and everyone entering had to step around the tools spread beside it. A boy carried filings away in a covered bowl.

Dawa gave their introduction to the woman. She wiped her hands before taking it, read the first lines and looked at Saikou.

“Show me.”

He unwrapped the Symbol.

She examined it without touching the opened line. Her hands were stained blue at the nails, and there was a little burn beneath the thumb which had healed into a smooth pale patch.

“What did you keep out?”

He described the passage, the broken ward and the people withdrawing through the turn. She asked where the remaining keeper had been. He answered. She asked how long the new boundary had stood after he left.

“I could not see it after the turn.”

She nodded at that.

“My name is Metho. You may stay. I will find you a dry place for your papers.”

Saikou glanced towards the inner court, where an older man sat beneath a little shrine roof.

“Should I speak with the keeper first?”

“You are.”

He bowed his head. “Then I have begun poorly.”

“You have begun at the gate. That is usual.”

She went back to the fastening and asked him to hold the wooden leaf while she drove a pin through it.

The house served more than travellers.

People brought household seals, damaged lamps and pieces of worked stone whose purpose Riarhin could not identify. Some came to pray beneath the shrine roof. Others came to argue about charges, borrow tools or ask whether a threshold repaired before the snow would need tending again after the thaw. The older man listened to the prayers and directed practical questions to Metho. She sometimes sent the questions back when their owners wanted an assurance no repair could provide.

Their room looked onto a yard where women were dyeing cloth. Steam rose from the vats. The smell was sharp enough to remain in Riarhin’s clothes after she crossed the yard. At the far end, a little girl sat with a bowl of undyed thread, keeping it well away from the splashes.

Aurine went to speak with a practitioner who visited the house on certain mornings. Berent and Dawa inspected the loads for the next stage. Nalia found the person who kept the route accounts. Everyone appeared to have something useful to do except Riarhin.

She went back to the gate.

Saikou had taken off his coat. He and Metho were examining the cut beneath the sill, where meltwater had carried grit into a narrow recess. A second woman held the light. Saran stood beside them with a piece of paper but had not been asked to draw.

“Can I help?” Riarhin asked.

“Yes,” Metho said. “Keep people off this side until we are finished.”

Riarhin took her place. A man carrying a basket immediately tried to pass her.

“The other side.”

“I always go here.”

“Today, the other side.”

He looked at Metho, received no assistance and went round. Riarhin found the work less restful than it had sounded.

The first attempt did not take.

Saikou told her that himself when he returned after dark. He set the wrapped Symbol on the table and sat with his hands open on his thighs.

“Why?”

“The consecration would answer at the outer edge and fail at the place I opened. It is clean. The form is sound. I have not restored the relation through it.”

“Can Metho?”

“She can help. She cannot have made the choice I made.”

Riarhin put his food before him. He ate before continuing.

“The gate below their winter store is failing. It keeps more than animals from the grain. The drainage has shifted, and the old threshold was built for ground which no longer lies as it did.”

“You’re going to repair it.”

“With her.”

“Is that payment?”

“Part of what I can offer.”

He looked towards the wrapped iron.

“I asked for a boundary when there was no time to make one properly. I have time here.”

She sat opposite him. Outside, someone was emptying the dye vats. Water ran along the stone channel, stopped at a gate and was diverted into another yard.

After a while Saikou asked whether she had found anything to occupy herself.

“I prevented a man from walking through your work.”

“Only one?”

“Several. I was tactful with almost all of them.”

He smiled and went back to the food.

The winter store stood partly within the hill. Its door opened onto a covered platform where bags could be weighed out of the weather. The people using it disliked the proposal to close it for most of a day. Metho made them choose which supplies they would need before work began, then barred the door and kept the key herself.

Riarhin helped carry those supplies to the temporary store. She spent the morning with a woman who could lift more than she appeared able to and who refused every offer to exchange the heavier end. At last Riarhin stopped asking and kept pace.

Through the open space beneath the platform, she could see Saikou at work.

He had identified the old boundary before any stone was moved. Metho marked the revised course while he checked how it met the surviving work inside. Their disagreement concerned one turn of the line. She wanted to preserve it because the store’s inner seal had been renewed against that form for years. He showed her where the new watercourse would cross it after heavy rain.

They fetched the woman responsible for opening the store in winter. She described what froze first, what remained wet and where she had once found a row of small bare footprints after every entrance had been locked.

Saikou asked whether the footprints stopped at the same place each time.

“Until last winter.”

Metho turned back to her drawing.

They changed the line.

Through the next hours they lifted, packed and set the stone. Saran copied the old form before it was altered. The boy brought clean grit and took the fouled material away. Saikou spoke over the new work while Metho pressed the joining compound into the cuts, and the air beneath the platform became still enough that Riarhin noticed when a fly refused to enter it.

By evening, the storekeeper could open the door without the low whistling sound she had heard since the thaw. She tried it several times. Then she went inside and came out with a bag of grain for Metho’s household.

Metho accepted it.

Saikou stood aside while the store was reopened. He looked tired, but differently tired from the man they had brought across the bridge. When Riarhin offered water, he took it with a steady hand.

The restoration was made at dawn.

Riarhin watched from outside the work’s marked place. Metho sat opposite Saikou with the Gate Symbol between them. The keeper of the winter store attended too. She had brought the old key, which lay beside the iron without touching it.

Saikou spoke first. Riarhin understood the account of the mine and the people brought through. What followed used words she knew in relations she did not. Metho answered once, then let him continue.

Light reached the yard while he worked. The girl with the thread appeared at the doorway, saw the gathering and sat down without being told. Somewhere beyond the wall an animal complained about its breakfast.

The fine line inside the Symbol darkened.

Saikou stopped. Metho leaned forward, examined it and moved back. He began again from the place he had left, neither hurrying nor repeating what had already been done.

When he lifted the iron, the little door lay whole within its frame.

Riarhin had expected relief to arrive at once. Instead he tested it. A cord marked the limit of a small space, and the Symbol faced the opening. He spoke, waited, then asked Metho to try the boundary in the manner they had agreed.

The loose thread at her wrist lifted towards the iron and stopped.

She lowered her hand.

“It answers.”

Saikou closed his eyes for a moment. When he opened them, he thanked both women by name.

The storekeeper picked up her key. “You will come back and see the winter work if your road brings you here.”

“I will.”

Metho wrapped the Symbol and gave it to him.

They left two mornings later.

Riarhin had learned where the spare bowls were kept, how to reach the roof without crossing the wet dyeing cloth and which child would carry a message accurately if given the whole of it rather than a version thought suitable for children. Leaving required more farewells than arriving had suggested.

Metho gave Saikou an introduction to the keeper of the upper records. She described a path which had changed since the last account he carried. Dawa listened, asked about a damaged stair and drew the correction on the map.

At the gate, Riarhin looked at the repaired fastening.

“Does it open now?”

Metho pushed the leaf aside. It moved without scraping.

“Try it on the way back,” she said.

Riarhin stepped through with the others.

Chapter 35 — The Frozen Record

The keeper would not show them the record until they helped move a shelf.

He stood in the cave mouth with water running past his boots. Behind him, lamps gave the passage a soft yellow depth. The air coming out was colder than the air outside, and Riarhin’s first breath caught in her throat.

“The request is in your hand,” Nalia said.

“So is the shelf’s corner. Take it.”

She took it. Berent went to the other end. The keeper directed them towards a dry recess while a younger woman lifted the things from its lowest level: wrapped boards, two small boxes, a roll secured between strips of wood.

Saikou put his satchel down beyond the water and knelt to look at the channel.

“Where should this run?”

“Outside,” the keeper said.

“Show me.”

The younger woman took him to the side of the entrance. Dawa followed. Riarhin helped Nalia set the shelf down without putting one leg into the runoff.

The keeper’s name was Vesk. He had worked at the Frozen Record long enough to remember when a different approach had been used, and he regarded the newer path with the contempt people generally reserved for an unreliable relative. A retaining stone had moved above it. Meltwater now entered through a seam which ought to have remained dry.

Some people in the settlement said a promise had broken. Others said the new path had disturbed the support. Vesk asked the people offering explanations whether any of them had brought a shovel.

“The inner slabs are cold,” he said. “Keep the water from reaching their beds.”

Riarhin looked beyond him. Shapes stood in the cave’s deeper darkness, tall and pale, their surfaces marked by lines which caught the lamplight differently from the ice around them.

“Those are the records?”

“Some.”

He handed her a wrapped board.

“This is also a record. Hold it level.”

The older promises had been cut into frozen slabs standing on stone feet. Their writing lay within the surface, visible at one angle and lost at another. On some, the characters seemed to breathe against the light. A crack ran through one slab without reaching its edge. Riarhin could hear a faint ringing from it when she passed, too low to be a bell and too regular to be water.

The younger keeper, Tserin, saw her stop.

“Don’t touch it.”

“Does it always sound like that?”

“Since before I came here.”

“Whose promise?”

“Three households. A pasture, a winter crossing and a marriage. People generally want me to say a king’s.”

She took the board from Riarhin and placed it where Vesk indicated. It contained a later copy of a portion already lost from the slab. The archive kept both.

Saran followed the line of inscriptions without approaching them. Riarhin noticed his gaze catch more than once, then move away. She let him look. There would be time to ask what he recognised once the floor was dry.

Outside, Saikou and Dawa had found the blocked outlet. It could be cleared, but the new course would run against a support for the entrance steps. The mason who attended the archive had been sent for. Until he came, they needed a temporary diversion.

Berent fetched boards. Cadan and two people from the settlement carried stone. Riarhin worked with Tserin inside, lifting the smaller records out of reach of the spreading water. One wrapped bundle began to come apart in her hands. She stopped.

Tserin set down her own load and slid a board beneath it. Between them they lifted it without losing the loosened pieces.

“What is it?”

“A release from service. The family still asks for copies.”

“How old?”

“Old enough that they should not have to keep asking.”

Riarhin held the board while Tserin replaced the wrapping. The marks meant nothing to her. She kept them dry.

By midday the runoff had been turned into the old drain. The entrance steps remained closed. People went in and out along a narrower path which Dawa marked with stones. The mason arrived, examined the shifted support and began an argument with Vesk about what could have been expected to happen when the upper path was cut.

Vesk let him finish.

“Can you secure it?”

“Yes.”

“Then do that first. We can be right about the cause afterwards.”

Saikou had found a disturbance in the mark which preserved the cold of the inner beds. He showed it to Tserin, who brought the record of the last renewal. The keeper’s work maintained the cold; Saikou could help isolate the damaged stretch while she attended to it. He could not supply the missing piece of their observance from his own learning.

They arranged the lamps and cleared a place to work. Vesk required everyone else to remain outside the marked area. Riarhin sat on a stone by the entrance with Nalia and waited.

There was no rush in Saikou’s movements now. He knew what he could hold and placed his boundary where it would serve that purpose. Tserin worked inside it with a bowl whose contents smoked faintly in the cold. Riarhin watched the water on the nearest stone bead, tremble and become still.

A pale line spread through the bed beneath the first slab.

Tserin spoke to Vesk. He answered. She repeated the words while touching the stone with the rim of the bowl, and the pale line passed beneath the next slab without entering the crack above it.

When they finished, the sound from the cracked promise remained. No one had pretended to repair that.

Vesk brought Saikou a cup, then took Riarhin’s request from Nalia and read it properly.

The record they wanted had a paper copy.

Riarhin was relieved. Vesk appeared to find this faintly insulting to the cave, but he brought it out and weighted the corners with clean stones. The original lay farther in, preserved among undertakings made by houses serving the high sanctuaries. The copy had been checked by three keepers in different years. Each had left a note where the older hand admitted more than one reading.

Saran bent over it.

Saikou watched him before looking at the page himself.

The first drawing resembled the one from the tomb. The next did not. It showed a narrow passage beside the receiving place, with several positions marked along its inner wall. Lines ran from those positions beneath the central cradle. One stopped short of the others and was joined by a separate sign.

“The alignment,” Saikou said.

Vesk nodded. “The returning of the work to its appointed relation. That is the heading, as nearly as we render it now.”

Saran said that his house called it the attendance passage.

“That name also appears.”

Saikou laid the tomb drawing beside it. Riarhin could see the difference without understanding the mechanism. What had looked like decoration beside a route now had instructions attached: check, move, check again, close. The central place was reached afterwards.

“Afterwards,” Saikou repeated.

Saran looked up.

“If the relation has been disturbed,” he said.

“Yes. Has anyone established that it has not?”

“No.”

Vesk turned to the account beneath the drawing.

A power had been bound below the receiving place. The record named it through titles Riarhin did not know, then one she did: the Unmaker. The keeping work drew upon that bound presence to prevent the object above it from reaching beyond its appointed space.

“Another fragment?” she asked.

“Another holding,” Saikou said. “This is not the seal beneath the mine.”

Saran nodded quickly. “The houses did not administer them together.”

Vesk looked at him over the page.

“Not all the houses continued together at all.”

He turned the sheet.

The division had begun before the raid.

The receiving office claimed that its inherited ceremony was sufficient to maintain the holding. The people responsible for the inner work had required inspections which kept the outer house from exercising that office without their attendance. There had been petitions, counterclaims and a period in which the lamps were maintained while access to the inner controls was withheld.

Riarhin listened to Nalia render the older wording. She thought of the household arguments they had left below. The resemblance frightened her more than an account of men serving evil would have done.

Then came the entry concerning the prince.

Members of the outer house had admitted his people under a claim of restitution. The record did not say that every keeper agreed. It named objections, though some of the names had been lost where the slab cracked. It also named the opening of an approach which should have remained shut.

Saran stood back from the table.

“You knew there had been a reception,” Saikou said.

“I told you the word could mean entry.”

“Did you know this claim?”

“Yes.”

Riarhin felt anger arrive cleanly, without the confusion she had brought to the bridge.

“And you let us call it an accusation.”

“It was contested.”

“By your house.”

“By people who had lost their place and were being made responsible for every death that followed.”

His voice had risen. Vesk put a hand on the page.

“Lower it.”

Saran did, with visible effort.

“My teachers kept a lamp in a borrowed room. They fed people who arrived with nothing. They learned the names of places they were never allowed to enter. I do not know which of their ancestors opened that door.”

“But you knew some had,” Riarhin said.

He looked at her. There was hurt in his face, and anger, and a pride which would not let either become an appeal.

“Yes.”

She moved away from the table before she said something which would prevent him telling them the rest.
