At the entrance, the mason was fitting the repaired support. The ordinary knocks of his hammer reached her through the cold. She put a hand against the stone and waited until she could return.

Saikou was asking about the corrections made to the receiving sequence. Saran answered each question. He did not touch the page.

They made three copies of the alignment instructions.

One remained with Vesk, checked against the original while Tserin attended. One would go to the threshold house so that the work was held in more than a single place. The third travelled with them, accompanied by the notes recording what had been uncertain in the reading.

No copy supplied the full arrangement of the temple. Walls might have fallen. Controls might have been moved. A diagram which assumed a maintained approach could not tell them what the prince’s men had broken on their way out.

Riarhin asked Vesk whether they might take the older paper for comparison on the mountain.

“No.”

She nodded. She had expected the answer and needed to hear it anyway.

Before they left, Tserin showed her the slab from which the account had been made. Frost lay across part of the writing. Behind it, characters caught the light and vanished as she shifted her head.

“The Ice-Lama sat without warmth until her body became still,” Tserin said. “Some people tell it as if stillness were all she left us.”

“What do you say?”

“She left people to tend what she would no longer tend herself. We keep the records. We do not sit beside them and hope to become equally cold.”

She put a cover back over the opening through which they had brought the light. The marks disappeared.

Outside, the repaired steps had been reopened. Saran waited below them, holding his covered lamp in both hands. Riarhin walked past him to join the others. He followed without asking whether he still might.

Chapter 36 — The Keeper’s Meal

Vesk’s daughter wanted the stove moved.

Her father said it had stood in that place since before she was born. She replied that she had been cold at that end of the table for her entire life and therefore knew the matter thoroughly. Tserin agreed with her. Vesk looked towards the visitors, found no assistance and sat where the cold was said to be worst.

Riarhin took the chair beside him. The food came in a large pot, carried by a young man who paused at the doorway until somebody moved a basket from his path. He put it down, flexed his hands and accused his sister of filling it more heavily because guests were present.

She told him to acquire stronger hands.

The argument continued while they ate. Nalia listened, smiling to herself. Riarhin caught enough to follow its shape, though not every insult. For once she left the parts she did not understand alone.

Saikou sat across from Vesk. Saran had chosen the end of the table, close to the door. Nobody had excluded him. Nobody asked him to come nearer.

After the first bowls, Vesk’s daughter asked where the company would spend the night beyond the maintained road. Dawa described the staging camp and the household whose guides might go higher. The woman knew one of them and supplied an unflattering story about a young man who had once attempted to impress her by walking through a stream without removing his boots.

“He was fourteen,” Dawa said.

“He was wet.”

Berent asked whether it had worked. She considered this, then admitted that she had lent him dry stockings. The room enjoyed her defeat. Vesk took another serving while attention was elsewhere.

Saran spoke when the bowls had been cleared.

The younger people had gone to attend to the animals. Tserin stayed, mending a glove in the lamplight. Riarhin had thought Saran might leave with the others. Instead he set his cup down and looked towards her.

“There are eleven people at the house now.”

She waited.

“Two children. An old woman who was taught the receiving verses by someone who could no longer walk to a shrine. People who mend lamps in other households and are paid less if they give our name.”

“Do they know why?”

“They know the account told against us.”

“Your account.”

He pressed his lips together. Then he nodded.

“I thought I might return with something more than another denial.”

Riarhin looked at the covered lamp beside his chair. Its metal had been polished where fingers lifted the lid. He had carried it through rain, the bridge and the mine without once setting it where it could be kicked.

“What would the office give them?”

“A place which was ours. Work recognised as ours. Food supplied because the work mattered.”

“And who would lose their place?” Cadan asked.

Saran turned towards him.

“There may be no one there.”

“Then who would you refuse when they came?”

Tserin stopped sewing. Saikou remained still.

Saran looked down at his cup. “People who sought to take what was kept.”

“The prince’s people said they were returning something,” Riarhin said.

“Yes.”

She wanted him to defend himself again. Anger was easier while he gave it something to strike. Instead he sat with his hands beside the cup and waited for her decision.

“You come as someone who knows part of the work,” she said. “You have no office to claim from us. If the instruction and the work disagree, we stop. You do not complete a ceremony while we are finding out why.”

“I understand.”

“I need you to agree.”

He raised his head.

“I agree.”

She believed he meant it. That did not settle whether he would recognise the moment at which the promise cost him what he had come to obtain.

The stove was moved the next morning.

Not far. Far enough for Vesk to insist that no meaningful change had occurred and for his daughter to stand at the previously cold end of the room with an expression of satisfaction. Berent helped lift the base. Cadan held the flue while the young man fitted it. Riarhin opened the door when smoke began to come the wrong way and was told she should have opened it sooner.

Saikou watched from a chair while polishing the mirror’s cover. At last he told them about a stove Amari had once bought for a room which possessed no chimney.

“Did he know?” Berent asked.

“He had inspected the room.”

“Then why buy it?”

“It was an excellent stove.”

Dawa laughed. Saikou continued, warming to the account. Amari had proposed several ways of venting the smoke, each of which required the landlord to overlook damage to a part of the house he valued. The stove remained outside under a covering. Every guest admired it. Amari sold it at a profit when they left.

“Was that his intention?” Riarhin asked.

“He said so afterwards.”

Saikou began to laugh before finishing the sentence. He put the cover down because his hands were no longer steady. The others laughed with him, and when the laughter passed he remained smiling at the empty space before the hearth.

Riarhin took the mirror cover and set it where no ash would reach it.

Tserin met them at the archive path with a small wrapped token from the keeper.

It bore the mark under which the copies had been made. Vesk’s message permitted them to show the record to custodians farther along the route. It did not authorise entry to anyone else’s holding. He had made that distinction twice, Tserin said, and expected her to repeat it.

She gave Saikou a second instruction concerning the temple diagram.

“The empty interval here must remain empty until the alignment is tested.”

She pointed to the place where one line stopped before joining the others.

“Why?”

“The old reading calls it the listening space. Filling it completes the presentation. The keeping work is meant to be established first.”

Saikou made her show him on the copy and repeated the sequence back. Saran watched without speaking.

Riarhin took the document after him. The distinction was no wider than her smallest finger. She imagined someone eager to finish a damaged pattern, to make every part agree with the beautiful whole, and felt cold despite the morning sun.

“Leave it open,” she said.

“Until you know what it closes,” Tserin answered.

They parted at the bend. Dawa led them towards the upper road, where the air was clear enough to show a long white ridge beyond the nearer hills.

Riarhin walked with Saran for a while. She did not ask him for another assurance. At a narrow place he waited for her to pass, then followed, keeping the covered lamp clear of the rock.

Chapter 37 — Yak-Mother’s Camp

Pema would sell them directions. She would not guide them.

She made the distinction while removing a stone from a young animal’s hoof, and required Saikou to wait until she had finished before asking the next question. A bone needle held her hair in place. When the animal shifted its weight, she spoke to it in a low voice which contained more irritation than tenderness. It became still.

“There,” she said, setting the foot down. “You may complain about something else.”

The animal nosed her coat. She pushed its head away and turned to the travellers.

Black wool tents stood along the sheltered slope. Smoke moved low between them before rising into the clear air. Riarhin could smell milk turning sour, dung fuel and the sharp grease of a cooking pot. Far above, the ridgeline appeared close enough to reach before the light changed. Her lungs disagreed with the appearance.

Pema looked at each of them while Dawa made the introductions. Her gaze lingered on Saikou, who had stopped speaking to recover his breath.

“Good,” she said.

He looked up.

“You stop before pretending you need not.”

Dawa laughed. Pema embraced her, then called for someone to show the visitors where to put their loads.

They stayed through several clear mornings.

The first walk took them no farther than a spring beyond the camp. Riarhin reached it angry at how little ground they had covered. She drank, stood too quickly and had to sit again while the light altered at the edges of her sight.

Somo, one of the guides willing to discuss the higher route, sat beside her without comment. She wore her boots tied differently at each ankle and carried a short staff worn smooth near its head.

“How long?” Riarhin asked.

“Until you can stand.”

“I meant the walk.”

“I know.”

Riarhin almost laughed, then discovered she lacked the breath for it.

Somo waited. When they returned, she carried no part of Riarhin’s load, but she made her reduce it before the next morning’s attempt. The youngest guide, Dorun, took the removed items to the store tent and marked the bundle in front of Nalia.

“Will we need them?” Nalia asked.

“On the way back, perhaps. Which is why they remain here.”

Pema provided a leather map. She made Dawa identify the route on it before showing the two places where the last thaw had changed the approach. One crossing was no longer used. The other might be used in the morning if the night remained cold enough. Somo disagreed about the second. Pema heard her out, asked when she had last walked it and amended the instruction.

Riarhin watched Saikou copy the correction.

Berent’s first hauling station failed with a sack of fuel in place of a person.

The brace held. The line ran cleanly. The man at the return end could not reach the stopping knot without leaving the place where his weight mattered. Dorun caught the sack before it struck the ground and put it down with exaggerated care.

“I would prefer not to be that one.”

Berent looked at the arrangement, rubbed his face and said something rude about his own intelligence.

They moved the return line. Cadan took the place beside the brace and practised holding while Nalia gave the signals. Riarhin went over the short drop with the rope secured around her, found a foothold and waited to be lowered the rest of the way.

The line stopped.

She looked up. Berent was breathing too hard to answer her at once. Somo stood behind him with one hand raised. Hold.

Riarhin waited. The rope pressed into her coat. She could have climbed down, but that would have taught them nothing about bringing someone up when climbing was impossible.

At last the line moved. They lowered her smoothly, paused and brought her back without anyone changing stations. Berent remained seated until his breathing settled.

“We need shorter pulls,” he said.

“And another person ready to take your place,” Somo answered.

He nodded. “Show me where you want the change.”

They worked until they could do it without speaking. The ground was easy. The weather was good. Riarhin found herself grateful for both rather than impatient to make the practice resemble danger.

Saikou checked his Symbols inside the tent while Aurine inspected the medical supplies with Somo.

Some things would be left behind. Others were divided so that losing one pack would not leave them without cloth, food or the means to make warmth. Nalia objected to the abandonment of a thick packet of copied accounts until Saikou identified the pages they actually needed. She kept a separate list of what stayed in Pema’s store.

Saran opened his lamp for inspection. The guide weighed it in her hand.

“This goes instead of something.”

“I will carry it.”

“You already carry something. Show me what leaves the pack.”

He took out a second garment. Somo returned it at once.

“Something which does not keep you alive.”

At last he removed the little wooden box in which the lamp usually travelled. They wrapped it in spare cloth and fitted it among the softer things. He disliked the arrangement but accepted it.

Riarhin laid Remorse beside her pack. The Last Blade remained at her belt. She considered the weight of each and put the sword back. She distrusted a choice made solely because it resembled an earlier version of herself.

She took out the linen bundle last.

Everyone looked towards her.

“We should speak before we go farther.”

She told them what she knew and what she had done.

Not all at once. Saikou stopped her when she passed too quickly over the temple account. Nalia asked which history had come from him and which Riarhin had heard before. Aurine asked when she had last seen anyone wear the amulet.

“Veralyn.”

The name remained between them.

Riarhin described the gift, the queen’s pleasure in it, the success which had seemed at first an extension of things already possible. The army, the court, the people who found themselves agreeing. She could not measure which part belonged to the object. She could name what happened while it was worn.

“You thought it was a trinket,” Nalia said.

“I wanted to.”

She looked down at the wrapping. There was a darkened place in the linen where her thumb had held it often.

“I had worked for it. People had died getting me far enough to take it away. I brought it back and gave it to someone who wanted it. That is what I did.”

No one hurried to improve the statement for her.

Saikou set the copied diagrams on the cloth between them.

“The accounts give us reason to believe the holding can separate it from those beyond the chamber. They do not give us certainty about the condition of the apparatus now.”

“Nor about what we will become while carrying it,” Aurine said.

“No.”

Somo had listened without looking at the bundle. Now she asked whether it would endanger the camp if they left it there.

“It may,” Riarhin said.

“Then it goes with you. No opening it here to satisfy anyone’s curiosity.”

Riarhin nodded.

Berent asked what would make them turn back.

They named the conditions while Nalia wrote. Too little breath to work safely. An approach they could not secure for retreat. A failed alignment test. A sequence which drew power towards the receiving place before the keeping field was established. An injured person who could not be brought out by the people remaining.

Cadan asked who would give the order if Riarhin wished to continue.

She looked at him.

“Somo on the approach. Saikou on the working. Any of you can tell me what I am failing to see.”

“And if you see it?”

She looked round the faces turned towards her. She had hoped to answer him alone.

“Then the same conditions hold.”

Nalia wrote that too.

The guides agreed to take them to the outer approach and establish the retreat line. They would enter no inner holding. If the company failed to return within the agreed window, they would wait through the reserve period only while weather and supplies permitted it. Pema would hold the lower stores and send help along the maintained route when there was someone to send.

Riarhin listened without trying to purchase a larger promise.

That evening she sat outside the tent while the animals were brought into shelter. Dorun passed carrying a small one which objected to every step. He put it down beside its mother, who smelled it, accepted the return and immediately began pushing it towards the place she preferred.

“You see?” he said to Riarhin. “Everyone has instructions.”

She laughed. Above them the last light held on the snow long after the camp had gone dark.

Chapter 38 — Beyond the Bell

Khardung’s message arrived with a man who refused to come inside until the sweat beneath his clothes had cooled.

Dawa brought him tea. He drank standing, holding the cup with both hands. The message was carried on a small slate which had to be warmed against the body before its writing became clear. Nalia thought at first that the surface was blank. Saikou put it inside his coat and waited.

The Vent-Prior wanted an account of the disturbed workings, assurances concerning the road and an undertaking about the danger beneath the Ninth Seal. Riarhin heard the proposed promise and shook her head before Nalia finished translating it.

“We cannot promise to seal that forever.”

The messenger said the wording was customary.

“Then he can have an uncommon answer.”

Saikou examined the slate again.

“We can report what we found. We can identify the door and the broken ward. We can undertake not to disturb the remaining seal on our return. That is what we know how to promise.”

The man looked towards Dawa. She gave him no rescue from the answer.

He stayed until they had written their terms. The Vent-Prior’s introduction would carry weight among the houses under his charge; beyond those, Pema’s directions and the guides’ relationships would matter more. Nalia had the limits entered alongside the promise.

When the messenger left, he carried the honest account, a refusal and a copy of the route they intended to take. Riarhin watched him descend until the path hid him.

“He may be angry,” she said.

Saikou folded the cloth in which the slate had come.

“He has reasons.”

The coast’s last packet had travelled with the same supply train as the messenger.

Riarhin found Ailsa’s letter beneath a note about the delivered cloth. Its edges had softened in transit, but the writing remained clear. The border had pleased the buyer. The girl had begun a collection of shells too large for the shelf originally assigned to it. The boy had been invited to visit a coastal school where his tutor believed he might obtain a place.

He is trying very hard not to seem pleased. I am trying less hard.

Riarhin read that line to the end and felt the small uneasiness it brought. The school belonged to the coastal order whose ships and courts they had spent so much effort escaping. It would also teach the boy things she could not teach him. Ailsa had met the tutor, spoken to two other mothers and asked what the fees would become after the first term. Riarhin read the account of the visit twice before turning the page.

There was a stain beside the closing lines, perhaps tea, perhaps water from the crossing. Riarhin held the page closer to the light.

I miss the weight of you beside me. That is the only sensible account I can give. Come back when the work is done. Tell me when it cannot be done. Do not leave me to invent both answers.

She kept the letter open on her knee while the light moved across the tent wall.

Then she wrote where she was, who would know if they returned, and how the reply might reach Pema’s camp. She described the morning walks, the animals and Saikou repairing his own strap. She told Ailsa that the healer’s work had held. Her left hand was strong. She could carry her share.

I have not done what I came to do yet, she wrote. I have people with me who will tell me if I cannot. I have promised to listen.

She nearly added that there was no reason to worry. Instead she finished the letter with what she wanted and sealed it before she could make it easier to read.

Dawa went down with the returning carriers.

Her part of the route was complete. She had arranged where the company’s stores would wait and given Somo the account of their preparation. Riarhin paid what remained due. Dawa counted it, put it away and embraced Pema with the ease of someone who expected another meeting.

At the edge of the camp, Berent offered his hand. She took it.

“Short pulls,” she told him.

“I remember.”

“Remember while tired.”

He nodded. She went down the path with the others. Riarhin remained until the loads disappeared below the first turn.

The camp felt quieter afterwards.

They left before the sun reached the tents.

Somo led. Dorun moved along the line as needed, checking a fastening, helping an animal around a turn, waiting for someone whose breath had shortened. The company carried less than it had ever carried together. Every omitted thing had been discussed until Riarhin could have named its place in the store without looking at Nalia’s list.

The first day ended at a shelter built low against a slope. The entrance faced away from the wind. Inside, old soot blackened the stone. They heated food, dried what needed drying and slept close enough that Riarhin woke once with somebody else’s knee against her back.

The next morning, frost held on the shaded side of their packs.

The path crossed a pale field of broken stone. Somo halted them where a line of cairns divided. The older route continued straight. The newer one bent towards a ridge which looked needlessly steep.

“We go round,” she said.

No one argued. Riarhin looked along the abandoned line and saw where the far cairn stood on a shelf with nothing beneath one edge. Snow had filled the gap beyond it, making the way appear continuous.

At the top of the ridge she stopped to breathe. Below, the world opened into a basin of dark rock and old snow. A wall crossed its far side. From this distance it resembled a natural seam until she saw the right angle where it met the slope.

The temple stood beyond it.

There were no bright roofs. Much of the outer building lay beneath drifted snow, and a broken line of pillars followed a court whose floor she could not see. Higher up, a dark opening faced the basin. The air before it seemed clearer than the air around it, as though distance had been cut away from that one place.

Saikou came beside her and stopped.

“Do you see it?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“What is it?”

“A reason to begin from farther away.”

Somo had already turned towards a sheltered hollow on their side of the basin. They would make the last camp there, inspect the approach in the remaining light and enter only after rest.

Riarhin followed her down.

At the camp, Berent checked the retreat line while Dorun prepared the anchors. Somo marked the place beyond which the guides would not go. Saikou and Saran laid the copied plans beneath a stone so the wind could not turn them. Aurine made everyone drink before they began discussing the next day.

The bell at Dorun’s throat gave a small clear sound. He touched it, waited and continued with his work.

Riarhin sat apart for a moment, facing the wall across the basin. The linen bundle lay inside her coat. She had carried it through rooms where people laughed, through arguments over bowls, beside children who wanted to keep small beautiful things. It had remained small through all of it.

She took out the girl’s shell instead.

Its worn edge caught against the rough skin of her thumb. She turned it once and put it back, separate from the other object.

Somo called her to eat.

Riarhin rose and went.

Part V — The House That Refuses

Chapter 39 — The Outer Wall

In the morning Dorun dropped a spoon, and everyone watched it fall.

It struck the lip of the cooking pot and landed in the snow. He picked it up, wiped it on his sleeve and looked round at them.

“What?”

Nobody had an answer worth giving him. Aurine held out her bowl.

The sun reached the upper wall while they ate. It brought out colour in the stone: a broad band of red beneath the black weathering, yellow still caught in the underside of a carved lip. The pillars belonged to a court enclosed on three sides. Along the fourth, a covered walk had fallen outwards, leaving square sockets in the wall and a drift which concealed whatever lay beneath it. From camp the wreck had looked smaller. Riarhin could now distinguish a stair rising behind it, wide enough for four people abreast, with landings hidden by projecting screens.

She disliked those screens.

A defender could wait behind any one of them until the people below had committed themselves to the next flight. There was nowhere on the stairs to spread out. A body falling from above would carry others with it.

Saran was looking higher, at the lintel over the dark opening.

“Can you read it?” she asked.

“Some.”

“What does it say?”

He moved his lips before answering. “The duty of those who remain.”

“The rest?”

“The stone has gone.”

Saikou had put his bowl down unfinished.

They agreed the last details beside the tent. Somo and Dorun would keep the outer camp for three nights, with food for another if the weather held. A storm warning would take them lower. They would leave the spare provisions in the stone shelter below the ridge; no one was to come looking inside the wall. Somo made each of them answer that part.

“If we cannot return,” Riarhin said, “you go.”

“Yes,” said Somo. “You have paid for a guide.”

She fastened the last strap herself, hard enough to make Riarhin exhale.

They crossed the basin by a broken watercourse. Somo chose it for the footing, Riarhin for the cover. There were places where they could walk upright between the banks, though no running water had touched the stones that morning. Frost filled the hollows of old wear. Twice they passed blocks with carving on one face, fallen so far from the buildings that Riarhin looked back at the ridge to judge the distance.

At the first, Saikou stopped them.

He crouched without touching it. The carving was a row of small seated figures beneath an overhang. One had lost its head. Snow lay in the empty space, packed by the wind into something round and white.

“Keep below the top of the bank,” he said.

“Something there?” Berent asked.

“Yes.”

Saikou took a length of cord from his bag. He tied a stone into its end, then raised the stone above the bank on the tip of his staff. Nothing happened. He lowered it and changed his position by a pace.

The cord parted.

There was no flash. The stone dropped on the far side. The cut end hung from the staff, and a strand of fibre drifted past Saikou’s cheek.

Riarhin pulled Saran down by his sleeve. He had begun to rise for a better look.

“Stay here.”

Saikou was already moving along the bank. He tested once more, found the edge and marked it with a piece of red thread tied below the lip. Riarhin watched his hands. They were steady.

“Can we get under it?”

“At this point.”

He showed Somo where he had tested. She looked along the watercourse, then at a shallow channel branching towards the west end of the wall.

“That way gives us more depth.”

Saran unrolled the copy against his knee. “There should be an entrance there. For the lower cistern.”

“Show me from here,” said Riarhin.

He did. A buttress partly concealed the place. Between it and the slope, a narrow strip of shadow continued down to ground level.

Saikou tested their way to it. They moved one at a time through a place where a fallen slab had narrowed the channel. Berent took each pack as its owner wriggled past. Cadan, coming last, passed his through before him and received it back without a word.

At the buttress they could stand.

A small doorway remained intact beneath the shadow. Its stone frame carried the same seated figures, smaller here, their faces worn almost smooth. Beside it a bronze plate had been fixed into the masonry. Someone had tried to prise it out. The bent edge stood far enough from the wall to admit Riarhin’s fingers.

She kept them at her side.

Saran brushed loose snow from the bottom of the plate with his sleeve. His breath came quickly from the passage along the channel. He waited until it slowed, then spoke towards the door in a language Riarhin did not know.

The air carried his voice away. Nothing in the doorway moved.

He tried a shorter phrase.

“Enough,” Saikou said.

“I have not asked it to open.”

“I know. Something beyond it answered the first time.”

Riarhin had heard nothing.

Saran drew back. Saikou unwrapped the Gate Symbol, held it where he could see both the doorway and its edge, and waited. His mouth tightened.

“The lower right,” he said to Saran. “What belongs there?”

Saran knelt again. Beneath the snow was a recess about the width of a palm. Its cover had been broken off long ago; inside, a stone tongue stood across a groove.

“A drain stop.”

“For water?”

Saran studied the small carving above it. He looked at the copy, then back. “For the cleaning water. It should be down before the door is opened.”

Saikou examined the groove with the light from Aurine’s lamp. A thread of ice ran behind the tongue. Riarhin could see where the lower end had been chipped by tools.

“Do not move it yet.”

They worked slowly. Dorun fetched a flat stone to stand the lamp upon; Nalia held the copy away from its heat. Saikou laid a cord along the sill and brought it back unchanged. He repeated the test through the recess. At his direction Saran pressed the tongue down with the end of a wooden peg. It moved reluctantly, scraping across something inside the wall.

The lamp flame leaned towards the doorway.

Then it straightened.

Saikou waited until the last sound had stopped. He crossed the sill alone.

Riarhin hated watching him disappear into that narrow dark. She listened to his steps, counted them until counting became useless, and watched the cord he had taken with him. It paid out from Berent’s hand. After a while it stopped.

Saikou returned with dust on one shoulder.

“There is a room. A second exit up the back. I have not tested beyond it.”

Somo would go no farther. She showed Berent where she had set the outer anchor and made him put his own weight upon it. Dorun helped bring the last packs beneath the buttress. They left the spare lamp and the extra rope there, out of the wind.

Riarhin thanked them. Dorun touched the bell at his throat.

“Bring that one back,” he said, pointing at Berent. “He still owes me a spoon.”

“I washed it.”

“You bent it.”

They were smiling when Somo led him away.

The room smelt of old ash.

A shelf ran along one side, with shallow hollows where vessels had stood. Beneath it were two little shoes, stiff and grey with age. One lay on its side. Riarhin had almost put her pack on them before she saw the curved seam of the toe.

She set the pack elsewhere.

The others came through behind her. There was room for all seven if they kept close to the walls. Saikou was studying the inner passage. Berent crouched at the entrance, drawing in the slack from the outside line.

Riarhin unfastened her coat.

The linen bundle pressed against her ribs. She shifted it to make room for the strap of her water bottle.

The room tipped.

She struck the shelf with her hip. For an instant she thought she had fainted. Her stomach rose into her throat and the lamp travelled sideways through the air, shedding oil in a long bright string.

Berent caught Nalia by the coat. Both of them fell against the wall beside the door.

“Down!”

Saikou’s voice arrived after she was already on her knees. She had her left hand clamped around the edge of the shelf. The oil should have fallen. It stretched across the room, separate drops turning slowly, the little flame burning at the mouth of the lamp as it hung above Aurine’s outstretched hand.

Outside, stone struck stone.

The floor came back beneath her.

The lamp smashed. Aurine smothered it with her folded cloak. Someone cried out, a short furious sound. Riarhin crawled towards the door and saw daylight cut into strips by snow falling sideways past it.

“Leave the pack,” Saikou said.

His hand was against her shoulder. She could feel each finger through the coat.

“The bundle. Keep it where it is.”

She looked down. The fastening still held. Tigist’s linen showed between the edges of her coat.

“Did I—”

“Stay still.”

The noise outside grew deeper. Riarhin saw the cut above Berent’s eyebrow, saw his lips moving, heard nothing he said. He had wound the rope around his forearm. She seized his wrist and helped him free it before the line drew tight.

It snapped against the sill.

For a moment the loose end stood straight out of the door. Then it fell.

Riarhin pushed past Saikou.

“Somo!”

He caught the back of her coat. She fought him, reaching for the sill. Beyond it the buttress stood unbroken, its bronze plate dull in the light. The watercourse was gone beneath moving white. Above the drift a stone larger than a horse turned upon its narrow end and struck the slope.

“Somo!”

Saikou hauled her back. She drove an elbow into his chest before she knew it was him.

He let go only when she stopped fighting.

They listened.

One last stone rolled somewhere out of sight. Snow whispered across the entrance. Inside, Aurine was swearing as she pressed cloth to Berent’s forehead. Nalia had both hands over her ears.

Saran stood against the rear wall. His face had lost its colour.

“The cistern stop was right,” he said.

Nobody answered him.

Riarhin lay on her stomach behind the sill, with Cadan holding her ankles. She could see farther from there without putting her head above the broken masonry.

The drift had stopped short of the buttress. Beyond it, the channel they had followed lay under a steep new slope of snow and rock. The red thread was gone. She found the ridge above it, followed it with her eyes and saw two figures moving.

She shouted again.

One of them stopped. After a moment an arm lifted.

Riarhin put her head down on the cold stone.

“All right?” Cadan asked.

“Both.”

He held her while she looked again. Somo pointed away from the temple, then down. Riarhin raised her arm. She could not tell whether Somo saw it. The two figures picked their way along the ridge above the slide.

Riarhin backed into the room. Her hands were shaking so badly that she could not fasten the coat.

Aurine did it for her.

“What happened?” Nalia asked.

Saikou sat with his back against the shelf. He was holding his chest where Riarhin had struck him.

“I crossed that sill twice,” he said. “The same working held both times.”

“Then it was me.”

He looked at the closed coat.

“I should have allowed more distance.”

“How much?”

Dust continued to fall from a crack above the inner passage. On the floor, the two little shoes had slid together. Riarhin watched a grain of grit fall beside them and stop halfway down.

Saikou got to his feet.

“Everybody away from that wall.”

They moved. Cadan lifted Nalia’s pack without waiting for her. The grain hung there, turning. Riarhin could not make herself look away from it.

“Can we get out?” Berent asked.

“Not through the slide,” she said.

“The upper passage,” Saran said. “There was a stair to the court.”

“Was?” Nalia’s voice broke on the word.

Saikou put the Gate Symbol into his hand and went to the passage. He stopped well short of its mouth.

Riarhin gathered the remaining lamp. Behind her Aurine cut the burnt edge from her cloak with a pair of shears. Each small bite sounded painfully loud.

They had food, rope, the copies. Somewhere above them was the stair Saran remembered from a drawing. Outside, Somo and Dorun were making their way back to the camp.

She wanted to be with them.

She lifted the lamp so Saikou could see.

Chapter 40 — The Three Hundred

Berent wanted the outside rope.

Riarhin was already carrying the lamp towards the passage when he went back for it. She opened her mouth to call him away, then saw what he was doing. The snapped end had fallen clear of the sill. He drew it in, examined the damage and cut back to sound fibre. There was less than half the length they had brought, but he coiled it carefully and passed it over his shoulder.

“Now,” she said.

He came.

The passage turned twice before reaching the stair. At each turn there was a recess for a lamp and, below it, a shallow basin with a hole in its base. Saran pointed these out without being asked. He seemed unable to bear anything passing unremarked which he could still explain.

“Those fed the cleaning channel.”

“Watch your head,” Aurine said.

He ducked beneath a cracked lintel. Riarhin waited while Saikou tested the first stair with his staff. She could see his caution becoming an effort of will. Nothing in him was slow by nature. He wanted to move, wanted the next piece of ground under his feet before it could change, and made himself wait.

Behind her, something struck the floor of the room they had left.

All seven of them stopped.

“The grit,” Nalia whispered.

Perhaps it was. Riarhin had heard a much heavier thing fall.

They climbed.

Near the top, daylight showed through a doorway blocked by a stone bench. Someone had dragged it across from the other side. Cadan put his shoulder against the exposed end. Riarhin caught his sleeve.

“Wait.”

He held still. She lowered the lamp. A narrow wire ran from the foot of the bench into the wall, green with age but unbroken. The bench itself rested upon two little rollers. A man shoving hard from below could have moved it easily.

Cadan looked down at the wire.

“Thank you.”

They went over instead. Berent climbed first and lay across the top, reaching back. On the far side his boots found a step, then paving. He helped Nalia down. She made a little involuntary noise when she saw what lay beneath the window, and turned away before Riarhin could ask.

The dead occupied most of the room.

Some still wore plates of metal. Others were wrapped in the remnants of quilted cloth, bound at the waist with belts whose buckles had fallen inward as the bodies shrank. At the far door, three lay across one another beneath a spray of short bronze darts. One had reached the threshold. His right hand was still extended beyond it, fingers curled against the stone.

Riarhin counted the people she could distinguish. Nine. Possibly ten; the heap beside the door would have to be moved to know.

She had no intention of moving it.

The room had been a kitchen. There were hooks above a hearth, a grinding slab worn smooth at one end, a row of jars sealed with lids. A narrow window gave onto a roofed yard. Snow had come through the roof and filled the trough beneath it, making a long white bed in the middle of the paving.

Saikou crossed to the window. Riarhin followed, picking her way between the dead.

Across the yard, a gate stood open.

Beyond it she could see the upper part of the basin and, much farther away, a strip of canvas at their camp.

“There.”

Her relief was so sudden that she had already taken two steps when Saikou caught her arm.

“Look at the snow.”

The trough was full. The yard around it was bare.

Riarhin looked up through the broken roof. A fine fall of powder blew in from the western slope, passed between the rafters and vanished above the paving. She watched until her eyes hurt. The flakes did not melt. One moment they were white against the dark floor. The next there was nothing.

“Can you hold it off?”

“Perhaps. While everyone crosses, with that against your ribs?” He shook his head. “I cannot promise that.”

“Can we go round?”

Saran was at the hearth. He had knelt beside a body half hidden by the bench of the grinding slab. His hand hovered over a small bronze badge on the dead person’s breast.

“Saran.”

He looked up.

“Another way out.”

“The receiving rooms have a terrace.”

“Can you get us there?”

He stood too quickly, staggered and caught the edge of the slab. “I think so.”

They did not count three hundred.

There was a second barricade behind the kitchen, made of heavy tables stacked with their legs pointing towards the attackers. Two people lay on the sheltered side. One wore the same badge Saran had recognised. Beside the other was a wooden mallet and a bowl turned upside down.

Farther on, in a covered walk, they found shields abandoned in a neat line. The men behind them had died facing a wall. Riarhin could see no wounds from where she stood. She kept the company close to the pillars and told them not to touch the shields.

A bronze shutter lay flat across the next doorway. Its hinges had been cut. Above it a maker’s inscription had been overlaid by another set of marks, sharp and deep enough to survive the weather that had worn everything else.

Saran read them.

Then he read them again, tracing nothing, his hands folded into his sleeves.

“What?” Nalia asked.

“A name.”

“Whose?”

He swallowed. “Someone who was said to have held this door.”

Riarhin looked at the cut hinges. Tool marks ran up their inner faces. Whoever had worked on them had stood where they were standing now, behind the defence.

“Held it for whom?”

Saran stepped over the shutter.

Nalia said his name sharply. He stopped on the other side, his face turned away.

“I can read it to you when we are out.”

Riarhin let him go first. She had seen the mark. It would remain there whether he spoke or not.

The next room held no dead. Its walls were painted with a procession of people carrying bowls, flowers and bundles of something tied in cloth. Small patches of colour survived beneath the soot. One painted woman had turned her face towards a child pulling at her sleeve. The painter had given her an expression Riarhin knew very well.

A crack divided the child from the hand it held.

At the end of the procession a doorway opened onto the terrace.

Real wind struck Riarhin’s face. She shut her eyes for a moment and tasted snow.

The terrace overlooked the watercourse from which they had approached. Most of the lower slope was buried. To the right, however, a rib of rock rose clear of the new drift and ran out towards the basin. A descent from the end of the terrace would put them on it. There were handholds, then a drop too far to jump, then a shelf wide enough to stand upon.

Berent came beside her. He looked at the shelf, looked at the rope and began examining the pillars behind them.

“I can rig that.”

Riarhin put her hand on his shoulder. She squeezed it once, hard.

Cadan went down first.

He had taken off his pack and wrapped spare cloth around the rope where it crossed the parapet. Berent fed it through his hands while Aurine watched the anchor. Riarhin leaned far enough over to see Cadan’s pale head below her. He paused at the broken section, tested a foothold and lowered himself another pace.

Then he was standing on the shelf.

He walked to its end and returned. A few minutes later he raised both arms.

A route. A difficult route, with a traverse that would have to be crossed one at a time, but ground leading away from the temple.

They sent the packs down. Nalia followed, trembling with anger at the tremor in her hands. Aurine called instructions only when she needed them. Saran went next. He nearly lost his footing where the rope crossed the broken face, recovered and reached the shelf with his forehead pressed against the rock.

Saikou remained beside Riarhin.

“You next,” he said.

She stood away from the parapet. The amulet lay quiet inside her coat. She could feel nothing from it. Her own pulse beat painfully in her throat.

“If it happens again?”

“Then I want you on the rope.”

Berent checked the fastening around her waist. She climbed over.

For the first few feet there was only cold stone beneath her palms. She put her left foot into the place Cadan had used and lowered herself until the rope took her weight.

A little stone came loose beside her boot.

It fell upward.

Riarhin pressed herself against the wall. Above her Berent shouted. The rope pulled sideways, driving her hip into a projecting edge. She held on with both hands and felt the stone beneath her fingers begin to hum.

“Up!” Saikou called.

She climbed. Her boot slipped. Berent hauled and she caught the parapet with her left hand, skin tearing against the rough edge. Saikou seized her wrist. Together they dragged her over and rolled her away from the drop.

Below, Cadan had thrown himself over Nalia. The little stone struck the underside of the terrace and broke.

Nothing else moved.

Riarhin lay with her cheek against the paving. Her hand bled into the groove between two slabs.

“Everyone below?” she asked.

Berent leaned over. He called their names, waited, called again.

“All standing.”

Saikou knelt close enough for her to see the whitened hair at his temples and the dust caught in his eyelashes.

“We can bring them back,” he said. “Or they can go.”

“And us?”

He looked at the blood beneath her hand.

“We have to find somewhere you can put it down.”

Chapter 41 — The Observation Passage

They asked the others from the terrace.

Riarhin stood well back while Berent relayed what had happened. Nalia asked three questions, each louder than the last. Could the object be left inside? Could Riarhin leave it and follow? Were they proposing to stay simply because they had come this far?

“No,” Riarhin called down.

The word left her breathless. She waited before speaking again.

“We are going to look for a place to leave it. If we cannot, we come back here and try something else. You can go now.”

Cadan shaded his eyes. “Will you need your pack?”

“Yes.”

“Then someone should bring it.”

It was not an answer she could bear to hear just then. She went away from the parapet and sat beside the painted woman with the child until the first pack came over the edge.

Nalia was the last to return. She landed awkwardly, unfastened the rope and came straight to Riarhin.

“I am frightened,” she said.

“So am I.”

“I know. You nearly broke my arm getting past me in that room.”

Riarhin looked at her sleeve. There was a dirty handprint above the elbow.

“I’m sorry.”

“Ask next time.”

Nalia went to Aurine, who checked the arm and found it bruised. Riarhin remained where she was. Through the open doorway she could see the rope hanging down the outside of the terrace. Berent had left it in place.

They found the observation passage behind a row of sleeping cells.

Each cell held a raised stone bed, a niche and a little square opening high in the wall. The bedding had mostly vanished. In one, a comb lay beside a heap of brown dust. In another, somebody had scratched a game into the floor and left the counters in their last positions.

Behind a fallen wooden screen, Aurine found a second door. Cold air rose through the opening; the lamp showed a flight of steps descending into the wall. They marked it and went on towards the higher passage, still looking for daylight.

The passage beyond was so narrow that Riarhin’s pack brushed both sides. Saran carried the lamp. Its light moved over a long strip of carved instructions, broken in places by sockets for fittings. He stopped at a low door and called Saikou forward.

The two men studied it together. Riarhin leaned against the opposite wall and waited for her breathing to settle. Aurine had cleaned the torn palm and wrapped it lightly enough that she could close her hand. It throbbed when she let it hang.

Saran pressed a recessed stone. A cover slid aside with a rush of dust.

Darkness opened below them.

At first Riarhin could make out only a pale circle far beneath the opening. Then her eyes adjusted. A broad chamber lay under the mountain, its roof carried by columns which narrowed towards the floor. The pale circle was a raised platform. A bridge reached it from the near wall, stopping short of its edge at a gap a person could jump on ordinary ground.

There was nothing ordinary about the space below the bridge.

Fragments of stone hung at different heights. One turned slowly. Another was moving with such patience that she could not be certain of its motion until she looked away and back. Between them was a dark structure built into the chamber floor, enclosed by bands of metal and stone. She could not keep its whole shape in sight. Looking at one edge made another seem to withdraw.

She stepped back from the opening.

Saran held the lamp at the opening without lowering it.

“That is the holding,” he said.

“The fragment?”

He nodded.

“Still here.”

Saikou did not approach the opening immediately. He stood with his head bent, listening. When he moved, it was towards a narrow recess beside the door.

“Here,” he said.

Inside was a stone shelf divided by a thick vertical plate. Fine grooves ran down from it into the wall. Saran lifted the lamp and began to read the marks above it.

“A waiting place,” he said. “For the bearer.”

“For what the bearer carries,” Saikou said. “Read the next line.”

Saran did.

Riarhin did not ask him to translate. She could hear enough in the way his voice changed.

They tested the shelf with a stone, with an iron buckle, with Saikou’s cord. Nothing happened. Saikou made her stand three paces away while he held the Gate Symbol across the mouth of the recess. Saran watched a narrow slit in the wall through which part of the pale platform could be seen.

“Very slowly,” Saikou said.

Riarhin unfastened the coat.

Her fingers wanted to hurry. She forced them to finish each movement before beginning the next. The linen came free of the inner pocket. Its folds were darkened by long handling. Tigist had wrapped the thing with such care, and she had carried it into Veralyn’s hands and watched the queen’s face change.

She nearly drew it back.

“Riarhin.”

Saikou was watching her, not the shelf.

She laid the bundle down.

The stone shelf rang beneath it.

In the chamber below, every suspended fragment turned at once.

Saikou spoke. The Gate’s edge shone between his fingers, bright enough to hurt her eyes. Air pressed against her ears. Then the brightness narrowed to a line across the recess and held.

Riarhin stood with her empty hands in front of her.

Saran was speaking too quickly. Nalia caught his sleeve and made him repeat the words. The slit showed a thread of pale light moving along the outer edge of the platform. It travelled halfway round, reached a dark break and vanished.

The light began again.

Saikou leaned against the wall.

“Leave it.”

Riarhin stepped away. The floor stayed beneath her.

She took another step.

Berent went with her to the terrace.

She had asked for him without explaining why, and he had come without making her explain. They walked through the sleeping cells, past the kitchen door and the fallen shutter, to the cold daylight. The rope was where they had left it.

Riarhin climbed over the parapet. Her injured hand hurt as she lowered herself, but nothing changed around her. She reached the first foothold, then the broken section. Berent paid out the rope. When her boots touched the lower shelf, she stood there for a while with her forehead against the rock.

She could leave.

The thought was so large that there was no room beside it for anything else.

She walked to the end of the shelf. Beyond the traverse lay the basin, the ridge, a way down to people who knew what to do with cold feet and torn palms. Farther still, Ailsa had a letter to answer and cloth spread across a table. There would be a quarrel about a school. A bowl set out for her. A child who had forgotten to ask for the shell because other things had happened since.

Riarhin closed her eyes.

Then she went back to the rope.

Berent helped her over. He waited while she sat down.

“It works,” she said.

“Yes.”

“We can all go.”

He nodded.

She rubbed her face with the uninjured hand. “I want to.”

He sat beside her, his legs stretched out. The cut above his eye had stopped bleeding.

“Then we should tell them that.”

The waiting shelf had bought them distance, not quiet.

By the time Riarhin returned, the line of light in the chamber was moving faster. Saikou had withdrawn his Symbol; the recess itself was holding the bundle apart from whatever filled the room below, but each circuit ended at the same dark break. At that point a sound passed through the walls. Riarhin felt it in her teeth.

Nalia had laid the archive copy upon a dry slab. Beside it Saran had set a second sheet and was drawing what they could actually see.

“The outer band is incomplete,” he said. “That is the first correction.”

He pointed to a small assembly fixed beyond the observation opening. A rod had come out of its seat. Its far end rested against a broken support. Beyond it, a hinged plate stood at the wrong angle.

“Could the slide have done that?”

“The break is old.”

Saikou put a little splinter of wood on the sill. At each pulse it lifted, turned and settled. He watched three pulses before moving it a hand’s breadth farther along. There it remained still.

He marked both positions.

“Do we have to do this?” Riarhin asked.

Saran looked at her. “You brought it to—”

“I know why I brought it. Can we leave it there and go?”

Saikou looked towards the recess. “We could.”

“For how long would that hold?”

“I don’t know.”

Below them the light reached the break. The wall shuddered. A sliver of mortar fell from above Saran’s head and broke on the copy.

Nalia brushed it away.

“If we leave,” she said, “somebody has to come back to this.”

“Possibly no one comes,” said Aurine.

Riarhin looked at the bundle. Its shape was absurdly familiar. A small wrapped possession set on a shelf, the sort of thing a person might forget when leaving a room in haste.

She could not make herself say that somebody else would be better suited to it. She could not promise the opposite either.

“We rest,” she said. “Then each of us decides.”

Saikou closed his eyes and let his head rest against the wall.

Chapter 42 — A Night Inside

They slept in the room with the painted procession.

It was farther from the chamber than Saikou wanted and nearer than Riarhin would have chosen. The terrace door could be closed against the wind. Two other doors gave them a choice if something happened in the passage. Berent tied a cord between them at knee height so that anyone waking in darkness would know which way led outside.

Aurine made them eat before discussing anything else.

The food was thick and salty. There was grit in Riarhin’s first mouthful. She spat it into her hand, looked at it and ate the next mouthful anyway. Cadan found a crack in the cooking vessel and bound it with wire. Saran tried to apologise for having packed it against the lamp box. Aurine told him to hold it still.

They had left one lamp beside the waiting shelf. Every so often Saikou rose to look down the passage. The third time, Aurine caught his wrist.

“Tell me what you expect to see.”

“A change.”

“And if there is one?”

“I come back.”

“Will it cease because you are awake?”

He pulled his wrist free and stood over Aurine. His lips parted, then closed. Aurine waited.

“No,” he said at last.

“Then lie down.”

He did, though he kept his boots on.

Riarhin took the first watch with Nalia. They sat near the inner door. The others settled behind them, wrapping themselves in everything they had brought. Saran lay on his side facing the painted wall. Cadan fell asleep so quickly that Riarhin envied him until she noticed his hands still clasped around the strap of his pack.

Nalia rubbed her bruised arm.

“Do you think anyone knows we are here?” she asked.

“Somo.”

“I mean someone who would come after us.”

“Not soon.”

Nalia nodded. From somewhere inside the mountain came a muffled sound, as if a great vessel had been struck with a padded mallet.

“I used to know who would complain if I failed to return,” she said. “At the office. It was mostly because I had the keys.”

“Someone would have come.”

“Yes. For the keys.”

Riarhin looked at her. Nalia’s mouth twitched.

“You needn’t be kind about it. They were expensive locks.”

Riarhin laughed, and had to stop because the bruising at her hip hurt.

They listened to the sleepers. Berent snored through a partly blocked nose. Aurine reached over without opening her eyes and nudged him onto his side.

“If we do it,” Nalia said, “what happens to the record?”

“The archive.”

“All of it?”

“The things they need to know.”

“Who chooses those?”

Riarhin leaned her head against the wall. She was too tired to be pleased by a good question.

“You tell me.”

Nalia drew her knees up. “I don’t want my old employer owning the only useful account. I don’t want Saran deciding who is fit to read it. I also don’t want to sell a road to that chamber to the first rich man who asks.”

“Vesk. Tserin. Metho.”

“Three people can die.”

“Yes.”

Nalia stared at the lamp. “We will have to leave enough people able to argue about it.”

Riarhin pictured Anju standing in the rain with her arms folded, refusing a place obtained at someone else’s expense. She tried to remember the last thing Lali had said to her and found that she could recall only the child’s wet hair stuck to her cheek.

She wanted to hear it again.

Saran sat up before the watch changed.

He had not slept. He came to the door with the copy in his hands and knelt between the two women, careful not to touch either.

“There is another procedure,” he said.

Riarhin waited.

“The receiving ceremony. The lamp is lit from within the outer band. The bearer is admitted by the person attending the inner seat.”

“We have read that.”

“The order matters. If the seat is acknowledged first, the bearer has protection.”

“Show us without the bundle.”

Saran’s fingers tightened upon the paper.

“It cannot be received if it is absent.”

“Then show us the part before that.”

He shook his head.

Nalia moved slightly away from him. Riarhin noticed. Saran noticed too.

“I am trying to keep you alive.”

“So are we,” Riarhin said. “Show us.”

“If I begin it wrongly—”

“The way you want to begin it. With everything except the amulet.”

He looked towards Saikou. The invoker’s eyes were open.

“You remember what Tserin said,” Saikou murmured.

Saran folded the copy. He folded it badly, across the writing, and then unfolded it again as though that small damage had shocked him.

“I remember.”

“Then tomorrow,” Riarhin said.

He stayed kneeling. He kept the folded paper raised between them, waiting to be allowed to continue. It would have been easy to fill the silence with anger. She had enough of it. Her palm hurt; her hip hurt; she was frightened of the thing she had brought and furious with everyone who could not make it harmless.

“Go to sleep, Saran.”

He returned to his blanket.

Saikou watched him lie down. Then he closed his eyes again.

Riarhin dreamed of the estate well.

She had dropped the bucket and could hear it striking the sides all the way down. Ailsa was asking why she had let go. Riarhin kept trying to tell her that the rope had gone through her hands, that she had held it as hard as she could, but Ailsa was walking away with the empty cups and would not turn round.

She woke with her fists closed.

Berent was beside the door. Grey light showed beneath the terrace shutters. He looked round when she sat up.

“Morning?”

“Nearly.”

“Anything?”

“The lamp is still there. The sound comes less often.”

She almost took comfort from that. Then she heard the next sound, deeper than those of the night before, and saw dust tremble from the painted child’s face.

Aurine was awake. She had heated a little water and was washing the soot from the lamp glass with the corner of her burnt cloak. Riarhin went to help her.

Later they sat together with their bowls and made the decision.

Cadan would stay. Berent wanted to keep the outside line ready and would leave if Riarhin ordered it; she told him he could also leave if he chose. He frowned at her, then nodded. Nalia wanted to see the proposed ceremony tested before answering. Aurine said the same. Saran looked at Saikou.

Saikou finished drinking.

“I will examine the correction,” he said. “I will not promise you a working until I have seen it.”

Riarhin put down her bowl.

She could still go to the terrace. They could all go. The wrapped object would remain on the shelf, and someone might reach it in a year or a century, or the shelf might hold until the mountain wore away. She had no vision to tell her which. She had a room full of tired people waiting for her to stop looking towards the door.

“I will try,” she said.

Nobody thanked her.

Aurine handed her the clean lamp glass. Riarhin dried it, fitted it into its frame and turned the wick up until the flame burned evenly.

Chapter 43 — The Inner Lamp

Saran washed his hands before the test.

The water was almost too precious to spare. He asked Aurine for a little and caught it in his bowl, rubbing each finger with his thumb. Dirt came away from the creases. A bruise had risen beneath one nail where he had caught it on the rope.

Riarhin watched him dry his hands upon the inside of his coat. She had seen priests prepare to bless marriages, soldiers check straps before battle, Bea sit very still while someone else fastened a clasp behind her neck. Saran’s attention had the same quality. He had ceased to look at the people waiting for him.

Nalia put the archive copy on the sill.

“We keep that open.”

“Yes,” he said.

She laid a pebble upon each corner.

The recess containing Kalrantha was behind them. Cadan stood between it and the others, not touching the bundle. He had chosen the position while they were arranging the lamps. When Saran looked towards him, Cadan smiled briefly and remained where he was.

The inner seat was a narrow standing place reached from the observation passage by three steps. It faced the raised platform across the chamber. A bronze socket stood at waist height, empty except for a little white sediment in its bottom. Above it, a hinged strip could be lowered to connect two fittings.

There was a gap in the strip exactly where Tserin had left the drawing unfinished.

Saran pointed to the socket. “The lamp belongs here.”

“Set it there unlit,” Saikou said.

Saran obeyed. They had brought his small ceremonial lamp, the one Pema had allowed him to retain when he gave up the box. It fitted the socket. Riarhin heard the little click and felt Saran’s relief before he spoke.

“It is the right size.”

Nalia leaned over the copy. “Several lamps might be.”

He looked hurt. She did not take it back.

Saikou examined the hinged strip from the passage. He kept one foot behind the sill. The Gate Symbol lay ready in his left hand, the Witness covered at his breast.

“Now tell me the next movement.”

“The connection is made.”

“Why?”

“To acknowledge the attendant.”

“What moves?”

Saran’s eyes went to the chamber. “The receiving way opens.”

Saikou held out his right hand. “Give me the peg.”

For a moment Saran did not move. Then he placed the wooden peg in Saikou’s palm.

They lowered the strip together, Saran guiding its hinge, Saikou controlling the last distance with the wood. It stopped short of contact. The gap remained open.

Below, the pale light changed course.

Instead of running round the platform, it left the outer band and rose along a narrow line cut into one of the supporting columns. It climbed towards the standing place. Riarhin could hear a thin ringing sound, almost beautiful, which made her want to move closer to identify its source.

Saikou pulled Saran back.

The wooden peg blackened between his fingers. He let it fall. Before it reached the floor, the end nearest the fitting disappeared.

Saran stumbled against Riarhin. She caught him with her good hand and shoved him into the passage.

“Up,” Saikou said.

Nalia had already grasped the long handle by which the strip could be lifted from outside the seat. She hauled it back. The light fell away from the column.

For several breaths no one spoke.

The remains of the peg lay upon the floor. Half its length was gone. The surviving end was clean, without ash or a splinter.

Saikou had to loosen his grip before he could put the Symbol away.

“Who is acknowledged?” he asked.

Saran was shaking his head.

“Who receives what comes up that line?”

“The attendant.”

“And can send it elsewhere.”

Saran closed his eyes.

Riarhin wanted to strike him. The desire came whole and easy. He stood within reach, hands empty, with no defence she could not break. She imagined the satisfaction of making his head turn.

She stepped away.

Cadan remained beside the recess.

“Did you know?” Nalia asked.

“There are two readings.”

“Did you know that was one of them?”

“Yes.”

The word was barely audible. Nalia went red across the cheekbones. She picked up the copy, put it down again and planted both palms on the sill.

“You let us carry this.”

“I believed—”

“You let me argue for you.”

He could not look at her.

Saikou was studying the severed peg. He did not pick it up. Riarhin watched the bent line of his back and remembered him lying against the shelf with her elbow’s bruise upon his chest.

“Why wouldn’t you do it without the amulet?” she asked.

Saran swallowed. “The account says the attendant must not call on an empty house.”

“You thought it might kill you.”

“I thought it might.”

“But with me there?”

He turned towards the recess. Cadan shifted one foot. It was a small movement, but Saran stopped looking that way.

“With the return completed,” he said, “there would be an office again.”

They made him sit in one of the sleeping cells.

Cadan sat outside the door. He did not bind Saran. Riarhin had told him to prevent anyone reaching the bundle alone, and he had asked whether that included her. She had said yes. He had nodded as though she had finally supplied an omitted measurement.

In the observation passage, Nalia traced the second route on the copy without ink. It branched from the outer band towards the attendant’s seat and continued beyond the edge of the preserved drawing.

“A house within a house,” she said.

Saikou touched the untouched part of the sill. “An opening.”

“Can we close it?”

“Ask him.”

Riarhin looked towards the sleeping cells.

Saikou’s face hardened. “I can identify the working. He may know where its keeper stood to end it. We can spend a day finding out ourselves. Perhaps two.”

Another sound passed through the walls. Riarhin felt her bandaged palm throb.

She went to Saran.

He sat on the stone bed with his hands between his knees. The little square window above him admitted a colourless light. Cadan rose to let her pass and sat down again outside.

“I thought there would be something left for us,” Saran said.

She remained standing.

“The rites were all we had. Other houses could say we were disgraced. They could refuse our people work. But they could not say we had forgotten.”

“You could have told us.”

“I nearly did.”

Riarhin waited until he looked at her.

“You had a long way to do it.”

His face crumpled, then tightened. He put a hand over his mouth and kept it there until he could lower it without shaking.

“What do you need?”

“The way to close that branch.”

He stared at the opposite wall. For a moment she thought he would refuse. She was trying to decide what she would do when he rose and reached towards the lamp.

Cadan stood.

“Only the lamp,” Saran said.

Riarhin took it herself and followed him.

Behind the inner seat, concealed by the socket’s projecting base, was a slot narrow enough to disappear in shadow. Saran showed them how to lift a flat locking piece without lowering the strip. Beneath it was the end of a long stone pin.

“If that goes down,” he said, “the opening is closed from below.”

“Can it be raised again?” Nalia asked.

“From the chamber. Before the receiving place is occupied.”

“After?”

He held the lamp higher. His hand shook against the frame.

“No.”

Saikou crouched to examine the pin. Riarhin stood where she could see both men.

They left the ceremonial lamp unlit in its socket while they worked out how to reach the pin without standing beneath the open line.

Chapter 44 — The Work Between

By midday they had used half the water carried inside.

The terrace snow was clean enough to melt, but fetching it required someone to leave the work and cross the sleeping cells. Aurine assigned the task to Saran. He looked towards Saikou, perhaps expecting him to object to losing the one person who could read the smallest marks. Saikou handed him the pot.

Berent went with him.

Riarhin sat on the floor while Nalia read the repair sequence aloud. Raise the displaced rod into its seat. Bring the hinged plate into line. Close the attendant’s branch. Verify the continuous band. Move the object by the receiving bridge. Withdraw before the cradle locked.

The spaces between those instructions contained the chamber.

From the observation opening Riarhin could see the rod, a black length of metal thicker than her wrist, lodged against the broken support. Its near end lay beyond a ledge just wide enough for a foot. The ledge continued to a maintenance station overlooking the receiving bridge. A narrow service stair reached the same station from below, but its lowest steps had collapsed into the space of hanging stone.

She would have to cross the ledge to free the rod. Saran and Nalia could then turn it from the passage using the surviving lever. Berent would control her rope from a pillar behind the opening. Cadan would stay at the station once she had reached it, carrying the second line and watching the bridge. Aurine would move between the workers, which meant she would see less of any one thing than she wanted.

Saikou would hold the working boundary along the ledge.

Riarhin looked out at the ledge again.

Riarhin loosened Remorse in its sheath, then fastened it again. There was nothing out there she could cut. She left the sword where it would not catch against the opening and kept the Last Blade on her belt.

Saikou watched her do it.

“When I say stop, stop wherever you are.”

“I know.”

“Even if you can see the next foothold.”

She looked at him. “Yes.”

He invoked standing squarely in the observation doorway, one hand upon each side of the stone frame.

Riarhin had heard him work through whispers and low measured phrases. This voice filled the passage. It seemed to find edges in the stone which she had not known were there, drawing them out into pale lines. The doorway lengthened in her sight without moving. Beyond it, a strip of darkness separated the ledge from the chamber below.

Saikou spoke again.

The hanging fragments turned towards him.

Riarhin climbed through the opening.

Her boots found the ledge. It was colder than the air. She kept her left shoulder towards the wall and slid one foot along until she felt the next shallow cut in the stone. Behind her, Berent gave the rope enough slack to move and no more.

At the first break she had to turn outwards. The chamber opened beneath her, full of motion too slow for the eye to follow honestly. She fixed her attention on the wall. Someone had cut small marks above the footholds. She touched the first with a finger, reached for the next and shifted her weight.

There was a sound like a door struck from the other side.

She froze.

Saikou gave no warning. His voice continued, deeper now, working through a phrase that seemed to require more breath than he had. The dark strip beside the ledge thickened.

Riarhin moved again.

The rod lay at knee height. Its end had slipped behind a broken stone lip, pinning it under its own weight. She could not lift it from where she stood. She knelt, set the short iron bar they had brought beneath it and tried the leverage.

Nothing.

She shifted the bar. A flake of stone broke away and floated beside her face.

“Slack on the lever,” she called.

Nalia relayed the words to Saran. The rod moved a little, enough for Riarhin to get the bar farther underneath. She braced her boot against a cut in the wall and pushed.

The rod came free.

At once the bar grew heavier. It dragged her hand down, pulled her shoulder outwards and struck the edge of the ledge. She let go. Berent’s rope caught her as her knee slipped.

The bar fell towards the chamber floor, stopped and shot sideways into a column.

“Stop!” Saikou shouted.

Riarhin clung to the wall. The rod was rolling towards its seat. She could reach it. One push would put it where it belonged.

“Riarhin!”

She brought her hand back against her chest.

The rod struck the lip of the seat and stayed there.

Saikou’s voice broke.

The dark boundary surged inwards. Riarhin flattened herself against the wall as something passed within a finger’s breadth of her back. She felt no blow. A fold of her coat vanished. Cold struck the exposed cloth beneath it.

Berent hauled.

She scraped along the ledge, found a foothold and pushed with both legs. Cadan reached through the opening and caught the back of her belt. Together they dragged her into the passage.

Saikou fell to one knee. The pale lines went out.

For a moment Riarhin could hear only her own breathing. Then Aurine was beside her, pulling at the coat, asking where it hurt. Riarhin tried to answer and discovered she was laughing.

“Stop that,” Aurine said, frightened.

“I am trying.”

She pressed both hands over her mouth. The laughter turned into a sound she could not name. Aurine held her shoulders until it passed.

They rested in the passage because no one could bear to walk farther.

Saikou sat with the Gate Symbol on his knee. Its edge was whole. He turned it once, examined it and covered it again. Riarhin watched his hands until he looked up.

“I did not open it.”

“I know.”

“There would have been nowhere left to put that force.”

She looked at the whole edge of the Symbol and let him wrap it.

Nalia sat on the other side of the doorway, staring at the rod. It had settled into a new position. The end still missed the seat, but it was no longer trapped beneath the broken lip.

“We could pull it,” she said.

Saran shook his head. “The handle will turn it too far.”

“Not with the handle.”

She pointed to the rope around Riarhin’s waist, then to the near end of the rod.

Berent rose. He leaned through the opening just far enough to look.

“A loop,” he said. “If we can get it over that end.”

They made three attempts with a weighted cord from inside the doorway. The first fell short. The second caught and slipped free. Before the third, Cadan asked Berent to move his elbow. He sent the loop out with a short underhand cast. It settled behind the rod’s collar.

“Like catching a pig,” he said.

Nobody asked him where he had learned.

They drew the heavier rope through. Saran watched the far plate while Nalia and Berent took the strain. The rod lifted, scraped and dropped into its seat with a sound that travelled through Riarhin’s feet.

Below them the band of light ran three quarters of the way round the platform.

Then the chamber answered.

A column on the far side seemed to bend. Its top remained fixed; its base remained fixed; the length between them moved slowly sideways. Riarhin gripped Aurine’s sleeve. The movement continued until she could see through a strip of air where solid stone had been.

Saikou was on his feet.

“Back from the opening.”

The column straightened.

Something struck the other side of the wall hard enough to throw dust from every joint. Berent went down on one knee. The lamp beside the bundle guttered but stayed alight.

No one moved until Saikou lowered his hand.

Nalia’s rope-burned palms were open in front of her. Saran was saying the same words repeatedly under his breath.

Riarhin caught his shoulder.

He stopped.

“Is that in your book?”

He shook his head.

She let go. Her anger had nowhere useful to land.

They ate again. Nobody wanted the food. Aurine put it into their hands and watched until it was gone.

The remaining adjustment could be made from the passage. It took longer than the first because Saran could no longer hold the fine marks in focus. Nalia read them back to him. Twice he corrected her; once she made him compare the copy before accepting the correction.

Riarhin watched the chamber through the slit. With each small movement of the plate, the line of light lengthened. Suspended pieces of stone settled lower, then rose again. One disappeared. She could not tell whether it had passed behind something or ceased to be.

At last the line reached the point where it had begun.

It went round again without breaking.

For three circuits they stood still.

Then Saikou put the splinter of wood upon the marked place. It remained on the sill. He moved it to the second mark. It remained there too.

Saran reached for the lever which would close the attendant’s branch.

“Not yet,” Nalia said.

He stopped with his hand above it.

They still had to bring the bundle from the waiting shelf. No test they had made could tell them how the chamber would behave when it was carried towards the cradle.

Riarhin went to fetch her sword.

Chapter 45 — The Return

Cadan took the maintenance ledge while the amulet remained on its shelf.

He moved differently from Riarhin. He was heavier and had less room to turn, but he did not look down. Saikou held the boundary, Berent the rope. At the broken section Cadan waited for a breath between phrases and stepped across. He reached the station, crouched and fastened the second line around a carved stone post.

His signal came back through the rope.

Riarhin went by the inner stair.

It descended from the sleeping passage to the root of the receiving bridge. Saran had believed it blocked. Aurine had found its door behind the fallen wooden screen while they were searching for a way out. They cleared only enough to pass. The lowest flight was narrow, but intact; the broken steps seen from above belonged to the separate service stair below Cadan’s station.

Riarhin walked it twice without the bundle. She learned where her shoulder touched the wall and where the last step was shallower than the others. At the bottom, the bridge ran out towards the pale platform, ending at the gap she had seen from above.

A shallow tray stood on the platform beyond it. Three upright pieces surrounded the tray. They were shaped to close inward.

The cradle.

She stood looking at it until Cadan called softly from the station above.

“The line is ready.”

She nodded and returned to the passage.

They had made a sling from a piece of spare cloth, with separate cords for carrying and releasing it. The wrapped amulet would lie inside. Riarhin could lower it onto the tray and pull the cloth free without touching the metal. They had practised with a stone until Nalia was satisfied that the cords would not catch.

The practice stone lay beside the real bundle. Riarhin looked from one to the other.

Neither moved.

Saikou came to stand beside her. There was a smear of soot on his jaw. Aurine had tied his sleeve back where it had begun to fray.

“When you reach the bridge, wait for me.”

“Yes.”

“If the line draws you back, come back.”

She nodded.

He looked towards the recess. “I should have brought Amari here.”

Riarhin turned to him.

“Years ago,” he said. “When it was a question in a book. When I could still have asked him.”

She stood beside him until he took a breath and reached for the Gate.

Then she lifted the bundle into the sling.

The sound began at once.
