It was low enough to be felt in the chest, and within it Riarhin heard another sound, a thin uneven tapping. She thought of the little shoes in the first room. She thought of a child alone behind a door.

Saikou’s voice cut through it.

“Look at me.”

She did.

“Carry it.”

She went down the stair.

The lamp in her right hand showed her the steps. The sling hung from her bandaged left. She had shortened its cords so that the bundle would not strike her knees. Behind her, Aurine paid out the first rope from the passage. Above the bridge, Berent controlled the line which passed through Cadan’s station and down to her harness.

She could no longer see any of them.

At the bottom she stopped.

The chamber was brighter. Light ran through joints in the floor and climbed the columns. The suspended stones had begun to move towards the platform, slowly enough that she could walk between their paths if she had been foolish enough to try. Far below, the dark holding altered each time she looked at it.

Saikou spoke from the observation doorway.

A boundary rose along the sides of the bridge. It had the depth of night and the narrowness of a blade. On its farther side the floating fragments stopped. One pressed against it, trembling.

Riarhin set down the lamp.

She walked.

The bridge was wider than the ledge. Her body did not believe that. Each step required an argument with the part of her that wanted to crouch and hold the stone. The sling swung gently beside her leg. She kept it steady with her right hand.

Halfway across she heard Berent call.

She stopped. The rope moved against her back, then slackened by a finger’s breadth. Cadan called something she could not distinguish. Above, Saikou’s voice continued without pause.

The platform was close now. She could see scratches in the tray where something had once been dragged across it. The three upright pieces were open, each ending in a blunt curved lip.

She reached the gap.

It was less than a long stride, wider than she wanted to cross with one hand occupied. She lowered herself to one knee. The sling could reach the tray from here if she leaned far enough, but she would have no way to see whether the cloth caught beneath the bundle.

They had planned for her to cross.

She stood.

The bridge moved under her.

She did not see it bend. The platform simply became farther away. A pace became two, then a distance she could not jump. The line at her back pulled tight. She staggered and caught herself before the edge.

“Back!” Berent shouted.

She obeyed.

The second step landed where the first had been. She looked down and saw the same chipped mark beneath her boot. She took another step. The chip remained.

The rope was cutting into her waist.

Riarhin began to run towards the stair. The stone moved beneath her feet with the smoothness of water. She made no progress. Beyond the dark boundary, one floating block pressed through far enough to show its sharp edge.

Saikou’s voice rose.

She stopped running.

“Pull!” she shouted.

The line lifted her off her feet.

For a terrible instant she hung over the bridge, sling clamped against her chest, with nothing beneath her boots. Then Cadan’s line drew her sideways towards the maintenance station. She struck the low wall shoulder first. Hands caught the harness and hauled her over.

Cadan rolled away from her. Blood ran from one nostril. He had wrapped the line around his forearm and the skin was torn where it had tightened.

Riarhin pushed herself onto her knees.

The bridge below them was still there. It looked exactly as it had before.

“Do not go back on it,” Cadan said.

“I won’t.”

She was almost shouting. She could not hear herself properly.

Above, Nalia called for Saikou to stop. His answer came in a language Riarhin did not know. The dark boundary sagged towards the bridge but held.

He could not stop while she carried the thing here.

She looked at the platform. From the station it lay below them and a little to the left. A person could be lowered onto it. They had discussed that route and rejected it because the line would pass close to the open attendant’s branch.

Saran was at that branch now.

Riarhin raised her head.

“Close it!”

Nalia turned towards her. Even from this distance Riarhin could see that she had not understood.

“The branch! Close it now!”

Saran stood motionless beside the lever.

The line of light climbed towards his seat. His unlit lamp stood waiting in the socket. Riarhin saw him look at it, saw his hand begin to move in that direction, and felt the old murderous certainty rise in her.

Then he struck the locking piece with the heel of his hand.

The stone pin dropped.

It went farther than she expected, sinking below the surface until only a dark hole remained. The lamp split from rim to base. Saran caught one half before it fell, cut his hand and let it go.

The light left him.

Cadan had already shifted the rope.

There was no time to practise the new descent.

Berent could see enough to control the haul. Cadan could see the platform. Riarhin had to believe both of them. She climbed over the station wall, turned to face the stone and let the harness take her weight.

“Lower.”

The rope moved.

She kept the sling high against her chest. Her shoulder hurt where she had struck the wall. The bandage on her palm had come loose, and blood made the carrying cord slippery.

Below, the platform turned.

She shut her eyes, opened them again. Its relationship to the walls had not changed. The scratches in its surface moved beneath her as though she were being lowered towards running water.

“Keep going,” she said.

No one could hear her from there.

She descended another arm’s length.

The low tapping returned. This time she knew the sound. A ring against the side of a cup. Veralyn turning her hand while someone spoke, bored, listening, deciding what might be made of the person standing before her.

Riarhin gripped the sling.

The sound became a child coughing. Then a woman laughing, then nothing she could name. It filled her with the wish to turn her head and find who was there.

She watched the tray.

Her boot touched stone.

For a moment she had weight again. She bent her knees, lowered the sling and saw the bundle settle in the shallow hollow.

The three upright pieces moved.

“Wait,” she said, foolishly, to the cradle.

She pulled the release cord. The outer sling came free. Tigist’s linen remained wrapped around the amulet. One fold had caught against the curved lip on the left.

Riarhin could reach it.

She could also see that the upright piece was closing faster than her hand could withdraw.

She took the Last Blade from her belt and hooked the fold with its tip. The cloth lifted. Beneath it, green shone against brass.

It was beautiful.

Even here, suspended over that dark thing, with blood running down her wrist and people she loved straining against the mountain, she wanted one more look.

She tore the cloth free and pulled the knife after it. The closing edges clipped its point. A chip struck her wrist.

The cradle shut.

She thrust the Last Blade into its sheath and reached for the haul line.

Light passed through her.

She felt neither heat nor pain. For an instant she saw the chamber from every place in which she had stood: the doorway, the ledge, the bridge, the station. She saw herself below Cadan, herself beside Saikou, herself climbing towards the terrace. Each figure moved away from her. The one on the terrace went over the edge and did not return.

Then the line jerked her upward.

She screamed. She did not know whether it was fear or the harness crushing the breath out of her. Her boots struck the station wall. Cadan caught her by the coat and Berent hauled again, and she was lying across the parapet with the empty linen crushed against her mouth.

Below, the chamber went dark.

Chapter 46 — A Door Still Closed

Riarhin could not make her legs work.

Cadan pulled her clear of the wall and sat her against the stone post. Her boots scraped uselessly along the floor. She looked at them with bewilderment, then tried to lift one knee and felt the pain in her hip arrive all at once.

“Give it a moment,” Cadan said.

He was winding cloth around his injured forearm. He used his teeth to hold one end. Riarhin watched him until she remembered there were other people.

“Saikou?”

Cadan looked up towards the observation opening.

No voice answered from there.

Riarhin tried to stand. Her leg folded. Cadan caught her before she struck the post.

“Wait.”

“Call him.”

Cadan did. Berent’s answer came after a pause.

“Alive.”

She sat down again.

A little light returned to the platform.

It ran round the outer band without breaking. Nothing hung in the air above it. The pieces of stone lay below the bridge, where they had fallen when the dark holding drew back behind its enclosure. Riarhin could hold the enclosure in sight now: its bands, the joints between the stones, a replacement block paler than its neighbours.

She drew the Last Blade far enough to see the broken point. Kargath’s knife. Her thumb found the old notch in the grip, and stayed there until Cadan called her name.

She pushed it back into the sheath.

They remained at the station until her legs would bear her. Nalia was watching through the observation slit. She called the passage of each circuit of light, then stopped when Aurine told her that five was enough. Saran checked the lowered pin from where he could see it without approaching the seat. It had disappeared into its housing. The split lamp lay beside him.

Saikou had not risen. Aurine spoke to him in a low voice. Eventually he answered.

“Wood,” Riarhin heard.

Nalia put the splinter on the first mark. It stayed there. On the second, it stayed again.

The waiting shelf was empty. Nothing moved in the room around it.

Those were the things they had come prepared to look for. Riarhin wanted something else, a word spoken from the mountain, a certainty large enough to encompass what they had done.

Berent called down the rope.

“Can you climb?”

She got her feet beneath her and tried.

The ledge would not let them back.

Cadan went first, feeling each foothold before trusting it. At the first break he held out the end of a spare cord. It parted cleanly, the cut so quick that the loose end remained in place before drifting away.

He drew back.

The ward Saikou had held aside was active again. The restoration had done nothing to soften it.

Riarhin leaned against the station wall. There was a second route: down the damaged service stair, across the root of the bridge and up the intact inner stair. The missing lower steps made the descent dangerous, but the rope could cover that distance.

She looked towards the bridge.

Cadan shook his head. “We do not have to cross it.”

He was right. The service stair met the chamber wall on their side of the bridge’s root. She made herself follow the route with her eyes until she could see it independently of what had happened there.

They called the plan to Berent. He moved the haul line while Nalia watched the route below. No one asked Saikou to stand.

Riarhin descended first. This time the stone stayed still. At the missing steps she hung for a moment, searching with one boot until she found the broken lower tread. She set her weight on it, breathed and moved down.

Cadan followed.

At the bottom they found another defence.

A bronze arm extended across the stair, ending in a hooked blade. It emerged from a figure carved into the wall, one of the attendants whose worn faces lined the outer doorway. Its mate stood opposite. Between them, the path narrowed to a passage no wider than Riarhin’s shoulders.

The left arm moved when she approached.

She stepped back. It returned to its place.

Cadan lifted a broken piece of stair and pushed it towards the gap. Both arms struck. The stone split. One half fell beyond the figures, the other at his feet.

He looked at the shattered piece. “Not that.”

Riarhin sat down upon the last whole step. She could see the upper pivot of the nearer arm. Something ran behind it into the wall, too deep to reach and too strong to cut. She could break the arm if she had tools and time, perhaps. She did not know what else would break with it.

Above them Nalia called a question. Riarhin told her what they had found.

Saran’s answer came faintly down the shaft.

“Below the hands.”

She stared at the figures.

“Say it again.”

“The water went below the hands.”

At the base of the wall a groove ran beneath the carvings. It was broad enough for a cleaning channel and deep enough for a person to lie within it, if that person could bear the weight of the arms moving overhead.

Riarhin took a strip from the ruined coat and pushed it forward on the end of a splinter. The arms stayed still.

She tried higher. They struck.

Cadan knelt beside her. He watched her test the lower space twice more, then lay down in it.

“Wait.”

He looked back.

She pulled the coat from her shoulders and rolled it around his injured forearm. “You will catch that on the stone.”

He smiled at her, tiredly this time, and began to crawl.

The arms did not move until he was nearly clear. His pack rose as he drew one knee forward. Bronze scraped across it, cut through the upper strap and drove the whole thing sideways. Cadan flattened himself, reached back and cut the remaining strap with his knife. He left the pack beneath the arms and crawled out.

Riarhin waited until he was standing safely beyond them.

She took off her own pack, unfastened Remorse and pushed both ahead through the channel. The sword lay flat. The pack caught once, and she shifted it with her boot before lowering herself into the groove.

Stone pressed against her bruised hip. She moved a finger’s breadth at a time. Halfway through she had to turn her head to breathe.

The bronze blade passed above her ear.

She lay still until it withdrew.

Then she crawled on.

Aurine had Saikou sitting upright when they reached the passage.

He looked older than he had that morning. His mouth was dry, his eyes slow to follow them. Riarhin knelt before him and took one of his hands. It was cold.

“We’re out of the chamber.”

He nodded.

“It is holding.”

Another nod.

“We are leaving.”

His fingers closed around hers.

Berent had repacked what they could carry. Cadan’s lost pack meant less bedding and food, though the remaining provisions would get them to camp. Nalia carried the records inside her coat. Saran stood beside the split ceremonial lamp with one half in his hand.

Riarhin looked at it.

He set it down.

They helped Saikou through the sleeping cells. At the fallen shutter he stumbled and swore with such force that Berent laughed in surprise. Saikou looked at him, offended, then began to laugh too. It lasted only a moment. He leaned more heavily against them afterwards.

The terrace was full of late light.

Riarhin had forgotten there could be so much sky.

Getting Saikou down took nearly everything they had left.

He could use his legs, but could not be trusted to keep hold of the rope. They lowered him in a sling between two lines, one controlled from above, the other by Cadan on the lower shelf. Aurine went beside him as far as the broken section, then climbed down ahead. Riarhin watched from the parapet with her jaw clenched so hard that it hurt.

Nalia followed. Saran went after her. Berent remained until the last person was clear, then brought down the running end of the rope and left the anchor behind.

By then the light had begun to leave the basin.

They crossed the traverse on their hands and knees. Saikou moved between Aurine and Cadan. The new slide made the old approach unusable, but the upper rib held, and beyond it they found the marks Somo had left: three stones turned pale side up, a strip of cloth tied where the path bent out of sight.

Riarhin touched the cloth as she passed.

A bell sounded above them.

Dorun stood on the ridge, one arm raised. Behind him Somo was already coming down with a coil of rope across her shoulder.

Riarhin sat where she was. She could not have stopped herself if anyone had ordered it.

Somo reached them, counted, looked at their faces and began giving instructions. Dorun took Saikou’s other side. Warm drink appeared in a vessel. Somebody loosened Riarhin’s harness.

“Both of you,” Riarhin said to Somo.

“Yes.”

“I saw you on the ridge.”

“We saw you.”

Somo put the vessel into her hands and closed her fingers around it.

The drink was salty and too hot. Riarhin drank it anyway.

Part VI — The Way Down

Chapter 47 — The Descent

Somo would not let them leave the next morning.

Riarhin had expected to argue. She woke with a plan for packing, found that someone had removed her boots and went back to sleep before locating them. When she woke again, the tent was warm with bodies and the smell of food. Nalia was sitting cross-legged beside the entrance, trying to comb the dust out of her hair with her fingers. Cadan was asleep with one arm outside his blanket. Aurine had dressed it properly. The bandage reached almost to his elbow.

Saikou lay facing the other side of the tent. Riarhin watched until she could see him breathing.

“Eat,” Somo said.

Riarhin took the bowl.

Her hunger frightened her. She finished everything, scraped the bottom and found herself watching the pot. Somo filled the bowl again without comment.

Outside, Dorun was repairing a fastening. Berent sat beside him with his head bent over something small. The two men spoke quietly, then Dorun laughed.

“What?” Riarhin asked.

Berent held up the spoon.

He had straightened it badly. The bowl now sat at an angle to the handle.

“It will reach round corners,” Dorun said.

Riarhin laughed until the food in her mouth nearly choked her.

They stayed two nights at the outer camp.

Saikou slept through most of the first day. On the second he walked as far as the ridge with Dorun and came back furious at how far it had felt. Aurine examined him, listened to what he said and asked him to do three ordinary things with his hands. He did them. Then she asked him to eat before attempting anything more ambitious.

He obeyed with poor grace.

Riarhin went to the ridge alone that afternoon. Somo watched from the tent until Riarhin reached the turn.

The temple stood across the basin. The slide had altered the shape of its approach. From this distance she could not see the terrace or the little figures at the doorway. Wind lifted a plume of snow from the upper roof and carried it away.

She waited for a feeling.

She had imagined, without admitting it, that the moment would know what it was. She would look at the mountain and feel the weight leave her. Instead her hip hurt, her palm itched beneath the bandage and she was cold through the place where her coat had been cut away.

She looked once more at the dark opening.

Then she went back to the tent. Nalia had saved her a bowl.

At Pema’s camp, the animals recognised Dorun’s voice before the people saw him.

A great shaggy head rose above a wall. Something bellowed. Pema emerged from between two tents carrying a length of harness, took one look at the company and turned back inside. By the time they reached her, sleeping places had been cleared and water was heating.

She asked where the missing pack was.

“In the temple,” Cadan said.

“Will you want it?”

“No.”

“Good. I was not going to fetch it.”

She inspected his boots and sent a child for another pair. They were too large, but whole. Cadan thanked her as gravely as if she had given him a house.

Saran remained outside until Pema called him by name. He entered with his hands empty. When Pema passed him a bowl, he looked at Riarhin before taking it. They offered him the same food as the others. He sat down carefully and ate very little.

That evening Riarhin found him behind the tent, washing the bowl he had been given.

“I will tell them,” he said.

She did not ask whom he meant.

“Tell the part you would rather omit.”

He kept washing. The bowl was already clean.

The descent took longer than the ascent between camps, though the days of acclimatisation were no longer necessary.

They moved slowly because they could. Somo chose the easier ground when it cost them half a day. Once she turned them back from a crossing which Dorun had made safely that morning; meltwater had risen in the interval. Berent began to say that he could carry the lighter packs through, looked at Saikou and stopped. They slept in a shelter smelling strongly of old animals and crossed after dawn.

Riarhin began to hear birds again.

At first it was a single sharp call from a patch of scrub. Later there were whole arguments going on above the path. She noticed green on a wet stone, then a flower so small she would have stepped over it on the way up. She did not point it out. She stopped long enough to look, and when she raised her head the others were waiting at the bend.

Dawa met them at the lower camp. She had taken another party up and returned two days before. She embraced Somo, examined Berent’s new scar and asked what had happened to Cadan’s pack.

He told her.

“Expensive place,” she said.

“Yes.”

She had letters. None was from Ailsa. Riarhin hid her disappointment badly enough that Dawa smiled and told her the coastal packet had gone by the river road, not this one.

There was a message for the Vent-Prior and a question about the Ninth Seal Mine. Saikou read both. He sat a long time with the second paper upon his knee, then dictated an answer to Nalia. He described what had happened to Hadrik, what had been left holding beneath the mine and which questions required a keeper to visit the actual place.

When he reached the end, Nalia asked whether he wanted to add his titles.

“Enough for them to know who is answerable.”

She wrote his name.

Vesk’s daughter had moved the stove again.

This time the table had moved with it, and Vesk had taken to sitting beneath the window, where he claimed the light was better. Tserin said this was true for approximately one hour of the day. He ignored her and offered the company food.

They gave the archive three days.

On the first, Nalia wrote while each of them described the same sequence. The accounts disagreed. Riarhin remembered the light passing through her after the cradle closed; Berent had seen her stop moving before it closed. Cadan had been watching the rope and could not say. Saikou remembered speaking throughout the return. Aurine said there had been a silence long enough for her to think he had died.

Nalia left the disagreements in.

Saran described the attendant’s branch. He gave the disputed reading, the reason he had refused the first test and the final position of the locking pin. His voice failed once. Tserin brought water and waited. No one helped him over the missing words.

On the second day they discussed custody.

Vesk wanted the repaired copy with its annotations. Tserin wanted an account that could be shown to the people who maintained the routes without giving every visitor instructions for reaching the chamber. Nalia asked who would decide whether a proposed inspection was an inspection or another restoration of an office.

Vesk put down his cup.

“Not a traveller who wishes to return to the sea.”

Nalia went quiet. Then she bowed her head.

“No. But I would like to know before I go.”

The argument lasted until the food grew cold.

They settled upon copies held here and at Metho’s house. The route keepers would receive a shorter warning: what the amulet’s approach had disturbed, which places remained unsafe. The full account would require two custodians to release it, and neither the Outer Lamp nor a foreign patron could appoint both. Vesk would take that proposed arrangement to the neighbouring keepers. He would not promise their agreement in advance.

Riarhin listened from beside the stove. She had imagined leaving the papers and going. Now she watched Tserin make a list of people who would need to be fed while they discussed them.

On the third day they carried the first copy into the cold chamber.

The archive was still damaged. Water had been diverted from the worst crack, but a stretch of wall needed rebuilding before the next hard season. A mason was measuring it with a string. He asked Vesk to move. Vesk moved.

Tserin placed the new account in a dry chest rather than among the frozen slabs.

“It needs reading first,” she said.

Riarhin put her hand on the closed lid. Then she took it away.

Metho made Saikou inspect the winter-store threshold before she would hear about the temple.

He knelt at the place they had repaired. The cold line held. The watercourse remained clear. He asked after the little barefoot child whose tracks had helped them find the fault and learned that she had acquired boots, disliked them and been caught trying to exchange them for a painted cup.

Saikou laughed. Metho did not. They were good boots.

He stayed another day to help check the outer stones. Riarhin found him there with a bucket between his knees and the sleeves of his borrowed coat rolled up. He looked tired. He also looked absorbed in what his hands were doing.

Peli’s message reached them at Metho’s house.

Toran had seen the healer in the lower town. The first work had eased the stiffness enough for him to open his hand in the morning without using the other to force it. More would be needed. Peli had arranged a place for him to stay through the next treatment, and he had found paid work which did not require lifting. There was a receipt for the money Riarhin had advanced, with the unused part enclosed.

She counted it twice, angry at the smallness of the sum, at its return, at her own wish to send it back in a way that might oblige them to thank her again.

Aurine read the message and smiled at one line.

“What?”

“He is doing the exercises.”

Riarhin looked at the paper. Peli had written that her son complained about them every day.

She wrote a reply asking whether the healer wanted the account of Dema’s work, and left the money in her purse.

Saikou spoke to his mother from a room Metho lent him above the dyeing yard.

He asked Riarhin to remain. She sat near the open window while he prepared the mirror. Below, cloth moved between women’s hands, bright blue water running into a gutter. Someone was singing and getting the same line wrong each time. Another voice corrected her. The singer ignored it.

The mirror took a long time to answer.

When it did, Saikou’s face changed. Affection moved through the exhaustion before he could conceal it. He listened, smiled once and lowered his head.

Riarhin looked out of the window.

Later his voice became formal. He described the completed return, the tests they had made and the custodians who held the account. He did not give the route, the positions of the controls or the words spoken over them.

The room cooled. Riarhin drew the borrowed coat around herself.

“No,” Saikou said.

He listened.

“No one has given me the right to do that.”

The conversation continued beyond the point at which Riarhin could understand it. When it ended, Saikou covered the mirror and remained seated with his hands upon the cloth.

“What does she want?”

“Me in Kassamira. To give an account.”

“When?”

“We have not agreed.”

Riarhin waited. He rubbed a thumb along the edge of the covered frame.

“She asked whether I was eating.”

“Are you?”

He looked at her, then laughed without much strength.

“Apparently everyone intends to ask me that for the rest of my life.”

She went downstairs and brought him a bowl.

They found the remaining people of the Outer Lamp in a rented hall beyond the lowland road.

Saran had sent word ahead from the archive. Three had come out to meet him; the others waited beneath a patched awning. There were indeed two children. One ran towards him, stopped at the sight of the company and went back to stand beside an older woman.

Saran took off his shoes before entering the hall.

Riarhin remained outside. Through the open door she could see the lamps along the wall, each cleaned and trimmed. No two were alike. One was a cooking vessel altered to hold a wick. Another had been repaired with a band of wire.

He was still speaking when the light reached the lamps along the wall.

The older woman interrupted him. Her voice was sharp enough to carry into the road. Saran answered. She struck the floor with her stick and spoke again.

Nalia, beside Riarhin, folded her arms.

“Do you understand?” Riarhin asked.

“Enough.”

“What is she saying?”

“She wants to know whether he thought they needed a stolen office badly enough to risk them losing him.”

Inside, Saran had covered his face.

Riarhin went to sit beneath the awning. Cadan came with her. After a while one of the children brought them water and stayed to inspect his bandaged arm. He told her it was healing. She asked if it had hurt. He said yes.

Saran did not leave with them the next morning.

He came to the road carrying a packet of bread. He gave it to Aurine, then stood before Riarhin.

“I will answer if they call me to the archive.”

“They should.”

“Yes.”

She held out her hand. He took it. Neither said anything more.

Lower down, the air grew soft enough that Riarhin woke at night too warm beneath her blanket.

They passed new planting, repaired banks, a stretch of road where carts had cut so deep into the mud that everyone had to get down and walk. At Darava, Anju’s claim had been heard and partly upheld. The compensation was late. Devaki’s work continued on the wall; Arun had disputed which revenues should pay for it. Mira was elsewhere, engaged for a ceremony at which, according to the woman who gave them the news, she had already quarrelled over the food.

Riarhin bought fruit for Lali and found that the child had lost a tooth.

Lali showed her the gap with great pride. Then she asked whether there had been snow.

“Yes.”

“Did you bring any?”

“No.”

The disappointment was severe. Riarhin offered the fruit. Lali accepted it but made clear, by her silence, that the two things were not equivalent.

They stayed long enough to eat with Anju. When they left, she gave Riarhin a bundle for the road and told her to keep it away from the damp.

By the time they reached the river, she could smell the sea before she could see it.

Chapter 48 — A House Inhabited

There was a different gate.

Riarhin stood in front of it with her pack on one shoulder and stared at the new latch. The old one had lifted from the left. This one opened from the right. She was trying to decide whether they had come to the wrong entrance when Nalia observed that someone had painted the gate green.

“Yes,” Riarhin said. “I can see that.”

Nalia turned to help unload the cart.

Behind them the hired cart was being unloaded. Berent argued with the driver about which bundle belonged to whom. Cadan stood in the shade holding a pot someone had put into his hands. Saikou had sat on the low wall and closed his eyes. Aurine was paying the driver, who abandoned the argument at once and came to attend to the money.

Riarhin lifted the new latch.

The gate stuck.

She lifted harder.

“Push first,” a boy called from inside.

She pushed, lifted and nearly fell through when it opened.

The boy stood beneath the tree beside the well. He had grown. She saw that before anything else, and felt a small unreasonable grief for the version of him who had left on the voyage with a loose tooth and an opinion about the ship. This boy had longer legs, ink on his thumb and a scraped place beneath one knee.

He looked at her torn coat.

“Did you fall off the mountain?”

“Some of it.”

He considered this, then ran towards the house.

Riarhin remained in the courtyard. There were drying frames where the spare pots had stood. A length of cloth hung beneath the awning, its narrow dark border unmistakable. Someone had added a little bench beside the well. The bucket had a new handle.

Ailsa came out with a needle still in her hand.

She stopped at the top of the steps.

Riarhin had thought of what to say. On the road, during the slowest part of the descent, she had made several beginnings and rejected them. Now she could remember none.

Ailsa came down the steps. She put the needle into the front of her own sleeve and reached for Riarhin’s face.

“Oh,” she said.

Riarhin bent her head. Ailsa’s hands moved over her cheeks, the back of her neck, the place where the coat had been mended with mismatched cloth. Then she pulled her close.

The pack struck the ground.

Riarhin held on. She could hear people moving through the gate behind her and Berent telling someone to mind the wheel. Ailsa’s hair smelt of warm cloth and the oil she had used since Ubral. Riarhin put her face against it and did not lift her head until Ailsa loosened her arms.

“You’re thin.”

“I’ve been eating.”

“Then you can do it again.”

Savi took charge of the arrivals.

She sent the washing one way, the packs another and the people towards the shaded side of the courtyard. When Cadan attempted to carry his own bedding upstairs, she asked whether he intended to put mountain dust into a clean room. He apologised and brought it back down.

Iona appeared with cups. Mairi followed with a complaint about the number of cups, which became a complaint about nobody having sent an accurate arrival date, which ended when she saw Saikou trying to stand and pushed him back onto the bench.

The girl came last.

She had been at a neighbour’s house and was angry that her brother had seen them first. She crossed the courtyard with great dignity, embraced Riarhin quickly and then stood back.

“Have you got it?”

Riarhin reached into the inner pocket of her coat.

The familiar movement stopped her for a breath. The pocket on the other side lay flat. She put her hand into the correct one and brought out the shell.

The girl took it, examined the worn edge and looked inside.

“There’s dirt.”

“Mountain dirt.”

That improved it. She carried it away to show her brother.

Ailsa sat beside Riarhin and put a cup into her hands.

“You needn’t tell everything today.”

“I don’t think I could.”

“Good. I have things to tell you.”

She began with the gate. The old one had sagged until Savi trapped two fingers trying to close it. The carpenter had fitted the new latch the other way because that was how he preferred them. Savi had expressed her views. The carpenter had charged less.

Riarhin listened. She drank. The cup remained warm against her palms.

They ate late.

The children’s father arrived while the food was being carried out. He had been at the harbour and smelt faintly of tar. He greeted Riarhin, asked after the journey and did not insist upon an answer longer than she could give. Then he took the heavy dish from Ailsa and carried it to the table.

There were too many people for the usual places. They moved a smaller table alongside the larger one and discovered the legs were of different heights. Berent found a flat stone. Nalia objected that it wobbled. He found another. By the time everyone sat, the food nearest the kitchen had cooled and the food nearest the fire was too hot to eat.

Nobody cared for long.

The boy began explaining the coastal school. He had visited its yard, seen the instruments and been allowed to look through a device which made a distant boat appear close enough to touch. His father corrected one part of the account. The boy corrected the correction. Ailsa let them argue until the girl announced that her own tutor could read three kinds of writing, which was more useful than staring at a boat everybody could see already.

Saikou asked her which kinds.

She named two and invented a third.

He nodded gravely. Her father laughed into his cup.

Riarhin watched Ailsa serving food. She had acquired an ease with the household’s movements, reaching for things before they were requested, calling to someone inside without raising her voice. It hurt a little to see how much had happened without her. It pleased her too. The two feelings occupied the same place and would not separate.

Nalia was telling Iona about a dispute at the port. She had called on Revati before coming to the estate, using the delay while the cart was loaded to deliver a letter. Revati wanted a continuing agent who could speak to both the warehouse clerks and the people whose cargo they detained. Nalia had named a fee. Revati had laughed at it.

“Too much?” Berent asked.

“Too little. Apparently I should have known that.”

“Did you change it?”

“Before she could change her mind.”

Iona asked whether Nalia would take private commissions. Within a few minutes they were discussing the cloth beneath the awning. Riarhin looked at Ailsa, who smiled and took another mouthful before entering the argument.

At the far end of the table Cadan was listening to the girl describe a quarrel with her friend. He seemed entirely absorbed. When she demanded whether he thought she was right, he asked what the friend had said.

The girl sighed. It was evidently the least useful question an adult could ask.

That night Ailsa helped Riarhin out of the repaired coat.

She did not speak when she saw the bruising. Her fingers paused at the edge of the dark mark on Riarhin’s hip, then moved away. She brought water, washed the places Riarhin could not comfortably reach and put the basin down.

“Dema tomorrow.”

“It’s mostly bruises.”

“Then she can say so.”

Riarhin sat on the bed. The ugly yellow bird was still on the shelf. Someone had placed a little flower beside it in a cup.

“I broke the point off the Last Blade.”

Ailsa looked at the knife Riarhin drew from her belt. She held it towards the lamp.

“Can it be mended?”

“Shortened.”

“Will you keep it?”

“Yes.”

Ailsa sat beside her. They looked at the bird together.

“Did you finish?”

Riarhin drew a breath and had to let it out before she could speak.

“We left it where it belonged. It was holding when we came away.”

“Can you sleep?”

“I don’t know.”

Ailsa lay down and drew her with her. Riarhin turned carefully onto her unbruised side. For a while they kissed, stopping when a movement hurt and beginning again more gently. Later Ailsa’s hand rested warm against her back. Riarhin could hear the house settling around them, a door closing below, the children speaking when they should have been asleep.

She lay awake after Ailsa’s breathing changed.

There was no tapping in the room. Only a branch against the shutter when the wind reached it.

She slept before she had decided whether she could.

Dema came the following afternoon and was displeased by the state of several people.

She examined Riarhin’s palm, shoulder and hip, listened to the account of the old injuries and tested the arm she had healed aboard ship. The strength remained. The new damage would need care, but nothing she found required Riarhin to spend the next season behaving as if she had been broken beyond repair.

“Do less with this for a few days,” she said, touching the torn palm.

“I can use the other.”

Dema looked at her.

“I said do less.”

Berent received much the same instruction. Cadan’s forearm required longer work. Saikou was sent to eat, rest and return for another examination rather than attempt to demonstrate that he could still invoke.

Aurine stayed through the examinations, giving the history of each injury where it was needed. Dema asked her to remain afterwards. The two women spoke beneath the awning while Savi cleared the bowls.

When Aurine came back, she was smiling in a way Riarhin had not seen for some time.

“She knows someone who needs an assistant inland. Paid. With room to learn.”

“Do you want it?”

“I want to meet her.”

“When?”

“Next week.”

Riarhin nodded. Aurine waited, perhaps expecting a question about Nakato, perhaps something else.

“Will you eat with us before you go?” Riarhin asked.

“Yes,” Aurine said. “I would like that.”

They settled the travelling money three days later.

Nalia had the accounts. There were missing receipts, a payment made in goods whose value nobody quite agreed upon and Cadan’s pack, which he insisted had been worth very little. Berent named the things inside it until Cadan conceded that replacing them would cost more than very little.

Saran’s share was sent to the hall, addressed to him rather than to his order. The archive’s expenses went separately. Somo and Dorun had been paid in full, including the extra nights. Riarhin added the cost of a spoon. Berent protested. Dorun had given it to him, he said.

“Then buy him one,” Nalia replied.

They did not dissolve the company with a speech.

Nalia had work at the port. Aurine had her meeting. Berent wanted to see the coast road beyond the estate and had accepted a few days’ employment escorting a merchant’s family; the merchant had asked whether he could ride, not who his grandfather had been. Cadan had agreed to accompany him as far as a shrine where he wished to stay for a time.

Saikou had written to his mother. He would answer her summons when the terms of the journey were settled, and he would not bring the archive’s private account as part of his luggage. He showed Riarhin the letter before sealing it.

“She will dislike that.”

“Yes.”

“Will you go?”

He rested a hand upon the covered mirror. “I should see her.”

Riarhin did not try to tell him what that meant.

Later, while the others carried their cups away, he began repairing the strap of his bag. His fingers were still clumsy when tired. Berent came back, watched him miss the hole twice and held out his hand for the needle.

Saikou looked at him for a moment.

Then he gave it over.

Ailsa wanted to discuss the school.

They walked to the shore in the late afternoon, leaving the children with their father. Riarhin had expected the conversation sooner and had prepared too many careful things to say. Ailsa undid most of them by asking whether she could read the contract, rather than whether she approved of the school.

“Nalia should read it.”

“She has. I want you to read it too.”

“Then I will.”

They walked a little farther. Boats moved beyond the harbour mouth. A boy on the sand was trying to persuade a dog to release a length of rope. The dog plainly regarded the argument as the reason to keep it.

“There is a place near Iona’s new buyer,” Ailsa said. “Two rooms above the workroom. A little yard. We could keep the cloth there without having to clear it from the table every evening.”

“Are you leaving the estate?”

“Perhaps. Not tomorrow.”

Riarhin looked back towards the palms. The roof was hidden from here.

“Would you have room?”

“For what?”

“For me.”

Ailsa stopped walking.

Riarhin wished she had said it better. She wished she had asked with less need in her voice. Ailsa took her good hand before she could repair the question.

“Yes.”

They stood together while the boy lost the rope completely and chased the dog along the water’s edge.

“I don’t know how long,” Riarhin said.

“Then we will begin with dinner.”

On the way back they bought fish.

Ailsa chose them. Riarhin carried the parcel in her uninjured hand and tried to remember whether there was enough oil in the kitchen. She could have asked. Instead she enjoyed the small effort of remembering, the shelf, the vessel with the chipped lip, Savi saying that no one ever put it back where it belonged.

At the gate she pushed before lifting the latch.

The girl was in the courtyard with her shells spread across the new bench. She called Riarhin over to see the one she had carried. The mountain dirt had been washed out. Beside it lay a much larger shell, pink within, found by her friend that morning.

Riarhin admired both. The girl explained why hers was better, then admitted that she had promised to lend it for a day.

Inside, someone laughed. Berent was telling a story in which the spoon had become bent through an act of considerable courage. Dorun would have hated it. Riarhin hoped someone would tell him.

Ailsa took the fish to the kitchen and called for a clean board.

Riarhin washed her hands, keeping the dressing dry. She found the board beside the hearth, carried it to the table and moved the chairs to make room.

Reader’s Companion

A selective reference to the people and terms in this volume. Descriptions identify relationships and roles without recounting the ending.

Dramatis Personae

Ailsa — Riarhin’s partner.

Anju — A local traveller with knowledge and responsibilities beyond the expedition.

Aurine — A member of Riarhin’s company, with obligations of her own.

Dema — The healer engaged for the sea passage.

Lyra — Riarhin’s friend and a survivor of the Valoran court.

Mab Tulley — The barge-woman who carries the survivors at the opening.

Nakato Ira — Saikou’s mother, whose arrangements assist and complicate the voyage.

Riarhin — A half-Lethai traveller and former scout, left-handed by preference.

Saikou Ira — An invoker and exorcist of the Kahfagian shrines.

Places and Terms

Bronze Coast — The coastal region contested by trading and colonial powers.

Dhahara — The region through which the expedition travels.

Himadri — The mountains toward which the expedition travels.

Kalrantha — The amulet whose custody and history drive the journey.

Remorse — Riarhin’s double-edged elven sword.

The Last Blade — Riarhin’s other Ykrul duelling knife, distinct from Toothpick.

Toothpick — Riarhin’s name for the knife once called Fate’s Edge.
